chemistry.bigb
= Chemistry
{wiki}

Chemistry connects the composition and structure of <chemical substances> to their properties and transformations. Its central surprise is that rearranging the same <chemical atoms> can produce a different substance: <molecular hydrogen> and <molecular oxygen> can become <water> without changing which <chemical elements> are present. <Chemical bonds> explain the connections; <chemical reactions> describe the transformations; <stoichiometry> makes quantitative predictions from the bookkeeping.

This gives a useful route from an invisible world to measurable quantities. A <molecule> has a definite composition, such as two <hydrogen> <chemical atoms> and one <oxygen> <chemical atom> in <water>. A sample contains an enormous number of such <molecules>. Counting them through <amount of substance>, measured in <moles>, lets the same small-number ratios describe a macroscopic sample. The beauty is that the bookkeeping survives this huge change of scale.

\Include[chemistry-author]
\Include[chemistry-bibliography]

= Erlenmeyer flask
{c}
{parent=Chemistry}

= Conical flask
{synonym}

A <conical flask> has a narrow neck and a wider body that permits swirling a <chemical solution>, as in a <redox titration>.

= Radical
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

= Free radical
{synonym}

A <radical (chemistry)> has one or more unpaired <electrons>. In a simple <atomic orbital> or <molecular orbital> occupancy picture, an unpaired <electron> occupies a spatial orbital without an opposite-<spin> partner in that orbital. A dot such as $\mathrm H^{\bullet}$ marks this electronic property; it does not mean a missing <electron> or positive <electric charge>. <Free radicals> can be neutral or charged. Terminology also has a boundary: metal <ions> exhibiting <paramagnetism> are not normally called <free radicals> just because they have unpaired <electrons>.<Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry (IUPAC Recommendations 1994): R entries by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry, IUPAC Recommendations 1994: radical (or free radical), opening definition and metal-ion qualification]{ref}

Compare three species containing the same single <proton>. The <hydrogen atom> $\mathrm H^{\bullet}$ has one <electron> in its $1s$ <atomic orbital>, and is a neutral <radical (chemistry)>. The <hydrogen> <cation> $\mathrm H^+$ has no <electrons>, so no unpaired <electron>. The <hydrogen anion> $\mathrm H^-$ has two <electrons> paired in its $1s$ <atomic orbital>. It is charged but is not a <radical (chemistry)> in its <ground state>. With $N_p$ <protons>, $N_e$ <electrons>, and positive <elementary charge> $e$, the independent <electric charge> count is
$$
q=(N_p-N_e)e;\qquad
\mathrm H^+:q=+e,\quad \mathrm H^{\bullet}:q=0,\quad \mathrm H^-:q=-e.
$$
The <electron pair> and <electric charge> questions require different information. An odd $N_e$ ensures that not all <electrons> can be paired; an even $N_e$ alone does not ensure pairing, as the <molecular orbital> description of <molecular oxygen> illustrates.

\Image[radical-charge-and-orbital-occupancy.png]
{id=image-radical-charge-and-orbital-occupancy}
{title=<Electric charge> and unpaired <electrons> are different properties}
{description=Left: actual <ground state> $1s$ occupancies of $\mathrm H^+$, $\mathrm H^{\bullet}$, and $\mathrm H^-$. Right: stipulated two-orbital models with fixed $N_p=2$ illustrate neutral, positive, and negative <free radicals>; those boxes are not assignments for particular <chemical substances>. Arrows label <spin> occupancy, not particle trajectories.}

The distinction matters whenever a <chemical reaction> mechanism uses dots and charge signs together. <Homolysis> gives one bonding <electron> to each fragment, while <heterolysis> assigns both to one fragment. A <radical chain reaction> can pass an unpaired <electron> from one species to another without generating net <electric charge>. Detection then exploits the unpaired <electron>'s <spin> and <magnetic moment>, rather than merely measuring whether the sample carries an <electric charge>.

= Methyl radical
{parent=Radical (chemistry)}
{title2=$\mathrm{CH_3}^{\bullet}$}

The <methyl radical> is the neutral <radical (chemistry)> $\mathrm{CH_3}^{\bullet}$, with an unpaired <electron>. It carries the <methane chlorination chain> between its two propagation steps.

= Radical ion
{parent=Radical (chemistry)}

A <radical ion> is both a <radical (chemistry)> and an <ion>. Removing one <electron> from neutral <benzene> can produce $\mathrm{C_6H_6}^{\bullet+}$, a <radical cation>; adding one can produce $\mathrm{C_6H_6}^{\bullet-}$, a <radical anion>. These formulae specify net <electric charge> and an unpaired <electron>, without claiming that both reside on one <chemical atom>.<Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry (IUPAC Recommendations 1994): R entries by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry, IUPAC Recommendations 1994: radical ion, benzene examples and dot/charge placement]{ref}

= Radical cation
{parent=Radical ion}

A <radical cation> has positive net <electric charge> and an unpaired <electron>.

= Radical anion
{parent=Radical ion}

A <radical anion> has negative net <electric charge> and an unpaired <electron>.

= Radical chain reaction
{parent=Radical (chemistry)}

A <radical chain reaction> regenerates a <radical (chemistry)> during propagation. Initiation supplies <free radicals>; propagation consumes one and produces another; termination removes chain-carrying <free radicals>. This explains why one initiating event can lead to many product-forming <chemical reactions>. The <chain-growth polymerization> article develops the corresponding growth of <polymers>.

= Methane chlorination chain
{parent=Radical chain reaction}

Light initiates <methane> chlorination by <photodissociation> of $\mathrm{Cl_2}$ through <homolysis>. The elementary bookkeeping is
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{initiation:}\quad &\mathrm{Cl_2}\xrightarrow{h\nu}2\mathrm{Cl}^{\bullet},\\
\text{propagation:}\quad &\mathrm{Cl}^{\bullet}+\mathrm{CH_4}\longrightarrow\mathrm{HCl}+\mathrm{CH_3}^{\bullet},\\
&\mathrm{CH_3}^{\bullet}+\mathrm{Cl_2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{CH_3Cl}+\mathrm{Cl}^{\bullet}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Here $h\nu$ denotes the <photon> <energy>. The first propagation step replaces a <chlorine> <radical (chemistry)> with a <methyl radical>; the second regenerates a <chlorine> <radical (chemistry)>. Adding the two propagation equations cancels both <free radicals> and gives $\mathrm{CH_4+Cl_2\longrightarrow CH_3Cl+HCl}$: <chloromethane> and <hydrogen chloride> are made while the chain carrier is regenerated.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/6.6 Radical Reactions>[Organic Chemistry by John McMurry, §6.6: chlorine photolysis and the two-step methane chlorination cycle]{ref}

\Image[methane-chlorination-radical-bookkeeping.png]
{id=image-methane-chlorination-radical-bookkeeping}
{title=<Methane> chlorination: initiation supplies carriers, propagation replaces them, termination removes them}
{description=The left panel follows the reacting <electron pair> in <homolysis>, then counts chain carriers through two propagation steps. The right panel shows recombination termination. Every <chemical species> in this example is neutral. Electron marks are bookkeeping, not a measured electronic <wavefunction> or a reaction-rate plot.}

For example, recombination $2\mathrm{CH_3}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\mathrm{C_2H_6}$ of <methyl radicals> makes <ethane> and a <covalent bond> from two unpaired <electrons>, removing two carriers. Recombination of two $\mathrm{Cl}^{\bullet}$ also terminates carriers; in the gas phase, stabilization of the product requires disposal of released <energy>, for example to a third collision partner. The figure omits that partner. Termination competes with propagation, and further chlorination can consume <chloromethane>, so this cycle alone does not predict a pure product or a measured <reaction rate>.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/10.2 Preparing Alkyl Halides from Alkanes: Radical Halogenation>[Organic Chemistry by John McMurry, §10.2, Figure 10.2 and following paragraphs: radical combination terminates the chain; further substitution gives product mixtures]{ref}

= Stable radical
{parent=Radical (chemistry)}

An unpaired <electron> does not set a universal <reaction rate>. A <stable radical> can retain radical character during isolation and storage under specified conditions; a persistent <radical (chemistry)> survives long enough under specified conditions to be observed. These descriptions concern lifetime and handling, rather than immunity to every possible <chemical reaction>. <DPPH> is a practical counterexample to the claim that all <free radicals> react immediately.

Two mechanisms help explain persistence. Spreading electronic character over several <chemical atoms>, represented by <chemical resonance>, can lower the <energy> relative to a localized description. Bulky surrounding groups can impede approach to a reactive site and slow recombination. Lower <energy> and slower <chemical kinetics> are different advantages: neither alone guarantees a long lifetime. A primary computational study explicitly separates these effects, using electronic and geometric descriptors; it does not directly measure every predicted lifetime.<A quantitative metric for organic radical stability and persistence using thermodynamic and kinetic features by Shree Sowndarya S. V. and others>[A quantitative metric for organic radical stability and persistence using thermodynamic and kinetic features, Shree Sowndarya S. V., Peter C. St. John and Robert S. Paton, 2021: Abstract and Introduction, thermodynamic stabilization versus kinetic persistence]{ref}

= 2,2-Diphenyl-1-picrylhydrazyl
{parent=Stable radical}
{wiki}

= DPPH
{c}
{synonym}

<DPPH> is a neutral organic <radical (chemistry)> used as a <spin> reference in electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR), also called electron spin resonance (ESR). Its unpaired <electron> remains bound within the <chemical species>: “free radical” does not mean a freely moving <electron>. The <DPPH electron spin resonance experiment> makes its <magnetic moment> observable.

= DPPH electron spin resonance experiment
{c}
{parent=2,2-Diphenyl-1-picrylhydrazyl}

The University of Iowa's <Electron Spin Resonance, University of Iowa laboratory protocol> (revised July 2007, Experiment and Resonance Data Analysis, printed pp. 2–6) specifies <DPPH> on cotton in a tube inside an RF coil, between <Helmholtz coils>. A bridge monitors RF absorption; an <oscilloscope> records this output and the voltage across a $1\,\Omega$ current-monitor resistor. Sweeping the coil current passes through the resonant <magnetic field>. The protocol describes two dips when the sweep revisits resonance; these are repeated crossings, not two distinct spectral lines.

Its calibration replaces the sample with a <Hall effect> probe, maps the <magnetic field> in $2\,\mathrm{mm}$ steps, and accounts for sample dimensions. At least four RF <frequencies> test the resonance relation. Sweep speed is adjusted to obtain comparable passages. Only a local peak-plus-background fit is prescribed, because the full shape is not Gaussian. The protocol also flags unexplained trailing spikes and possible electronics contributions. These controls constrain field and sweep errors; an empty-tube/cotton blank would be an additional background control, not a reported measurement in this protocol.<Electron Spin Resonance, University of Iowa laboratory protocol>[Electron Spin Resonance, revised July 2007: printed pp. 4–7, sweep behavior, field mapping, local fitting and anomalous spikes]{ref}

For an isolated effective <spin one-half> with dimensionless positive factor $g$, the basic resonance model is
$$
\Delta E=g\mu_{\rm B}|B|=h\nu,\qquad
g=\frac{h\nu}{\mu_{\rm B}|B_{\rm res}|}.
$$
Here $\mu_{\rm B}$ is the <Bohr magneton>, $h$ is the <Planck constant>, $\nu$ is the applied <frequency>, and $B_{\rm res}$ is the calibrated resonant <magnetic field>. A change in <frequency> should shift the resonance field proportionally in this model. The factor $g$ describes the electronic <magnetic moment> coupling, not the species' net <electric charge>.

A resonance supports the presence of detectable <paramagnetism>. It does not, by itself, identify an unknown <radical (chemistry)>: other species exhibiting <paramagnetism>, impurities, overlapping responses, and interactions with <atomic nuclei> can complicate interpretation. Conversely, absence of a detectable resonance need not mean absence of <free radicals>; a low <concentration>, a short lifetime, or an unsuitable measurement range can conceal them. The occupancy and chain diagrams are original bookkeeping illustrations; neither is a measured or simulated <DPPH> spectrum.

= Molecule
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

A molecule consists of two or more <chemical atoms> joined by <chemical bonds>, with zero net <electric charge>. This follows the terminology of the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/M04002[IUPAC Gold Book, molecule]{ref}. The <continuum approximation> uses bulk fields in place of individual molecular positions and motions.

= Molecular rotation
{parent=Molecule}

<Molecular rotation> changes a <molecule>'s orientation. Its <rotational kinetic energy> depends on the arrangement and <masses> of its <chemical atoms>. A <rigid rotor> freezes internal distances to isolate this motion; <rotational spectroscopy> tests the resulting <energy eigenvalues> through transitions between <quantum states>. The <molecular beam electric resonance> experiment provides a complementary probe of the coupling to an <electric field>.

= Rigid rotor
{parent=Molecular rotation}
{wiki}

A <rigid rotor> models rotational motion with fixed internal separations. Its use in the synthetic <Stark effect> figure omits vibrational motion and additional interactions in real <molecules>.

Rigidity fixes the <molecular geometry>, not the orientation, and does not require a spherical mass distribution. Remove motion of the <centre of mass>, choose <principal axes> of the <inertia tensor>, and write the classical <rotational kinetic energy> as
$$
T_{\rm rot}=\frac12\left(I_a\omega_a^2+I_b\omega_b^2+I_c\omega_c^2\right)
=\frac{L_a^2}{2I_a}+\frac{L_b^2}{2I_b}+\frac{L_c^2}{2I_c}.
$$
Here $I_a,I_b,I_c$ are the principal <moments of inertia>, $\omega_a,\omega_b,\omega_c$ components of <angular velocity>, and $L_a,L_b,L_c$ components of <angular momentum>. Quantization replaces these last components by <angular momentum operators>. Different principal <moments of inertia> produce different spectra: the single-constant <linear rigid rotor> is a special case, not a formula for every <molecule>.<Lecture 24 Supplement: Energy levels of a Rigid Rotor by Dudley Herschbach>[printed p. 1: principal-axis energy and classification; pp. 4–5: asymmetric rotors]{ref}

= Linear rigid rotor
{parent=Rigid rotor}
{title2=$E_J=\hbar^2J(J+1)/(2I)$}

For a <diatomic molecule>, let $m_1,m_2$ be the two <masses> and $r$ their fixed separation. The distances from the <centre of mass> are $r_1=m_2r/(m_1+m_2)$ and $r_2=m_1r/(m_1+m_2)$. A perpendicular axis through the <centre of mass> therefore has <moment of inertia>
$$
I=m_1r_1^2+m_2r_2^2=\mu r^2,\qquad \mu=\frac{m_1m_2}{m_1+m_2}.
$$
The <reduced mass> $\mu$ here is not the <electric dipole moment>, for which we use $\mathbf d$. For a longer <linear molecule>, replace $\mu r^2$ by $\sum_i m_i\rho_i^2$, where $\rho_i$ is each distance along the axis from the <centre of mass>. In the point-mass idealization, the axial <moment of inertia> is zero; turning about that axis changes no nuclear positions, so no independent axial rotational degree of freedom is included.

For the field-free <linear rigid rotor>, assume a fixed internal <quantum state> with no electronic <angular momentum> or electronic <spin>, and omit couplings to nuclear <spin>. The rotational <Hamiltonian> is $\widehat H_{\rm rot}=\widehat{\mathbf L}^{\,2}/(2I)$. Its orientation <wavefunctions> are <spherical harmonics> $Y_{JM}$, and its <angular momentum eigenstates> satisfy
$$
\widehat{\mathbf L}^{\,2}Y_{JM}=\hbar^2J(J+1)Y_{JM},\qquad
\widehat L_zY_{JM}=\hbar M Y_{JM},\qquad
E_J=\frac{\hbar^2}{2I}J(J+1)=hB J(J+1),
$$
where $J=0,1,2,\ldots$ and $M=-J,-J+1,\ldots,J$. Each $E_J$ belongs to $2J+1$ <angular momentum eigenstates> distinguished by the <magnetic quantum number> $M$, an instance of <degenerate energy levels>. The <Planck constant> is $h=2\pi\hbar$; $B$ is the frequency-valued <rotational constant>. Notice that the <energy eigenvalues> scale as $J(J+1)$, not $J^2$.

\Image[linear-rigid-rotor-levels-and-transitions.png]
{id=image-linear-rigid-rotor-levels-and-transitions}
{title=<Linear rigid rotor>: increasing gaps between <energy eigenvalues> and equally spaced transition <frequencies>}
{description=Original dimensionless model: $E_J/(hB)=J(J+1)$. Upward arrows show <photon absorption> with $\Delta J=1$; the sticks show positions only, not measured or predicted intensities. The $2J+1$ states at each <energy eigenvalue> are represented by one horizontal line.}

Subtracting consecutive <energy eigenvalues> gives
$$
\nu_{J\to J+1}=\frac{E_{J+1}-E_J}{h}=2B(J+1).
$$
Thus increasing level gaps produce a regularly spaced series of lines at $2B,4B,6B,\ldots$, with spacing $2B$. Allowed positions do not guarantee that every line is visible: <electric-dipole selection rules>, occupation of the initial <quantum state>, and instrument response also matter.

= Rotational constant
{parent=Linear rigid rotor}
{title2=$B=h/(8\pi^2I)$}

In this article the <rotational constant> is a <frequency>,
$$
B=\frac{h}{8\pi^2I}.
$$
Its dimensions are $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, since the <Planck constant> has dimensions $\mathrm{kg\,m^2\,s^{-1}}$ and a <moment of inertia> has dimensions $\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$. Some authors instead call $hB=\hbar^2/(2I)$ the energy-valued <rotational constant>; this is the convention in the earlier synthetic <molecular beam electric resonance> diagram. A third convention uses the <spectroscopic wavenumber> $\widetilde B=B/c$, with $c$ the <speed of light>. Always check units before using $\nu=2B(J+1)$.

If $B$ is known and the two <masses> are specified, the rigid two-atom model gives the <bond length> $r=\sqrt{h/(8\pi^2\mu B)}$. This is why <rotational spectroscopy> can constrain <molecular geometry>. A real fitted constant can depend on <molecular vibration>, so this inference alone does not establish an equilibrium <bond length>.

= Pure rotational electric-dipole selection rules
{parent=Linear rigid rotor}
{title2=$\Delta J=\pm1$}

For the field-free, spin-free <linear rigid rotor> in one fixed internal <quantum state>, the <electric-dipole interaction> is $-\mathbf d\cdot\mathbf E(t)$. A nonzero permanent <electric dipole moment> along the molecular axis allows pure rotational <photon absorption> with $\Delta J=+1$ and <photon emission> with $\Delta J=-1$. The <matrix element> between initial and final <quantum states> must be nonzero: the existence of an <energy> gap alone is insufficient. Relative to a chosen $z$ axis, <polarization of an electromagnetic wave> along $z$ selects $\Delta M=0$; transverse <polarization of an electromagnetic wave> permits $\Delta M=\pm1$.<Lecture 24 Supplement: Energy levels of a Rigid Rotor by Dudley Herschbach>[printed pp. 2–3: dipole transition integrals, polarization and selection rules]{ref}

<Hydrogen chloride> and <carbon monoxide> are examples with a permanent <electric dipole moment>. By symmetry, <molecular hydrogen> and <carbon dioxide> have no permanent <electric dipole moment> in the ideal symmetric internal state. They still rotate and have rotational <energy eigenvalues>, but the simple permanent-<electric-dipole> spectrum described here is absent. This restriction concerns this interaction and these assumptions, not every possible way of detecting <molecular rotation>.

= Isotope shift of a linear rotor
{parent=Linear rigid rotor}

Changing an <isotope> changes a <mass> and therefore the <moment of inertia>. If the <bond length> is held fixed, $B\propto1/\mu$ and every corresponding transition shifts by the same fractional amount. This leading inverse-<reduced-mass> scaling is also used in the more complete diatomic formulation.<Diatomic Spectral Database by National Institute of Standards and Technology/1Σ-Ground State Molecules>[§2a, equations (4)–(5): isotope dependence of rotational constants and reduced mass]{ref}

For a deliberately stipulated <carbon monoxide> example, take $m_{12}=12u$, $m_{13}=13u$, $m_{16}=16u$, $u=1.66053906660\times10^{-27}\,\mathrm{kg}$, $r=1.13\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm m$ for both isotopic forms, and $h=6.62607015\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}$. The integer <masses> are simplifying inputs, not accurate isotopic atomic <masses>; the shared <bond length> is an imposed model assumption. Calculation gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mu_{12,16}&=\frac{48}{7}u,& I_{12,16}&\approx1.45395\times10^{-46}\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2},& B_{12,16}&\approx57.7188\,\mathrm{GHz},\\
\mu_{13,16}&=\frac{208}{29}u,& I_{13,16}&\approx1.52080\times10^{-46}\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2},& B_{13,16}&\approx55.1817\,\mathrm{GHz}.
\end{aligned}
$$
In particular,
$$
\frac{B_{13,16}}{B_{12,16}}=\frac{\mu_{12,16}}{\mu_{13,16}}=\frac{87}{91}\approx0.956044.
$$
The heavier form's calculated $J=0\to1$ line is $110.363\,\mathrm{GHz}$ rather than $115.438\,\mathrm{GHz}$, a decrease of about $4.396\%$. These are model predictions from the stated inputs, not published measured <frequencies>. An <isotope> changes the spacing as well as the first line; the shift is not a fixed additive offset for the whole spectrum.

\Image[molecular-rotation-isotope-and-distortion-spectra.png]
{id=image-molecular-rotation-isotope-and-distortion-spectra}
{title=Synthetic <rotational spectroscopy> comparison: a heavier <isotope> compresses the rigid spectrum, while <centrifugal distortion> bends the spacing pattern}
{description=Original equal-height sticks for $J=0\to1$ through $4\to5$, using only the stipulated <masses> and <bond length> above. The lower panel additionally stipulates $D/B=0.001$ for the lighter form and compares $2B(J+1)-4D(J+1)^3$ with dashed rigid positions. Neither heights, distortion constant nor line <frequencies> are measured data; no thermal-population or instrument model is included.}

= Centrifugal distortion
{parent=Rigid rotor}
{title2=$E_J/h=BJ(J+1)-D[J(J+1)]^2+\cdots$}

Real <chemical bonds> can stretch as <molecular rotation> increases. The resulting larger <moment of inertia> lowers the <energy eigenvalues> relative to a <rigid rotor>. Within one vibrational <quantum state>, a leading effective correction is
$$
\frac{E_J}{h}=B_vJ(J+1)-D_v[J(J+1)]^2+\cdots,\qquad
\nu_{J\to J+1}=2B_v(J+1)-4D_v(J+1)^3+\cdots.
$$
Both $B_v$ and $D_v$ are in <frequency> units here; $v$ labels the vibrational <quantum state>. For positive $D_v$, the adjacent-line spacing becomes
$$
\nu_{J+1\to J+2}-\nu_{J\to J+1}=2B_v-4D_v(3J^2+9J+7),
$$
so the departures grow with $J$. <Molecular vibration> also changes the effective <rotational constant>: even the lowest vibrational <quantum state> does not have motionless <atomic nuclei>.<Diatomic Spectral Database by National Institute of Standards and Technology/1Σ-Ground State Molecules>[§2a, equations (7)–(8): distortion and vibration-dependent constants]{ref}

The quartic correction is a limited expansion, not a model to extrapolate to arbitrary $J$. It is small relative to the rigid contribution only while $(D_v/B_v)J(J+1)\ll1$; eventually higher terms, changed internal motion, or <chemical bond> breaking matter. In the synthetic plot, the largest upper-state label is $J=5$, giving a stipulated correction ratio of $0.03$. No universal maximum valid $J$ follows without a particular <molecule> and precision target.

The <quantum numbers> also have limits of meaning. Here $J$ describes spin-free rotation. In a <molecule> with electronic <spin>, rotation alone is commonly labelled $N$, while $J$ includes electronic <angular momentum>; including nuclear <spin> introduces additional total-<angular-momentum> labels and resolved splittings. An applied <electric field> can mix different field-free $J$ states through the <Stark effect>. For identical <atomic nuclei>, the <Pauli exclusion principle> can restrict combinations of rotational and nuclear-<spin> states. The simple $2J+1$ degeneracy and every-integer-$J$ ladder therefore describe the stated idealization, not all observed sublevels.<Diatomic Spectral Database by National Institute of Standards and Technology/1Σ-Ground State Molecules>[§2a: hyperfine discussion; §2b: distinct definitions of J, N and F]{ref}

= Rotational spectroscopy
{parent=Molecular rotation}
{wiki}

<Rotational spectroscopy> probes transitions between <molecular rotation> <quantum states>. In the simple <linear rigid rotor>, line spacing determines a <moment of inertia>, while an <isotope shift of a linear rotor> tests the mass dependence. More general <molecular geometry> requires several principal <moments of inertia> and more complicated assignments. The combination makes spectra sensitive fingerprints of both composition and shape.

= Fourier transform microwave spectroscopy
{parent=Rotational spectroscopy}

= FTMW spectroscopy
{c}
{synonym}

<Fourier transform microwave spectroscopy> excites gaseous <molecules> with a pulse of <electromagnetic radiation>, then records the emitted time-dependent <electric field>. A <Fourier transform> extracts transition <frequencies> from the decaying signal. In a cavity implementation, two facing mirrors form a resonator; tuned excitation, receiver electronics and digitization replace the molecule-counting readout of <molecular beam electric resonance>. The pulsed radiation prepares <quantum coherence> between rotational <quantum states>; it does not set the whole gas spinning at one classical <angular velocity>.

For documented laboratory context, <Evolution of Microwave Spectroscopy at the National Bureau of Standards (NBS) and the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) by F. J. Lovas and others> (§5, printed pp. 280–282) describes the NIST cavity instrument, phase-locked sources, mirror tuning and detector-mixer modifications. That account explains one instrument family; it does not establish the electronics of every later commercial <spectrometer>.

= Rotational monitoring of deuterium exchange
{parent=Fourier transform microwave spectroscopy}

A distinct experiment in <An Automated, Highly Selective Reaction Monitoring Instrument Using Molecular Rotational Resonance Spectroscopy by Ann Adele Byars and others> (Figure 1 and Figure 3) tracked exchange of <hydrogen> for <deuterium> in <ethyl acetoacetate> mixed with deuterated <methanol>. A pump circulated <chemical solution> through a valve; a calibrated loop injected aliquots into a temperature-controlled inlet, held at room <temperature> for this exchange experiment. <Neon> carried the vaporized sample to a cavity <spectrometer>. Separately recorded broadband spectra and fitted rotational <Hamiltonians> identified transitions for the four isotopic forms before targeted monitoring.<An Automated, Highly Selective Reaction Monitoring Instrument Using Molecular Rotational Resonance Spectroscopy by Ann Adele Byars and others>[apparatus and assignment paragraphs around Figure 1; deuterium-exchange method and Figure 3]{ref}

The readout was each assigned line's signal over reaction time, normalized by the sum of the four signals. Transitions with the same <quantum numbers> reduced response-factor differences. A solvent wash addressed observed sample carryover. The signals distinguished the unsubstituted, two singly substituted, and doubly substituted forms. Only one gas-phase structural form was detected; the authors explicitly caution that their readout does not recover the structural <chemical equilibrium> in <chemical solution>.<An Automated, Highly Selective Reaction Monitoring Instrument Using Molecular Rotational Resonance Spectroscopy by Ann Adele Byars and others>[deuterium-exchange paragraphs surrounding Figure 3; sample carryover and wash paragraph]{ref}

This example uses nonlinear <molecules>, so neither the single-constant <linear rigid rotor> nor the synthetic comb above is a fit to its spectrum. Its connection to the model is the sensitivity of <moments of inertia> to <isotope> placement. Line position helps assign a species; signal size additionally depends on sample transfer, initial <quantum state> populations and instrument response. Matching <quantum numbers> is a useful control, not a proof of identical response factors. These distinctions explain why resolving species and determining their <concentrations> are separate inference steps.

= Linear molecule
{parent=Molecule}

A <linear molecule> has collinear <atomic nuclei> in its idealized <molecular geometry>. Both a <diatomic molecule> and <carbon dioxide> can supply <linear rigid rotor> examples, although their <electric-dipole selection rules> need not give an observable permanent-<electric-dipole> spectrum.

= Diatomic molecule
{parent=Linear molecule}
{wiki}

A <diatomic molecule> contains two <chemical atoms>; <hydrogen chloride> and <carbon monoxide> are examples. Their fixed-separation <moment of inertia> is $I=\mu r^2$ in the <linear rigid rotor> model.

= Molecular vibration
{parent=Molecule}
{wiki}

<Molecular vibration> changes internal separations and <bond angles>, unlike the fixed internal <molecular geometry> of a <rigid rotor>. Its coupling to <molecular rotation> makes measured <rotational constants> depend on the vibrational <quantum state>.

= Molecular geometry
{parent=Molecule}
{wiki}

Why do <methane>, <ammonia>, and <water> have different shapes when each central <chemical atom> is surrounded by eight <valence electrons>? The answer separates the positions of <atomic nuclei> from the <electron domains> used in a <VSEPR model>.

<Molecular geometry> describes the three-dimensional arrangement of the <atomic nuclei> in a <molecule>, including <bond lengths> and <bond angles>. For a larger <molecule>, a shape name often describes just the neighbours of one central <chemical atom>. A <Lewis structure> records connections and electron bookkeeping; its flat layout does not fix <molecular geometry>.

In the <VSEPR model>, <electron pair geometry> includes both bonding <electron domains> and <lone pairs>, whereas <molecular geometry> counts only the <chemical atoms>. In <methane>, four <bond pairs> and no <lone pairs> give <tetrahedral molecular geometry>. In <ammonia>, three <bond pairs> and one <lone pair> give <trigonal pyramidal molecular geometry>. In <water>, two <bond pairs> and two <lone pairs> give <bent molecular geometry>. All three use the same ideal tetrahedral <electron pair geometry>; this does not make their actual <bond angles> equal. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/7-6-molecular-structure-and-polarity[Flowers, Theopold, Langley and Robinson, Chemistry 2e, §7.6: electron-pair and molecular geometries].

\Image[molecular-geometry-domains-and-atoms.png]
{id=image-molecular-geometry-domains-and-atoms}
{title=Four <electron domains>, three arrangements of <atomic nuclei>: <methane>, <ammonia>, and <water>}
{description=Original geometric constructions. The upper row is an ideal <VSEPR model> counting scaffold: orange LP markers identify <lone pairs>, not additional <chemical atoms> or measured <electron density>. The lower row shows only <chemical atoms>; <ammonia> and <water> use the sourced approximate <bond angles> discussed below. Sizes and <bond lengths> are arbitrary. Three-dimensional projections can change the apparent <bond angles> on the page. Dotted outlines are geometric guides, not extra <chemical bonds>.}

A static drawing also needs a physical interpretation. The <atomic nuclei> undergo <oscillations>; an arrangement at a minimum of <potential energy> and an experimentally averaged arrangement need not have identical <bond lengths> or <bond angles>. This distinction matters when comparing measurements with <quantum mechanics> calculations; see the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/BT07003[IUPAC Gold Book: experimental bond-length conventions]. <Gas electron diffraction> gives a concrete route from a laboratory signal to <molecular geometry> rather than inferring shape from a drawing alone.

= Bond angle
{parent=Molecular geometry}
{title2=$\theta$}

A <bond angle> is the <angle> between two <chemical bonds> sharing a central <chemical atom>. In H–O–H, the middle symbol identifies the vertex: the <angle> is at <oxygen>. Let $u$ and $v$ be the nonzero displacement <vectors> from that <atomic nucleus> to the two neighbours. The actual three-dimensional <bond angle> satisfies
$$
\cos\theta=\frac{u\cdot v}{\|u\|\|v\|},\qquad 0\leq\theta\leq\pi,
$$
using the <dot product> and <Euclidean norm>. Rotating the whole <molecule> preserves this expression; projecting it onto a page generally does not.

There is another useful way to find a <bond angle>. If the two <bond lengths> are $a,b$ and the distance between the outer <atomic nuclei> is $d$, the <law of cosines> gives
$$
d^2=a^2+b^2-2ab\cos\theta.
$$
For equal <bond lengths> $a=b$, this becomes $d=2b\sin(\theta/2)$. Thus a measured outer-atom separation contains information about a <bond angle> even though those outer <chemical atoms> are not directly joined by a <chemical bond>.

For a stipulated rigid, equal-<bond-length> model using <water>'s $104.4776^\circ$ equilibrium <bond angle>, $d/b\approx1.5811$, whereas a straight H–O–H arrangement would give $d/b=2$. These are calculated geometric ratios, not new measured distances. <Gas electron diffraction> illustrates how pair-distance information can constrain <molecular geometry>. Substituting independently averaged distances into the rigid <triangle> formula does not automatically recover an equilibrium <bond angle>, because averaging and this nonlinear calculation need not commute.

= Tetrahedral molecular geometry
{parent=Molecular geometry}
{wiki}

In <tetrahedral molecular geometry>, four neighbouring <chemical atoms> surround a central <chemical atom> in the directions of the vertices of a <tetrahedron>. In the regular idealization, the central <atomic nucleus> lies at the centre of a <regular tetrahedron>, and every pair of <chemical bonds> makes the same <bond angle>. <Methane>, $\mathrm{CH}_4$, provides the standard example: its <carbon> has four bonding <electron domains> and no <lone pair>. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/7-6-molecular-structure-and-polarity[Chemistry 2e, §7.6: tetrahedral methane].

The exact ideal <bond angle> follows from four directions proportional to $(1,1,1)$, $(1,-1,-1)$, $(-1,1,-1)$, and $(-1,-1,1)$. Each has squared <Euclidean norm> $3$, and each distinct pair has <dot product> $-1$. Hence
$$
\theta=\arccos(-1/3)\approx109.4712^\circ.
$$
This is a statement about a <regular tetrahedron>, rather than a universal exact value for every <molecule> labelled tetrahedral. It also explains why a cross-shaped flat drawing with $90^\circ$ gaps is misleading: four equal directions can spread out more evenly in three dimensions.

The same <vectors> sum to zero. Consequently, four equal bond-dipole contributions along those directions cancel. The symmetric <methane> <molecule> is <nonpolar>, but a tetrahedral shape alone does not imply a <nonpolar molecule>: replacing one neighbour can make the electronic distribution and bond contributions unequal. <Chemical polarity> depends on both <molecular geometry> and <electron density>. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/2-2-polar-covalent-bonds-and-dipole-moments[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §2.2: molecular dipoles and methane].

= Trigonal pyramidal molecular geometry
{parent=Molecular geometry}
{wiki}

In <trigonal pyramidal molecular geometry>, three neighbouring <chemical atoms> form a triangular base and the central <chemical atom> lies outside its plane. The pyramid has four <atomic nuclei>; its central <chemical atom> is the apex, not the centre of the base. <Ammonia>, $\mathrm{NH}_3$, has this arrangement. The https://cccbdb.nist.gov/exp2x.asp?casno=7664417&charge=0[NIST CCCBDB experimental ammonia entry, geometric data, citing Herzberg (1966)]{ref} lists an H–N–H <bond angle> of $106.67^\circ$, commonly rounded to $106.7^\circ$ or about $107^\circ$.

The <Lewis structure> of <ammonia> allocates eight <valence electrons> as three <bond pairs> and one <lone pair>. Four <electron domains> therefore suggest tetrahedral <electron pair geometry>; removing the <lone pair> marker leaves three <hydrogen> <chemical atoms> below <nitrogen>. The <VSEPR model> treats the <lone pair> as more space-demanding and predicts compression relative to $109.4712^\circ$, but it does not calculate the precise $106.67^\circ$ value. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/7-6-molecular-structure-and-polarity[Chemistry 2e, §7.6: lone-pair effects].

Three equal bond-dipole contributions in this pyramid cancel sideways, but retain a component along its axis. By contrast, three equal contributions lying in one plane at $120^\circ$ would cancel completely. This shows how a small change in <molecular geometry> can permit a permanent <electric dipole moment>. <Ammonia> is a <polar molecule>; NIST lists a measured magnitude of about $1.476$ debye in the same https://cccbdb.nist.gov/exp2x.asp?casno=7664417&charge=0[experimental entry, electric dipole moment, citing Shimizu (1970)]{ref}. The vector argument predicts allowed direction and cancellation, not the full measured magnitude.

= Bent molecular geometry
{parent=Molecular geometry}
{wiki}

In <bent molecular geometry>, two neighbours and their central <chemical atom> form a noncollinear <triangle>, with an interior <bond angle> strictly between $0^\circ$ and $180^\circ$. <Water>, $\mathrm H_2\mathrm O$, is the standard example. The https://cccbdb.nist.gov/exp2x.asp?casno=7732185&charge=0[NIST CCCBDB experimental water entry, internal coordinates, citing Hoy and Bunker (1979)] gives the equilibrium H–O–H <bond angle> as $104.4776^\circ$ with quoted uncertainty $0.0019^\circ$, usually rounded to $104.5^\circ$.

The <oxygen> in <water> has two bonding <electron domains> and two <lone pairs>. Its ideal <electron pair geometry> is tetrahedral, while the three <atomic nuclei> give <bent molecular geometry>. Three points always lie in a plane; this does not make the four-domain scaffold planar. Nor does the word bent by itself determine an <electron domain> count: the same atomic shape can arise from other domain arrangements.

In an illustrative model with two equal bond-dipole contributions of magnitude $p$ separated by <bond angle> $\theta$, their sum of <vectors> points along the bisector and has magnitude $2p\cos(\theta/2)$. At $180^\circ$ it vanishes; at $104.4776^\circ$ it is approximately $1.2247p$. This ratio is a geometric calculation, not a prediction of <water>'s measured <electric dipole moment>. The entire <electron density>, including contributions represented by <lone pairs>, matters. <Water> is a <polar molecule>; <chemical polarity> helps explain its interactions with other <polar molecules>. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/2-2-polar-covalent-bonds-and-dipole-moments[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §2.2: water, lone-pair contributions, and polarity].

= Gas electron diffraction
{parent=Molecular geometry}
{wiki}

= GED
{c}
{synonym}

<Gas electron diffraction> infers <molecular geometry> from the <diffraction> of <electrons> by a gaseous sample. It makes a useful experimental test of shape: distances between both bonded and nonbonded <atomic nuclei> affect the signal, so a proposed straight arrangement and a bent arrangement can be distinguished through their pair separations.

A concrete modern apparatus is the Bielefeld instrument described by https://doi.org/10.1515/znb-2015-0186[Reuter, Vishnevskiy, Blomeyer and Mitzel (2016), Gas electron diffraction of increased performance, §§1, 3 and 12]{ref}. A hot cathode supplies <electrons>; apertures and magnetic lenses shape the beam. A temperature-controlled nozzle injects sample vapour across it inside a <vacuum> chamber. Pumps and a cold trap reduce unwanted residual gas. A central beam stop catches the strong unscattered beam; off-axis scattered <electrons> expose imaging plates. A rotating sector can attenuate the intense small-angle region to accommodate detector range. The authors checked both the spatial scale of the plate scanner and its response to electron dose. These checks matter because an error in detector position or intensity calibration can masquerade as a structural difference.

\Image[molecular-geometry-electron-diffraction.png]
{id=image-molecular-geometry-electron-diffraction}
{title=<Gas electron diffraction>: apparatus, pair-distance inference, and a <bond angle>}
{description=Original schematic, not an engineering drawing or a recorded <diffraction> pattern. Rays identify possible incident and scattered paths; the detector is deliberately shown without invented rings or intensity traces. The lower <triangle> is a normalized rigid-model construction using $104.4776^\circ$; it illustrates a geometric inference, not distances measured in this apparatus.}

What is measured is detector signal as a function of position, converted to scattering <angle> using the sample-to-detector distance. Randomly oriented <molecules> give radial information. After extracting the molecular intensity, compare it with predictions from a candidate arrangement and adjust the <bond lengths> and <bond angles> to improve the fit. The https://www.york.ac.uk/chemistry/people/dwann/wanngroup/time-averagedged/[Wann Electron Diffraction Group's account of time-averaged GED, experiment and theory] describes radial averaging and this refinement. The apparatus does not photograph one <molecule> with labelled <atomic nuclei>.

Why can distances survive random orientation? In a simplified rigid pair model with separation $r$, write $s$ for the magnitude of the difference between incident and scattered <wave vectors>. Averaging the interference factor over uniformly distributed directions gives
$$
\frac12\int_{-1}^{1}\cos(srt)\,dt=\frac{\sin(sr)}{sr},
$$
with limiting value one at $sr=0$. This follows by integrating the <cosine>; $t$ is the <cosine> of the angle between the pair and the scattering direction. The <oscillations> depend on $r$, although the orientation has disappeared. A real calculation weights many pairs by their <scattering amplitudes> and includes nuclear motion. The <wave interference> explanation and superposed pair-distance contributions are discussed in the same https://www.york.ac.uk/chemistry/people/dwann/wanngroup/time-averagedged/[Wann group theory account]{ref}.

Once a consistent structural model supplies two equal <bond lengths> $b$ and an outer-atom separation $d$, the <law of cosines> converts these to a <bond angle>. For example, the illustrative $d/b=1.5811$ corresponds to approximately $104.5^\circ$, while $d/b=2$ is straight. This geometric step explains what a shape measurement is trying to establish; it is not a new <water> measurement.

There are two essential limits. First, recovering labelled three-dimensional positions from a summed radial signal is an <inverse problem>: composition, candidate connectivity, and other structural information can be needed to distinguish competing fits. Karle's authored review https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC432024/?page=-1[Structure determination of gaseous and amorphous substances by diffraction methods (1977), abstract] explains why too few independent observations make supplementary structural information indispensable. A good fit alone does not prove that the proposed arrangement is unique. Second, <atomic nuclei> are moving. Distances inferred by <gas electron diffraction> are motion-dependent averages; corrections are required before identifying them with a minimum-<potential-energy> arrangement. For experimental distance conventions, see the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/BT07003[IUPAC Gold Book: bond length]. These limitations explain why model assumptions and the averaging convention must accompany a precise <bond angle>.

= Nucleoside
{parent=Molecule}
{wiki}

A <nucleoside> combines a <nucleobase> with a <sugar>, without the <phosphate> groups of a <nucleotide>. <Adenosine> is the <nucleoside> built from <adenine> and <ribose>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26883/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: nucleosides and nucleotides].

= Adenosine
{parent=Nucleoside}
{wiki}

<Adenosine> consists of <adenine> joined to <ribose>. Adding one, two, or three <phosphate> groups at the <ribose>'s $5'$ position gives <AMP>, <ADP>, or <ATP>, respectively. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26883/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: the components of nucleotides].

= Nucleotide
{parent=Molecule}

A <nucleotide> contains a <nucleobase>, a <sugar>, and one or more <phosphate> groups. In <DNA> and <RNA>, linked <nucleotides> form a <polymer> whose <nucleotide sequence> carries information. The <sugar> is <deoxyribose> in <DNA> and <ribose> in <RNA>. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Nucleotide[NHGRI: nucleotide].

= Adenosine triphosphate
{parent=Nucleotide}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{ATP}$}

= ATP
{c}
{synonym}

<ATP> is a <nucleotide> consisting of <adenosine>, itself <adenine> joined to <ribose>, carrying three <phosphate> groups. From the <ribose> outward, these groups are labelled $\alpha$, $\beta$, and $\gamma$. <ATP> connects <energy>-supplying processes to <energy coupling> for synthesis and <active transport>; the same carrier can be regenerated and reused. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/6-4-atp-adenosine-triphosphate[OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.4: ATP composition and recycling]{ref}.

\Image[atp-adp-phosphate-cycle.png]
{title=<ATP> has three <phosphate> groups; <ADP> has two, and regeneration requires <energy> input}
{description=Original component schematic, rather than a molecular structure or a <reaction mechanism>. Lines indicate component order and omit connecting <oxygen> <chemical atoms>, <electric charges>, and three-dimensional geometry. Both sides retain <adenine> and <ribose>; <hydrolysis> consumes <water>, whereas the reverse net <chemical reaction> forms <water>. The <energy> labels describe transfers, not extra material <reactants> or <reaction products>.}

The net <hydrolysis> commonly used in <energy coupling> is
$$
\mathrm{ATP}+\mathrm{H_2O}\longrightarrow\mathrm{ADP}+\mathrm P_i.
$$
Here $\mathrm P_i$ denotes <inorganic phosphate>. At buffered <pH> $7$, this biochemical shorthand groups the appropriate <protonation> states into pools and leaves <proton> exchange with the <buffer solution> implicit. An explicit, charge-balanced choice is $\mathrm{ATP}^{4-}+\mathrm{H_2O}\longrightarrow\mathrm{ADP}^{3-}+\mathrm{HPO_4}^{2-}+\mathrm H^+$, without implying these are the only <chemical species> present. See the https://iubmb.qmul.ac.uk/thermod2/[IUBMB recommendations for biochemical thermodynamics].

The quoted textbook biochemical <standard state> value is approximately
$$
\Delta G^{\circ\prime}=-30.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}.
$$
This convention uses fixed <pH> $7$, <temperature> $298.15\,\mathrm K$ ($25\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$), reference <pressure> $100\,\mathrm{kPa}$ ($1\,\mathrm{bar}$), unit reference <thermodynamic activities> for dissolved <reactants> and <reaction products>, and <water> <thermodynamic activity> approximated by one. The prime indicates the fixed-<pH> biochemical convention. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/6-4-atp-adenosine-triphosphate[OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.4: standard ATP hydrolysis value] and https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/6-2-potential-kinetic-free-and-activation-energy[Biology 2e, 6.2: biochemical standard conditions]. This is a reference value, rather than a universal value inside a <biological cell>; precise tables must also specify <chemical solution> conditions and any <magnesium> <ion> binding convention. Different reference conventions and data treatments give different tabulated values, as discussed in https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3299200/[Li et al., 2010: a database of thermodynamic quantities for biochemical reactions].

An <exergonic reaction> has a negative change in <Gibbs free energy>, so the overall <ATP> <hydrolysis> is favourable under those reference conditions. Breaking a <chemical bond> by itself costs <energy>; forming new <chemical bonds> can release <energy>. The net change compares all <reactants> with all <reaction products>, including their interactions with <water> and the <entropy> contribution. Calling the phosphate connection a "high-energy bond" describes a large favourable change in <Gibbs free energy> on <hydrolysis>, rather than <energy> released by bond breaking alone. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/7.5 Strengths of Ionic and Covalent Bonds> and https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9903/[Cooper, The Cell: ATP and metabolic energy].

Composition changes the driving force. At the same fixed <pH>, <temperature>, <pressure>, and binding convention, write the molar reaction change as
$$
\Delta G=\Delta G^{\circ\prime}+RT\ln Q,\qquad
Q=\frac{a_{\mathrm{ADP}}a_{\mathrm P_i}}{a_{\mathrm{ATP}}}.
$$
Here $R$ is the <molar gas constant>, $T$ the absolute <temperature>, and $a$ a <dimensionless> <thermodynamic activity> for a biochemical pool. Constant <water> <thermodynamic activity> is absorbed into the reference term; fixed <pH> accounts for buffered <protons>. The shorthand $\Delta G$ means transformed molar reaction <Gibbs free energy> change, $\Delta_rG'$, rather than a finite sample's total change. The <natural logarithm> acts on a <dimensionless> <reaction quotient>, never on <concentrations> with units. See the https://iubmb.qmul.ac.uk/thermod2/[IUBMB biochemical thermodynamics recommendations] and the general <equilibrium constant> relation.

For a stipulated ideal dilute <chemical solution>, let $a_i=[i]/c^\circ$, with $c^\circ=1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$. Choose $[\mathrm{ATP}]=5\,\mathrm{mmol\,L^{-1}}$, $[\mathrm{ADP}]=0.5\,\mathrm{mmol\,L^{-1}}$, and $[\mathrm P_i]=1\,\mathrm{mmol\,L^{-1}}$, ignoring <activity coefficient> corrections and <magnesium> binding. These are illustrative inputs, not a claim about a particular <biological cell>. Then
$$
Q=\frac{(0.0005)(0.001)}{0.005}=10^{-4},\qquad
RT=(0.008314462618)(298.15)=2.47895703\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},
$$
and
$$
\Delta G=-30.5+2.47895703\ln(10^{-4})
\approx-53.332\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}.
$$
Increasing $Q$ tenfold raises $\Delta G$ by $RT\ln10\approx5.708\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ at this <temperature>. Thus the <ATP> to <ADP> ratio and available <inorganic phosphate> matter, even with the same <standard state> value. These are local molar predictions at the stipulated composition, rather than a constant total yield as an isolated sample reacts.

= Adenosine diphosphate
{parent=Nucleotide}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{ADP}$}

= ADP
{c}
{synonym}

<ADP> contains the same <adenosine> unit as <ATP>, with two <phosphate> groups instead of three. One <ATP> converted by the stated <hydrolysis> gives one <ADP> and one <inorganic phosphate>: $3=2+1$ accounts for the <phosphate> groups while <adenine> and <ribose> are retained. <ADP> is therefore a reusable material carrier, rather than <ATP> with its entire structure discarded.

Regeneration reverses the net <chemical reaction>:
$$
\mathrm{ADP}+\mathrm P_i\longrightarrow\mathrm{ATP}+\mathrm{H_2O}.
$$
At the same stipulated reference conditions its $\Delta G^{\circ\prime}$ is $+30.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, the negative of the <hydrolysis> value. It is an <endergonic reaction> and requires <energy> input through <energy coupling>; a <catalyst> alone cannot supply that input. Recycling the material carrier does not recycle the expended driving <energy>. <Cellular respiration> and <light-dependent reactions> supply routes for regeneration. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9903/[Cooper, The Cell: ATP generation and energy coupling].

<ADP> is a <nucleotide> with two <phosphate> groups. Formation of <ATP> from <ADP> and <phosphate> couples to <energy> supply in <light-dependent reactions>; the <Calvin cycle> returns <ADP> after using <ATP>. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: recycling ATP and ADP]{ref}.

= Adenosine monophosphate
{parent=Nucleotide}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{AMP}$}

= AMP
{c}
{synonym}

<AMP> is <adenosine> carrying one <phosphate> group at the <ribose>'s $5'$ position. The count distinguishes <AMP> from <ADP> with two and <ATP> with three; <adenosine> itself has none. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9903/[Cooper, The Cell: ATP, ADP, and AMP].

= Nucleotide base
{parent=Molecule}
{wiki}

= Nucleobase
{synonym}

= Adenine
{parent=Nucleotide base}
{title2=$\mathrm A$}
{wiki}

= Guanine
{parent=Nucleotide base}
{title2=$\mathrm G$}
{wiki}

= Cytosine
{parent=Nucleotide base}
{title2=$\mathrm C$}
{wiki}

= Thymine
{parent=Nucleotide base}
{title2=$\mathrm T$}
{wiki}

= Uracil
{parent=Nucleotide base}
{title2=$\mathrm U$}
{wiki}

= Quantum chemistry
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

= Electron density
{parent=Quantum chemistry}
{title2=$n(\mathbf r)$}

The electron density describes the expected number of <electrons> per unit <volume> at position $\mathbf r$. For a system with a fixed number $N$ of <electrons>, $\int n(\mathbf r)\,d^3\mathbf r=N$. A <Lewis structure> allocates electron counts without specifying this spatial density.

= Electron configuration
{parent=Quantum chemistry}
{wiki}

An <electron configuration> records the occupation of <electron shells> and <electron subshells> by <electrons>. For neutral <sodium> in its <ground state>, $1s^2\,2s^2\,2p^6\,3s^1$ gives $2+2+6+1=11$ <electrons>; adding the occupations within each <electron shell> gives $(2,8,1)$. A superscript counts <electrons>, rather than multiplying the preceding labels. Removing the outer <electron> gives the <sodium> <ion> $\mathrm{Na}^{+}$ with $1s^2\,2s^2\,2p^6$, or $(2,8)$. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/6-4-electronic-structure-of-atoms-electron-configurations[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, electron configurations of atoms and ions].

= Electron shell
{parent=Electron configuration}
{title2=$n$}
{wiki}

An <electron shell> collects the allowed electronic <quantum states> sharing a <principal quantum number> $n$. Its occupancy is the number of <electrons> assigned to those states in an <electron configuration>. An <electron shell> is not a material surface or a prescribed orbit.

= Electron subshell
{parent=Electron shell}

An <electron subshell> groups the allowed electronic <quantum states> within an <electron shell> by their <orbital angular momentum> quantum number $\ell$. Labels $s$ and $p$ mean $\ell=0$ and $\ell=1$, respectively; their capacities are two and six <electrons>. The leading number in $2p$ identifies the <electron shell>, while $p$ identifies the <electron subshell>.

= Atomic orbital
{parent=Quantum chemistry}
{wiki}
{title2=$\phi_{n\ell m}(\mathbf r)$}

An atomic orbital is a one-<electron> spatial <wavefunction> used to describe an <electron>'s state in a <chemical atom>. For a one-electron <hydrogen atom>, orbitals can be exact <energy eigenstates>; descriptions of many-electron atoms instead use an orbital approximation. A normalized atomic orbital has $\int|\phi|^2\,d^3\mathbf r=1$, and the <Born rule> gives the position <probability density> $|\phi|^2$. It is a function extending through space, not a prescribed classical orbit. https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/6-3-development-of-quantum-theory[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, 6.3: atomic orbitals and the wavefunction description]{ref}

The <linear combination of atomic orbitals> combines these single-centre functions to approximate a <molecular orbital>. The <orbital overlap> measures their <inner product>, and their relative <quantum phase> controls the <interference> shown in <molecular orbital theory>.

= Molecular orbital theory
{parent=Quantum chemistry}
{wiki}

<Molecular orbital theory> describes the electronic arrangement of a <molecule> using <molecular orbitals>: one-<electron> spatial <wavefunctions> extending over the combined <atomic nuclei>. Filling <bonding molecular orbitals> can stabilize a <chemical bond>; filling <antibonding molecular orbitals> opposes that stabilization. A <molecular orbital> is a building block of a many-<electron> description, rather than the entire many-<electron> <wavefunction>. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-61-physical-chemistry-fall-2017/ef9da1132ff6233d732a1d6115099467_MIT5_61F17_lec24.pdf[Robert W. Field, MIT 5.61, lecture 24, pp. 1–4: molecular orbitals and the LCAO approximation].

First make the <interference> visible with an exactly normalized one-dimensional toy. Replace the three-dimensional $1s$ <atomic orbitals> of the <hydrogen atom> by two <Gaussian functions> centred at $x=\pm a$, with <dimensionless> $x$ and $a>0$:
$$
\phi_A(x)=\pi^{-1/4}e^{-(x+a)^2/2},\qquad
\phi_B(x)=\pi^{-1/4}e^{-(x-a)^2/2},\qquad
S=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_A\phi_B\,dx=e^{-a^2}.
$$
The normalized sum and difference are $\psi_\pm=(\phi_A\pm\phi_B)/\sqrt{2(1\pm S)}$, following the same <linear combination of atomic orbitals> algebra. At $a=1$, the <orbital overlap> is $S=e^{-1}\approx0.367879$. At the midpoint, $\phi_A(0)=\phi_B(0)\approx0.455581$, giving
$$
\psi_+(0)\approx0.550879,\qquad |\psi_+(0)|^2\approx0.303468,\qquad
\psi_-(0)=|\psi_-(0)|^2=0.
$$
Both <probability densities> have total <integral> one: <interference> redistributes <probability>, rather than creating an extra <electron>. These are toy <wavefunctions>, not actual <hydrogen atom> orbitals or predictions of <molecular hydrogen> <energies>.

\Image[molecular-orbital-interference.png]
{title=Normalized one-dimensional <Gaussian function> toy: signed <probability amplitudes> add or cancel before their squares give <probability densities>}
{description=Centres at $x=-1$ and $x=1$ give <orbital overlap> $S=e^{-1}$. The difference has a zero at the midpoint; its negative lobe does not carry positive <electric charge>. All density curves shown are normalized on the line, rather than in three-dimensional space.}

Now fill the two $1s$-derived <molecular orbitals> in the minimal model. The lower <bonding molecular orbital> is labelled $\sigma_g$; the upper <antibonding molecular orbital> is $\sigma_u^*$. The <Pauli exclusion principle> permits at most two <electrons> in each spatial <molecular orbital>, with opposite <spin> projections along a chosen axis. For the lowest <electron configuration> in this model, fill $\sigma_g$ first. The subscripts $g$ and $u$ denote <even> and <odd> behaviour under inversion through the midpoint; the star marks an <antibonding molecular orbital>. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/8-4-molecular-orbital-theory[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, 8.4: occupancy and bond order] and https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-61-physical-chemistry-fall-2017/e9f9413400be87f6a0a512e757d52e3c_MIT5_61F17_lec25.pdf[Field, MIT 5.61, lecture 25, pp. 1–3: bonding, antibonding, and symmetry labels].

With $N_b$ <electrons> in <bonding molecular orbitals> and $N_a$ in <antibonding molecular orbitals>, the model's <bond order> is $b=(N_b-N_a)/2$. The three elementary counts are:
* <Molecular hydrogen>, $\mathrm H_2$: two <electrons> give $(\sigma_g)^2$ and $b=(2-0)/2=1$.
* The <hydrogen> molecular <ion> $\mathrm H_2^+$: one <electron> gives $(\sigma_g)^1$ and $b=(1-0)/2=1/2$.
* Two neutral <helium> <chemical atoms>, $\mathrm{He}_2$: four <electrons> give $(\sigma_g)^2(\sigma_u^*)^2$ and $b=(2-2)/2=0$ in this minimal $1s$ model.

\Image[molecular-orbital-occupancy.png]
{title=Schematic <molecular orbital> occupancy for $\mathrm H_2$, $\mathrm H_2^+$, and $\mathrm{He}_2$: <bond orders> one, one half, and zero}
{description=Each horizontal line is one spatial <molecular orbital>; paired arrows represent opposite <spin> projections. Vertical positions show ordering only, with arbitrary gaps rather than measured <energies> or a common scale across species.}

The zero <bond order> says this minimal model gives no net <covalent bond> for $\mathrm{He}_2$. It does not rule out every bound pair of <helium> <chemical atoms>: a very weak <van der Waals force> can bind a neutral <helium> dimer, which has been observed experimentally. See https://www.atom.uni-frankfurt.de/publications/files/ZellerPNAS-2016.pdf[Zeller et al., Imaging the He2 quantum halo state using a free electron laser, PNAS 113, 14651–14655 (2016), p. 14651: van der Waals binding] and https://scholarworks.gvsu.edu/chm_articles/6/[Luo et al., The weakest bond: Experimental observation of helium dimer, Journal of Chemical Physics 98, 3564–3567 (1993)]. The <bond order> count does not include that weak binding mechanism.

The useful surprise is that sharing alone does not determine a <chemical bond>: the relative <quantum phase> and the occupancy matter together. The same two shapes explain why adding an <electron> to a <bonding molecular orbital> increases the model's <bond order>, while adding one to an <antibonding molecular orbital> decreases it. <Molecular orbital theory> also accounts for the unpaired <electrons> and <paramagnetism> of <molecular oxygen>, which its all-paired <Lewis structure> misses. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/8-4-molecular-orbital-theory[Chemistry 2e, 8.4: the molecular orbital description of oxygen].

= Molecular orbital
{parent=Molecular orbital theory}
{title2=$\psi(\mathbf r)$}
{wiki}

A <molecular orbital> is a one-<electron> spatial <wavefunction> for the combined <atomic nuclei> of a <molecule>. For a normalized <molecular orbital>, the <Born rule> gives the position <probability density> $|\psi(\mathbf r)|^2$, with $\int_{\mathbb R^3}|\psi|^2\,d^3\mathbf r=1$. The <molecular orbital> is the amplitude, rather than its squared <modulus> or a classical trajectory.

In the independent-orbital approximation, a doubly occupied <molecular orbital> contributes $2|\psi|^2$ to the <electron> number density, whose <integral> is two. An empty <molecular orbital> contributes zero, even though its shape is still part of the model. The <molecular hydrogen> example therefore uses one occupied spatial <molecular orbital> for two <electrons>, distinguished by their <spins>. A negative real <probability amplitude> means a relative <quantum phase> of $\pi$, not positive <electric charge>; multiplying the whole <wavefunction> by $-1$ changes only its <global phase>.

= Bonding orbital
{parent=Molecular orbital}
{title2=$\sigma_g$}
{wiki}

= Bonding molecular orbital
{synonym}

Occupying a <bonding molecular orbital> favours a <chemical bond>. For the two equivalent $1s$ <atomic orbitals> of the <hydrogen atom>, the sum $\sigma_g=\sigma_+$ has constructive <interference> between the <protons>. The resulting <electron> density in that region helps stabilize the combined system. Its lower <energy> in this model follows from the <quantum Hamiltonian>, not from the plus sign alone; see https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-61-physical-chemistry-fall-2017/e9f9413400be87f6a0a512e757d52e3c_MIT5_61F17_lec25.pdf[Field, MIT 5.61, lecture 25, pp. 1–2].

For example, $(\sigma_g)^2$ in <molecular hydrogen> supplies $N_b=2$, hence <bond order> one when $N_a=0$. The sum in the <molecular orbital theory> toy has midpoint <probability density> $0.303468$, whereas the difference has zero there. The normalization factors must be included in this comparison.

= Antibonding orbital
{parent=Molecular orbital}
{title2=$\sigma_u^*$}
{wiki}

= Antibonding molecular orbital
{synonym}

Occupying an <antibonding molecular orbital> opposes a <chemical bond>. For equivalent $1s$ <atomic orbitals> of the <hydrogen atom>, the difference $\sigma_u^*=\sigma_-$ vanishes on the plane halfway between the <protons>: every point there is equally far from both centres, so the amplitudes cancel. This nodal plane removes the contribution of this <molecular orbital> to <electron> density on that plane; it does not make an entire finite region empty. The $1s$-derived <antibonding molecular orbital> lies above its <bonding molecular orbital> partner in <energy>. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-61-physical-chemistry-fall-2017/e9f9413400be87f6a0a512e757d52e3c_MIT5_61F17_lec25.pdf[Field, MIT 5.61, lecture 25, pp. 1–2].

In the $\mathrm{He}_2$ minimal model, $(\sigma_u^*)^2$ adds $N_a=2$ and cancels the <bond order> contribution of $(\sigma_g)^2$. Opposite signs on the two lobes describe <probability amplitudes>, while both lobes have nonnegative <probability density> after squaring. The star is an orbital label, not an extra <electron> or an <electric charge>.

= Linear combination of atomic orbitals
{parent=Molecular orbital theory}
{wiki}

= LCAO
{c}
{synonym}

The <LCAO> approximation builds a <molecular orbital> as a <linear combination> of single-centre <atomic orbitals>. For two equivalent normalized real $1s$ functions $\phi_A$ and $\phi_B$, choose their signs positive and define their <orbital overlap> $S$. At nonzero separation, $0<S<1$, and the normalized combinations are
$$
\sigma_\pm(\mathbf r)=\frac{\phi_A(\mathbf r)\pm\phi_B(\mathbf r)}{\sqrt{2(1\pm S)}},\qquad
S=\int_{\mathbb R^3}\phi_A(\mathbf r)\phi_B(\mathbf r)\,d^3\mathbf r.
$$
Here $\sigma_+=\sigma_g$ and $\sigma_-=\sigma_u^*$. Equal coefficients use the equivalence of the two centres; a general <LCAO> need not have equal coefficients. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-61-physical-chemistry-fall-2017/ef9da1132ff6233d732a1d6115099467_MIT5_61F17_lec24.pdf[Field, MIT 5.61, lecture 24, pp. 3–6: LCAO coefficients and overlap integrals].

For the <Gaussian function> toy's $a=1$, the two denominators are $\sqrt{2(1+S)}\approx1.654013$ and $\sqrt{2(1-S)}\approx1.124385$. To check <wavefunction normalization>, expand the square: $\int(\phi_A\pm\phi_B)^2=1+1\pm2S$. Dividing by $2(1\pm S)$ gives one. The local <probability density> is
$$
|\sigma_\pm|^2=\frac{\phi_A^2+\phi_B^2\pm2\phi_A\phi_B}{2(1\pm S)}.
$$
The cross term is the <interference>; adding the separate densities first would lose it. These formulas normalize the chosen <linear combination> exactly, while the restricted choice of <atomic orbitals> remains an approximation to an actual <molecular orbital>.

= Orbital overlap
{parent=Linear combination of atomic orbitals}
{title2=$S$}

The <orbital overlap> of normalized real <atomic orbitals> is their <inner product> $S=\int\phi_A\phi_B$. It is a <dimensionless> measure of their common amplitude, not an <electron> count. For the <Gaussian function> toy, increasing $a$ from $1$ to $2$ reduces $S$ from $e^{-1}\approx0.367879$ to $e^{-4}\approx0.018316$. As $S$ tends to zero, both <LCAO> normalization denominators tend to $\sqrt2$.

= Bond order
{parent=Molecular orbital theory}
{title2=$b=(N_b-N_a)/2$}
{wiki}

In the elementary <molecular orbital theory> count, <bond order> is half the excess of <electrons> in <bonding molecular orbitals> over those in <antibonding molecular orbitals>. Thus adding one <electron> to a <bonding molecular orbital> raises $b$ by $1/2$, while adding one to an <antibonding molecular orbital> lowers it by $1/2$. The configurations drawn above give $1$, $1/2$, and $0$. This count summarizes net <covalent bond> occupancy; it is neither a measured <energy> nor a universal test for every kind of binding. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/8-4-molecular-orbital-theory[Chemistry 2e, 8.4: molecular orbital bond order].

= Chemical bond
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

A <chemical bond> associates <chemical atoms> or groups of <chemical atoms> into a sufficiently stable structure to treat it as a distinct entity. The concept covers connections within <molecules> and in extended solids; see the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/CT07009[IUPAC Gold Book, chemical bond]. It is useful because composition alone does not specify how the <chemical atoms> are connected, and those connections help determine a <chemical substance>'s properties and possible <chemical reactions>.

In a <covalent bond>, <electrons> are shared between <chemical atoms>. Two <hydrogen> <chemical atoms> can form a <molecular hydrogen> <molecule>, with its <electrons> interacting with both <atomic nuclei>. In the usual description of this diatomic <molecule>, the <potential energy> as a function of nuclear separation has a minimum: moving the nuclei too close or too far raises the <energy>. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/7.2 Covalent Bonding>.

Separating bonded <chemical atoms> requires <energy>; forming the corresponding <chemical bonds> releases <energy>. Whether an overall <chemical reaction> releases <energy> depends on both the <chemical bonds> broken and those formed. The lines in the <stoichiometry> diagram indicate connections schematically, rather than literal rods or a sequence of microscopic events.

= Bond-dissociation energy
{parent=Chemical bond}
{wiki}
{title2=$D$}

A <bond-dissociation energy> specifies the <energy> needed for a stated dissociation into separated fragments. An <energy> from the lowest vibrational state, an <energy> from the bottom of a <potential energy> well, and a finite-temperature molar dissociation <enthalpy> require different conventions; do not silently mix them in an <electronegativity> formula.

Which fragments, in which <quantum states>, and at which <temperature>? For a neutral gaseous <molecule> with a specified two-electron <covalent bond>, the usual thermochemical convention is <homolysis>,
$$
\mathrm{A{-}B}(g)\longrightarrow\mathrm A^{\bullet}(g)+\mathrm B^{\bullet}(g).
$$
Each fragment receives one of the two bonding <electrons>; the dots denote unpaired <electrons>. The final fragments are isolated from each other, with their own relaxed <molecular geometry> and specified electronic <quantum states>. The <energy> cost includes this relaxation, rather than merely stretching the initial <chemical bond> while freezing everything else. For a neutral parent, <heterolysis> instead produces $\mathrm A^+(g)+\mathrm B^-(g)$, or the opposite assignment of <electric charges>; those are different final states and different <energies>. A value for gaseous neutral fragments cannot be transferred to <ions>, a <chemical solution>, or a <solid> without accounting for those changed conditions.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/bond-dissociation energy>[definition of bond-dissociation energy: molar enthalpy for homolysis of a specified molecular entity]{ref}

The distinction is useful beyond terminology. <Pauling electronegativity> compares consistently defined <bond-dissociation energies>; <photodissociation> can reveal a specified ground-state threshold; a thermal <chemical reaction> balance requires compatible <enthalpies>. A threshold is also different from an <activation energy>: it compares initial and final <quantum states>, whereas a barrier can obstruct the path between them.

= Bond potential-well depth
{parent=Bond-dissociation energy}
{title2=$D_e$}

For a <diatomic molecule>, let $V(r)$ be the electronic <potential energy> at <bond length> $r$, with the <atomic nuclei> treated as fixed while finding the electronic <energy>. Choose zero at infinitely separated <chemical atoms> in the specified electronic <quantum states>. If the minimum occurs at $r_e$, the <bond potential-well depth> is
$$
D_e=V(\infty)-V(r_e)=-V(r_e).
$$
The bottom of this curve describes an equilibrium <bond length>, not an attainable motionless vibrational <quantum state>. Changing the zero of <potential energy> shifts both endpoints equally and leaves $D_e$ unchanged. A <quantum harmonic oscillator> approximates the curve near its minimum but rises without limit, so that local approximation alone cannot supply a dissociation asymptote. A <Morse potential> supplies both a minimum and a finite separated-fragment limit.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/dissociation energy>[definition: subscripts e and 0 distinguish the potential-energy minimum and ground state]{ref}

= Ground-state dissociation energy
{parent=Bond-dissociation energy}
{title2=$D_0$}

The <ground-state dissociation energy> starts at the lowest vibrational <quantum state>, with the lowest allowed rotational <quantum state> and the fragment states explicitly specified. For a <diatomic molecule> whose fragments are ground-state <chemical atoms>, omitting any additional rotational correction,
$$
D_0=D_e-E_{\rm ZP},
$$
where $E_{\rm ZP}$ is the vibrational <zero-point energy> measured upward from the well minimum. The initial <molecule> already has this <energy>, so less additional <energy> is needed to reach the same final limit.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/bond-dissociation energy in theoretical chemistry>[definition of bond-dissociation energy in theoretical chemistry: D0 = De minus zero-point vibrational energy]{ref}

For fragments that are themselves <molecules>, include their <zero-point energies> too. Define $D_e$ as the electronic <energy> difference between separately relaxed products and reactant; then, with consistent rotational conventions,
$$
D_0=D_e+\sum_{\rm fragments}E_{\rm ZP}-E_{\rm ZP,parent}.
$$
Thus “subtract the parent's <zero-point energy>” is sufficient for atomic products but generally incomplete for molecular products. An excited final <quantum state> raises its channel threshold by its excitation <energy>; that threshold does not become the ground-product $D_0$ just because a detector selects it.

= Bond-dissociation enthalpy
{parent=Bond-dissociation energy}
{title2=$\Delta_{\rm diss}H^{\circ}(T)$}

A <bond-dissociation enthalpy> is the molar <enthalpy> change for the specified <homolysis> at <temperature> $T$, with reactant and products in specified <standard states>. Its usual units are $\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The superscript $\circ$ specifies <standard states>; it does not fix $T$ to room <temperature>.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/bond-dissociation enthalpy>[definition and symbols: standard molar enthalpy for homolysis, Δdiss H°]{ref}

Write $\delta h_i(T)=H_{m,i}^{\circ}(T)-H_{m,i}^{\circ}(0)$ for each species' molar thermal <enthalpy> increment. With $D_0$ an <energy> per parent <molecule>,
$$
\Delta_{\rm diss}H^{\circ}(T)=N_{\rm A}D_0+\delta h_A(T)+\delta h_B(T)-\delta h_{AB}(T).
$$
Here $N_{\rm A}$ is the <Avogadro constant>. The thermal increments include translational <kinetic energy>, <molecular rotation>, thermally excited <molecular vibration> and electronic states when populated, and the $pV$ contribution to <enthalpy>. They are additional to the <zero-point energies> already included in $D_0$. If values of <standard enthalpy of formation> at the same $T$ are available, <Hess's law> gives the same quantity as $\Delta_fH^{\circ}(A)+\Delta_fH^{\circ}(B)-\Delta_fH^{\circ}(AB)$.

Units alone do not identify the convention. Multiplying either $D_e$ or $D_0$ by the <Avogadro constant> gives molar <energy>, but does not add thermal <enthalpy>. One <electronvolt> per dissociation corresponds to $96.485332\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. A <spectroscopic wavenumber> $\widetilde\nu$ represents <energy> $hc\widetilde\nu$, using the <Planck constant> $h$ and <speed of light> $c$; $1\,\mathrm{cm^{-1}}$ corresponds to $0.011962657\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$.<Fundamental Physical Constants — Complete Listing by National Institute of Standards and Technology and others>[SI defining constants h, c, e and Avogadro constant; conversions calculated from them]{ref}

= Stipulated bond dissociation model
{parent=Bond-dissociation energy}

Take an invented <diatomic molecule> with a <Morse potential>, setting the separated-atom <energy> to zero:
$$
V(r)=D_e\left[\left(1-e^{-a(r-r_e)}\right)^2-1\right],\qquad
D_e=2.0000\,\mathrm{eV},\qquad A=\hbar\omega_e=0.2000\,\mathrm{eV}.
$$
Here $a$ is an inverse length, $r_e$ the equilibrium <bond length>, and $\omega_e=a\sqrt{2D_e/\mu}$, with <reduced mass> $\mu$. These stipulated parameters describe a simple model, not a measured <chemical substance>. For the one-dimensional <Morse oscillator>, <energy eigenvalues> above the minimum are
$$
\epsilon_v=A\left(v+\tfrac12\right)-\frac{A^2}{4D_e}\left(v+\tfrac12\right)^2,
\qquad v=0,1,\ldots,\quad v+\tfrac12<\frac{2D_e}{A}.
$$
Consequently,
$$
E_{\rm ZP}=\frac A2-\frac{A^2}{16D_e}=0.09875\,\mathrm{eV},\qquad
D_0=2.0000-0.09875=1.90125\,\mathrm{eV}.
$$
The harmonic estimate $A/2=0.1000\,\mathrm{eV}$ is slightly larger than this model's exact <zero-point energy>. The physical bound-level restriction matters: the quadratic expression is not an infinite ladder of vibrational <quantum states>.

\Image[bond-dissociation-well-and-zero-point.png]
{id=image-bond-dissociation-well-and-zero-point}
{title=<Bond potential-well depth> and <ground-state dissociation energy> reach the same separated-atom limit from different starting <energies>}
{description=Original stipulated <Morse potential> with $D_e=2\,\mathrm{eV}$ and $A=0.2\,\mathrm{eV}$. The horizontal coordinate is $a(r-r_e)$; the horizontal $v=0$ line is an <energy eigenvalue>, not a classical trajectory or a measured spectrum.}

For a separate thermal illustration using this same stipulated $D_0$, assume an <ideal gas>, fully active <molecular rotation> of the parent, negligible thermal <molecular vibration>, and negligible electronic excitation for all species. Each monatomic product contributes $\delta h=5RT/2$; the <diatomic molecule> contributes $7RT/2$. Thus the net increment is $3RT/2$, with <molar gas constant> $R$. At $T=300\,\mathrm K$,
$$
N_{\rm A}D_e\approx192.971\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},\qquad
N_{\rm A}D_0\approx183.443\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},\qquad
\Delta_{\rm diss}H^{\circ}\approx187.184\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}.
$$
This last result follows from the stated thermal model, not a universal correction for every <chemical bond>. Frozen rotations, accessible excited states, different fragment composition, or a condensed <phase> change the thermal increments.

= Sequential bond-dissociation energies
{parent=Bond-dissociation energy}
{title2=$D_1,D_2,\ldots$}

Equivalent <chemical bonds> in an intact <molecule> need not have equal successive <bond-dissociation enthalpies>. Once one <chemical bond> is removed, the next reactant has a different electronic <quantum state> and may relax to a different <molecular geometry>. The successive steps compare different species. This distinction also explains why a tabulated average <chemical bond> strength is not necessarily the cost of the first cleavage.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/7.5 Strengths of Ionic and Covalent Bonds>[“Bond Strength: Covalent Bonds”, methane paragraph: equivalent initial bonds and unequal sequential breaking costs]{ref}

For an invented species $AB_2$, stipulate the following gas-phase steps, with all <enthalpies> at the same <temperature> and the same <standard states>:
$$
\begin{aligned}
AB_2(g)&\longrightarrow AB(g)+B(g),&D_1&=300\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},\\
AB(g)&\longrightarrow A(g)+B(g),&D_2&=400\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}.
