\end{aligned}
$$
The symbols stand for hypothetical species with consistently chosen electronic states. They are not values for <water> or another measured <molecule>. By <Hess's law>, the total <enthalpy of atomization> for $AB_2(g)\to A(g)+2B(g)$ is $700\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$; the <arithmetic mean> per broken <chemical bond> is $350\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Neither individual step equals that mean. Nor must the second step be cheaper: here it is deliberately more expensive. An exact sequence sums exactly; substituting averages taken from other <molecules> generally gives only an estimate.

\Image[bond-energy-conventions-and-sequential-cleavage.png]
{id=image-bond-energy-conventions-and-sequential-cleavage}
{title=Two stipulated examples: changing the <energy> convention for one dissociation, and changing the reactant during sequential cleavage}
{description=Left: $N_{\rm A}D_e$, $N_{\rm A}D_0$, and the model <bond-dissociation enthalpy> at $300\,\mathrm K$. Right: cumulative <enthalpy> levels $0,300,700\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ for two successive steps, with costs $300$ and $400$ and mean $350$. The panels are separate hypothetical systems; neither contains molecular measurements.}

= Homolysis
{parent=Chemical bond}

<Homolysis> splits a <covalent bond> with one bonding <electron> assigned to each fragment. A neutral closed-shell <molecule> thereby produces fragments with unpaired <electrons>, each a <radical (chemistry)>. The usual <bond-dissociation enthalpy> uses this product convention.

= Heterolysis
{parent=Chemical bond}

<Heterolysis> assigns both bonding <electrons> to one fragment. For an initially neutral <molecule>, the resulting <cation> and <anion> define a different dissociation channel from <homolysis>; their isolated gas-phase <energy> differs from that of solvated <ions>.

= Photodissociation
{parent=Chemical bond}
{wiki}

<Photodissociation> separates a <molecule> into fragments after <photon absorption>. For an isolated parent with a single absorbed <photon>, <conservation of energy> gives
$$
h\nu+E_{\rm initial,int}=D_0+E_{\rm products,int}+E_{\rm recoil}.
$$
Internal excitation <energies> here are relative to the parent and product ground states used to define $D_0$; $E_{\rm recoil}$ is the total product translational <kinetic energy> in the parent's <centre of mass> frame. Measuring one product's <kinetic energy> is insufficient by itself. For two fragments of <masses> $m_A,m_B$, <conservation of momentum> gives $E_{\rm recoil}=E_A(m_A+m_B)/m_B$ when $A$ is detected. Unknown product excitation can conceal part of the available <energy>.

= Photofragment-ion imaging
{parent=Photodissociation}
{wiki}

<Photofragment-ion imaging> detects fragment <velocities> by ionizing neutral products and mapping their motion onto a position-sensitive detector using an <electric field>. In a velocity-mapping implementation, the image radius measures transverse speed under fixed lens settings. Selecting an arrival-time slice helps extract the central plane of the three-dimensional distribution. Species identification and electronic <quantum state> selection are essential for turning a radius into a particular dissociation threshold.

= Nitrogen dissociation threshold from fragment images
{parent=Photofragment-ion imaging}

A <molecular nitrogen> experiment used a $0.5\,\mathrm{mm}$ pulsed nozzle and two skimmers. A $5\,\mathrm{cm}$ tube raised the rotational <temperature>. Opposed vacuum-ultraviolet <laser> pulses excited selected molecular levels and, about $10\,\mathrm{ns}$ later, selectively ionized excited <nitrogen> fragments. A <microchannel plate detector>, phosphor screen and camera recorded radii.<Accurate measurements of the bond dissociation energies of 14N2, 14N15N and 15N2 by Liya Lu and others>[Lu, Jiang and Gao, §2, printed manuscript pp. 2–3: nozzle, tube, pump/probe and imaging readout]{ref}

The measured squared radii were fitted against molecular excitation <energies>, referenced to the parent <ground state>:
$$
E_j=D_{0,\rm channel}+\kappa R_j^2.
$$
The intercept at zero radius gave the excited-product threshold; the fitted slope absorbed the detector scale. A wavemeter monitored the excitation source; scanning the probe covered the recoil <Doppler shift>. Subtracting the atomic excitation <energies> converted the intercept to ground-product $D_0$.<Accurate measurements of the bond dissociation energies of 14N2, 14N15N and 15N2 by Liya Lu and others>[§1: image-radius equation; §2: wavemeter and probe scan; §3 and Figure 4: fits; §4: atomic excitation correction]{ref}

An apertured grounded plate and detector timing rejected faster competing channels. Selecting fragment electronic states distinguished nearby thresholds. Agreement of $D_e$ across <isotopes> after <zero-point energy> correction tested consistency. The method still requires correctly assigned levels, adequate near-threshold signal and stable imaging; a signal drop alone can reflect another channel or a barrier.<Accurate measurements of the bond dissociation energies of 14N2, 14N15N and 15N2 by Liya Lu and others>[§2: shielding plate and timing; §3: state-selected channels; §4 and Table 2: isotope consistency and limitations]{ref}

This measurement determines a state-resolved <ground-state dissociation energy>. Obtaining a finite-<temperature> <bond-dissociation enthalpy> requires the thermal increments above; obtaining a <bond potential-well depth> requires the <zero-point energy> correction. The three quantities describe different starting conditions for the same specified separation.

= Electronegativity
{parent=Chemical bond}
{title2=$\chi$}
{wiki}

= Electronegative
{synonym}

<Electronegativity> describes a <chemical atom>'s tendency to attract <electron density> towards itself in a <chemical bond>. It helps answer a useful question: when two different <chemical atoms> share <electrons>, which end of the <chemical bond> tends to be richer in <electron density>? For <hydrogen chloride>, the answer is <chlorine>: the <covalent bond> is a <polar covalent bond>, conventionally written $\mathrm H^{\delta+}-\mathrm{Cl}^{\delta-}$. The symbols indicate <partial atomic charges>, not isolated <ions> carrying whole elementary <electric charges>.<Chemistry 2e>[§7.2, “Pure vs. Polar Covalent Bonds”, HCl example]{ref}

<Electronegativity> is a comparison scheme, not the number of <electrons> on a <chemical atom>. A <partial atomic charge> depends on how the <electron density> is partitioned; <formal charge> instead follows an integer allocation in a <Lewis structure>. Neither is numerically equal to <electronegativity>. Nor is <electronegativity> identical to <electron affinity>, which concerns adding an <electron> to an isolated species. Different <electronegativity> scales use different measured inputs and conventions; compare numbers only after specifying the scale.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/electronegativity>{ref}

For a concrete comparison, the Royal Society of Chemistry lists <Pauling electronegativities> $\chi_{\rm P}(\mathrm H)=2.20$ and $\chi_{\rm P}(\mathrm{Cl})=3.16$. Their difference is $0.96$, so the <electronegativity> rule predicts <bond polarization> towards <chlorine> in <hydrogen chloride>. An isolated <molecular hydrogen> <molecule> has identical ends and zero <electronegativity> difference. These examples explain a direction of unequal sharing, not a quantitative <partial atomic charge>.<Periodic Table by Royal Society of Chemistry/Hydrogen — Element information, properties and uses>{ref}<Periodic Table by Royal Society of Chemistry/Chlorine — Element information, properties and uses>{ref}

\Image[electronegativity-scales-hydrogen-chlorine.png]
{id=electronegativity-scales-hydrogen-chlorine}
{title=<Pauling electronegativity> and <Mulliken electronegativity> for <hydrogen> and <chlorine>: separate scales, separate units; the <Mulliken electronegativity> values are calculated from the stated ground-state inputs}

A larger <electronegativity> difference often suggests stronger <bond polarization>, but there is no universal numerical boundary where a <covalent bond> suddenly becomes <ionic bonding>. Tables of cutoffs are classification aids with exceptions. <Hydrogen fluoride>, for example, is described by a <polar covalent bond> even when its <electronegativity> difference exceeds a textbook's nominal ionic threshold. The actual <electron density>, physical setting, and <chemical substance>'s structure matter.<Chemistry 2e>[§7.2, “Electronegativity and Bond Type”, general-guide warning and HF exception]{ref}

The distinction from <ionization energy> is equally useful: an <ionization energy> measures the cost of removing an <electron> from a specified isolated species, whereas <electronegativity> compares attraction for <electron density> in a bonded environment. Neither the removal cost alone nor the attachment release alone supplies a unique bonded-atom <electronegativity>. A scale combines selected measured inputs with a model and a convention.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/electronegativity>[E01990: multiple definitions; Mulliken average versus Pauling bond-energy differences]{ref}

= Pauling electronegativity
{c}
{parent=Electronegativity}
{title2=$\chi_{\rm P}$}

= Pauling scale
{c}
{synonym}

<Pauling electronegativity> is a relative, <dimensionless> scale motivated by <bond-dissociation energies>. The interesting observation is that a <chemical bond> between unlike <chemical atoms> can be stronger than a simple baseline inferred from the corresponding like-atom <chemical bonds>. An empirical excess <energy> can then be associated with an <electronegativity> difference. The 1932 paper <The Nature of the Chemical Bond. IV. The Energy of Single Bonds and the Relative Electronegativity of Atoms> built such a map; its original coordinates used <hydrogen> as zero, so those coordinates should not be mistaken for the modern tabulated <Pauling electronegativities>.<The Nature of the Chemical Bond. IV. The Energy of Single Bonds and the Relative Electronegativity of Atoms by Linus Pauling>[pp. 3576–3578, equation (1), Table III: H 0.00, Cl 0.94]{ref}

One arithmetic-baseline version of the <Pauling scale> relation is
$$
\Delta_D=D_{AB}-\frac{D_{AA}+D_{BB}}{2},\qquad
\left|\chi_{\rm P}(A)-\chi_{\rm P}(B)\right|\approx\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_D}{1\,\mathrm{eV}}}.
$$
Here $D_{AB}$, $D_{AA}$ and $D_{BB}$ are consistently defined <bond-dissociation energies> per <molecule>, and the <arithmetic mean> is the chosen baseline. With molar <energies> instead, write $\sqrt{\Delta_D/(96.485\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}})}$, approximately $0.102\sqrt{\Delta_D/(1\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}})}$. The denominator makes the result <dimensionless>; the factor $0.102$ is not a unit-independent magic constant. This is an empirical relation with a specified baseline, not a definition valid for every <chemical bond>.<Energy Electronegativity and Chemical Bonding by Stepan S. Batsanov>[§1 equation (1), coefficients for eV, kcal/mol and kJ/mol; §2.2, dependence on other bond properties]{ref}

For an explicitly synthetic example, suppose $D_{AA}=D_{BB}=300\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ and $D_{AB}=396.485\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ with the same temperature and dissociation convention. Then $\Delta_D=96.485\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ and the model gives an <electronegativity> difference of $1$. Replacing $D_{AB}$ by $300\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ gives zero; a negative excess would make this real square-root model inapplicable, not produce a negative <electronegativity> difference. These invented inputs illustrate the formula and are not measurements of named <chemical substances>.

Only differences appear, so adding the same constant to every <Pauling electronegativity> leaves this relation unchanged. A reference value must fix the origin. The <bond-dissociation energies> alone also do not tell which endpoint attracts <electron density> more strongly, because interchanging $A$ and $B$ leaves the formula unchanged. Chemical comparisons fix that direction. Thus <Pauling electronegativity> is useful as a compact ordering, while the underlying measured <energies> and approximations remain essential.

= Pauling bond-energy excess
{c}
{parent=Pauling electronegativity}
{title2=$\Delta_D$}

A <Pauling bond-energy excess> subtracts a chosen like-atom baseline from the unlike-atom <bond-dissociation energy>. For an unequal-input arithmetic example, stipulate $D_{AA}=200$, $D_{BB}=450$ and $D_{AB}=421.485\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, all with one dissociation convention. The <arithmetic mean> baseline and excess are
$$
D_{\rm base}=\frac{200+450}{2}=325\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},\qquad
\Delta_D=421.485-325=96.485\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}.
$$
The arithmetic-baseline <Pauling scale> model therefore gives $|\chi_{\rm P}(A)-\chi_{\rm P}(B)|=\sqrt{96.485/96.485}=1$. The <bond-dissociation energy> $421.485$ is not itself the excess: subtracting the baseline is essential. All three numbers are stipulated, not experimental values for named <chemical elements>.

\Image[pauling-bond-energy-excess-construction.png]
{id=image-pauling-bond-energy-excess-construction}
{title=Constructing a <Pauling bond-energy excess> from stipulated <bond-dissociation energies> and converting it to a <dimensionless> <electronegativity> difference}

Changing the baseline changes the model. For the same stipulated inputs, a <geometric mean> baseline would be $\sqrt{200\times450}=300\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, yielding an excess of $121.485\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ and a difference of approximately $1.122$. It is a different construction, even though both start from the same <bond-dissociation energies>. State which baseline is used before comparing <electronegativity> differences.

The arithmetic-baseline <Pauling scale> also has a useful inverse: a difference of $0.5$ corresponds to an excess of $24.12125\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, and a difference of $2$ corresponds to $385.94\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Doubling the <electronegativity> difference quadruples the modeled excess. Interchanging the endpoint labels changes neither quantity; the <square root> alone cannot choose the direction of <bond polarization>.

In <The Nature of the Chemical Bond. IV. The Energy of Single Bonds and the Relative Electronegativity of Atoms> (printed pp. 3576–3578, Table II and equation (1)), <Linus Pauling> organized excess <energies> for <chemical bonds> into an approximately additive map of their <square roots>. He also identified a limitation: his H–F map separation of $2$ predicted an excess of $4.00\,\mathrm{eV}$, exceeding his table's value by $1.23\,\mathrm{eV}$. Those are historical values and a historical model discrepancy, not modern recommended inputs.<The Nature of the Chemical Bond. IV. The Energy of Single Bonds and the Relative Electronegativity of Atoms by Linus Pauling>[printed pp. 3576–3578, Table II, equation (1), and paragraph beginning “These coördinates”]{ref}

= Mulliken electronegativity
{c}
{parent=Electronegativity}
{title2=$\chi_{\rm M}$}

= Mulliken scale
{c}
{synonym}

<Mulliken electronegativity> uses both the cost of removing an <electron> and the <energy> released by accepting an <electron>. For specified isolated-atom states, the common ground-state convention is
$$
\chi_{\rm M}=\frac{I+A_{\rm ea}}{2},\qquad
I=E(X^+)-E(X),\qquad A_{\rm ea}=E(X)-E(X^-).
$$
The free <electron>'s <energy> is the zero reference. Here $I$ is the <ionization energy> and $A_{\rm ea}$ is the <electron affinity>, positive for exothermic attachment. If attachment is instead tabulated as the signed <energy> change $\Delta E_{\rm attach}=E(X^-)-E(X)$, the same expression is $(I-\Delta E_{\rm attach})/2$. Stating the sign convention prevents adding the wrong quantities. The 1934 original treated suitable valence states, which are not automatically the ground states used in this example.<A New Electroaffinity Scale; Together with Data on Valence States and on Valence Ionization Potentials and Electron Affinities by Robert S. Mulliken>{ref}

<Mulliken electronegativity> has <energy> units before any additional rescaling. With $I$ and $A_{\rm ea}$ in <electronvolts> per <chemical atom>, $\chi_{\rm M}$ is in <electronvolts>; with both in $\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, it is a molar <energy>. For <hydrogen>, the RSC ground-state inputs are $I=1312.05$ and $A_{\rm ea}=72.769\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, giving $\chi_{\rm M}=692.4095\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\approx7.176\,\mathrm{eV}$ per <chemical atom>. For <chlorine>, $I=1251.186$ and $A_{\rm ea}=348.575\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ give $799.8805\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\approx8.290\,\mathrm{eV}$. These are calculated ground-state <Mulliken electronegativities>, not the RSC's tabulated <Pauling electronegativities>.<Periodic Table by Royal Society of Chemistry/Hydrogen — Element information, properties and uses>{ref}<Periodic Table by Royal Society of Chemistry/Chlorine — Element information, properties and uses>{ref}

Both schemes place <chlorine> above <hydrogen> here, but their numerical differences cannot be subtracted from one another or equated. Dividing a molar <Mulliken electronegativity> by an <energy> conversion factor changes units; it does not transform it into a <Pauling electronegativity>. A further empirical mapping would require its own calibration and stated domain.

= Electron affinity
{parent=Mulliken electronegativity}
{wiki}
{title2=$A_{\rm ea}$}

<Electron affinity> measures how strongly an isolated neutral species binds an additional <electron>. In the spectroscopic convention used here, it is the positive <energy> required to detach that <electron> from a bound <anion>. With the free <electron> at rest assigned zero <energy>,
$$
A_{\rm ea}=E(X)+E(e^-)-E(X^-),\qquad
\Delta E_{\rm attach}=-A_{\rm ea}.
$$
The <quantum states> of $X$ and $X^-$ must be specified, usually their <ground states>. Positive $A_{\rm ea}$ means that attachment lowers the <energy>; a signed attachment <energy> is then negative. This convention agrees with the IUPAC definition and with the atomic tables used for <Mulliken electronegativity>.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/electron affinity>{ref}

For <chlorine>, a published <photodetachment threshold> is $A_{\rm ea}/(hc)=29138.59(22)\,\mathrm{cm^{-1}}$, approximately $3.612725\,\mathrm{eV}$ per <chemical atom>. Here $h$ is the <Planck constant>, $c$ the <speed of light>, and the inverse-length unit expresses an <energy> through a spectroscopic <wavenumber>. The parentheses give the reported uncertainty in the last digits, not a range of possible sign conventions.<Isotope shift in the electron affinity of chlorine by U. Berzinsh and others>[§2.3, printed pp. 6–7: reported threshold 29138.59(22) inverse centimetres]{ref}

\Image[electron-affinity-attachment-detachment-levels.png]
{id=image-electron-affinity-attachment-detachment-levels}
{title=<Electron affinity> of <chlorine>: positive detachment <energy> and negative attachment <energy> connect the same isolated-species levels; a finite-temperature <electron attachment enthalpy> needs additional corrections}

An <electron affinity> is neither an <electronegativity> nor a <lattice energy>. The first describes a specified isolated-species <energy> difference, <electronegativity> compares attraction for <electron density> in a <chemical bond>, and <lattice energy> refers to an entire <ionic crystal> and separated gaseous <ions>. A stable <anion> can therefore help explain <ionic bonding> without describing the full <energy> balance for making a <salt>.

= Electron attachment enthalpy
{parent=Electron affinity}
{title2=$\Delta H_{\rm attach}^{\circ}(T)$}

An <electron attachment enthalpy> is the signed molar <enthalpy> change for a stated attachment reaction,
$$
X(g)+e^-(g)\longrightarrow X^-(g).
$$
For favourable attachment such as <chlorine>'s first attachment, the <electron attachment enthalpy> is negative. Its leading isolated-ground-state <energy> contribution is $-N_{\rm A}A_{\rm ea}$, where $N_{\rm A}$ is the <Avogadro constant>. At finite <temperature>, consistent thermal populations, translational contributions and the $pV$ term in <enthalpy> must also be included, together with the same gaseous-<electron> reference used in the corresponding <ionization energy> term. Consequently a measured spectroscopic <electron affinity> is not automatically an exact $298.15\,\mathrm K$ attachment <enthalpy> with a minus sign. A <Born-Haber cycle> must use compatible <enthalpies> throughout.

= Photodetachment
{parent=Electron affinity}

<Photodetachment> removes an <electron> from an <anion> using a <photon>. For <chlorine>, the reaction is $\mathrm{Cl}^-(g)+\gamma\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cl}(g)+e^-$. Measuring the minimum <photon> <energy> for the specified final <quantum state> gives a <photodetachment threshold> and hence an <electron affinity>.

= Photodetachment threshold
{parent=Photodetachment}
{title2=$E_{\rm th}$}

In the published <chlorine> experiment, negative <ions> were accelerated and mass-selected, then overlapped with a tunable <laser> beam. A downstream <electric field> deflected charged <ions>; neutral <chemical atoms> continued to a plate, whose secondary <electrons> were counted. Scanning the <laser> <frequency> produced an onset in this neutral-atom signal. The apparatus therefore inferred an <electron affinity> from a threshold, rather than measuring the <heat> of attachment.<Isotope shift in the electron affinity of chlorine by U. Berzinsh and others>[§2, printed pp. 2–4, Figure 1: ion preparation, deflectors, neutral-atom secondary-emission detector and threshold fitting]{ref}

Reversing the <laser> direction and averaging the two thresholds corrected the first-order <Doppler shift>. A <Fabry–Pérot interferometer> and reference spectral lines calibrated the <frequency>. Finite <laser> bandwidth, unresolved <quantum state> splitting and shifts of the calibration lines limited the inference; the authors included these effects in fitting and the error estimate. The level diagram shows only the extracted gap, not a measured signal curve.<Isotope shift in the electron affinity of chlorine by U. Berzinsh and others>[§§2.1 and 2.3, printed pp. 3–7, equation (2), Figures 2 and 5: direction reversal, calibration, bandwidth and reference-line systematic shifts]{ref}

= Electronvolt
{parent=Mulliken electronegativity}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{eV}$}

An <electronvolt> is $1.602176634\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J$. One <electronvolt> per particle corresponds to approximately $96.485\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$.<Fundamental Physical Constants — Complete Listing by National Institute of Standards and Technology and others>{ref}

= Experimental inputs to electronegativity scales
{parent=Electronegativity}

The contrast between <Pauling electronegativity> and <Mulliken electronegativity> becomes clearer by following the measurements into the formulas. A <Pauling bond-energy excess> compares three compatible <bond-dissociation energies>; <Mulliken electronegativity> takes the <arithmetic mean> of an <ionization energy> and an <electron affinity>. The former asks how much stronger an unlike-atom <chemical bond> is than a stipulated baseline; the latter combines two different isolated-species <energy> gaps.

Actual readouts precede those model choices. In the <nitrogen dissociation threshold from fragment images>, fitted squared image radii versus assigned excitation <energies> reveal a threshold after accounting for the product <quantum states>. In the <chlorine> <photodetachment threshold> experiment, mass-selected <anions> overlap a tunable <laser>, and neutral-atom counts reveal the onset of <electron> detachment. Opposite <laser> directions control the <Doppler shift>. That second threshold supplies an <electron affinity>, not an <electronegativity>; the <Mulliken scale> still needs an independent <ionization energy>.<Accurate measurements of the bond dissociation energies of 14N2, 14N15N and 15N2 by Liya Lu and others>[§1, recoil-energy/image-radius equation; §3.2, Figure 5, squared image sizes versus term energies; §3.2, threshold channel assignment]{ref}<Isotope shift in the electron affinity of chlorine by U. Berzinsh and others>[§2, printed pp. 2–4, Figure 1: mass selection and neutral counting; §2.1, direction reversal]{ref}

For an original arithmetic comparison, stipulate isolated-species $I=(8,7,6)\,\mathrm{eV}$ and positive attachment-release $A_{\rm ea}=(0.5,3,3)\,\mathrm{eV}$ for fictitious species $A,B,C$. Their <Mulliken electronegativities> are $(4.25,5,4.5)\,\mathrm{eV}$. The largest <ionization energy> belongs to $A$, while the largest <Mulliken electronegativity> belongs to $B$: accepting an <electron> matters as well as retaining one. Using signed attachment <energies> $(-0.5,-3,-3)\,\mathrm{eV}$ in $(I-\Delta E_{\rm attach})/2$ gives exactly the same results.

\Image[electronegativity-scale-inputs-and-orderings.png]
{id=image-electronegativity-scale-inputs-and-orderings}
{title=Stipulated inputs to <Mulliken electronegativity> and arithmetic-baseline <Pauling electronegativity>: different <energies>, units and orderings}

Independently stipulate $D_{RR}=300$ and $D_{XX}=(200,300,450)\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ for $X=A,B,C$, with unlike-atom values
$$
D_{RX}=\frac{D_{RR}+D_{XX}}{2}+96.485\,s_X^2\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},\qquad
(s_A,s_B,s_C)=(0.5,1,1.5).
$$
Thus $D_{RX}=(274.12125,396.485,592.09125)\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, and the <Pauling scale> differences from $R$ are $(0.5,1,1.5)$. If all three fictitious species are placed above $R$ on that scale, their ordering is $A<B<C$, whereas the stipulated <Mulliken scale> gives $A<C<B$. The <bond-dissociation energies> and isolated-species <energies> were invented independently: this demonstrates what the formulas do, not a measured disagreement for actual <chemical elements>. A unit conversion cannot force these two input models to agree.

= Chemical environment dependence of electronegativity
{parent=Electronegativity}

A tabulated <electronegativity> is useful because it compresses many comparisons, but one number per <chemical element> suppresses chemical context. Changing the bonded partners, <bond length> or <electron configuration> changes the conditions under which a <chemical atom> attracts <electron density>. The <bond-dissociation energy> can change for reasons beyond <bond polarization>, so attributing every extra unit of <energy> solely to an <electronegativity> difference is an approximation.<Energy Electronegativity and Chemical Bonding by Stepan S. Batsanov>[§2.2, opening paragraph: bond distance and atomic valence affect bond energy]{ref}

An <oxidation state> labels a formal allocation of <electrons>; it is not a measured <partial atomic charge>. State-sensitive <electronegativity> models may give different values for different <oxidation states> or <electron configurations>, while a simple <chemical element> table intentionally provides a common comparison convention. Likewise, replacing an isolated <chemical atom> by a site in an <ionic crystal> introduces a surrounding <electric field> and many neighbours. Applying the same tabulated <electronegativity> without adjustment becomes an additional assumption.<Energy Electronegativity and Chemical Bonding by Stepan S. Batsanov>[§2.1, Table 1 and valence footnotes; §2.2, Table 2; §2.3, opening paragraph on cooperative effects in solids]{ref}

For the ground-state <Mulliken scale>, the state dependence is visible directly in the formula. Replacing $X$ by $X^+$ replaces $E(X^+)-E(X)$ with $E(X^{2+})-E(X^+)$ as the removal cost, and replaces $E(X)-E(X^-)$ with $E(X^+)-E(X)$ as the attachment release. These are different <ionization energies> and <electron affinities>. This isolated-<ion> arithmetic does not claim that a bonded <chemical atom> with the same formal <oxidation state> is an isolated <ion>. In a <molecule>, the actual <electron density> and <dipole evidence for bond polarization> supply complementary information about that environment.

= Dipole evidence for bond polarization
{parent=Electronegativity}

A permanent <electric dipole moment> gives evidence of separated positive and negative <electric charge> in an overall neutral <molecule>. This is a more direct probe of <chemical polarity> than an <electronegativity> table. For <hydrogen chloride>, NIST lists about $1.109$ <debye> for the lowest vibrational state and $1.093$ <debye> for the equilibrium value. They describe different averaging conventions, not two contradictory measurements. The NIST WebBook traces the vibrational value $1.1085$ <debye> to molecular-beam electric-resonance measurements in 1970.<HCl (Hydrogen chloride): experimental data by National Institute of Standards and Technology>{ref}<Hydrogen chloride: Constants of diatomic molecules by Klaus P. Huber and others>{ref}

How does <molecular beam electric resonance> reveal an <electric dipole moment>? A source and apertures form a beam of <molecules> in <vacuum>. A first nonuniform <electric field>, A, selects <quantum states> through their different <Stark effects>. In a central region, C, electrodes supply a nearly uniform <electric field> and applied radiation drives transitions at selected <frequencies>. A second nonuniform <electric field>, B, analyzes the outgoing <quantum states>; a detector measures the rate of arriving <molecules>. In a transmission-loss arrangement, resonant radiation transfers some <molecules> to a <quantum state> rejected by B, so the measured arrival rate dips. The observable is a change in detected <molecules> as the radiation <frequency> is scanned, rather than light absorption itself. This general apparatus description is documented in a later experimental thesis; it is not a reconstruction of the exact 1970 HCl instrument.<High Resolution Molecular Beam Study of Alkali Cyanides by Johannes Jacobus van Vaals>[§2, printed pp. 11–14: fields A, C and B, detected flux, parallel plates, radiation and detector]{ref}

\Image[bond-dipole-beam-resonance.png]
{id=bond-dipole-beam-resonance}
{title=<Molecular beam electric resonance>: schematic state selection and a synthetic <Stark effect> signal from a <rigid rotor> model; neither apparatus dimensions nor signal curves are measured HCl data}

The inference comes from how transition <frequencies> change with the known <electric field>. The interaction $-\boldsymbol\mu\cdot\mathbf E$ couples the <electric dipole moment> to the <electric field>; fitting the <quantum mechanics> of the relevant rotational states extracts its magnitude. A free rotating <molecule> need not show a first-order shift proportional to $|\mathbf E|$: the weak-field <Stark effect> can be quadratic. The diagram uses a simplified <rigid rotor>, with $B$ an <energy> constant, to illustrate this behavior. It omits the fine details of actual spectra.

Useful controls include scans with the radiation off to measure the baseline arrival rate, repeated scans at several calibrated <electric fields>, and agreement between independently assigned transitions. These are methodological checks, not claims about undocumented steps of the 1970 experiment. Field calibration and state assignment limit the inferred <electric dipole moment>. The published 1970 abstract confirms analysis of HCl and DCl beam-resonance spectra and combination with <infrared spectroscopy> intensities to obtain a distance-dependent <electric dipole moment>; its full apparatus and calibration record were unavailable here.<Dipole Moment and Hyperfine Parameters of H35Cl and D35Cl by E. W. Kaiser>[pp. 1686–1703, authored abstract; full text unavailable]{ref}

Even an accurately measured <electric dipole moment> is not an absolute <partial atomic charge>. Replacing a <hydrogen chloride> <molecule> by two point <electric charges> $\pm q$ separated by <bond length> $r$ gives $|\mu|=|q|r$. Using $1.109$ <debye> and $r=1.27455\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm m$ gives $|q|\approx0.181$ times the elementary <electric charge>. This is an effective two-point model, combining a vibrationally averaged <electric dipole moment> with an equilibrium <bond length>, not a unique partition of the actual <electron density> or a measured “18.1 percent ionic bond”.<Hydrogen chloride: Constants of diatomic molecules by Klaus P. Huber and others>{ref}

For a <molecule> with several <chemical bonds>, <molecular geometry> can cancel opposing contributions to the total <electric dipole moment>. <Carbon dioxide> illustrates why an overall <nonpolar molecule> need not have unpolarized individual <chemical bonds>. An <electronegativity> comparison helps anticipate local <bond polarization>; the measured molecular <electric dipole moment> tests the entire <electric charge> distribution.

= Debye
{disambiguate=unit}
{parent=Dipole evidence for bond polarization}
{wiki=Debye}
{title2=$\mathrm D$}

= Debye
{synonym}

The <debye> is a unit of <electric dipole moment>: $1\,\mathrm D\approx3.33564\times10^{-30}\,\mathrm{C\,m}$.<Triatomic Spectral Database by National Institute of Standards and Technology/5. Special Units, Fundamental Constants, and Useful Conversion Factors>{ref}

= Molecular beam electric resonance
{parent=Dipole evidence for bond polarization}

The apparatus and dipole inference are described under <dipole evidence for bond polarization>. The <rigid rotor> supplies a general starting model for rotational <quantum states>; <rotational spectroscopy> measures their transition <frequencies>, while <molecular beam electric resonance> can select and analyze states through their <Stark effects>.

= Bond polarization
{parent=Electronegativity}

<Bond polarization> is unequal sharing of <electron density> across a <chemical bond>. An <electronegativity> difference is a heuristic for its direction and tendency, not a unique <partial atomic charge>.

= Polar covalent bond
{parent=Bond polarization}

A <polar covalent bond> shares <electrons> unequally, as in <hydrogen chloride>.

= Hydrogen chloride
{parent=Polar covalent bond}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{HCl}$}

= Hydrogen fluoride
{parent=Polar covalent bond}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{HF}$}

= Ionic bonding
{parent=Chemical bond}
{wiki}

<Ionic bonding> binds oppositely charged <ions> through attraction governed by <electrostatics>. In ordinary solid <sodium chloride>, each <cation> interacts with many <anions>, and also with other <cations>: attraction and repulsion together contribute to the binding of an extended <ionic crystal>. The <chemical formula> $\mathrm{NaCl}$ specifies equal numbers of the two kinds of <ions>; it does not divide the <solid> into isolated two-atom <molecules>.<Chemistry 2e>[§7.1, “The Formation of Ionic Compounds”, Figure 7.3]{ref}

This distinction explains a striking combination of properties: ordinary dry <sodium chloride> conducts <electric current> poorly, while its molten form or a sufficiently concentrated <chemical solution> in <water> conducts well. The <ions> already exist in the <solid>; their ability to move through the sample changes. A <conductivity experiment on salt solutions> tests this transport picture, rather than simply asserting that charged constituents must make every material conductive.

\Image[ionic-crystal-pair-and-binding-energy.png]
{id=image-ionic-crystal-pair-and-binding-energy}
{title=An isolated <ion pair>, a plane of an extended <ionic crystal>, and a synthetic <potential energy> model with a stable spacing}
{description=Original schematic geometry and calculated model curves, not a measured <electron density>, <diffraction> pattern, or experimental <lattice energy>. Four nearest neighbours are visible in the plane of the <rock salt structure>; two more lie perpendicular to it. Disk sizes are arbitrary. The right-hand curves use the explicitly stipulated <dimensionless> model below.}

= Salt
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Ionic bonding}
{wiki}

= Salt
{synonym}

= Ionic compound
{synonym}

A <salt> is an assembly of <cations> and <anions> with zero total <electric charge>. Its constituents can be single <chemical atoms> or groups joined internally by <covalent bonds>. Thus <ionic bonding> and <covalent bonds> can occur in the same <chemical substance>, at different structural levels.<Chemistry 2e>[§2.6, polyatomic ions and ionic compounds]{ref}

= Potassium permanganate
{parent=Salt (chemistry)}
{title2=$\mathrm{KMnO}_4$}

A <salt> of <potassium> <cations> and <permanganate> <anions>, supplying <permanganate> in a <redox titration>.

= Stoichiometry of ionic compounds
{parent=Salt (chemistry)}
{title2=$\sum_i\nu_i z_i=0$}

For a neutral <formula unit>, let $\nu_i$ count <ions> of type $i$ with <electric charge> $z_i e$, where $e$ is the <elementary charge>. The <stoichiometry> must satisfy
$$
\sum_i\nu_i z_i=0.
$$
For $\mathrm{Na}^+$ and $\mathrm{Cl}^-$, the smallest positive counts are $1:1$. For $\mathrm{Ca}^{2+}$ and $\mathrm{Cl}^-$, they are $1:2$, giving <calcium chloride> $\mathrm{CaCl_2}$: $1(2)+2(-1)=0$. The formula counts <ions>, not the number of nearest neighbours. A <counterion> is the oppositely charged partner needed to balance a charged species; its identity can change <solubility> and other properties without changing the identity of that species.<Chemistry 2e>[§7.1, neutral ionic formulas and charge balance; §2.6, ionic compound examples]{ref}

= Calcium chloride
{parent=Stoichiometry of ionic compounds}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{CaCl_2}$}

= Ionic crystal
{parent=Ionic bonding}
{wiki}

An <ionic crystal> repeats an arrangement of <ions> in three dimensions. A <unit cell> describes that repetition, whereas a <formula unit> describes composition. The two accounting devices answer different questions. <Coulomb's law> is not restricted to an arbitrarily drawn line connecting one <cation> to one <anion>: every <ion> contributes to the <electric field> experienced by the others.

= Rock salt structure
{parent=Ionic crystal}

The <rock salt structure> of ordinary <sodium chloride> has a <coordination number> of six for each kind of <ion>: six nearest neighbours of the opposite sign surround each <ion> along three perpendicular directions. Its conventional cubic <unit cell> contains four <formula units>; sharing corner, face, and edge sites between adjacent cells must be included in that count. Sixfold local coordination is compatible with $1:1$ overall <stoichiometry>.<Chemistry 2e>[§7.1, Figure 7.3; §10.6, “The Structures of Ionic Crystals”, NaCl unit-cell accounting]{ref}

The checkerboard plane in the diagram is a slice through this three-dimensional arrangement. It leaves out the two nearest neighbours above and below the plane. Repeating a complete even-by-even patch gives equal numbers of signs; the displayed odd-by-odd crop has a boundary imbalance and is not a complete neutral <ionic crystal> or a <unit cell>. Other <salts> need not have the <rock salt structure> or the same <coordination number>.

= Coordination number
{parent=Ionic crystal}
{title2=$\mathrm{CN}$}

In this structural use, the <coordination number> counts nearest neighbouring constituents. State which neighbours are counted and which <ionic crystal> structure is being described; it is not an <ion>'s <electric charge> number.

= Ion pair
{parent=Ionic bonding}

An <ion pair> is an associated <cation> and <anion>. An isolated <ion pair> lacks the extended neighbourhood of an <ionic crystal>. In a <chemical solution>, <ion pairs> can coexist with more widely separated <ions>; their abundance depends on conditions. A neutral gas-phase <sodium chloride> <molecule> can be treated as a discrete entity, with its own <quantum mechanics> and dissociation products. That use of <molecule> does not describe the constituents of ordinary solid <sodium chloride>.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others>[§8–3, distinction between vaporized NaCl pairs and separated gaseous ions]{ref}<Conductivity of Solutions by Harvard Natural Sciences Lecture Demonstrations>{ref}

= Electron transfer and ionic character
{parent=Ionic bonding}

The bookkeeping steps $\mathrm{Na}\to\mathrm{Na}^++e^-$ and $\mathrm{Cl}+e^-\to\mathrm{Cl}^-$ conserve <electric charge> and describe the <electron> inventories of separated species. They do not establish a chronological mechanism in which a labeled <electron> jumps between two pre-existing <chemical atoms> and becomes a classical object owned exclusively by one of them. In a bound material, <quantum mechanics> determines the <electron density> and the total <energy>.

<Ionic bonding> and a <covalent bond> are useful limiting descriptions with a continuum between them. <Electronegativity> helps anticipate unequal sharing of <electron density>, but a numerical cutoff does not measure a universal fraction of <ionic bonding>. A <partial atomic charge> depends on how the <electron density> is partitioned; integer <formal charges> are a bookkeeping convention. Using $\pm e$ in a point-charge model therefore does not claim that every partition of the actual <electron density> assigns exactly those values.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others>[§30–2, “Chemical bonds in crystals”: gradations between covalent and ionic bonding]{ref}<Chemistry 2e>[§7.2, “Electronegativity and Bond Type”: general guide and exceptions]{ref}

= Electrostatic model of ionic binding
{parent=Ionic bonding}
{title2=$U_{ij}=z_i z_j e^2/(4\pi\epsilon_0r_{ij})$}

For two point <electric charges> in <vacuum>, <Coulomb's law> gives the interaction <potential energy>
$$
U_{ij}(r_{ij})=\frac{z_i z_j e^2}{4\pi\epsilon_0 r_{ij}},
$$
with zero at infinite separation. Here $r_{ij}$ is the distance between them and $\epsilon_0$ is the <vacuum permittivity>. Opposite signs give negative <potential energy> and attraction; equal signs give positive <potential energy> and repulsion. For a finite collection of distinct point <electric charges>, sum over $i<j$ to count each pair once. A bulk <ionic crystal> requires the extended lattice sum, including both signs, rather than multiplying one attractive pair by the <coordination number>.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others>[§8–1, equations (8.1)–(8.3); §8–3, many-neighbour lattice sum]{ref}

At short distances, overlapping <electron density> makes the simple point-charge picture inadequate. <Quantum mechanics>, including the <Pauli exclusion principle>, matters to the steep repulsive contribution. Pure attraction proportional to $-1/r$ would collapse as $r\to0$; it cannot set an equilibrium spacing on its own. A useful illustrative model is
$$
U(r)=-\frac{A}{r}+\frac{B}{r^n},\qquad A>0,\quad B>0,\quad n>1.
$$
Here $r$ scales an assumed fixed arrangement, $A$ packages its attractive contribution, and $B/r^n$ represents short-range repulsion. The constants have units <energy> times length and <energy> times length to the power $n$, respectively. This is a model, not a fitted description of a named <salt>.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others>[§8–3, close-distance repulsion and limits of a purely electrostatic lattice calculation]{ref}

Differentiating gives $r_0=(nB/A)^{1/(n-1)}$ and $U''(r_0)=(n-1)A/r_0^3>0$, so the stationary spacing is a stable minimum. For the diagram's stipulated $n=9$, put $x=r/r_0$ and $E_0=A(1-1/n)/r_0>0$. Then
$$
\frac{U(r)}{E_0}=\frac{-9/x+1/x^9}{8},\qquad U(r_0)=-E_0.
$$
The <dimensionless> curves show how competing interactions select a spacing. Their <energy> scale and exponent are synthetic; neither is a measured <lattice energy> or a <melting> prediction.

= Lattice energy
{parent=Ionic bonding}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Delta H_{\rm latt,diss}$}

A <lattice energy> needs a reaction, a sign convention, and a stated amount. Using the lattice-dissociation <enthalpy> convention for <sodium chloride>,
$$
\mathrm{NaCl}(s)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Na}^+(g)+\mathrm{Cl}^-(g),\qquad
\Delta H_{\rm latt,diss}>0.
$$
The reverse lattice-formation <enthalpy> is negative with the same magnitude when initial and final states match. <Chemistry 2e> gives $769\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ for this separation, per <mole> of <sodium chloride> <formula units>. This is about $7.97$ <electronvolts> per <formula unit>, not per individual <ion>. It concerns separated gaseous <ions>, rather than neutral <chemical atoms>, molten <salt>, or hydrated <ions> in <water>.<Chemistry 2e>[§7.5, “Ionic Bond Strength and Lattice Energy”, positive convention, reverse convention and NaCl value]{ref}

Also distinguish an <internal energy> change from an <enthalpy> change: $H=U+pV$. A static point-charge calculation omits motion and thermal contributions. Reversing a reaction reverses its <energy> change; replacing <internal energy> with <enthalpy> additionally changes the defined quantity. The <Born-Haber cycle> infers a lattice-formation <enthalpy> by combining other <enthalpies>, <ionization energies>, and <electron affinities>. These imagined stages are a thermodynamic accounting path, not a claim about the actual sequence of a <chemical reaction>.<Chemistry 2e>[§7.5, “The Born-Haber Cycle”, Figure 7.13 and Table 7.4]{ref}

= Born-Haber cycle
{c}
{parent=Lattice energy}

A <Born-Haber cycle> uses <Hess's law> to infer a <lattice energy> from measurable <enthalpy> differences and isolated-species data. The motivating difficulty is that separating a <mole> of an <ionic crystal> into mutually separated gaseous <ions> is not a convenient direct <calorimetry> experiment. Instead, compare two accounting routes between the same elemental reference states and the same <solid>. Because <enthalpy> is a <state function>, the signed differences close exactly when all inputs describe compatible states. These routes do not assert a <reaction mechanism> or a temporal sequence of <reaction intermediates>.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/7.5 Strengths of Ionic and Covalent Bonds>[“The Born-Haber Cycle”, Figure 7.13 and Table 7.4]{ref}

For one <mole> of <sodium chloride> <formula units>, the overall formation reaction is
$$
\mathrm{Na}(s)+\tfrac12\mathrm{Cl}_2(g)\longrightarrow\mathrm{NaCl}(s),\qquad F=\Delta H_{\rm f}^{\circ}.
$$
The <stoichiometry> requires half a <mole> of diatomic <chlorine> <molecules>, supplying one <mole> of <chlorine> <chemical atoms>. A half coefficient counts an <amount of substance>; it does not describe half of an individual <molecule>. Write each auxiliary <chemical reaction> with its own sign:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{Na}(s)&\longrightarrow\mathrm{Na}(g) && S>0,\\
\tfrac12\mathrm{Cl}_2(g)&\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cl}(g) && B>0,\\
\mathrm{Na}(g)&\longrightarrow\mathrm{Na}^+(g)+e^-(g) && I>0,\\
\mathrm{Cl}(g)+e^-(g)&\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cl}^-(g) && A=\Delta H_{\rm attach}^{\circ}<0,\\
\mathrm{Na}^+(g)+\mathrm{Cl}^-(g)&\longrightarrow\mathrm{NaCl}(s) && L_{\rm form}<0.
\end{aligned}
$$
Here $S$ is the <sublimation> <enthalpy>, $B$ the <enthalpy of atomization> of <chlorine> per <mole> of <chemical atoms>, and $I$ the molar ionization <enthalpy> represented by the <ionization energy> input in elementary treatments. If the <bond-dissociation energy> table gives the compatible molar <enthalpy> for $\mathrm{Cl}_2(g)\to2\mathrm{Cl}(g)$, use half that number for $B$. Both <ions> in the last step are gaseous, and no aqueous <hydration of ions> belongs in that step.

Adding these equations cancels the gaseous <chemical atoms>, the gaseous <ions> and the free <electron>, leaving precisely the overall formation reaction. Thus
$$
F=S+B+I+A+L_{\rm form},\qquad
L_{\rm diss}=-L_{\rm form}=S+B+I+A-F.
$$
The closed traversal returns from the <solid> to the elemental reference states, adding $-F$; therefore $S+B+I+A+L_{\rm form}-F=0$. Notice that the signed attachment term $A$ is negative. Substituting a positive spectroscopic <electron affinity> for $A$ would reverse the attachment contribution.

\Image[sodium-chloride-born-haber-enthalpy-cycle.png]
{id=image-sodium-chloride-born-haber-enthalpy-cycle}
{title=A closed <Born-Haber cycle> for <sodium chloride> using explicitly synthetic molar <enthalpies>: levels represent complete material inventories, and horizontal position is bookkeeping rather than a <reaction coordinate>}

The diagram deliberately uses invented, internally consistent inputs in $\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$: $S=100$, $B=120$, $I=500$, $A=-350$ and $F=-400$. Preparation of separated gaseous <ions> raises the <enthalpy> by $370$, so $L_{\rm form}=-770$ and $L_{\rm diss}=+770$. The closure check is $100+120+500-350-770+400=0$. These numbers are not measured <sodium chloride> data and do not replace the source's $769\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ example in <lattice energy>. Every level retains one <sodium> nucleus, one <chlorine> nucleus and zero total <electric charge>; the ionization level explicitly retains the free <electron>.

= Lattice enthalpy from solution calorimetry
{parent=Born-Haber cycle}

A second indirect route makes the experimental distinction especially clear: a <calorimeter> can measure dissolution of a weighed <salt> into a known amount of <water>, but its final <ions> are hydrated, not separated gaseous <ions>. At compatible <standard states> and the same <temperature>,
$$
\Delta H_{\rm sol}^{\circ}=L_{\rm diss}+\Delta H_{\rm hyd,pair}^{\circ},\qquad
L_{\rm diss}=\Delta H_{\rm sol}^{\circ}-\Delta H_{\rm hyd,pair}^{\circ}.
$$
Here the combined <hydration of ions> term takes one <mole> each of gaseous $\mathrm{Na}^+$ and $\mathrm{Cl}^-$ to the specified aqueous reference state. Its sum suffices: dissolution <calorimetry> alone cannot independently assign single-ion <hydration of ions> <enthalpies>, or determine $L_{\rm diss}$ without another input. A finite-<concentration> measurement also requires dilution corrections before it can supply $\Delta H_{\rm sol}^{\circ}$.

In a simple constant-<pressure> solution <calorimeter>, an insulated vessel, stirrer and <thermometer> follow the <temperature> change after mixing. A calibrated effective <heat capacity> $C_{\rm eff}$ converts the corrected <temperature> change to <heat>. With no applied heating, negligible external <heat transfer>, and only pressure-volume <work>, the approximate molar result is
$$
\Delta H_{\rm sol}\simeq-\frac{C_{\rm eff}\Delta T}{n}.
$$
Here $n$ is the <amount of substance> dissolved and $C_{\rm eff}$ includes the <chemical solution> and the vessel. Cooling means positive dissolution <enthalpy>, because the dissolving sample takes <heat> from its surroundings.<Chemistry 2e>[§5.2, Figures 5.11–5.13 and discussion of including solution and calorimeter heat capacities]{ref}

An actual reference measurement used <potassium chloride>, not <sodium chloride>: a platinum-lined <calorimeter> with an adiabatic shield and a quartz-oscillator <thermometer>. Because dissolution absorbed <heat>, measured electrical heating prevented loss of shield control; the readouts were electrical input and the vessel/shield <temperature> record. Stirring was checked visually in a dummy vessel. The <thermometer> was compared with a calibrated resistance <thermometer>, and electrical calibration bracketed the reaction. The reported result was $+(17.584\pm0.017)\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ at $298.15\,\mathrm K$, for one <mole> of <potassium chloride> in $500$ <moles> of <water>.<The Enthalpy of Solution of SRM 1655 (KCl) in H2O by Marthada V. Kilday>[§3 and §4: apparatus, calibration, readouts and specified dilution; inspected through indexed article text]{ref}

Checks on residual <water>, particle size and final <concentration>, and agreement with a second <calorimeter>, constrained systematic errors. Imperfect shield tracking required <heat transfer> corrections. This experiment measures an <enthalpy of solution>, not a direct <lattice energy>; the hydration and dilution bookkeeping still has to be supplied. The full article could not be opened here; the inspected indexed text supports the stated apparatus and checks, without reconstructing its complete uncertainty budget.<The Enthalpy of Solution of SRM 1655 (KCl) in H2O by Marthada V. Kilday>[§§2–4 and conclusion: moisture, particle-size and dilution studies; confirmation with an isoperibol calorimeter; full-page access blocked]{ref}

= Potassium chloride
{parent=Lattice enthalpy from solution calorimetry}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{KCl}$}

= Physical properties of salts
{parent=Ionic bonding}

Many <ionic crystals> have high <melting> <temperatures> compared with small molecular <solids>, because reorganizing their strongly interacting <ions> costs <energy>. But <melting> leaves a dense <liquid> with interactions; it does not perform the complete separation defining a <lattice energy>. At fixed pressure, the transition occurs when the <Gibbs free energies> of the <solid> and <liquid> match. The relevant <enthalpy> and <entropy> differences both matter, so <lattice energy> alone is not a universal ordering of <melting> <temperatures>. Strong <covalent bonds> in extended networks can also give high <melting> <temperatures>.<Chemistry 2e>[§10.5, “Ionic Solids” and “Covalent Network Solid”; §16.4, equations defining Gibbs free energy and its change]{ref}

= Solubility
{parent=Physical properties of salts}
{wiki}

<Solubility> specifies the amount that can remain dissolved at <chemical equilibrium> under stated conditions; it is different from how quickly a sample dissolves. Separating <ions> from an <ionic crystal> costs <energy>, but <solvation> establishes new interactions with the <solvent>. In <water>, <hydration of ions> couples charged <ions> to polar <water> <molecules>. The net <enthalpy> of dissolution can have either sign; <entropy> and <concentration> also enter the <Gibbs free energy> balance. Consequently, being a <salt> does not guarantee high <solubility> in <water> or any other <solvent>.<Chemistry 2e>[§11.1, “The Formation of Solutions”, Figure 11.4; §11.3, definition of solubility; §16.4, free energy]{ref}

Low <solubility> must also be distinguished from weak <electrolyte> behavior. A small amount of an <ionic compound> can dissolve essentially completely as <ions>, giving few charge carriers because little material dissolved. Calling that a <weak electrolyte> solely because its <chemical solution> conducts poorly would conflate two different questions.<Chemistry 2e>[§11.2, “Ionic Electrolytes”: sparingly soluble compounds can be strong electrolytes]{ref}

= Solvation
{parent=Solubility}
{wiki}

<Solvation> is stabilization through interactions between a <solute> and its <solvent>. It involves both the <solute>'s surroundings and reorganization of the <solvent>: neighbors move, orientations change, and existing interactions compete with new ones. <Electric forces>, <van der Waals forces>, and specific interactions such as <hydrogen bonds> can contribute. <Solvation> can also occur at groups on an insoluble material, so it does not require complete <dissolution>.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/solvation>{ref}

<Solvation> by <water> is called hydration. <Hydration of ions> is the charged special case, but neutral <molecules> such as <sucrose> can also interact with <water> without becoming <ions>. A hydrated <cation> retains its positive net <electric charge>; orienting neutral <water> <molecules> around it does not supply an extra <electron>. <Dissolution>, <desolvation>, and <ionization> describe different changes, as the examples below show.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/11.1 The Dissolution Process>[Opening sucrose example]{ref}<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/11.2 Electrolytes>[“Ionic Electrolytes” and “Covalent Electrolytes”]{ref}

The useful question is therefore not just whether a <solute> attracts a <solvent>, but how much the whole system gains after paying for the rearrangements. <Solvation free energy> describes a specified transfer into a <solvent>; <solvation thermodynamic balance> explains why even cooling during <dissolution> can accompany favorable mixing. The existing <solubility> discussion concerns how much remains dissolved at <chemical equilibrium>.

= Solvation shell
{parent=Solvation}

A <solvation shell> is the region of <solvent> constituents surrounding a <solute>, with local arrangements influenced by their interactions. The first <solvation shell> contains the closest neighbors under a stated distance or interaction criterion. A <coordination number> counts those neighbors; it requires such a criterion and can fluctuate. Neither a drawn ring nor a few illustrated <molecules> determines a measured <coordination number>.

In a <liquid>, a <solvation shell> is an evolving neighborhood: <solvent> <molecules> reorient and exchange with the surroundings. Different <ions> can produce different local dynamics. <Spectroscopic measurement of hydration dynamics> provides an experimental way to distinguish some of those environments without pretending that a shell is a fixed cage.

= Hydration shell
{parent=Solvation shell}

A <hydration shell> is a <solvation shell> in <water>. The existing <hydration of ions> discussion describes preferential orientations around <cations> and <anions>.

\Image[hydration-shell-charge-orientation.png]
{id=image-hydration-shell-charge-orientation}
{title=Qualitative <hydration shells>: opposite orientations of polar <water> <molecules> around a <cation> and an <anion>, with each <ion>'s net <electric charge> retained}
{description=Original schematic, not measured molecular positions. Atom spacings and the dashed shell boundary are illustrative. Five selected <water> glyphs in each panel do not assert a <coordination number>. Each <water> <molecule> is neutral despite its <partial atomic charges>.}

= Ion-dipole attraction
{parent=Solvation shell}
{title2=$U=-\mathbf p\cdot\mathbf E$}

An <ion-dipole attraction> couples an <ion>'s <electric field> to a <molecule>'s <electric dipole moment>. In the elementary point-<electric dipole> approximation, the interaction <potential energy> is $U=-\mathbf p\cdot\mathbf E$. An orientation with the <electric dipole moment> along the <electric field> lowers this <energy>. For <water>, the <oxygen> end preferentially faces a <cation>, and the <hydrogen> end an <anion>. At nonzero <temperature>, fluctuations and competing <hydrogen bonds> prevent perfect alignment of every <molecule>.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/11.2 Electrolytes>[“Ionic Electrolytes”, Figure 11.7 and its explanatory paragraph]{ref}

= Spectroscopic measurement of hydration dynamics
{parent=Solvation shell}

What separates a <hydration shell> from the surrounding <water> experimentally? <Electrical conductivity> addresses a different question. The documented lamp demonstration immerses metal <electrodes> in a <chemical solution>, rinses them between samples, and records brightness; adding <sodium chloride> to distilled <water> makes it conductive. A <water> blank and dissolved <sucrose> comparison test whether dissolved material alone explains the signal. Fixed geometry, <temperature>, and stated <concentrations> are additional proposed controls. This reveals mobile <electric charge>, with limits from contamination, electrode interfaces and detection threshold; it supplies no shell positions. <Conductivity experiment on salt solutions> explains the apparatus and controls in detail.<Conductivity of Solutions by Harvard Natural Sciences Lecture Demonstrations>[“Setting it Up” and “Comments”]{ref}

In the authored study <Influence of ions on the hydrogen-bond structure in liquid water by Anne Willem Omta and others>, <pump-probe spectroscopy> uses tunable mid-<infrared> pulses traversing a sample. The probe's <wave polarization> is set at $45^{\circ}$ to the pump; after the sample, splitting and <polarizers> select parallel and perpendicular transmission. Measuring each transmission with and without the pump gives $D_{\parallel}$ and $D_{\perp}$, the respective logarithms of the transmission ratios. Their <dimensionless> <anisotropy> is
$$
R(t)=\frac{D_{\parallel}(t)-D_{\perp}(t)}{D_{\parallel}(t)+2D_{\perp}(t)}.
$$
Its decay probes reorientation rather than merely loss of vibrational excitation. Dilute <semiheavy water> in <heavy water> separates the O–H and O–D bands. Varying the <semiheavy water> <concentration> checked that transfer of excitation between nearby O–H groups did not determine the decay. These apparatus and control details are from the inspected author-uploaded article, not a new experiment.<Influence of ions on the hydrogen-bond structure in liquid water by Anne Willem Omta and others>[§II, printed p. 12458, Figure 1 and equations (2)–(3); author-uploaded article text inspected]{ref}

The related thesis chapter documents two O–H absorption bands in a <sodium perchlorate> <chemical solution>: approximately $3400\,\mathrm{cm^{-1}}$ for groups forming <hydrogen bonds> to <water>, and $3575\,\mathrm{cm^{-1}}$ for groups forming <hydrogen bonds> to the <anion>. Selecting pump and probe bands, then a later delay window, separated their responses. The anion-associated <anisotropy> decayed more slowly; the water-bonded component showed no resolved change in decay time across the tested <sodium perchlorate> <concentrations>. The chapter also used O–D excitation to extend observation beyond the shorter O–H vibrational lifetime. These results support distinct local dynamics rather than a permanently fixed <hydration shell>. Spectral overlap, finite observation windows, and the selected <salts> limit the inference: this does not prove that every <ion> leaves all distant <water> properties unchanged.<Ion solvation in water: femtosecond spectroscopy of hydrogen-bond dynamics by Michel F. Kropman/8 The influence of ions on the hydrogen-bond structure of water>[§8.2 and §8.3, printed pp. 82–89, Figures 8.1–8.7; PDF text inspected, numerical decay-time OCR unreliable and not reproduced]{ref}

= Pump-probe spectroscopy
{parent=Spectroscopic measurement of hydration dynamics}

<Pump-probe spectroscopy> measures a sample's response to a probe pulse at a controlled delay after an exciting pump pulse. Varying delay follows dynamics; selecting <frequency> and <wave polarization> can distinguish different contributions. <Spectroscopic measurement of hydration dynamics> is an application to <water> surrounding <ions>.

= Heavy water
{parent=Spectroscopic measurement of hydration dynamics}
{title2=$\mathrm D_2\mathrm O$}

<Heavy water> replaces both ordinary <hydrogen> nuclei of <water> with <deuterium>.

= Semiheavy water
{parent=Spectroscopic measurement of hydration dynamics}
{title2=$\mathrm{HDO}$}

<Semiheavy water> contains one ordinary <hydrogen> nucleus and one <deuterium> nucleus. Dilute <semiheavy water> in <heavy water> provides relatively isolated O–H groups for <infrared spectroscopy>.

= Dissolution
{parent=Solvation}

<Dissolution> transfers material into a <chemical solution>. It can include separation from an <ionic crystal> and <solvation>, or dispersal of neutral <molecules>. For <sucrose> and <sodium chloride>, respectively,
$$
\mathrm{C}_{12}\mathrm{H}_{22}\mathrm{O}_{11}(s)\longrightarrow\mathrm{C}_{12}\mathrm{H}_{22}\mathrm{O}_{11}(aq),\qquad
\mathrm{NaCl}(s)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Na}^{+}(aq)+\mathrm{Cl}^{-}(aq).
$$
Here $(aq)$ specifies <water> as the <solvent>; it does not specify a fixed number of attached <water> <molecules>. The neutral <sucrose> remains molecular. <Dissociation of an ionic compound> separates pre-existing <ions> in the second example; <solvation> stabilizes their new surroundings.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/11.1 The Dissolution Process>[Opening molecular and ionic dissolution examples]{ref}<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/11.2 Electrolytes>[“Ionic Electrolytes”]{ref}

<Ionization> instead creates <ions> from neutral species. <Hydrogen chloride> reacting with <water> illustrates <protonation> of <water> rather than mere rearrangement of a <hydration shell>:
$$
\mathrm{HCl}+\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm O\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}_3\mathrm O^{+}+\mathrm{Cl}^{-}.
$$
The <hydronium> and negative <ion> together preserve total <electric charge>. <Solvation> can influence this <chemical reaction>, but an <ion>'s <solvation> does not itself change its net <electric charge>.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/11.2 Electrolytes>[“Covalent Electrolytes”, HCl proton-transfer example]{ref}

= Dissociation of an ionic compound
{parent=Dissolution}

<Dissociation of an ionic compound> separates its constituent <ions>. It differs from creating charged species by <ionization> of neutral <molecules>. Dissolved <ions> can also associate into <ion pairs>, so a dissociated description does not exclude all subsequent association.

= Sodium perchlorate
{parent=Dissociation of an ionic compound}
{title2=$\mathrm{NaClO}_4$}

<Sodium perchlorate> is the <salt> used in the spectrally selected example of <spectroscopic measurement of hydration dynamics>.

= Desolvation
{parent=Solvation}

<Desolvation> removes some or all of the <solvent> environment of a species. Removing a <hydration shell> does not remove an <ion>'s <electric charge>. Complete reversal of a specified <solvation> transfer reverses its <Gibbs free energy> and <enthalpy> changes when the same states and conventions are used. Partial <desolvation>, exchange of one <solvent> for another, and creation of a gaseous species have different endpoints; their costs need not match.

= Solvation free energy
{parent=Solvation}
{title2=$\Delta G_{\mathrm{solv}}^{\circ}$}

For transfer of the same species $X$ from a gaseous reference state into a <solvent> $S$, define the molar <solvation free energy> by
$$
X(g)\longrightarrow X(S),\qquad
\Delta G_{\mathrm{solv}}^{\circ}(S)=\mu_X^{\circ}(S)-\mu_X^{\circ}(g).
$$
Here $\mu^{\circ}$ is a standard <chemical potential>. Negative <solvation free energy> means that transfer into the stated <solvent> lowers the <Gibbs free energy> relative to the stated gaseous reference. Include <solute>-<solvent> interactions, making room in the <solvent>, and its reorganization; counting only a favorable pair interaction omits part of the transfer.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/solvation energy>{ref}

State the <temperature>, gaseous reference <pressure>, dissolved reference <concentration> or other <standard state> convention, and species identity. Reference-state changes alter the quoted <solvation free energy>. For example, with an ideal dilute <thermodynamic activity> $a=c/c^{\circ}$, replacing $c^{\circ}$ by $\alpha c^{\circ}$ while preserving the physical <chemical potential> requires $\mu^{\circ}$ to increase by $RT\ln\alpha$. Thus the corresponding <solvation free energy> increases by $RT\ln\alpha$ if the gaseous reference stays fixed. This follows directly from $\mu=\mu^{\circ}+RT\ln a$, with $R$ the <molar gas constant>.

= Solvent-specific stabilization
{parent=Solvation free energy}

<Solvent-specific stabilization> means that the same species need not have the same <solvation free energy> in different <solvents>. <Chemical polarity>, <hydrogen bonds>, and the cost of reorganizing each <solvent> all matter. <Water> can stabilize <ions> by <ion-dipole attractions>; a favorable isolated attraction alone still cannot decide the total <dissolution> balance.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/solvation energy>{ref}

With a common gaseous reference and compatible dissolved <standard states>, subtraction removes that reference:
$$
\Delta G_{\mathrm{transfer}}^{\circ}(A\to B)
=\mu_X^{\circ}(B)-\mu_X^{\circ}(A)
=\Delta G_{\mathrm{solv}}^{\circ}(B)-\Delta G_{\mathrm{solv}}^{\circ}(A).
$$
A negative result favors <solvent> B under those reference conventions. This comparison assumes the same species $X$: if <protonation>, <ionization>, or formation of <ion pairs> changes its identity, include a <chemical equilibrium> for each corresponding <chemical reaction>.

= Solvation thermodynamic balance
{parent=Solvation}
{title2=$\Delta G=\Delta H-T\Delta S$}

At fixed <temperature> and <pressure>, compare fully specified initial and final states using
$$
\Delta G=\Delta H-T\Delta S.
$$
The <entropy> change is for the whole selected system, including both <solute> and <solvent>. Dispersing a <solute> can increase accessible arrangements while orienting nearby <solvent> <molecules> restricts others. One picture of an ordered <hydration shell> cannot determine the sign of the total <entropy> change. A negative <Gibbs free energy> change favors the specified process; it does not determine its <reaction rate>.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/16.4 Free Energy>[Opening definition and Table 16.3]{ref}

An illustrative <enthalpy> accounting path separates <solute> constituents, reorganizes the <solvent>, and establishes their interactions:
$$
\Delta H=\Delta H_{\mathrm{separate}}+\Delta H_{\mathrm{reorganize}}+\Delta H_{\mathrm{interact}}.
$$
This is a chosen decomposition between compatible states, not a measured sequence. If a gaseous-ion-to-<chemical solution> <solvation> term already includes <solvent> reorganization, do not add that reorganization a second time. <Lattice enthalpy from solution calorimetry> gives the alternative <Hess's law> route that combines lattice separation with the complete <hydration of ions> term.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/11.1 The Dissolution Process>[“The Formation of Solutions”, Figure 11.4]{ref}

\Image[solvation-enthalpy-entropy-balance.png]
{id=image-solvation-enthalpy-entropy-balance}
{title=Synthetic <dissolution> bookkeeping: <solvent> interactions change the <enthalpy> balance, while total <entropy> also enters the <Gibbs free energy>}
{description=Original stipulated model for one <mole> of a hypothetical 1:1 <salt> at $300\,\mathrm K$, with two unspecified <solvents>. No plotted value is a measurement or a claim about a named <chemical substance>. The accounting path is not a <reaction coordinate> or an <activation energy>.}

The invented inputs are $+100$ for separation and $+20$ for <solvent> reorganization, in $\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Interaction terms of $-150$ in <solvent> A and $-90$ in <solvent> B give $\Delta H_A=-30$ and $\Delta H_B=+30$. Stipulated total <entropy> changes of $-50$ and $+150\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$ give $-T\Delta S_A=+15$ and $-T\Delta S_B=-45\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ at $300\,\mathrm K$. Both selected processes therefore have $\Delta G=-15\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. This deliberately constructed comparison shows why the signs of the <enthalpy> and <Gibbs free energy> changes need not agree; it does not predict either <solvent>'s <solubility>.

For incremental <dissolution> at a particular composition, the relevant <chemical potential> difference also depends on <thermodynamic activities>. At <chemical equilibrium> that driving difference vanishes. A quoted standard <Gibbs free energy> change and the finite change between arbitrary mixtures answer different questions; use compatible <standard states> and the <reaction quotient> when discussing <solubility>.

The motivating <calorimetry> experiment is especially instructive. A weighed <potassium chloride> sample dissolves in a known amount of <water> in a platinum-lined, shielded <calorimeter>; a quartz-oscillator <thermometer> and measured electrical heating provide the readouts. Electrical calibration before and after the reaction, comparison with a resistance <thermometer>, and checks on residual <water> and particle size constrain errors. The inspected authored article reports positive <enthalpy of solution> at $298.15\,\mathrm K$ for the specified dilution, despite complete <dissolution>. This is a combined <enthalpy> balance, not an isolated <solvation free energy> or direct single-ion <hydration of ions> measurement. Shield tracking and final <concentration> matter; the existing <lattice enthalpy from solution calorimetry> discussion gives the numerical result and the conversion limits.<The Enthalpy of Solution of SRM 1655 (KCl) in H2O by Marthada V. Kilday>[Abstract, §§3–4 and conclusion; inspected indexed authored text, direct PMC access challenged]{ref}

= Hydration of ions
{parent=Solvation}

<Hydration of ions> is <solvation> by <water>. The polar <water> <molecules> orient preferentially around <cations> and <anions>, though the surrounding arrangement fluctuates rather than forming a permanently fixed cage.<Chemistry 2e>[§11.2, Figure 11.7 and “Ionic Electrolytes”]{ref}

= Conductivity experiment on salt solutions
{parent=Physical properties of salts}

How can a clear <chemical solution> reveal otherwise invisible mobile <ions>? The documented <Conductivity of Solutions by Harvard Natural Sciences Lecture Demonstrations> demonstration uses metal <electrodes> enclosed in tubing, an electrical supply, and a lamp in the circuit. The protocol specifies about $150\,\mathrm{mL}$ samples in labeled $250\,\mathrm{mL}$ beakers and a separate deionized-<water> rinse station. The recorded readout is lamp brightness after the <electrodes> enter each sample; the simplified variant tests <water> before and after adding <sodium chloride>. These are apparatus and procedure specifications, not new measurements reported here.<Conductivity of Solutions by Harvard Natural Sciences Lecture Demonstrations>{ref}

\Image[salt-solution-conductivity-controls.png]
{id=image-salt-solution-conductivity-controls}
{title=<Electrical conductivity> comparisons with a lamp tester, and the opposite motions of <cations> and <anions> in an <electric field>}
{description=Original circuit schematic and qualitative model outcomes, not photographs or recorded brightness data. A dark lamp means a signal below this tester's visible threshold, not identically zero <electrical conductivity>. Arrows show <drift velocities> during one half-cycle of an alternating supply; both signs contribute to the same <conventional current>. <Water> <molecules> and <hydration of ions> are omitted from the microscopic panel.}

The useful comparison is <water>, a <sucrose> <chemical solution>, and a <sodium chloride> <chemical solution> with the same probe geometry. A documented university account reports no visible light with <water>, a glowing lamp with dissolved <sodium chloride>, and a weak filament glow with its <sucrose> sample. The diagram summarizes the latter as little or no visible light; the precise detection threshold depends on the tester. <Sucrose> is a control for the idea that any dissolved material must conduct: its dispersed neutral <molecules> do not provide the abundant mobile <ions> supplied by the <salt>. This contrast is a documented teaching observation, not a numerical calibration of lamp brightness.<Chemistry 2e>[§11.1, sucrose dissolves as molecules; §11.2, Figure 11.6 and ionic versus nonionic charge carriers]{ref}<Conductivity of solutions by the migration of ions>{ref}

Rinsing the <electrodes> between samples controls carryover of dissolved <ions>, and the <water> blank checks the solvent background. Repeating the blank after the <salt> sample would test whether that rinse was effective. Additional methodological controls are fixed immersed area, electrode separation, supply setting, <temperature>, and comparison at stated <concentrations>. These additional controls are proposed checks, not undocumented claims about the source demonstration.

The inference is that mobile <electric charge> carriers complete the circuit. In the <chemical solution>, an <electric field> drives positive <ions> along the field and negative <ions> against it; multiplying each <drift velocity> by its <electric charge> makes both contributions to <conventional current> point the same way. With an alternating supply the direction reverses each half-cycle. The external wires instead carry <electric current> through <electrons>. The lamp alone identifies neither the species nor an exact <electrical conductivity>.

Important limits remain. <Water> has a small intrinsic population of <hydronium> and <hydroxide>, so a dark lamp does not prove absence of <ions>. Changes in probe geometry affect measured <electrical conductance>; <electrolysis>, electrode interfaces, contamination, and heating can also affect a real circuit. The comparison supports a transport model but does not independently measure a <coordination number>, a partitioned <partial atomic charge>, or a <lattice energy>.

Nor does poor conduction by ordinary dry <sodium chloride> establish that all <ionic crystals> are electrical insulators. <Crystallographic defects> can allow transport, and <solid electrolytes> can have substantial ionic <electrical conductivity> without <melting>. The authored abstract of <A lithium superionic conductor by Noriaki Kamaya and others> reports room-<temperature> ionic conduction in a <solid electrolyte>; the full experimental article was not accessible here.<A lithium superionic conductor by Noriaki Kamaya and others>{ref}

= Crystallographic defect
{parent=Physical properties of salts}
{wiki}

A <crystallographic defect> is a departure from ideal repeated structure. In an <ionic crystal>, a missing <ion> can provide an available site for another <ion> to move into; movement still requires overcoming an <activation energy>. This is why an ideal immobile-site model is a limit rather than a universal law for <solid> <electrical conductivity>.<Chemistry 2e>[§10.5, “Crystal Defects”: vacancies and other imperfections]{ref}

= Solid electrolyte
{parent=Physical properties of salts}

A <solid electrolyte> carries <electric current> through mobile <ions> while remaining a <solid>. The distinction between ionic and electronic <electrical conductivity> matters: different charge carriers serve different roles in <electrochemistry>.

= Bond length
{parent=Chemical bond}
{wiki}
{title2=$r$}

A <bond length> describes the separation of the <atomic nuclei> of two bonded <chemical atoms>. Distinguish an equilibrium geometry from an instantaneous or experimentally averaged separation. Methods of determining <molecular geometry> can use different averaging conventions, so reported <bond lengths> need their measurement context. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/BT07003[IUPAC Gold Book: bond length]. Changing a <bond length> is one possible <reaction coordinate>.

= Partial atomic charge
{parent=Chemical bond}

A partial atomic charge assigns an <electric charge> to a <chemical atom> by partitioning the actual <electron density>. The result depends on the partitioning convention and need not be an integer. It differs from <formal charge>, which divides bonding <electrons> equally in a specified <Lewis structure>.

= Electron pair
{parent=Chemical bond}
{wiki}

An <electron pair> consists of two <electrons> with opposite <spins> sharing the same spatial part of their <quantum state>. The <Lewis structure> distinction between a <bond pair> and a <lone pair> records whether that pair is assigned to a connection between <chemical atoms> or to one <chemical atom>.

= Hydrogen bond
{parent=Chemical bond}
{wiki}
{title2=$X-\mathrm H\cdots Y$}

A <hydrogen bond> is an attractive association involving a <hydrogen> attached to another <chemical atom>, and an accepting atom or group. In the IUPAC 2011 definition, the atom $X$ in $X-\mathrm H$ is more <electronegative> than <hydrogen>, and evidence of bond formation is required. The familiar strong examples use O–H or N–H groups, but restricting the concept to only nitrogen, oxygen and fluorine misses weaker cases.
