Agreement between residual-group and independently calibrated chain-size readouts is stronger evidence than calculating both quantities from the same <Carothers equation>.

= Polycondensation
{parent=Step-growth polymerization}

<Polycondensation> is <step-growth polymerization> in which joining reactions eliminate small <molecules>. Its chain-bearing <molecule> count excludes those byproducts; otherwise two chains joining while releasing one small product would misleadingly appear to conserve the counted population.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[polycondensation, P04722, definition and growth-step equation]{ref}

= Functionality in polymerization
{parent=Polymerization}
{title2=$f$}

The <functionality in polymerization> is the number of reactive <functional groups> on a <molecule> for the specified joining chemistry. The <linear step-growth molecule count> assumes $f=2$ for every feed <molecule>. A <diol> supplies two <hydroxy groups> in the <polyurethane> example; an impurity supplying only one suitable group can cap a chain. A group count must reflect the selected <chemical reaction>, not simply every chemically recognizable group in the <molecule>.

= Gelation
{parent=Polymerization}

<Gelation> is formation of an extended connected network. In <step-growth polymerization>, feed <molecules> with <functionality in polymerization> above two can introduce branching and permit <gelation>. The <linear step-growth molecule count>'s two-ended-chain interpretation and bifunctional <Carothers equation> must not silently be applied to such networks. Each join between distinct connected pieces still reduces their count by one, but neither that identity nor average size alone specifies the onset of <gelation>.

= Chain-growth polymerization
{parent=Polymerization}
{wiki}

= Chain polymerization
{synonym}

In <chain-growth polymerization>, each growth step joins a <monomer> to a reactive site on a <polymer> chain and regenerates that reactive site. This is the precise mechanistic restriction in IUPAC's definition: growth occurs exclusively through these monomer–chain-site reactions. The word chain refers to the succession of site-regenerating <chemical reactions>. It does not merely mean that the product looks like a long chain. <Chain-growth initiation> starts this succession; <chain-growth propagation> continues it; <chain-growth termination> and <chain transfer in chain polymerization> can change its fate.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[chain polymerization, C00958, definition and notes 1–2; source recommendation p. 2306]{ref}

The contrast with <step-growth polymerization> concerns the permitted joining partners. There, suitable reactive groups can join two existing chains, for example a two-unit chain and a three-unit chain. In <chain-growth propagation>, a free <monomer> adds to a <polymerization-active site>. Release of a small byproduct is a separate classification question: IUPAC explicitly allows condensative <chain polymerization>.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[chain polymerization, C00958, notes 3–6]{ref} The useful consequence is that a few growing chains can incorporate many <monomer molecules> while much of the feed remains unreacted. Their <degree of polymerization> depends on how those units are shared among chains, as <finite chain-growth bookkeeping> makes explicit.

\Image[chain-growth-active-site-bookkeeping.png]
{id=image-chain-growth-active-site-bookkeeping}
{title=<Chain-growth initiation>, <chain-growth propagation>, and two possible <chain-growth termination> counts}
{description=Original schematic for linear chains with one <polymerization-active site> each. Blocks count <monomeric units>; stars identify reactivity, not <electric charge>. $I^*$ is a free initiating species, excluded from the count of polymer-bound sites. The two termination rows are alternative outcomes for two active chains, not consecutive reactions. Joining two chains preserves their incorporated-unit total while reducing the number of <polymer> <molecules>. Neither row specifies a universal <reaction mechanism> or rate.}

= Polymerization-active site
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

A <polymerization-active site> is the reactive location that enables the next <chain-growth propagation> step. In the schematic
$$
P_j^*+M\longrightarrow P_{j+1}^*,
$$
$M$ is one free <monomer molecule>, $j$ is the chain's <degree of polymerization> in incorporated <monomeric units>, and the star marks the <polymerization-active site>. The site's reactive character survives the growth step, although the <chemical atoms> carrying it can change. The star is not an extra unit, a literal particle, or necessarily a positive or negative <electric charge>. Actual <reaction mechanisms> can involve an unpaired <electron>, a <cation>, an <anion>, or a metal-associated site; the abstract notation does not identify which. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/31.1 Chain-Growth Polymers>[John McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §31.1: successive reactive intermediates and radical, cationic and anionic initiation]{ref}

For bookkeeping below, each linear active chain has exactly one <polymerization-active site>. This is a stipulated model, not a property of every <chain polymerization>: a chain can have several reactive sites, and a reversibly inactive chain can remain capable of future growth. Count currently active sites separately from all chains capable of later reactivation.

= Chain-growth initiation
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

<Chain-growth initiation> creates a chain capable of <chain-growth propagation>. Write
$$
I^*+M\longrightarrow P_1^*.
$$
This schematic includes the first incorporated <monomeric unit>; any preceding production of $I^*$ is a separate step. If $C$ counts <polymer> <molecules>, $A$ counts polymer-bound <polymerization-active sites>, and $S$ counts incorporated <monomeric units>, this event changes $(C,A,S)$ by $(1,1,1)$. The free $I^*$ was excluded from $A$, so this does not assert creation of reactivity from nothing. An initiator feed amount need not equal the number of successful chains: the efficiency of forming productive chains and the number of initiating species per initiator matter. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/31.1 Chain-Growth Polymers>[McMurry, §31.1, initiating species and the first reactive intermediate]{ref}

= Chain-growth propagation
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

In the one-site linear model, <chain-growth propagation> changes $(C,A,S)$ by $(0,0,1)$: one <monomer molecule> is consumed, one <monomeric unit> is incorporated, and the <polymerization-active site> is regenerated. Thus longer chains do not imply more active sites. Repeated <chain-growth propagation> can increase $S$ substantially with $A$ unchanged. This is a material count; it supplies no universal time dependence or empirical <chemical kinetics> formula.

= Chain-growth termination
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

<Chain-growth termination> removes the ability of a reactive chain carrier to continue the succession of growth steps.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[termination, T06274, definition; source recommendation p. 1173]{ref} Two illustrative outcomes have different molecule counts:
$$
\begin{aligned}
P_j^*+P_k^*&\longrightarrow P_{j+k}, &&\Delta(C,A,S)=(-1,-2,0),\\
P_j^*+P_k^*&\longrightarrow P_j+P_k, &&\Delta(C,A,S)=(0,-2,0).
\end{aligned}
$$
The first joins the two chains into one inactive <polymer> <molecule>. The second leaves two inactive <polymer> <molecules>, as can occur when one chain end transfers <hydrogen> to the other instead of their ends joining. End-group chemistry is suppressed in these unit-count symbols; they are not complete <molecular formulas>. Losing two <polymerization-active sites> does not mean losing two chains or any incorporated <monomeric units>. A temporarily inactive, reactivatable site must not be counted as irreversible <chain-growth termination>.

= Chain transfer in chain polymerization
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

In <chain transfer in chain polymerization>, a growing chain can lose its <polymerization-active site> while another <molecule> acquires reactivity. In a common atom-transfer picture, the old chain becomes inactive and the new carrier can initiate further <chain-growth propagation>.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[chain transfer, C00963, definition and subsequent-polymerization example; source recommendation p. 1094]{ref} The number of reactive carriers can survive even though the identity of the growing chain changes. If the new carrier successfully starts a new chain, $C$ increases; a fixed active-carrier count therefore does not imply a fixed <polymer> <molecule> count. Whether transfer is irreversible, reversible, or involves an already existing chain matters for the final bookkeeping.

= Finite chain-growth bookkeeping
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}
{title2=$X_n=S/C$}

Stipulate a feed of exactly $M_0=40$ <monomer molecules>, four successfully initiated linear chains, one <polymerization-active site> per chain, no <chain transfer in chain polymerization>, no loss of incorporated units, and no further <chain-growth initiation>. Count only initiated chains in $C$; exclude unreacted <monomers>. Assume each consumed <monomer molecule> contributes exactly one <monomeric unit>. With incorporated feed fraction $q=S/M_0$, the number-average <degree of polymerization> is the counting identity
$$
X_n=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{C}j_i}{C}=\frac{S}{C}=\frac{qM_0}{C}.
$$
This is conditional conservation bookkeeping, not a universal empirical law. Immediately after <chain-growth initiation>, the four lengths are $(1,1,1,1)$, so $(C,A,S)=(4,4,4)$ and $q=0.1$. Stipulate twelve subsequent <chain-growth propagation> events, three on each chain: the lengths become $(4,4,4,4)$, giving $(C,A,S)=(4,4,16)$, $q=0.4$ and $X_n=4$. Sixteen units have been incorporated, while twenty-four free <monomer molecules> remain. The chain-length assignment is deliberately chosen, not a prediction of equal growth in an experiment.

= Living versus terminating chain populations
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

The defining idealization of <living polymerization> excludes <chain-growth termination> and irreversible <chain transfer in chain polymerization>. Fast, effective <chain-growth initiation> is an additional condition behind a nearly fixed growing-chain population, not a guarantee contained in the name alone.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[living polymerization, L03597, definition and initiation/propagation comparison; living polymer, LT07156, definition specifies irreversible transfer]{ref} If all four chains in <finite chain-growth bookkeeping> remain active, completing the feed keeps $C=A=4$ and gives $X_n=10$. Stipulate six further additions to each chain, obtaining $(10,10,10,10)$. Other allocations with the same total also satisfy $X_n=10$: living does not mean identical lengths.

Alternatively, stipulate that the first two length-four chains undergo <chain-growth termination> without joining, while the other two each receive twelve more <monomer molecules>. The final lengths are $(4,4,16,16)$, with $(C,A,S)=(4,2,40)$. The mean <degree of polymerization> is still $10$, but two chains have stopped and two carry the remaining growth. The population <variance> of lengths is $36$, compared with zero in the deliberately equal living allocation. These are synthetic populations, not measured molecular distributions and not a claim that all terminating processes are broader than all living processes. Indeed, assigning all twenty-four remaining additions to one still-active chain gives the living allocation $(28,4,4,4)$, with the same mean but <variance> $108$. Comparable start times and opportunities for <chain-growth propagation> are extra assumptions behind an expectation of more uniform lengths.

\Image[chain-growth-synthetic-chain-populations.png]
{id=image-chain-growth-synthetic-chain-populations}
{title=Same incorporated total and mean <degree of polymerization>, different active-chain populations}
{description=Two explicitly stipulated continuations from four length-four chains and twenty-four remaining <monomer molecules>. Left: all four chains stay active and receive six additions each. Right: two become inactive without joining, and the two survivors receive twelve additions each. Blue identifies active chains and grey inactive chains. Bars are exact chosen integer counts, not sampled data or fits to a molecular distribution. Both final populations contain forty incorporated <monomeric units> in four chains.}

If the two terminated length-four chains instead join, their inactive product has length eight; with the same remaining additions the final lengths are $(8,16,16)$. Now $C=3$, $A=2$, $S=40$ and $X_n=40/3$. Thus <chain-growth termination> can change both which chains grow and which chains enter the denominator. Continuing <chain-growth initiation>, inefficient initiation, <chain transfer in chain polymerization>, and reactivation require their own counts. Predicting an entire length distribution additionally needs a specified <reaction mechanism> and growth statistics. A measured mean alone cannot establish <living polymerization>.

= NMR monitoring of chain polymerization
{c}
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

<NMR monitoring of chain polymerization> uses nuclear magnetic resonance signals to follow <monomer> and other distinguishable <chemical species>. In a proton experiment, suitable signal <integrals> can track changes in their relative amounts. Disappearance of a <monomer> signal is a consumption readout; it is not a direct count of <polymerization-active sites>. Overlapping signals, baseline errors and competing <chemical reactions> can obstruct the inference. A pre-reaction reference and independent product characterization make the consumption interpretation stronger.

= Size-exclusion characterization of chain polymers
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

<Size-exclusion characterization of chain polymers> separates dissolved chains using porous columns and records an elution signal. Calibrating that separation against appropriate standards supplies a size-related estimate of molecular masses. Detector response and calibration must accompany any claimed molecular distribution: an elution trace is not automatically a histogram of molecule counts or <degree of polymerization>. Similar signals can conceal chemically different chain populations, while a shift toward larger apparent size alone does not show that every chain retained a <polymerization-active site>.

= Monitoring chain polymerization in a sealed tube
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}

https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlepdf/2015/py/c5py00278h[A simple route to deuterated polystyrene block copolymers by reverse iodine transfer polymerisation by Trevor Gavin Wright and Harald Pasch] (2015, Experimental section, pp. 3237–3238) provides a concrete study of consumption and chain products. The authors used a valve-sealed J Young tube, freeze–pump–thaw preparation and <argon>. <NMR monitoring of chain polymerization> compared a $25\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ reference with spectra acquired at $70\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ every fifteen minutes for twenty-four hours. Their formulation contained an azo initiating reagent, molecular iodine and a vinyl <monomer>. Companion flask syntheses used stirring and a heated silicone-oil bath.

The authors followed initiating and transfer-agent signals; Figure 3 reports a transfer-agent signal rising during induction and then falling as <polymerization> proceeds. Their <size-exclusion characterization of chain polymers> used porous columns, refractive-index detection and polystyrene calibration. These distinct consumption and size readouts concern transfer-mediated chemistry; they do not establish strict <living polymerization>. <A simple route to deuterated polystyrene block copolymers by reverse iodine transfer polymerisation by Trevor Gavin Wright and others>[Wright and Pasch, pp. 3237–3239: Experimental section, Analyses and Figure 3]{ref}

Useful \i[proposed controls], rather than reported measurements, are a matched mixture omitting the initiating reagent, an unheated mixture, and a repeat of the complete formulation. Keep <monomer> amount, <solvent>, tube preparation and acquisition conditions matched where possible. The unheated comparison needs a readout that does not itself initiate <polymerization>; placing it in the hot probe would defeat that control. A mixture omitting iodine asks a different question: it changes <chain transfer in chain polymerization> and induction chemistry, so it is not necessarily a no-<polymerization> control. Record the pre-reaction baseline and compare crude material with isolated product; isolation can selectively remove shorter chains. These comparisons help separate initiation-dependent <monomer> consumption from signal drift and unrelated <chemical reactions>.

The mechanistic inference is deliberately limited. Consumption plus independently characterized larger chain products supports <polymerization>; evidence of an induction stage helps test a proposed <reaction mechanism>. Neither readout directly counts stars in $P_j^*$, proves the absence of <chain-growth termination>, nor recovers every chain length. A chain-extension test would probe retained growth capability, but even a successful extension would not by itself establish that \i[all] original chains extended. No measured molecular distribution is reconstructed here. IUPAC cautions that reversible deactivation can produce living-like behaviour even when some termination occurs.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[reversible-deactivation radical polymerization, 08916, notes 2 and 4: living terminology and remaining active/dormant population]{ref}

= Living polymerization
{parent=Chain-growth polymerization}
{wiki}

<Living polymerization> is <chain-growth polymerization> without chain termination or irreversible chain transfer. A linear mean <degree of polymerization> versus feed conversion additionally needs the fixed-chain assumptions in <polymerization>; reversibility of <monomer> addition is not required. See https://iupac.org/wp-content/uploads/2017/03/IUPAC_PolymEdu_EduWorkshop_PPT_Shipp2.pdf[Shipp, IUPAC workshop, slide 50].

= Polyurethane
{parent=Polymer}

A <polyurethane> contains repeated connections of the form $\mathrm{{-}O{-}C(=O){-}NH{-}}$. Suitable <hydroxy groups> and <isocyanate> groups can produce those connections by <polyaddition>; exactly two suitable groups per feed <molecule> give the bifunctional linear model only when competing reactions and ring closure are excluded.

= Degree of polymerization
{parent=Polymer}
{wiki}
{title2=$X,\ X_n$}

The <degree of polymerization> counts <monomeric units> in a chain. $X_n$ is its number average over a specified collection of chains. State whether unreacted <monomers> are included and distinguish this count from <constitutional repeating units> or statistical segments. See https://doi.org/10.1515/pac-2023-0304[IUPAC: degree of polymerization].

= Repeat unit
{parent=Polymer}
{wiki}

= Constitutional repeating unit
{synonym}

A <constitutional repeating unit> is the smallest chemical unit whose repetition describes a regular chain. Its boundaries mark repetition, not necessarily the origin of the <chemical atoms> from a single <monomer>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C01286/plain[IUPAC: constitutional repeating unit].

= Nylon 6,10
{c}
{parent=Polymer}

<Nylon 6,10> has an <amide>-linked chain whose <constitutional repeating unit> contains a six-<carbon> diamine contribution and a ten-<carbon> diacid-derived contribution. See <monomer> for its <stoichiometry> and <polymerization> for the authored demonstration.

= Polyethylene
{parent=Polymer}
{wiki}

In ideal linear <polyethylene>, incorporated <ethene> contributes $\mathrm{{-}CH_2{-}CH_2{-}}$ <monomeric units>; the smallest <constitutional repeating unit> is $\mathrm{{-}CH_2{-}}$. See <monomer> for the counting distinction.

= Monomer
{parent=Polymer}

A <monomer> is a <chemical substance> whose <molecules> can undergo <polymerization> and contribute units to the essential structure of a <polymer>. A <monomer molecule> is the individual reactant; its incorporated <monomeric unit> is the portion identifiable as derived from that reactant. Incorporation can change <chemical bonds> and release small byproducts, so the unit in the chain need not have the complete <molecular formula> of the feed <molecule>. See https://old.goldbook.iupac.org/html/M/M04019.html[IUPAC: monomer molecule] and https://doi.org/10.1515/pac-2023-0304[IUPAC, A brief guide to polymer terminology: monomeric units].

Three different counts answer three different questions. <Monomer molecules> describe the feed; <monomeric units> trace that feed's contributions to the product; a <constitutional repeating unit> describes the smallest chemical pattern repeating along a regular chain. In <nylon 6,10>, two different <monomers> supply one interior <constitutional repeating unit>,
$$
\mathrm{{-}NH{-}(CH_2)_6{-}NH{-}C(=O){-}(CH_2)_8{-}C(=O){-}}.
$$
Its <molecular formula> is $\mathrm{C_{16}H_{30}N_2O_2}$. The diamine feed is $\mathrm{C_6H_{16}N_2}$, and the diacid-chloride feed is $\mathrm{C_{10}H_{16}Cl_2O_2}$; subtracting two $\mathrm{HCl}$ gives the interior-repeat budget. For a finite linear chain made from $n$ of each reactant, there are $2n-1$ joining connections, hence $2n-1$ formal $\mathrm{HCl}$ eliminations, rather than $2n$. One <amine> end and one acid-chloride end remain in that ideal unmodified chain. This is a <stoichiometry> check on the depicted reaction, not a claim that the washed experimental thread retains those ends. The reacting end-group chemistry is documented in https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/10-569-synthesis-of-polymers-fall-2006/d9f9ac7021b0d0f418d3d3c90dbfb94b_lec05_09152006.pdf[Hammond, lecture 5, p. 4]; the specified <nylon 6,10> reactant pair is reported in https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/acs.jchemed.6b00767[Polymer Day, nylon demonstration].

<Ethene> shows the reverse counting mismatch. One $\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH_2}$ <monomer molecule> contributes a two-<carbon> $\mathrm{{-}CH_2{-}CH_2{-}}$ <monomeric unit> to ideal linear <polyethylene>, without a small byproduct in that growth step. But the smallest <constitutional repeating unit> of its uniform backbone is just $\mathrm{{-}CH_2{-}}$: a chain containing $m$ incorporated <ethene> <monomeric units> contains $2m$ such interior one-<carbon> repeats. End groups are additional bookkeeping. The frequently drawn $[\mathrm{{-}CH_2{-}CH_2{-}}]_m$ is useful for tracing the feed, although it is not the smallest constitutional repeat. The minimum-pattern definition is given in https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C01286/plain[IUPAC: constitutional repeating unit]; the <ethene> growth chemistry is described by <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/31.1 Chain-Growth Polymers>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, 31.1]{ref}.

A segment of a <freely jointed chain> or <Gaussian chain> instead belongs to a statistical model. It can represent many <chemical bonds> and several <constitutional repeating units>; its length and the segment count are chosen to describe chain conformation at the intended scale. The three-repeats-per-segment drawing in <polymerization-monomers-interface-units> is a deliberately arbitrary coarse-graining example, not a measured <Kuhn length>. Always state the counting convention before substituting a segment count for a <degree of polymerization>. This separation lets chemistry track which <chemical atoms> entered the material while statistical models track how the material bends and extends.

= Monomeric unit
{parent=Monomer}

An incorporated <monomeric unit> is the largest constitutional unit identifiable as derived from one <monomer molecule>. It need not be a whole unreacted <molecule> or the smallest <constitutional repeating unit>. See https://doi.org/10.1515/pac-2023-0304[IUPAC: monomeric unit].

= Monomer molecule
{parent=Monomer}

An individual <molecule> capable of contributing structural units through <polymerization>. See https://old.goldbook.iupac.org/html/M/M04019.html[IUPAC: monomer molecule].

= Ethylene
{parent=Monomer}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH_2}$}

= Ethene
{synonym}

<Ethene> is the two-<carbon> <monomer> in the <polyethylene> example in <monomer>.

= Nucleic acid
{parent=Polymer}
{wiki}

A <nucleic acid> is a <polymer> built from linked <nucleotides>. The two principal kinds are <DNA> and <RNA>. Each <nucleotide> contributes a <sugar>, a <nucleobase>, and <phosphate> to the chain; the <nucleobase> is only the letter-bearing component, not the entire <nucleotide>. <DNA> uses <deoxyribose>, whereas <RNA> uses <ribose>. A chain of six <nucleotides> is therefore not six free <nucleobases> placed beside one another. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Nucleic-Acids[NHGRI: nucleic acids].

The chemical scaffold and the variable lettering do different jobs. <Covalent bonds> maintain the <sugar>–<phosphate> backbone, while the order of attached <nucleobases> specifies the <nucleic acid sequence>. A <phosphodiester bond> links the $3'$ position of one <sugar> to the $5'$ position of the next through <phosphate>. These position numbers refer to <carbon> <chemical atoms> in the <sugar>; they define a strand's direction even when it bends or forms a circle. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK21134/[Brown, Genomes, 2nd edition: the human genome].

This division is useful: the backbone supplies a connected physical record, and <base pairing> allows its letters to recognize partners. <DNA replication> copies that record, and <transcription> uses a <DNA> template to make <RNA>. <RNA> can also fold into functional shapes through <base pairing> within one chain; a <nucleic acid sequence> specifies chemical order, not a requirement for a straight shape. The beauty is that the same kind of <polymer> can carry a message and form a structure that acts on messages.

= Nucleic acid sequence
{parent=Nucleic acid}
{wiki}

= Nucleotide sequence
{synonym}

A <nucleic acid sequence> records the ordered <nucleobases> of a <nucleic acid> strand, conventionally read from $5'$ to $3'$. The standard <DNA> alphabet is <adenine> A, <cytosine> C, <guanine> G, and <thymine> T; the standard <RNA> alphabet uses <uracil> U in place of <thymine> T. Each letter denotes the <nucleobase> attached to one <nucleotide>, so the six-letter <DNA> <nucleic acid sequence> $5'\text{-}\mathrm{ACGTAC}\text{-}3'$ describes six linked <nucleotides>. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Base-Pair[NHGRI: representing DNA by a sequence of letters].

Order carries information that composition loses. Both ACGTAC and AACCGT contain two <adenine>, two <cytosine>, one <guanine>, and one <thymine> <nucleobase>, but their <nucleic acid sequences> differ at positions 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6. At position 2, for example, the first has <cytosine> and the second <adenine>. Reporting only the four counts cannot reconstruct either ordered record.

To obtain the partner of $5'\text{-}\mathrm{ACGTAC}\text{-}3'$, apply the <DNA> <base pairing> rules position by position: A selects T, C selects G, G selects C, T selects A, A selects T, and C selects G. Alignment gives $3'\text{-}\mathrm{TGCATG}\text{-}5'$. Its opposite end labels matter because the two are <antiparallel nucleic acid strands>. Reading the partner from its own $5'$ end reverses the aligned lettering and yields the <reverse complement>, $5'\text{-}\mathrm{GTACGT}\text{-}3'$.

\Image[dna-reverse-complement.png]
{title=Complement, then reverse: <DNA> ACGTAC has aligned partner TGCATG and <reverse complement> GTACGT}

Reversal alone gives CATGCA; complementation alone gives aligned TGCATG. Neither operation alone gives the partner written in the usual $5'$-to-$3'$ convention. Applying the <reverse complement> operation twice returns ACGTAC: GTACGT complements to CATGCA, which reverses to ACGTAC. This convention makes a single reported strand sufficient to specify an ideally complementary <DNA> duplex, provided its direction is known.

= Reverse complement
{parent=Nucleic acid sequence}

A <nucleotide sequence> obtained by reversing the order and complementing each <nucleobase> according to the appropriate <base pairing> rules.

= Base pair
{parent=Nucleic acid}
{wiki}
{title2=bp}

A <base pair> is an association of two <nucleobases> through <hydrogen bonds> in a compatible arrangement. It commonly joins opposite strands of a <nucleic acid double helix>, but can also join two regions of one folded <RNA> strand. Canonical Watson–Crick <DNA> <base pairs> match <adenine> with <thymine> and <guanine> with <cytosine>; canonical <RNA> <base pairs> use <adenine> with <uracil> instead. The <nucleobases> belong to complete <nucleotides>, including their attached <sugars> and backbone <phosphate>. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Base-Pair[NHGRI: base pair].

The abbreviation bp also measures the length of a paired region. Our ACGTAC/TGCATG duplex has six <base pairs>, containing twelve <nucleotides> altogether. Its upper strand contains two <adenine>, two <cytosine>, one <guanine>, and one <thymine> <nucleobase>; the lower contains one, one, two, and two respectively. Combined, the counts are three of each <nucleobase>. Equality of total <adenine> and <thymine>, or of total <guanine> and <cytosine>, need not hold within either individual strand.

= Base pairing
{parent=Base pair}

<Base pairing> is the recognition and association of compatible <nucleobases>. In canonical Watson–Crick geometry, an <adenine>–<thymine> <base pair> has two <hydrogen bonds>, while a <guanine>–<cytosine> <base pair> has three. These counts refer to those specific geometries and usual chemical forms; they are not a definition of every possible <base pair>. <RNA> can also contain other pairings, and canonical <adenine>–<uracil> pairing replaces <adenine>–<thymine> pairing. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/14-2-dna-structure-and-sequencing[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: DNA structure].

\Image[dna-antiparallel-base-pairs.png]
{title=Six canonical Watson–Crick <DNA> <base pairs>: opposite backbone directions and two or three <hydrogen bonds> per pair}

For this illustrative duplex, the six <base pairs> are A–T, C–G, G–C, T–A, A–T, and C–G. Thus three contribute two <hydrogen bonds> each and three contribute three each:
$$
3\times2+3\times3=15.
$$
Each dotted connector in the diagram counts one <hydrogen bond>; coloured boxes denote whole <nucleobases>, not individual <chemical atoms>. The solid rails denote the <covalent bond> scaffold. Separating the two strands disrupts <base pairing> and <base stacking> without requiring the backbone to be cut.

The count of fifteen <hydrogen bonds> is not fifteen identical contributions to a duplex's <Gibbs free energy>. <Base stacking>, interactions with <water> and <ions>, <temperature>, and the order of neighbouring <base pairs> also matter. Knowing the fraction of <guanine>–<cytosine> <base pairs> alone does not fix a <nucleic acid double helix>'s shape or stability. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK21134/[Brown, Genomes: pairing and stacking interactions]. What makes <base pairing> especially useful is its selectivity: an exposed <DNA> strand constrains the partner's <nucleic acid sequence>, giving <DNA replication> a chemical recognition rule.

= Nucleic acid double helix
{parent=Nucleic acid}
{wiki}

A <nucleic acid double helix> consists of two <nucleic acid> strands winding around a common axis. In the familiar <DNA> arrangement, <sugar>–<phosphate> backbones lie outside and paired <nucleobases> lie inside, stacked along the axis. The strands are <antiparallel nucleic acid strands>. The flattened <base pairing> ladder above displays partners and direction; winding that ladder into a <helix> adds spatial organization. <RNA> can also form double-helical paired regions, so the geometry is not exclusive to <DNA>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26821/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: DNA structure].

The useful surprise is that a structure explains a copying principle. Complementary <base pairs> allow each <DNA> strand to specify the other's <nucleic acid sequence>; separate strands can each act as templates in <DNA replication>. This is a structural constraint, while the <enzymes> and stages of <DNA replication> supply the actual process. A <nucleic acid sequence> specifies letter order without, by itself, giving every detail of the three-dimensional <helix>.

On 25 April 1953, James Watson and Francis Crick published a model with two oppositely directed chains, outside <phosphate> groups, and specific <base pairs>. Their short paper explicitly connected this pairing to a possible copying mechanism, while describing the model as requiring further experimental tests. See the original https://doi.org/10.1038/171737a0[Molecular Structure of Nucleic Acids: A Structure for Deoxyribose Nucleic Acid, Nature 171, 737–738]{ref}, with an accessible https://inters.org/files/watsoncrick1953.pdf[reprint of the original paper]{ref}.

Rosalind Franklin and Raymond Gosling's paper in the same issue had a different role: it presented <X-ray> <diffraction> evidence from <DNA> fibres and its structural interpretation, including the helical character of the wetter B form and evidence for <phosphate> groups on the outside. They distinguished forms produced at different water contents and assessed compatibility with the Watson–Crick model. Read their original https://doi.org/10.1038/171740a0[Molecular Configuration in Sodium Thymonucleate, Nature 171, 740–741 (1953)]{ref}. The model and the experimental analysis are distinct contributions; a <diffraction> pattern is evidence constraining a <molecular geometry>, rather than a direct picture of letter-by-letter <base pairing>.

= Antiparallel nucleic acid strands
{parent=Nucleic acid double helix}

Two <nucleic acid> strands with opposite directional alignment: a $5'$-to-$3'$ direction on one is aligned with the $3'$-to-$5'$ direction on the other.

= Base stacking
{parent=Nucleic acid double helix}

<Base stacking> is the close arrangement and interaction of neighbouring <nucleobases> with their approximately planar faces above one another. Along a <nucleic acid double helix>, neighbouring <base pairs> stack successively; <base pairing> instead associates partners across the two strands. Both affect duplex stability, and the interactions depend on the identities and arrangement of neighbouring <nucleobases>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK21134/[Brown, Genomes: base pairing and base stacking].

= Stem-loop
{parent=Nucleic acid}
{wiki}

= Deoxyribonucleic acid
{parent=Nucleic acid}

= DNA
{c}
{synonym}

<Deoxyribonucleic acid>, or <DNA>, is a <nucleotide> <polymer> that stores biological information in its <nucleotide sequence>. The crucial variable is order: two <DNA> <molecules> can contain the same numbers of each <nucleobase> yet carry different <nucleotide sequences>. This makes a physical <molecule> an inheritable record, rather than merely a mixture of ingredients. <Genes> are functional regions of that record; the whole <DNA> <nucleotide sequence> is not one long <gene>. See https://www.genome.gov/about-genomics/fact-sheets/Deoxyribonucleic-Acid-Fact-Sheet[NHGRI: DNA fact sheet].

Each <DNA> strand has a backbone of alternating <deoxyribose> <sugars> and <phosphate> groups joined through <covalent bonds>. Attached <nucleobases> supply the familiar four-letter alphabet: <adenine> A, <thymine> T, <guanine> G, and <cytosine> C. In ordinary double-stranded <DNA>, <base pairing> matches A with T and G with C. The backbone connects successive <nucleotides> along a strand; <base pairing> connects partners across strands. Keeping these two connections distinct explains how strands can separate while each retains its own <nucleotide sequence>.

The two backbones have directions, conventionally labelled $5'$ and $3'$ after numbered <carbon> <chemical atoms> in <deoxyribose>. They form <antiparallel nucleic acid strands>: following one from $5'$ to $3'$ takes you opposite to the other's $5'$-to-$3'$ direction. For the chosen <DNA> <nucleotide sequence> $5'\text{-}\mathrm{ATGCC}\text{-}3'$, write its partner directly underneath as $3'\text{-}\mathrm{TACGG}\text{-}5'$. Now read that partner from its own $5'$ end: its <reverse complement> is $5'\text{-}\mathrm{GGCAT}\text{-}3'$. Complementing letters and reversing their order are separate operations. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26821/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: the structure and function of DNA].

\Image[dna-antiparallel-complement.png]
{title=<DNA> <base pairing> and <antiparallel nucleic acid strands>: an unrolled ladder for ATGCC and its <reverse complement>}

The diagram's rails represent <sugar>–<phosphate> backbones and its coloured blocks represent <nucleobases>, not individual <chemical atoms>. Reading left to right gives five matched positions: A–T, T–A, G–C, C–G, C–G. There are two A–T-type <base pairs> and three G–C-type <base pairs>; the complete two-strand example contains ten <nucleotides>. Reversing the bottom row changes the reading convention, not the physical partners. Real double-stranded <DNA> winds into a <nucleic acid double helix>; the flattened ladder emphasizes <base pairing> and direction without pretending to show its three-dimensional geometry.

The motivating consequence is copying by a template. After strands separate, each existing <DNA> strand specifies which incoming <nucleotides> should accompany it through <base pairing>. <DNA replication> uses <enzymes> to build complementary strands, allowing the record to persist through successive <biological cells>. <Base pairing> supplies a selection rule, while <enzymes> perform the chemistry: a matching diagram alone is not a complete copying mechanism. The connection between structure and copying was already central to the model published by Watson and Crick on 25 April 1953. See https://www.nature.com/articles/171737a0[Watson and Crick, Molecular Structure of Nucleic Acids, Nature 171, 737–738 (1953)].

Stored information needs interpretation. Some <DNA> regions specify <proteins> through <RNA>; others contribute to functional <RNA> or regulate <gene expression>, and some have no known function. <Non-coding DNA> means sequence that does not specify a <protein>'s sequence of <amino acids>, not automatically useless sequence. Nor does a <DNA> <nucleotide sequence> alone determine an <organism>'s fate: <phenotypes> also depend on biological context and environment. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Non-Coding-DNA[NHGRI: non-coding DNA]. Its double-stranded form is often modeled mechanically as a <worm-like chain>, with <elasticity> and effective <excluded volume> depending on solvent conditions.

= Ribonucleic acid
{parent=Nucleic acid}

= RNA
{c}
{synonym}

<Ribonucleic acid>, or <RNA>, is a <nucleotide> <polymer> whose <nucleotide sequence> can both carry information and organize a working molecular shape. Its backbone contains alternating <ribose> <sugars> and <phosphate> groups connected through <covalent bonds>. Its usual <nucleobases> are <adenine> A, <uracil> U, <guanine> G, and <cytosine> C. Thus <RNA> uses U where <DNA> uses T; its <ribose> also differs chemically from <DNA>'s <deoxyribose>. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Ribonucleic-Acid-RNA[NHGRI: RNA].

In <transcription (biology)>, an <enzyme> uses one <DNA> strand as a template for <RNA>. For example, the <DNA> template $3'\text{-}\mathrm{TACGG}\text{-}5'$ specifies $5'\text{-}\mathrm{AUGCC}\text{-}3'$ in <RNA>. The <base pairing> rules now include A–U rather than A–T, while G–C remains. This <RNA> matches the other <DNA> strand's $5'$-to-$3'$ lettering after T is replaced by U. Identifying the template strand matters: replacing T by U in an arbitrarily chosen <DNA> strand is not a general method for deriving the <RNA> product. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26887/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: from DNA to RNA].

<RNA> is often a single strand, but single-stranded does not mean straight or entirely unpaired. Complementary regions within one <RNA> <molecule> can undergo <base pairing>, bringing distant positions together. Consider the illustrative twelve-<nucleotide> <nucleotide sequence> $5'\text{-}\mathrm{GCAUUUUUAUGC}\text{-}3'$. Pair positions 1 with 12, 2 with 11, 3 with 10, and 4 with 9. The resulting <stem-loop> has four <base pairs>: G–C, C–G, A–U, U–A. Positions 5–8 remain unpaired in the loop. Both arms belong to the same continuous backbone, and the paired arms have the directions of <antiparallel nucleic acid strands>.

\Image[rna-sequence-and-folding.png]
{title=One <RNA> <nucleotide sequence>, two drawings: numbered letters and an illustrative <stem-loop> with four <base pairs>}

The diagram proposes a possible pairing pattern, not a prediction that this short <RNA> must adopt that shape under every condition. Actual <RNA> folding depends on additional interactions, <temperature>, <ions>, and associated <proteins>; the full three-dimensional arrangement contains more information than the drawn <base pairs>. The example's purpose is to show how a linear <nucleotide sequence> can specify contacts within itself.

Different <RNA> roles make this flexibility useful. <Messenger RNA> carries a message that a <ribosome> reads during <translation (biology)> to build a <protein>. <Transfer RNA> helps match that message to <amino acid> building blocks; see https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Transfer-RNA-tRNA[NHGRI: transfer RNA]. <Ribosomal RNA> forms structural and catalytic parts of the <ribosome>. Other <RNA> participates in <gene regulatory networks>, changing how strongly <genes> are expressed. Many <RNA> <molecules> therefore function without being translated into <proteins>; for some <genes>, functional <RNA> is the product itself.

Most strikingly, some folded <RNA> acts as an <enzyme>: these catalytic <RNA> <molecules> are <ribozymes>. The <ribosome>'s bond-forming <active site> is built from <RNA>, although the complete <ribosome> also contains <proteins>. Structural evidence appears in https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10937990/[Nissen et al., The structural basis of ribosome activity in peptide bond synthesis, Science 289, 920–930 (2000)]. <RNA> therefore connects a readable record with physical recognition, regulation, and catalysis: the same kind of <polymer> can be a message, an adaptor, or part of molecular machinery.

= Messenger RNA
{parent=Ribonucleic acid}
{wiki}

= mRNA
{c}
{synonym}

= Transfer RNA
{parent=Ribonucleic acid}
{wiki}

= tRNA
{c}
{synonym}

= Ribosomal RNA
{parent=Ribonucleic acid}
{wiki}

= rRNA
{c}
{synonym}

= Radius of gyration
{parent=Polymer}
{wiki}
{title2=$R_g^2=\frac1{2N^2}\sum_{m,n}\langle|\mathbf R_m-\mathbf R_n|^2\rangle$}

For $N$ equally weighted positions, the squared <radius of gyration> is the average squared distance from the <center of mass>: $R_g^2=N^{-1}\sum_n\langle|\mathbf R_n-\mathbf R_{\rm cm}|^2\rangle$, where $\mathbf R_{\rm cm}=N^{-1}\sum_n\mathbf R_n$. Expanding pair differences gives $\sum_{m,n}|\mathbf R_m-\mathbf R_n|^2=2N\sum_n|\mathbf R_n-\mathbf R_{\rm cm}|^2$, and therefore
$$
R_g^2=\frac1{2N^2}\sum_{m,n}\langle|\mathbf R_m-\mathbf R_n|^2\rangle.
$$
For a <Gaussian chain> or <freely jointed chain> with these $N$ positions and mean squared link length $b^2$, each pair <variance> is $b^2|m-n|$. Summing separations yields $R_g^2=b^2(N^2-1)/(6N)\sim Nb^2/6$. Counting all endpoints of $N$ links instead gives $N+1$ positions and the corresponding finite-size formula.

= Ion
{parent=Chemistry}
{title2=$q=ze$}

An <ion> is a <chemical atom> or a group of <chemical atoms> with a nonzero net <electric charge>. A positive <ion> is a <cation>; a negative <ion> is an <anion>. The whole entity carries the <electric charge>: a <hydronium> <ion> is a group of four <chemical atoms> with net charge $+e$. This definition agrees with https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/I03158[IUPAC Gold Book: ion]{ref}.

For an ordinary <ion> containing only <atomic nuclei> and <electrons>, let $N_p$ count all its <protons> and $N_e$ all its <electrons>. Each <proton> contributes the <elementary charge> $+e$, each <electron> contributes $-e$, and <neutrons> contribute zero. Therefore
$$
q=(N_p-N_e)e=ze,\qquad z=N_p-N_e.
$$
The signed integer $z$ is the net <electric charge> number. Removing an <electron> increases $z$ by one; adding an <electron> decreases $z$ by one. Changing the <electron> count leaves the <atomic number>, and therefore the identity of the <chemical element>, unchanged.

For example, a neutral <sodium> <chemical atom> has $11$ <protons> and $11$ <electrons>. Its <cation> $\mathrm{Na}^+$ still has $11$ <protons>, but only $10$ <electrons>, so $q=(11-10)e=+e$. A neutral <chlorine> <chemical atom> has $17$ <protons> and $17$ <electrons>; its <anion> $\mathrm{Cl}^-$ has $17$ <protons> and $18$ <electrons>, so $q=(17-18)e=-e$. These are particle inventories, not pictures of <atomic orbitals>. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/2.6 Ionic and Molecular Compounds> (§2.6: ions and ionic compounds).

\Image[ion-charge-bookkeeping.png]
{title=<Ion> <electric charge> bookkeeping: neutral <sodium> and <chlorine> <chemical atoms> compared with their <cation> and <anion>}
{description=Every colored dot counts one <proton> or one <electron>. Rows are counting arrays, not spatial models of <atomic nuclei> or <electron shells>. The net <electric charges> are $0,+e,0,-e$.}

One <sodium chloride> <formula unit> accounts for one $\mathrm{Na}^+$ <cation> and one $\mathrm{Cl}^-$ <anion>, whose net <electric charges> sum to zero. Ordinary solid <sodium chloride> is an extended arrangement of <ions>, rather than discrete $\mathrm{NaCl}$ <molecules>. This is a useful distinction: a neutral material can contain charged constituents, and its <chemical formula> tells us their ratio without claiming that each pair is a separate <molecule>.

<Ion> bookkeeping connects <chemistry> to <electric current>: moving <ions> can transport <electric charge>, while stationary <ions> cannot supply a transport <current density>. In <electrochemistry>, both signs of <ions> can contribute to the same <conventional current>. An <ion> opposing a fixed <electric charge> is called a <counterion>.

= Cation
{parent=Ion}
{wiki}

A <cation> is an <ion> with positive net <electric charge>, for example $\mathrm{Na}^+$ or <hydronium>.

= Anion
{parent=Ion}
{wiki}

An <anion> is an <ion> with negative net <electric charge>, for example $\mathrm{Cl}^-$ or <hydroxide>.

= Chloride
{parent=Anion}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Cl^-}$}

<Chloride> is the <anion> $\mathrm{Cl^-}$, with net <electric charge> $-e$. In the <glycine titration> model, it supplies the negative background <electric charge> introduced with <hydrochloric acid>.

= Permanganate
{parent=Anion}
{title2=$\mathrm{MnO}_4^-$}

A <manganese>-containing <anion> with net <electric charge> $-e$ and <manganese> <oxidation state> $+7$; $e$ is the positive <elementary charge>.

= Nitrate
{parent=Anion}
{title2=$\mathrm{NO}_3^-$}
{wiki}

= Nitrate ion
{synonym}

The <nitrate ion>, $\mathrm{NO}_3^-$, is an <anion> with one <nitrogen> connected to three <oxygen> <chemical atoms> and net <electric charge> $-1$ in units of <elementary charge>. Its 24 <valence electrons> admit three equivalent octet <resonance structures>; see <Lewis structure> for their construction and <chemical resonance> for their interpretation. The N–O connections are <covalent bonds> within the <ion>. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/7.2 Covalent Bonding>.

= Hydrogen ion
{parent=Ion}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm H^+$}

In <acid-base chemistry>, <hydrogen ion> usually refers to positively charged hydrogen, represented by $\mathrm H^+$. In <water>, this notation describes solvated species, commonly represented by <hydronium>, rather than an isolated bare <proton>. The <hydrogen ion> <thermodynamic activity> defines <pH>.

= Hydronium
{parent=Ion}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+$}

<Hydronium> is the <ion> formed when a <water> <molecule> accepts a <proton>. It provides the usual aqueous shorthand for the <hydrogen ion> used in <pH> calculations.

= Hydroxide
{parent=Ion}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{OH}^-$}

= Counterion
{parent=Ion}

A mobile <ion> whose charge has the opposite sign to a charged macromolecule, surface or other fixed species. Its local <concentration> can increase close to that fixed charge; <counterion condensation> distinguishes a bound fraction that remains localized in an infinite-dilution limit.

= Electrochemistry
{parent=Chemistry}

<Electrochemistry> studies how <chemical reactions>, transport, and <chemical equilibrium> couple to <electric charge> and <electric potential>. In a cell, <electrons> travel through the external circuit, while mobile <ions> carry <electric current> through the <electrolyte>. At the <electrodes>, <redox> reactions connect these two forms of <electric charge> transport. This connection makes it possible both to obtain electrical <work> from a <chemical reaction> and to use electrical <work> to drive a <chemical reaction>.

An <anode> is an <electrode> where <oxidation> occurs; a <cathode> is an <electrode> where <reduction> occurs. These definitions refer to the <chemical reactions>, rather than universal positive or negative terminal signs. In a <galvanic cell> delivering electrical <work>, the <anode> is negative and the <cathode> positive. In an <electrolytic cell> driven by an external source, the <anode> is positive and the <cathode> negative. See https://doi.org/10.1515/pac-2018-0109[Pingarrón et al., Terminology of electrochemical methods of analysis (IUPAC Recommendations 2019), §§3.1 and 3.4] and https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/17-2-galvanic-cells[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §17.2: galvanic cells].

For a worked example, consider an <electrolytic cell> containing molten <sodium chloride>, with the <anode> connected to the positive terminal and the <cathode> to the negative terminal of an external source. At the <cathode>, <sodium> <cations> gain <electrons>; at the <anode>, <chlorine> <anions> lose <electrons>:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{cathode, reduction:}\quad&2\mathrm{Na}^++2e^-\longrightarrow2\mathrm{Na},\\
\text{anode, oxidation:}\quad&2\mathrm{Cl}^-\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cl}_2+2e^-,\\
\text{overall:}\quad&2\mathrm{Na}^++2\mathrm{Cl}^-\longrightarrow2\mathrm{Na}+\mathrm{Cl}_2.
\end{aligned}
$$
The first line has net <electric charge> zero on each side; the second has $-2e$ on each side. Adding the lines cancels the transferred <electrons> and preserves both <chemical atom> counts and net <electric charge>. The <sodium> product is neutral, and the <chlorine> product is a diatomic <molecule>. These equations specify molten <sodium chloride>; aqueous <chemical solutions> introduce competing <water> reactions. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/17.7 Electrolysis> (§17.7: electrolysis of molten sodium chloride).

<Electrochemistry> underlies <battery> charging and the manufacture of chemicals by <electrolysis>. Its particularly useful idea is that an <electron> inventory in a balanced <chemical equation> also accounts for transported <electric charge>. Transport alone is a different question from an <electrode> <chemical reaction>: an <ion> may carry <electric current> through the <electrolyte> without itself being consumed at an <electrode>.

Historically, Michael Faraday introduced the terms <electrode>, <electrolyte>, <anion>, <cation>, and <ion> in the terminology section of his 1834 paper, <Experimental Researches in Electricity, Seventh Series by Michael Faraday> (§§662–667). His §667 explicitly treats the direction assigned to <electric current> as a convention. His <ion> language predates the modern <electron> and <atomic nucleus> picture used in the charge-counting example above.

For an <electrolyte> near <chemical equilibrium>, a <Poisson-Boltzmann equation> can approximate the distribution of <ions> in an <electric potential>. Such an equilibrium model does not by itself describe a cell carrying sustained <electric current>.

= Electrode
{parent=Electrochemistry}
{wiki}

An <electrode> is an electronic <electrical conductor> in contact with an <electrolyte>. In a cell carrying sustained <redox> <electric current>, <chemical reactions> at this interface exchange <electrons> with <chemical species>. An <electrode> can participate in the <chemical reaction>, as the dissolving <zinc> <anode> of a <Daniell cell> does, or provide a conducting surface without appearing in the net <chemical equation>. This distinction is illustrated by active and inert <electrodes> in https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/17-2-galvanic-cells[Flowers, Theopold, Langley and Robinson, Chemistry 2e, §17.2].

The interface connects two kinds of <electric charge> transport: <electrons> in the external <electrical circuit> and <ions> in the <electrolyte>. The same <electron> does not travel through the wire, swim through the <salt bridge>, and complete a circuit! The <half-reactions> account for how electronic and ionic transport meet. This is the useful idea behind both obtaining electrical <work> in a <galvanic cell> and supplying electrical <work> in an <electrolytic cell>.

Name an <electrode> from its net <chemical reaction>: <oxidation> means <anode>, and <reduction> means <cathode>. State the operating mode before assigning terminal signs. For a <galvanic cell> supplying a passive load, the <anode> is negative and the <cathode> positive; for an ordinary two-terminal <electrolytic cell> driven by a source, the <anode> is positive and the <cathode> negative. Here positive means higher terminal <electric potential> than the other <electrode>, not a claim that the bulk material has a large positive net <electric charge>. See https://doi.org/10.1515/pac-2018-0109[Pingarrón et al., Terminology of electrochemical methods of analysis (IUPAC Recommendations 2019), §§3.1 and 3.4].

= Anode
{parent=Electrode}
{wiki}

The <anode> is the <electrode> at which the net <electrode> <chemical reaction> is <oxidation>: the reacting <chemical species> loses <electrons>. This definition is independent of terminal sign. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/A00370[IUPAC Gold Book: anode].

During <Daniell cell> discharge, a <zinc> <chemical atom> becomes a $\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}$ <cation>, leaving two <electrons> in the <electrode>; those <electrons> leave through the external <electrical circuit>. The <zinc> <anode> is the negative terminal. In an externally driven <electrolytic cell>, <electrons> instead leave the <anode> through the lead to the source's positive terminal. <Conventional current> in that lead points toward the <anode>, opposite to <electron> motion. The <anode> can therefore be positive while still being the site of <oxidation>. See <Daniell cell> for the balanced <half-reaction> and <electrode> for the operating-mode sign conventions.

= Cathode
{parent=Electrode}
{wiki}

The <cathode> is the <electrode> at which the net <electrode> <chemical reaction> is <reduction>: the reacting <chemical species> gains <electrons>. This definition is independent of terminal sign. During <Daniell cell> discharge, $\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}$ <cations> accept <electrons> arriving at the <copper> <cathode> and become neutral <copper>. The <cathode> is then the positive terminal. In an externally driven <electrolytic cell>, the source's negative terminal supplies <electrons> to the negative <cathode>. See https://doi.org/10.1515/pac-2018-0109[Pingarrón et al., §3.4: cathode] and <Daniell cell>.

An <ion> carrying <electric current> need not undergo <reduction> at the <cathode>. For example, in the ideal <Daniell cell> charge-balance picture, <potassium> <cations> arriving from a <salt bridge> remain dissolved while $\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}$ <cations> are consumed. Follow the <half-reaction> to identify the reacting species; follow <electric charge conservation> to account for the other moving <ions>.

= Galvanic cell
{parent=Electrochemistry}
{wiki}

A <galvanic cell> supplies electrical <work> from a spontaneous <redox> <chemical reaction> under the specified conditions. In discharge through a passive load, <oxidation> occurs at its negative <anode> and <reduction> at its positive <cathode>. Separating the reacting compartments makes the <electrons> take an external route, where their transport can deliver useful <work>. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/17-2-galvanic-cells[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §17.2: separated reactions and electrical work].

\Image[galvanic-electrodes-daniell-paths.png]
{title=<Daniell cell> discharge: <electron> flow outside and <ion> transport inside}
{description=Original schematic of a <zinc>/<copper> <galvanic cell> connected to a passive load. A chosen <potassium> <nitrate> <salt bridge> supplies compensating <anions> to the <anode> compartment and <cations> to the <cathode> compartment. The arrows represent charge-balance roles, not measured trajectories or relative fluxes. The bottom inventory is an ideal accounting example for one <mole> of net cell reaction.}

In the metallic external <electrical circuit>, <electrons> move from <anode> to <cathode>, while <conventional current> goes from <cathode> through the load to <anode>. Inside the cell, net ionic <conventional current> closes the loop from the <anode> compartment toward the <cathode> compartment. Oppositely moving <cations> and <anions> can both contribute to that direction; see <electrolyte> for the signed <current density> calculation. A cell can maintain a nonzero terminal <electric potential difference> near open circuit while delivering almost no <power>; the <Daniell cell voltage and load experiment> makes this distinction concrete.

= Daniell cell
{c}
{parent=Galvanic cell}
{wiki}

A <Daniell cell> uses the <zinc>/$\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}$ and $\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}$/<copper> pairs. In the separated-compartment teaching version, a <zinc> <electrode> contacts an aqueous <chemical solution> containing $\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}$, and a <copper> <electrode> contacts one containing $\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}$. A <salt bridge> supplies an ionic connection; a wire and load supply an electronic connection. During discharge,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{anode, oxidation:}\quad&\mathrm{Zn}(s)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}(aq)+2e^-,\\
\text{cathode, reduction:}\quad&\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}(aq)+2e^-\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cu}(s),\\
\text{net:}\quad&\mathrm{Zn}(s)+\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}(aq)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}(aq)+\mathrm{Cu}(s).
\end{aligned}
$$
The <zinc> <electrode> loses material and <copper> is deposited at the <cathode>. Each <half-reaction> conserves <chemical atom> counts and <electric charge>: the first has net charge zero on each side, the second $+2e-2e=0$ on the left and zero on the right. The net reaction has $+2e$ on both sides, with no <electrons> left in its <chemical equation>. The Zn/Cu teaching cell and these <half-reactions> are described in https://www.chem.fsu.edu/chemlab/chm1lab/redox/background.html[Florida State University, Redox Reactions in Voltaic Cells: Construction of a Potential Series, lab background].

Creating one <mole> of $\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}$ adds $2F$ of positive <electric charge> to the <anode> compartment's ionic inventory; consuming one <mole> of $\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}$ removes $2F$ of positive <electric charge> from the <cathode> compartment. Here $F$ is the <Faraday constant>. In an ideal bookkeeping example using a <potassium> <nitrate> <salt bridge>, two <moles> of <nitrate> <anions> entering the <anode> compartment compensate the first change, and two <moles> of <potassium> <cations> entering the <cathode> compartment compensate the second. Other <ions> in the cell can share the actual transport. The two compensating inventory changes do not mean that $4F$ flowed through the load: the same series <electric current> transports $2F$ through each cross-section of the circuit per <mole> of net reaction. See <salt bridge>.

Under standard-state conditions near $298\,\mathrm K$, tabulated reduction potentials give a cell <electromotive force> of approximately $1.10\,\mathrm V$. This is a reversible reference value, not a promised loaded reading; actual composition matters through <thermodynamic activity>. The tabulated <copper> and <zinc> values and their subtraction are given in https://chemed.chem.purdue.edu/genchem/topicreview/bp/ch20/electro.php[Purdue University, Electrochemical Reactions: standard-state reduction potentials]. For the net <chemical equation> as written, the molar <Gibbs free energy> change obeys
$$
\Delta_r G=-2F\mathcal E.
$$
Thus $\mathcal E=1.10\,\mathrm V$ corresponds to about $-212\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ of net reaction. At fixed <temperature> and <pressure>, $-\Delta_r G$ is the maximum reversible electrical <work> per <mole> for this cell reaction. It is not automatically all the <heat> released by a finite-current cell. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/17.4 Potential, Free Energy, and Equilibrium> (§17.4: cell potential and free energy).

= Daniell cell voltage and load experiment
{c}
{parent=Daniell cell}

The motivating observation is that a <chemical reaction> can produce a signed electrical reading even when its reacting materials occupy separate vessels. UC San Diego's teaching demonstration places a <copper> strip in $0.1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ $\mathrm{CuSO_4}$ and a <zinc> strip in $0.1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ $\mathrm{ZnSO_4}$, using two $400\,\mathrm{mL}$ beakers with about $300\,\mathrm{mL}$ of <chemical solution> in each. The strips connect to a voltage meter. Initially there is no ionic connection; the demonstration specifies a zero reading. A filter-paper <salt bridge> containing aqueous $\mathrm{Na_2SO_4}$ then connects the <chemical solutions>. With the meter's positive lead on <copper> and negative lead on <zinc>, its reported readings are about $+1.1\,\mathrm V$; exchanging the leads reverses the reading. These are observations reported by the university, not data generated for this article. See https://chemistryandbiochemistry.ucsd.edu/undergraduate/teaching-labs/demos/demo9b[UC San Diego, demonstration 9B: apparatus, bridge control and meter polarity].

The controls answer different questions. Exchanging only the meter leads checks the sign convention; it does not reverse the <chemical reaction> or swap the <anode> and <cathode>. Interrupting only the <salt bridge> tests the need for an ionic return path. Its absence prevents sustained cell <electric current>, but a very high-input-resistance meter may still show a transient or unstable reading while interfaces store <electric charge>. The zero reading specified for the university's apparatus is therefore not a universal theorem for every meter. Reconnecting the same <salt bridge> distinguishes a reversible interruption from a disconnected wire or exhausted <chemical solution>. This interpretation follows from <electric charge conservation> in a model allowing interfacial <electric charge> storage; see https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/I03068[IUPAC Gold Book: integral capacitance of an electrode] for the <capacitance> associated with <electrode> charge.

To distinguish terminal <electric potential difference> from delivered <power>, compare the nearly open-circuit reading with readings across a known <resistor> load. In the nearly open-circuit condition, a meter with input <electrical resistance> much larger than the cell's effective internal <electrical resistance> draws little <electric current>. With a load, measure its terminal <electric potential difference> $V$ and infer $I=V/R$ using <Ohm's law>; track the readings over <time> and repeat the nearly open-circuit reading afterwards. Keep the <temperature>, <chemical solution> compositions, immersed <electrode> areas, bridge geometry and electrical contacts comparable. This is a model-based extension of the documented demonstration, not a claim that UC San Diego published a load sweep. A finite-current drop can reflect ionic <electrical resistance>, changes of interfacial <reaction rates>, or depletion near an <electrode>; a single voltage reading cannot identify which dominates. The interfacial and composition contributions are distinguished in https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/O04358[IUPAC Gold Book: overpotential] and https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C01230[IUPAC Gold Book: concentration overpotential]{ref}.

For a deliberately simplified calculation, model the cell by a constant <electromotive force> $\mathcal E$ in series with a constant internal <electrical resistance> $r$, and a passive load $R>0$. Take $I$ positive from the <cathode> through the load to the <anode>. Then
$$
I=\frac{\mathcal E}{R+r},\qquad V=IR=\mathcal E-Ir,\qquad P_{\rm load}=IV=I^2R.
$$
The nearly open-circuit limit $R\to\infty$ gives $V\to\mathcal E$ but $I\to0$ and $P_{\rm load}\to0$. A straight $V$-versus-$I$ line is a prediction of constant $r$, not a general law for <electrochemistry>. See https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/10-1-electromotive-force[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics II, §10.1: internal resistance, terminal voltage and load].

\Image[galvanic-electrodes-load-budget.png]
{title=Toy <Daniell cell> load calculation: terminal <electric potential difference>, <electric charge> and available <work>}
{description=Calculated line and energy bars, not measurements. Chosen inputs are $\mathcal E=1.10\,\mathrm V$, $r=100\,\Omega$, $R=100\,\Omega$ and a $600\,\mathrm s$ interval. The fixed-composition approximation and unit efficiency for the two intended <half-reactions> are assumed. The open-circuit marker is an ideal limit; no fit to experimental data is made.}

For the chosen values, $I=5.50\,\mathrm{mA}$ and $V=0.550\,\mathrm V$. Holding these toy parameters fixed for $600\,\mathrm s$ gives transported <electric charge> $Q=It=3.30\,\mathrm C$ and an <extent of reaction> $\xi=Q/(2F)\simeq17.1\,\mathrm{\mu mol}$, assuming all the <electric current> serves the two specified <half-reactions>. The same $\xi$ counts <zinc> consumed and <copper> deposited, rather than equal <masses>. The energy budget is
$$
\mathcal E Q=3.630\,\mathrm J
=\underbrace{VQ}_{1.815\,\mathrm J\text{ delivered to load}}
+\underbrace{I^2rt}_{1.815\,\mathrm J\text{ internal dissipation}}.
$$
The load's electrical <work> also becomes <Joule heating> when the load is a <resistor>. Here $\mathcal E Q$ accounts for the reaction's available reversible electrical <work>; a full cell thermal budget would also require its thermodynamic <entropy> contribution. Real measurements need uncertainty estimates, a specified meter input <electrical resistance> and calibration, and allowance for changing composition and unwanted <chemical reactions>. The toy value $r=100\,\Omega$ is not a measured property of the university's cell.

= Electrolysis
{parent=Electrochemistry}
{wiki}

<Electrolysis> uses an external source of electrical <work> to drive <redox> <chemical reactions> at the <electrodes> of an <electrolytic cell>. Its characteristic application is forcing a <chemical reaction> in a direction that is nonspontaneous under the specified conditions: a discharging <galvanic cell> supplies electrical <work>, whereas <electrolysis> consumes electrical <work>. This makes <electrolysis> useful for producing otherwise difficult-to-obtain <chemical substances>, charging a rechargeable <battery>, and depositing a metal coating on a conducting object.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/17.7 Electrolysis>[§17.7]{ref}

Always identify the <anode> by <oxidation> and the <cathode> by <reduction>. In ordinary driven two-terminal operation, the source withdraws <electrons> from the positive <anode> and delivers <electrons> to the negative <cathode>. In a discharging <galvanic cell>, the <anode> is negative and the <cathode> positive. Thus terminal signs change with operating mode, while the reaction definitions remain valid. Through the <electrolyte>, <ions> transport <electric charge>; they need not all be consumed at an <electrode>. The <electrolyte> and <electrolytic cell> articles explain this transport and its <conventional current> direction.

For a net <chemical equation> transferring $z$ <electrons> per reaction event, the molar reaction <Gibbs free energy> obeys
$$
\Delta_rG=-zF E_{\mathrm{chem}},
$$
where $F$ is the <Faraday constant> and $E_{\mathrm{chem}}$ is the reversible cell <electromotive force> for the reaction in the written direction. A nonspontaneous direction has $\Delta_rG>0$ and $E_{\mathrm{chem}}<0$ at fixed <temperature> and <pressure>. The ideal opposing voltage magnitude is $\Delta_rG/(zF)$; sustaining a finite <electric current> generally needs additional drive because of <electrical resistance> and finite <electrode> <reaction rates>. This thermodynamic voltage concerns the actual composition, rather than necessarily standard conditions.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/17.4 Potential, Free Energy, and Equilibrium>[§17.4]{ref} Not every use of <electrolysis> increases bulk chemical <Gibbs free energy>: transferring <copper> between equivalent surfaces can have zero bulk $\Delta_rG$ while the source still supplies dissipated electrical <work>. See the existing <electrolytic cell> example.

A particularly revealing experiment measures <copper> transfer instead of merely looking for a coating. Two separated <copper> <electrodes> stand in aqueous $\mathrm{CuSO_4}$ <electrolyte> in a vessel, with insulated leads to a regulated direct-current power supply, a series ammeter and a switch. A balance reads each <electrode>'s <mass> before and after a timed run. The Institute of Physics describes copper cells with ammeters, adjustable current and removable <cathodes>; its comparison uses three similar cells at $1\,\mathrm A$ for ten minutes, $0.5\,\mathrm A$ for ten minutes, and $0.5\,\mathrm A$ for twenty minutes. The specified observation to test is that deposited <copper> <mass> is proportional to <electric current> times <time>. These are documented experimental settings, not measurements generated here.<Electrolysis of copper sulfate solution by Institute of Physics>{ref}

The ideal <half-reactions> are
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{anode, oxidation:}\quad&\mathrm{Cu}(s)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}(aq)+2e^-,\\
\text{cathode, reduction:}\quad&\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}(aq)+2e^-\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cu}(s).
\end{aligned}
$$
Each deposited <copper> <chemical atom> requires two <electrons>, so $z=2$. The <anode> loses <copper> and the <cathode> gains it; ideally their <mass> changes have equal magnitudes, and the bulk <copper> <cation> inventory stays constant. Local <concentrations> near the <electrodes> can nevertheless differ. The sulfate <anions> participate in ionic transport without appearing in these net <half-reactions>; electronic <electric current> in the wires becomes ionic <electric current> in the <electrolyte> through the two interfaces.<Elektrolyse, Bestimmung von e by Karlsruhe Institute of Technology, Faculty of Physics teaching laboratory>{ref}

\Image[electrolysis-copper-process.png]
{title=Driven <copper> transfer: <oxidation> at the positive <anode> and <reduction> at the negative <cathode>}
{description=Original model schematic, not a measured trajectory map. The source withdraws <electrons> on the left and supplies them on the right; bulk ionic <conventional current> closes the circuit. Before/after dry <electrode> <masses> and the integrated <electric current> provide independent readouts. The two <half-reactions> count the same transferred <electric charge>, so their charges must not be added together.}

Clean, rinse, dry and identify the <electrodes> before their initial weighing. After interrupting the <electric current>, rinse away adhering <electrolyte>, dry again and weigh without rubbing off the deposit. KIT's university practical explicitly calls for distilled-water rinsing, careful drying and weighing both <electrodes>; it warns that loose <copper> grains can be lost during handling.<Elektrolyse, Bestimmung von e by Karlsruhe Institute of Technology, Faculty of Physics teaching laboratory>{ref} Useful additional controls are repeated drying and weighing until the reading stabilizes, and a matched unpowered immersion followed by the same rinse/dry cycle. These controls test retained liquid, residual dissolved salts, handling loss and chemical changes unrelated to passed <electric charge>; they are proposed checks, not additional observations reported by KIT. Keep immersed <electrode> area, spacing, <electrolyte> composition and <temperature> comparable across runs, and keep the <electrodes> from touching.

Record the <electric current> as a function of <time>, together with the exact switch-on and switch-off times. Taking $I(t)\geq0$ in the chosen deposition direction, the transported <electric charge> is
$$
Q=\int_{t_0}^{t_1}I(t)\,dt.
$$
For constant <electric current>, $Q=I(t_1-t_0)$; with sampled readings, use <numerical integration> rather than assuming that one ammeter reading remained constant. The two equal-charge Institute of Physics settings each give $600\,\mathrm C$, while its shorter $0.5\,\mathrm A$ setting gives $300\,\mathrm C$. With $M_{\mathrm{Cu}}=63.546\,\mathrm{g\,mol^{-1}}$, the <mass> per <amount of substance> tabulated by KIT, and $F\approx96485.33212\,\mathrm{C\,mol^{-1}}$, ideal <Faraday's laws of electrolysis> predict
$$
m_{\mathrm{Cu}}=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Cu}}Q}{2F}
\approx\begin{cases}
0.09879\,\mathrm g,&Q=300\,\mathrm C,\\
0.19758\,\mathrm g,&Q=600\,\mathrm C.
\end{cases}
$$
These are calculated predictions for complete conversion of the measured <electric charge> into retained <copper>, not experimental results. For a stable process, doubling the <electric charge> doubles the deposited <mass>; equal <electric charge> gives equal deposited <mass> even when the <electric current> and duration differ.

\Image[electrolysis-charge-mass.png]
{title=<Faraday's laws of electrolysis> connect transported <electric charge> to deposited <copper> <mass>}
{description=Original calculated model, with no experimental points. Left: assumed constant-current histories with equal areas $Q=600\,\mathrm C$. Right: ideal <copper> <mass> versus <electric charge> and a stipulated 80 percent current-efficiency comparison. The dashed line assumes that 20 percent of the <electric charge> drives other same-direction <electrode> reactions and that all deposited <copper> remains attached.}

The balance reading is a test of the whole model. Competing <reduction> of <water> or other <chemical species> can divert <electric current> from <copper> deposition; transient <electrode> <capacitance> can also consume part of a short pulse without producing <copper>. A fragile deposit can detach even when its formation used the expected <electric charge>. Conversely, retained <electrolyte> or salts can increase an apparent <mass> gain. Comparing <anode> loss with <cathode> gain helps detect such problems but does not identify every cause. A slope below $M_{\mathrm{Cu}}/(2F)$ alone cannot distinguish competing <chemical reactions> from lost deposit. Current efficiency measures the fraction of transported <electric charge> assigned to a specified <electrode> reaction, rather than the fraction of electrical <energy> stored chemically.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/current efficiency>{ref}

The beautiful connection is that an electrical measurement counts chemical events: integrating <electric current> and reading a balance provide two ways of following the same <electron> <stoichiometry>. This makes <electrolysis> useful for controlling coating quantity and for testing a charge-to-<amount of substance> relation. It does not determine coating uniformity, adhesion or the required voltage, which depend on transport and <electrode> <chemical kinetics> as well.

= Faraday's laws of electrolysis
{c}
{parent=Electrolysis}
{wiki}
{title2=$m=MQ/(zF)$}

<Faraday's laws of electrolysis> state that the <mass> of material formed or consumed by a specified <electrode> reaction is proportional to the transported <electric charge>, and that equal <electric charge> produces <masses> proportional to $M/z$ for different reactions. Here $M$ is the product's <mass> per <mole>, and $z$ is the positive number of <electrons> transferred per product entity. For complete use of the <electric charge> in that reaction,
$$
n_{e^-}=\frac QF,\qquad n_{\mathrm{product}}=\frac{Q}{zF},\qquad m=\frac{MQ}{zF}.
$$
The first relation uses the <Faraday constant>; the second uses <half-reaction> <stoichiometry>; the third converts <amount of substance> to <mass>. For equal transported <electric charge> in two ideal cells,
$$
\frac{m_1}{m_2}=\frac{M_1/z_1}{M_2/z_2}.
$$
The number $z$ belongs to the balanced <half-reaction>, rather than to the metal's name alone: <copper> deposited from $\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}$ needs $z=2$. These modern forms of both laws are given in KIT's practical.<Elektrolyse, Bestimmung von e by Karlsruhe Institute of Technology, Faculty of Physics teaching laboratory>{ref}

If other same-direction <electrode> reactions share the measured <electric current>, define an integrated current efficiency $\eta=Q_{\mathrm{product}}/Q$. Provided the product is neither subsequently consumed nor lost, the retained <mass> is
$$
m=\eta\frac{MQ}{zF},\qquad
\eta=\frac{\int I_{\mathrm{product}}(t)\,dt}{\int I(t)\,dt}.
$$
Here $0\leq\eta\leq1$ follows from the specified same-direction partition of <electric current>. It is not a universal bound on every signed partial-current convention: IUPAC notes that simultaneous <oxidation> and <reduction> at one <electrode> can produce a current-efficiency ratio above one.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/current efficiency>{ref} When efficiency varies during a run, use the charge-weighted ratio above, rather than an unweighted average over <time>. If deposition removes material from a multicomponent <chemical solution>, specify which <chemical species> is being counted and balance its <half-reaction> before choosing $z$.

Michael Faraday's accessible 1834 paper, Experimental Researches in Electricity, Seventh Series, §783 states proportionality between chemical action and the quantity of electricity passed. In §§789–791 he describes connecting a molten tin-chloride cell in series with a water-decomposition charge indicator, weighing the receiving <electrode> before and after, and comparing the deposited metal with the indicator's gas readout. The common series <electric current> makes these two readouts comparable. This was a quantitative test of chemical equivalents, rather than merely an observation that a deposit appeared.
