It is historical evidence, not a suggested classroom procedure; the modern <electron>-counting derivation above uses concepts unavailable to Faraday.<Experimental Researches in Electricity, Seventh Series by Michael Faraday>[§783; §§789–791; pp. 101–103]{ref}

= Electrolytic cell
{parent=Electrolysis}
{wiki}

An <electrolytic cell> uses an external source of electrical <work> to drive <electrode> <chemical reactions>. In the ordinary two-terminal driven mode, its positive <anode> supports <oxidation> and its negative <cathode> supports <reduction>. The source removes <electrons> from the <anode> and supplies <electrons> to the <cathode>; through the <electrolyte>, net <conventional current> goes from <anode> toward <cathode>. Terminal signs refer to the driven cell's terminals relative to each other. The source supplies electrical <power> to the cell. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/17.7 Electrolysis> (§17.7: electrolysis and electroplating).

For example, transferring <copper> from a soluble <copper> <anode> onto a conducting object at the <cathode> has the ideal <half-reactions>
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{positive anode:}\quad&\mathrm{Cu}(s)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}(aq)+2e^-,\\
\text{negative cathode:}\quad&\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}(aq)+2e^-\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cu}(s).
\end{aligned}
$$
Their sum transfers <copper> between surfaces. For identical bulk states, this transfer need not have a positive bulk <Gibbs free energy> change; finite <electric current> still needs applied drive to overcome losses. Thus "consumes electrical <work>" does not mean every <electrolytic cell> stores all that <work> as chemical <Gibbs free energy>. <Electrolysis> can instead redistribute material. The charge-to-amount relation remains $\xi=Q/(2F)$ when only these <half-reactions> carry the <electric current>; competing <water> reactions would change the deposited amount. The electroplating principle and charge accounting are described in <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/17.7 Electrolysis> (§17.7: electroplating and quantitative aspects).

The University of Washington's teaching demonstration makes this material transfer visible with a <copper> <electrode> and a stainless-steel <electrode> in a glass cell containing $0.5\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ $\mathrm{CuSO_4}$. The external source connects its positive lead to <copper> and negative lead to steel; the reported observation is a <copper> coating on the steel. Reversing the source polarity removes the deposited <copper> again: the coated surface now supports <oxidation> and acts as the <anode>. This polarity reversal changes the drive, unlike swapping the measurement leads in the <Daniell cell voltage and load experiment>. Coating appearance alone does not measure charge efficiency or exclude competing <chemical reactions>. See https://chem.washington.edu/lecture-demos/electroplating-copper-stainless-steel[University of Washington, Electroplating Copper on Stainless Steel: apparatus and reversible coating demonstration].

The <copper> <cathode> in this driven example is negative, while the <copper> <cathode> of a discharging <Daniell cell> is positive: both support exactly the same <reduction> <half-reaction>. Likewise, an <anode> need not lose material if its conducting surface is absent from its net <half-reaction>. Neither terminal sign nor a universal mass-change rule defines an <anode> or <cathode>.

= Half-reaction
{parent=Electrochemistry}
{wiki}

A <half-reaction> is a balanced <chemical equation> for either the <oxidation> or the <reduction> part of a <redox> <chemical reaction>, explicitly including exchanged <electrons>. Match the electron counts before adding the two <half-reactions>; the net equation then contains no transferred <electrons>. A <half-reaction> is bookkeeping, not necessarily an <elementary reaction>. See https://www.chem.fsu.edu/chemlab/chm1lab/redox/background.html[Florida State University, lab background: separate electrode reactions] and <Daniell cell>.

= Salt bridge
{parent=Electrochemistry}
{wiki}

A <salt bridge> is an ionic connection between <electrolyte> compartments that limits bulk mixing while permitting <electric charge> transport. Its mobile <ions> compensate changes caused by the <electrode> <half-reactions>, keeping the bulk <chemical solutions> approximately electrically neutral during sustained <electric current>. It does not conduct <electrons> like the external wire, and its <ions> need not be consumed at either <electrode>. The ionic return path and compensating ion flows are explained in https://chemed.chem.purdue.edu/genchem/topicreview/bp/ch20/electro.php[Purdue University, Electrochemical Reactions: salt bridge and charge balance].

In a discharging <Daniell cell>, <anions> moving toward the <anode> compartment and <cations> moving toward the <cathode> compartment both contribute to the internal <conventional current> from <anode> toward <cathode>. This charge-balance role is compatible with a negative <anode>: terminal signs do not imply that the whole <anode> <chemical solution> is negatively charged. The simple compensating-inventory example in <Daniell cell> does not fix all individual <ion> fluxes; <diffusion> and the cell's other mobile <ions> can contribute. A real bridge also introduces <electrical resistance> and may introduce a junction <electric potential difference>, so its presence does not guarantee an exact thermodynamic cell reading. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/09080[IUPAC Gold Book: salt bridge and liquid-junction potential].

= Electrolyte
{parent=Electrochemistry}

An <electrolyte> is a conducting medium in which mobile <ions> carry <electric current>, according to https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/09061[IUPAC Gold Book: electrolyte]. In introductory <chemistry>, a substance that yields <ions> when dissolved in <water> is also called an <electrolyte>. Both usages point to the same mechanism: an <electric field> gives mobile <cations> and <anions> directed <drift velocities>.

Mobility matters as much as the presence of <electric charge>. In a simple ideal model of solid <sodium chloride>, <ions> are confined to their sites and do not carry a sustained transport <electric current>. Molten <sodium chloride> has mobile <ions>; dissolved <sodium chloride> gives mobile $\mathrm{Na}^+$ and $\mathrm{Cl}^-$ <ions> in <water>. Real solids can have some ionic transport through imperfections; the immobile-solid picture is an introductory limit, rather than a claim that all solids are perfect insulators.

A <strong electrolyte> yields essentially all <ions> from the dissolved substance, whereas a <weak electrolyte> yields only a fraction under the specified conditions. Dissolved <sodium chloride> is a <strong electrolyte>. <Sucrose> is a <nonelectrolyte>: it dissolves as neutral <molecules>, so dissolving something does not automatically create mobile <ions>. <Water>'s own small population of <hydronium> and <hydroxide> remains possible. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/11.2 Electrolytes> (§11.2: strong, weak, and nonelectrolytes) and https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/11-4-colligative-properties[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §11.4, Table 11.3: sucrose and sodium chloride].

<Acetic acid> is a <weak electrolyte> and a <weak acid>. In <water>, some neutral <acetic acid> <molecules> transfer a <proton> to <water>, producing <hydronium> and the negatively charged partner:
$$
\mathrm{CH_3COOH}+\mathrm{H_2O}\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{CH_3COO^-}+\mathrm{H_3O^+}.
$$
Both sides have net <electric charge> zero. At <chemical equilibrium>, neutral <acetic acid> coexists with these <ions>; the fraction converted depends on the conditions. <Electrolyte> strength describes this extent of ion production, while <concentration> describes how much substance is present per specified quantity of <chemical solution>. A dilute <strong electrolyte> can contain fewer <ions> per volume than a concentrated <weak electrolyte>. Neither the words strong and weak nor a conductivity comparison alone specifies <concentration>.

Now specify an <electrolytic cell> with a positive <anode> on the left, a negative <cathode> on the right, and an approximately uniform <electric field> pointing right in the bulk <electrolyte>. With a steady external source and neglecting <diffusion> and <convection>, positive <cations> drift right toward the negative <cathode>; negative <anions> drift left toward the positive <anode>. Yet both populations contribute a rightward <conventional current>! For <number densities> $n_+,n_-$ and <drift velocities> $\mathbf v_+,\mathbf v_-$, the transport <current density> is
$$
\mathbf J=n_+(+e)\mathbf v_++n_-(-e)\mathbf v_-.
$$
The product of <electric charge> and <velocity> determines <conventional current>: for the <anion>, both the charge and its rightward velocity component are negative, making its rightward contribution positive. This is the same sign rule used for <electron> <conventional current>. It follows directly from the existing <charge carrier> formula.

\Image[electrolyte-ion-drift-current.png]
{title=Opposite <ion> <drift velocities> contribute to the same <conventional current> in a specified <electrolytic cell>}
{description=The positive <anode> is left and the negative <cathode> right. The upper arrows show <ion> motion; the lower arrows show the signs of $nqv_x$ for <current density>. A uniform bulk <electric field> is assumed; <diffusion>, <convection>, and <electrode> boundary layers are omitted. Arrow lengths are schematic.}

For an independent numerical example, stipulate $n_+=n_-=10^{25}\,\mathrm{m^{-3}}$, $v_{+,x}=2\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, and $v_{-,x}=-3\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. With the exact <elementary charge> $e=1.602176634\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C$, the contributions are
$$
J_{+,x}=320.4353268\,\mathrm{A\,m^{-2}},\qquad
J_{-,x}=480.6529902\,\mathrm{A\,m^{-2}},\qquad
J_x=801.088317\,\mathrm{A\,m^{-2}}.
$$
Equal <number densities> of singly charged <cations> and <anions> give zero bulk <charge density>, while their motion gives nonzero <current density>. This is why a neutral <electrolyte> can conduct. These numbers are chosen toy inputs, not measurements of <sodium chloride>; maintaining sustained cell <electric current> also requires suitable <electrode> reactions. Blocking <electrodes> can instead lead to charge accumulation and decay of the <electric current>.

An <electrolyte> connects the microscopic motion of <ions> to observable <electrical conductivity>. A counterion-only model keeps just the mobile <counterions> needed to oppose a fixed <electric charge>; <Poisson-Boltzmann equations> use ideal <Boltzmann distributions> to model their equilibrium distributions, rather than the drift example above.

= Strong electrolyte
{parent=Electrolyte}
{wiki}

A <strong electrolyte> yields essentially all <ions> from its dissolved substance in the specified <solvent> and conditions. Strength does not specify <concentration>.

= Weak electrolyte
{parent=Electrolyte}
{wiki}

A <weak electrolyte> yields only a fraction of <ions> from its dissolved substance under the specified conditions, as illustrated by <acetic acid> in <water>.

= Nonelectrolyte
{parent=Electrolyte}

A <nonelectrolyte> supplies neutral <molecules> rather than <ions> on dissolving in the specified <solvent>, as illustrated by <sucrose> in <water>.

= Chemical reaction
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

A <chemical reaction> interconverts <chemical species>, as defined by the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C01033[IUPAC Gold Book, chemical reaction]{ref}. The consumed species are <reactants>; the formed species are <reaction products>. In ordinary <chemical reactions>, <chemical atoms> are rearranged while each <chemical element>'s total atom count is conserved. For a complete <chemical equation>, net <electric charge> is conserved too.

For example, the balanced <chemical equation>
$$
2\mathrm{H}_2+\mathrm{O}_2\longrightarrow2\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}
$$
describes conversion of <molecular hydrogen> and <molecular oxygen> into <water>. Both sides contain four <hydrogen> <chemical atoms> and two <oxygen> <chemical atoms>. Three <reactant> <molecules> become two <reaction product> <molecules>: atom counts are conserved, but molecule counts need not be. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/4-1-writing-and-balancing-chemical-equations[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, balancing chemical equations].

The coefficients describe net consumption and formation, without asserting that three <molecules> collide in one step. A balanced <chemical equation> supplies quantitative constraints; it does not determine how quickly the <chemical reaction> occurs, or whether <chemical equilibrium> prevents complete conversion. Those questions need further information. Keeping these roles distinct makes <stoichiometry> useful even when the microscopic details are complicated.

= Precipitation
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

<Precipitation (chemistry)> is formation of a solid from a solution. A newly formed solid can create a surface for <heterogeneous catalysis>; observing it does not establish the composition or catalytic role of that surface.

= Redox
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

= Oxidation-reduction reaction
{synonym}

A <redox> <chemical reaction> couples <oxidation> and <reduction>. In the <electrode> examples here, <oxidation> releases <electrons> and <reduction> accepts them. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/4-2-classifying-chemical-reactions[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §4.2: oxidation-reduction reactions].

= Oxidation
{parent=Redox}

In the <electrochemistry> examples here, <oxidation> is loss of <electrons> by a <chemical species>; it occurs at the <anode>.

= Oxidation state
{parent=Oxidation}
{title2=$\mathrm{OS}$}
{wiki}

= Oxidation number
{synonym}

An <oxidation state> is an <electron>-bookkeeping assignment to a <chemical atom>: its <electric charge> number after the <ionic approximation of bonds>. It helps identify <oxidation> and <reduction> and determine <stoichiometry>, even for <covalent bonds> without separate charged <ions>. In <water>, each <hydrogen> has <oxidation state> $+1$ and <oxygen> has $-2$; this does not assert that a <water> <molecule> contains two free <protons> and a free oxide <ion>.<Comprehensive definition of oxidation state (IUPAC Recommendations 2016) by Pavel Karen and others>[Karen, McArdle and Takats, Comprehensive definition of oxidation state (IUPAC Recommendations 2016), §2, pp. 832–833: definition and equal splitting of same-element bonds]{ref}

Three conventions answer different questions. <Formal charge> divides each bonding <electron pair> equally in a chosen <Lewis structure>. <Oxidation state> uses the <ionic approximation of bonds>. <Partial atomic charge> instead partitions the actual <electron density> and depends on that partition. In the ordinary <water> <Lewis structure>, every <formal charge> is zero although the <oxidation states> are not. Neither bookkeeping convention supplies numerical <partial atomic charges>.

= Ionic approximation of bonds
{parent=Oxidation state}
{title2=$\mathrm{OS}_i=v_i-N_i^{\mathrm{assigned}}$}

For the routine examples here, assign all bonding <electrons> between different <chemical elements> to the more <electronegative> partner; divide bonding <electrons> between identical <chemical elements> equally. Retain each <lone pair> on its original <chemical atom>. Subtract the assigned <valence electron> count $N_i^{\mathrm{assigned}}$ from the neutral <chemical atom>'s <valence electron> count $v_i$. The IUPAC convention uses Allen <electronegativity>, based on free-<chemical atom> <valence electron> <energies>. Close <electronegativity> comparisons and some reversibly bound <chemical compounds> require care beyond this simple rule.<Comprehensive definition of oxidation state (IUPAC Recommendations 2016) by Pavel Karen and others>[§3 and §4.1, pp. 833–834: Allen electronegativity, electron allegiance and the reversible Lewis-acid-ligand caveat; §5.5, pp. 836–837: ambiguous assignments]{ref}

\Image[oxidation-state-electron-bookkeeping.png]
{id=image-oxidation-state-electron-bookkeeping}
{title=<Oxidation state> assignments for <water>, <hydrogen peroxide>, <molecular oxygen> and <sodium hydride>}
{description=Original bookkeeping diagrams, not <electron density> maps or measured <partial atomic charges>. Arrows indicate allocation of bonding <electrons>; an equal split is used for O–O. The <sodium hydride> panel represents a <formula unit> of an <ionic compound>, not an isolated gas-phase <molecule>.}

The examples explain exceptions to memorized rules:

* <Water>, $\mathrm{H{-}O{-}H}$: <oxygen> receives both O–H <bond pairs> plus its two <lone pairs>, giving eight assigned <valence electrons> and $6-8=-2$. Each <hydrogen> receives none, giving $1-0=+1$.
* <Hydrogen peroxide>, $\mathrm{H{-}O{-}O{-}H}$: each <oxygen> receives its O–H <bond pair>, one <electron> from O–O, and four <electrons> in <lone pairs>. Thus each has $6-7=-1$, while each <hydrogen> remains $+1$. The O–O connection is why the usual $-2$ <oxygen> shortcut fails.
* <Molecular oxygen>, $\mathrm{O}_2$: equal splitting of the O–O bonding <electrons> leaves each <oxygen> assigned six <valence electrons>, so each has <oxidation state> zero.
* <Sodium hydride>, $\mathrm{NaH}$: <hydrogen> is more <electronegative> than <sodium>. The <formula unit> is assigned <sodium> $+1$ and <hydrogen> $-1$, consistent with the <hydrogen anion>. The usual $+1$ <hydrogen> shortcut fails here.

These drawings record allocations, not <molecular geometry> or a physical process of breaking the <chemical bonds>. In particular, the O–O line in the <molecular oxygen> panel only marks the connection; it does not assert a complete <Lewis structure> or <spin> state.

= Oxidation state sum rule
{parent=Oxidation state}
{title2=$\sum_i\mathrm{OS}_i=z$}

Sum <oxidation states> over all <chemical atoms> of the specified <molecule>, <ion> or <formula unit>, including multiplicities:
$$
\sum_i\mathrm{OS}_i=z,\qquad q=ze.
$$
Here $q$ is the net <electric charge> and $e$ the positive <elementary charge>. For a neutral entity the sum is zero; for an <ion> it equals its signed <electric charge> number $z$. The reason is simple: the allocation assigns every <valence electron> exactly once, so the sum is the neutral <electron> budget minus the actual <electron> count.

For <water>, $2(+1)+(-2)=0$; for <hydrogen peroxide>, $2(+1)+2(-1)=0$. In <permanganate>, $\mathrm{MnO}_4^-$, assigning each <oxygen> $-2$ gives $x+4(-2)=-1$, so <manganese> has <oxidation state> $+7$. This is not a free $\mathrm{Mn}^{7+}$ <ion>: the entire <permanganate> <anion> has net <electric charge> $-e$. The sum rule supplies a constraint; without additional assignments it cannot determine every individual <oxidation state>.

= Acidic permanganate-iron balance
{parent=Oxidation state}

An increase in <oxidation state> records <oxidation>; a decrease records <reduction>. Thus $\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}\to\mathrm{Fe}^{3+}$ raises the <iron> <oxidation state> by one, while $\mathrm{MnO}_4^-\to\mathrm{Mn}^{2+}$ lowers the <manganese> <oxidation state> from $+7$ to $+2$. Five <iron> <ions> supply the five-<electron> inventory required per <permanganate> <ion>.

In an acidic <chemical solution>, the complete <half-reactions> are
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}&\longrightarrow\mathrm{Fe}^{3+}+e^-,\\
\mathrm{MnO}_4^-+8\mathrm H^++5e^-&\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mn}^{2+}+4\mathrm H_2\mathrm O.
\end{aligned}
$$
First balance <oxygen> with four <water> <molecules>, then <hydrogen> with eight <protons>, and finally <electric charge> with five <electrons>. Multiply the <iron> <half-reaction> by five and add; the exchanged <electrons> cancel:
$$
\boxed{\mathrm{MnO}_4^-+8\mathrm H^++5\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mn}^{2+}+4\mathrm H_2\mathrm O+5\mathrm{Fe}^{3+}.}
$$
Both sides contain one <manganese>, four <oxygen>, eight <hydrogen> and five <iron> <chemical atoms>, with net <electric charge> $+17e$. The $\mathrm H^+$ notation represents the acidic aqueous <proton> inventory; it does not imply bare <protons> moving independently of <water>. These <half-reactions> and their sum are the acidic balance used in the documented <permanganate-iron titration>.<Iron Tablet Determination Analytical Competition (Royal Society of Chemistry resource)>[Iron Tablet Determination Analytical Competition, “Setting the Scene”: both half-reactions and the overall equation]{ref}

\Image[permanganate-iron-electron-balanced-inventory.png]
{id=image-permanganate-iron-electron-balanced-inventory}
{title=<Acidic permanganate-iron balance>: five exchanged <electrons> and conserved <chemical atom> and <electric charge> inventories}
{description=Original exact <stoichiometry> diagram. Matching five formal <electron> slots is a bookkeeping operation, not a picture of free <electrons> in the <chemical solution>. The lower table verifies the net <chemical equation>; its $+17$ is the net <electric charge> number of the written reacting species, not the bulk <chemical solution>.}

The coefficients predict amounts consumed, not a collision of five <ions> or an <elementary reaction>. Bulk <chemical solution> also contains compensating <counterions>, which are omitted from this net <chemical equation>. The <hydrogen> and <oxygen> <oxidation states> are unchanged; balancing their inventories remains essential even though they do not account for the exchanged <electrons>.

= Redox titration
{parent=Redox}

A <redox titration> adds a measured <amount of substance> of one <reactant> to determine another through a known <redox> <chemical equation>. The <burette> reading becomes an amount through <concentration>; the balanced <chemical equation> supplies the amount ratio. A colour endpoint additionally needs a chemical explanation, as in the <permanganate-iron titration>.

= Permanganate-iron titration
{parent=Redox titration}

A proposed modern teaching measurement puts a <pipette>-measured portion of an <iron> $\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}$ <chemical solution> in a <conical flask>, acidifies with excess dilute <sulfuric acid>, and adds standardized <potassium permanganate> from a clamped <burette> while swirling. The documented endpoint principle is that added purple <permanganate> is consumed until the first small excess leaves a pale purple colour; no separate colour indicator is required. Insufficient acidity can produce brown <manganese dioxide>, invalidating the assumed five-<electron> <half-reaction>.<Iron Tablet Determination Analytical Competition (Royal Society of Chemistry resource)>[“Setting the Scene”, final two paragraphs: burette, conical flask, decolourisation, endpoint and excess sulfuric acid]{ref}

Record initial and final <burette> readings; their difference is the delivered <volume (thermodynamics)> $V$. The following controls are proposed additions to test the inference. Run a reagent blank with the same <water>, <sulfuric acid> and endpoint criterion but no sample, obtaining $V_b$. Use a separately established <potassium permanganate> <concentration> $c$, clear the <burette> tip of air, and repeat sample portions. A known added <amount of substance> of $\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}$ should increase consumption by one fifth of that amount. A blank estimates reagent demand and the small endpoint excess; it cannot remove an interfering sample <reactant>.

Assuming complete, selective reaction according to the <acidic permanganate-iron balance>, the corrected inference is
$$
n(\mathrm{Fe}^{2+})=5c(V-V_b).
$$
With $c$ in $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, use $V$ and $V_b$ in litres. As an original stipulated calculation, not an experimental result, take $c=0.02000\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, $V=12.60\,\mathrm{mL}$ and $V_b=0.10\,\mathrm{mL}$. Then the <permanganate> consumption is $0.2500\,\mathrm{mmol}$ and the inferred $\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}$ amount is $1.250\,\mathrm{mmol}$. In a $25.00\,\mathrm{mL}$ sample portion this corresponds to $0.05000\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$.

The endpoint detects residual <permanganate>, not the <iron> identity directly. Other sample <reactants> that consume <permanganate> raise the apparent $\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}$ amount; prior <oxidation> of sample $\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}$ to $\mathrm{Fe}^{3+}$ lowers it. A recovery check probes response to the added <iron> but does not prove absence of every interference. The readout determines the reacting $\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}$ inventory under the stated conditions, rather than total <iron> in an arbitrary mixture. No measured titres are asserted here.

= Reduction
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Redox}

= Reduction
{synonym}

In the <electrochemistry> examples here, <reduction> is gain of <electrons> by a <chemical species>; it occurs at the <cathode>.

= Hydrolysis
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

<Hydrolysis> is a <chemical reaction> involving <water> that breaks a <chemical bond>. The <free energy> released by suitably coupled <ATP> <hydrolysis> can drive <active transport>; the available <free energy> depends on the actual composition and conditions.

= Saponification
{parent=Hydrolysis}
{wiki}

<Saponification> is <hydrolysis> of an <ester> under basic conditions. The <conductometric saponification assay> follows <ethyl acetate> consumption by <hydroxide>, producing <ethanol> and an <acetate ion>; its equal-starting-<concentration> case provides a practical <second-order reaction> example.

= Energy coupling
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{title2=$\Delta G_{\mathrm{net}}=\Delta G_1+\Delta G_2$}

<Energy coupling> links an <endergonic reaction> to an <exergonic reaction> through a <reaction mechanism>, so that the combined transformation has a favourable change in <Gibbs free energy>. <ATP> <hydrolysis> is a common driving <chemical reaction>. A shared <reaction intermediate>, produced in one step and consumed in another, can physically connect the transformations; merely placing two independent <chemical reactions> in the same <chemical solution> does not establish <energy coupling>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26838/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: coupled reactions and activated intermediates].

\Image[atp-energy-coupling.png]
{title=<Energy coupling>: $+20-30.5=-10.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ under the same stipulated conditions}
{description=Original diagram of cumulative changes in <Gibbs free energy> as two contributions are added. The levels are bookkeeping steps, not a <reaction mechanism>, actual <reaction intermediates>, elapsed time, or <activation energy> barriers. A <reaction mechanism> must enforce the joint transformation for the net favourable value to drive the desired conversion.}

For a computed example, stipulate an <endergonic reaction> $\mathrm A\longrightarrow\mathrm B$ with $\Delta G_1^{\circ\prime}=+20\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ under precisely the same biochemical <standard state> conditions as the quoted <ATP> <hydrolysis> value. Assume a <reaction mechanism> couples one <ATP> <hydrolysis> to each A-to-B conversion. The formal sum is
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm A&\longrightarrow\mathrm B,
&\Delta G_1^{\circ\prime}&=+20\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},\\
\mathrm{ATP}+\mathrm{H_2O}&\longrightarrow\mathrm{ADP}+\mathrm P_i,
&\Delta G_2^{\circ\prime}&=-30.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},\\
\mathrm A+\mathrm{ATP}+\mathrm{H_2O}&\longrightarrow\mathrm B+\mathrm{ADP}+\mathrm P_i,
&\Delta G_{\mathrm{net}}^{\circ\prime}&=-10.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}.
\end{aligned}
$$
The sum $+20+(-30.5)=-10.5$ gives an <exergonic reaction>. The $+20$ is a chosen teaching value, not a measured property of an identified substance. Outside these reference conditions, add the actual $\Delta G_1$ and $\Delta G_2$, each evaluated using consistent <thermodynamic activities>; mixing a standard value for one <chemical reaction> with an unrelated cellular value for the other gives no justified prediction.

Why do the changes in <Gibbs free energy> add? <Gibbs free energy> is a <state function>. Adding consistent reaction changes cancels any shared <reaction intermediate> and leaves the difference between the combined <reactants> and combined <reaction products>. This explains the thermodynamic accounting; it does not construct the <reaction mechanism>. In many <ATP>-dependent routes, <phosphorylation> transfers a <phosphate> group to a <substrate> or an <enzyme>, and subsequent transformation consumes that modified material. The shared material is what connects the steps. Other <reaction mechanisms> use <ATP> differently. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26838/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: ATP-driven biosynthesis].

The useful achievement is that a common regenerated <nucleotide> can connect many different processes in <metabolism>. The net negative change in <Gibbs free energy> permits the coupled conversion, but supplies no <reaction rate>: <enzymes> can lower <activation energy> barriers without changing the endpoint difference. <Energy coupling> changes which overall <chemical reaction> is being considered; <catalysis> accelerates an available route. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/6-2-potential-kinetic-free-and-activation-energy[OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.2: free-energy changes and reaction barriers].

A historical landmark is Fritz Lipmann's https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470122464.ch4[1941 essay, Metabolic Generation and Utilization of Phosphate Bond Energy]{ref}. Its sections on the <phosphate> cycle, generation and utilization of phosphate-bond <energy>, and group transfer organized these processes around a reusable chemical carrier. The historical terminology should be read with the modern distinction between net <hydrolysis> and the cost of breaking a <chemical bond>.

= Exergonic reaction
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Delta G<0$}

An <exergonic reaction> has negative molar reaction change in <Gibbs free energy> under the specified conditions. At fixed <temperature> and <pressure>, its forward direction is thermodynamically favourable; its <reaction rate> remains a separate question. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/6-2-potential-kinetic-free-and-activation-energy[OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.2: exergonic reactions].

= Endergonic reaction
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Delta G>0$}

An <endergonic reaction> has positive molar reaction change in <Gibbs free energy> under the specified conditions. Sustained net forward conversion needs a driving process, such as <energy coupling>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/6-2-potential-kinetic-free-and-activation-energy[OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.2: endergonic reactions].

= Phosphorylation
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

<Phosphorylation> attaches a <phosphate> group to a <molecule>. Transfer from <ATP> to a <substrate> or <enzyme> can participate in <energy coupling>; the name alone does not specify the complete <reaction mechanism>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/6-4-atp-adenosine-triphosphate[OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.4: phosphorylation].

= Chemical kinetics
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

<Chemical kinetics> studies how quickly <chemical reactions> proceed and how their <reaction rates> depend on composition and conditions. It connects a measured change in <concentration> to a <rate law>, then uses a <rate equation> to predict future <concentrations>. This makes it useful for choosing how long to wait for conversion and for testing a proposed <reaction mechanism>. Changing <temperature>, <reactant> <concentrations>, or the presence of a <catalyst> can change <reaction rates>; see https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/12-2-factors-affecting-reaction-rates[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §12.2: factors affecting reaction rates].

<Chemical equilibrium> specifies the eventual balance under given constraints; <chemical kinetics> determines how rapidly it is approached. At fixed <temperature> and <pressure>, a decrease in <Gibbs free energy> favours conversion but does not guarantee a fast <chemical reaction>: reaching a <transition state> can require crossing a substantial barrier. Thus a mixture can change imperceptibly over an observation period without being at <chemical equilibrium>. The distinction separates which direction is favoured from how long conversion takes. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/12.5 Collision Theory> (§12.5: collision theory and reaction barriers) and https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/16-1-spontaneity[Chemistry 2e, §16.1: spontaneity and speed].

= Activation energy
{parent=Chemical kinetics}
{wiki}
{title2=$E_a$}

= Arrhenius activation energy
{c}
{synonym}

A small increase in <temperature> can produce a large increase in a <reaction rate>. In the simplest <Arrhenius equation> model, warming from $300$ to $310\,\mathrm K$ increases a <rate constant> with $E_a=80\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ by about $2.81$ times, even though the absolute <thermodynamic temperature> rises by only about $3.33\%$. This sensitivity helps explain why cooling can slow unwanted <chemical reactions> and why a hot process can become difficult to control. The comparison assumes the same <rate law> and <reactant> <concentrations>; the <activation energy> characterizes $k(T)$, rather than every change in the measured <reaction rate>.

The physical motivation is the distribution of molecular <energies>. Warming changes the <Boltzmann distribution>, increasing the relative population of energetically costly configurations. A <transition state> can therefore become much more accessible than the modest change in average <kinetic energy> might suggest. The <exponential function> in an <Arrhenius equation> captures such sensitivity in a simple model; it is not a universal formula for the fraction above every barrier in a <Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution>.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/12.5 Collision Theory>[§12.5, Activation Energy and the Arrhenius Equation: energy distributions and temperature sensitivity]{ref}

<Activation energy> quantifies how strongly a <rate constant> depends on <thermodynamic temperature>. For a positive <rate constant> $k(T)$, the empirical definition is
$$
E_a(T)=RT^2\frac{d}{dT}\ln\frac{k(T)}{k_{\mathrm{ref}}},
$$
where $R$ is the <molar gas constant> and $k_{\mathrm{ref}}>0$ is a fixed reference with the same units as $k$. The ratio is a <dimensionless quantity>, keeping the <natural logarithm> meaningful without changing its <derivative>. Thus $E_a$ is a molar <energy>, commonly in $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$ or $\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The <Arrhenius equation> makes $E_a$ constant over its assumed range. This rate-based definition, and the need for care when identifying it with a threshold on a <potential energy surface>, are stated in the <IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/activation energy>.

In a simple barrier picture, reaching a <transition state> costs <energy> relative to the starting <reactants>. The height of that barrier is different from the difference between the <reactants> and <reaction products>. An energetically favourable endpoint can still be reached slowly. An <energy profile (chemistry)> makes the distinction visible, while an <Arrhenius plot> tests temperature sensitivity from <rate constants>. A measured $E_a$ need not equal a barrier read from an arbitrarily chosen diagram.

For a computed example, assume a <first-order reaction> has $k(300\,\mathrm K)=0.100\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, constant $E_a=80\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, and a temperature-independent <pre-exponential factor>. The <Arrhenius equation> gives
$$
\frac{k(310\,\mathrm K)}{k(300\,\mathrm K)}
=\exp\left[\frac{80000}{8.314462618}\left(\frac1{300}-\frac1{310}\right)\right]
\approx2.81398.
$$
Hence $k(310\,\mathrm K)\approx0.281398\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. Its <half-life> falls from $\ln2/0.100\approx6.93147\,\mathrm s$ to $\ln2/0.281398\approx2.46322\,\mathrm s$. These are chosen model parameters, not measurements of an identified <chemical reaction>. The exponent uses <kelvin>, not unconverted Celsius temperatures, and $80000\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$ must match $R$ in $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$.

\Image[activation-energy-potential-barriers.png]
{id=image-activation-energy-potential-barriers}
{title=Synthetic <potential-energy profiles>: starting levels determine directional <reaction energy barriers>}
{description=Original chosen profiles have <reactant> level $0$, <reaction product> level $-20\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, and peak levels $80$ and $50\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Forward <reaction energy barriers> are $80$ and $50$; reverse <reaction energy barriers> are $100$ and $70\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The <reaction coordinate> is not time. These curves are illustrative functions, not laboratory measurements or calculated molecular <potential energy surfaces>; their heights do not by themselves specify <Arrhenius activation energies>.}

A microscopic <reaction energy barrier> belongs to a specified path on a <potential energy surface>. An experimental <activation energy> belongs to a specified <rate constant> and its <temperature> dependence. A multi-step <reaction mechanism> can change which step controls the observed conversion, and a temperature-dependent <pre-exponential factor> contributes to the observed <activation energy>. Even the <Gibbs energy of activation> of <transition state theory> is a different quantity: it incorporates <entropy> as well as <enthalpy> and depends on the <standard states>. These distinctions prevent a fitted <Arrhenius plot> from being mistaken for a direct microscopic energy measurement.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/activation energy>{ref}

<Catalysis> illustrates the separation between a path and its endpoints. At the same <temperature> and with the same <reactants>, <reaction products>, and <standard states>, a regenerated <catalyst> changes the available <reaction mechanism> and <rate constants>, while the overall standard change in <Gibbs free energy> and the <equilibrium constant> remain the same:
$$
K=\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta_rG^\circ}{RT}\right).
$$
For a reversible <first-order reaction> pair $\mathrm A\rightleftharpoons\mathrm B$, consistency at <chemical equilibrium> requires $k_f/k_r=K$. A model that multiplies only $k_f$ by a catalytic speedup while holding $k_r$ fixed would change this ratio and misrepresent <catalysis>. This simple ratio applies to that specified <rate law>; a multi-step catalytic <reaction mechanism> needs its own rate equations. A <potential-energy profile> alone cannot determine $K$, because its endpoint difference is not generally the standard change in <Gibbs free energy>.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/13.3 Shifting Equilibria: Le Châtelier’s Principle>[§13.3, Effect of a Catalyst: faster equilibration without changing equilibrium composition]{ref}

= Apparent activation energy
{parent=Activation energy}
{title2=$E_{a,\mathrm{app}}(T)=-R\,d\ln(k/k_{\mathrm{ref}})/d(1/T)$}

An <apparent activation energy> reports the <temperature> sensitivity of an observed <rate constant>, without assuming that it equals one microscopic <reaction energy barrier>. The <chain rule> converts the empirical <activation energy> definition into the local slope of an <Arrhenius plot>:
$$
E_{a,\mathrm{app}}(T)=-R\frac{d\ln(k/k_{\mathrm{ref}})}{d(1/T)}.
$$
The fixed reference $k_{\mathrm{ref}}$ keeps the <natural logarithm> dimensionless. A fitted straight-line slope estimates this quantity over the chosen <temperature> interval; curvature means that one number does not describe every local slope.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/activation energy>{ref}

For the explicit model $k(T)=A(T)\exp[-E_0/(RT)]$ with constant exponent parameter $E_0$, differentiation gives
$$
E_{a,\mathrm{app}}(T)=E_0+RT^2\frac{d}{dT}\ln\frac{A(T)}{A(T_*)},
$$
where $T_*$ is fixed. The second term demonstrates why a microscopic interpretation needs more than a slope. If $E_0$ were itself allowed to depend on <temperature>, differentiation would also contribute $-T\,dE_0/dT$; simply inserting a variable $E_0(T)$ and naming it the measured <activation energy> is generally incorrect.

An instructive <transition state theory> example for an effective <first-order reaction> is
$$
k(T)=\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{h}\exp\left(\frac{\Delta S^{\ddagger\circ}}{R}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta H^{\ddagger\circ}}{RT}\right).
$$
Here $k_{\mathrm B}$ and $h$ are the <Boltzmann constant> and <Planck constant>; $\Delta S^{\ddagger\circ}$ is the <entropy of activation>, and $\Delta H^{\ddagger\circ}$ the <enthalpy of activation>. With those two quantities constant over the chosen range and successful passage assumed, direct differentiation gives $E_{a,\mathrm{app}}=\Delta H^{\ddagger\circ}+RT$, while the <Gibbs energy of activation> is $\Delta G^{\ddagger\circ}=\Delta H^{\ddagger\circ}-T\Delta S^{\ddagger\circ}$. These are three distinguishable quantities. The stated <first-order reaction> and fixed <standard states> matter when comparing these formulas with measurements.<Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry (IUPAC Recommendations 1994): E entries>{ref}

= Arrhenius equation
{c}
{parent=Activation energy}
{wiki}
{title2=$k(T)=A\exp[-E_a/(RT)]$}

The <Arrhenius equation> models the <temperature> dependence of a <rate constant> by
$$
k(T)=A\exp\left(-\frac{E_a}{RT}\right),\qquad A>0,\quad T>0.
$$
Here $A$ is the <pre-exponential factor>, $E_a$ the <activation energy>, $R$ the <molar gas constant>, and $T$ the absolute <thermodynamic temperature>. In its original form, $A$ and $E_a$ are held constant over the range being modeled. It is an empirical relationship, not a universal rule for every <reaction mechanism>. See the <IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/Arrhenius equation>.

Eliminating $A$ between two positive <temperatures> gives the useful prediction
$$
\ln\frac{k(T_2)}{k(T_1)}
=\frac{E_a}{R}\left(\frac1{T_1}-\frac1{T_2}\right).
$$
For $E_a>0$, warming increases $k$. The logarithmic sensitivity is $d\ln(k/k_{\mathrm{ref}})/dT=E_a/(RT^2)$, so there is no universal doubling per ten degrees. The same $10\,\mathrm K$ rise from $300$ to $310\,\mathrm K$ gives a factor $1.67749$ for $E_a=40\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, versus $2.81398$ for $80\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$.

\Image[arrhenius-temperature-sensitivity.png]
{title=Temperature sensitivity and straight-line <Arrhenius plots> for two illustrative <activation energies>}
{description=Both models are anchored at $k(300\,\mathrm K)=0.100\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, so they have different <pre-exponential factors>. Left: the higher <activation energy> produces a larger relative change with <temperature>. Right: the <natural logarithm> of the dimensionless ratio $k(T)/k(300\,\mathrm K)$ is linear in inverse <temperature>. These exact model curves are not experimental data.}

The useful simplification is that a strong change in <reaction rate> becomes a straight-line relation after changing variables. Measurements at several <temperatures> can estimate the slope and test whether one pair of constants describes the chosen range. Curvature can indicate a failure of that approximation; fitting a line does not uniquely establish a microscopic <reaction mechanism>.

Svante Arrhenius's 1889 paper on <sucrose> conversion through <catalysis> by a <chemical acid> compared the strong observed <temperature> dependence with the much smaller increase in collision frequency. He discussed van't Hoff's earlier <temperature> relations and proposed a small population of hypothetical active <sucrose>, replenished from ordinary <sucrose>, to interpret the dependence. The inspected translated excerpt derives an inverse-<temperature> relation in its equations (6)–(7) and then applies it to the <reaction rate>. That historical hypothesis is distinct from a modern <transition state> description. See the <On the Reaction Velocity of the Inversion of Cane Sugar by Acids>. The excerpt omits parts of the original paper; it does not provide a complete original apparatus description or dataset.

The <pre-exponential factor> must have the same units as the <rate constant>, since the <exponential function> supplies a <dimensionless quantity>. For a <rate law> $v=k[\mathrm A]^m$, both $A$ and $k$ have units $(\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}})^{1-m}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. Thus a <first-order reaction> uses $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, whereas a <second-order reaction> uses $\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. Calling $A$ a frequency factor does not give it frequency units for every <order of reaction>. Its value includes the physical and geometrical factors allowed by the chosen <reaction mechanism>; equal <activation energies> need not mean equal <reaction rates>.

\Image[arrhenius-apparent-energy-and-prefactor.png]
{id=image-arrhenius-apparent-energy-and-prefactor}
{title=Synthetic <Arrhenius plots>: a constant <pre-exponential factor> and a temperature-dependent <pre-exponential factor>}
{description=Both chosen <first-order reaction> models have $k(300\,\mathrm K)=0.100\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and the same exponent parameter $E_0=60\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Blue uses a constant <pre-exponential factor>; orange multiplies it by $(T/300\,\mathrm K)^2$. All markers are synthetic evaluations, not observations. Left: <natural logarithm> of $k/k(300\,\mathrm K)$ against $1000\,\mathrm K/T$. Right: the corresponding <apparent activation energies>, $60$ and $60+2RT/1000$ in $\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The dashed orange tangent at $300\,\mathrm K$ has numerical slope $-7.81634$ on the displayed horizontal scale.}

To read the blue <Arrhenius plot>, define the <dimensionless quantity> $X=1000\,\mathrm K/T$ and $y=\ln[k/k(300\,\mathrm K)]$. Its numerical slope is $m_X=-60\,000/(1000R)\approx-7.21634$, giving $E_a=-(1000\,\mathrm K)Rm_X=60\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The slope against $1/T$, by contrast, is $-7216.34\,\mathrm K$. The orange tangent at $300\,\mathrm K$ gives $E_{a,\mathrm{app}}\approx64.9887\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$; its finite-interval slope between $280$ and $340\,\mathrm K$ gives about $65.1227\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The distinction between a local <derivative> and a fitted interval matters even with perfectly noise-free synthetic evaluations.

Two measured positive <rate constants> give a two-point estimate
$$
E_{a,\mathrm{fit}}=R\frac{\ln[k(T_2)/k(T_1)]}{1/T_1-1/T_2}.
$$
Several <temperatures> let <linear regression> test the constant-parameter approximation: inspect the <regression residuals>, keep the same <rate law> and composition convention, and account for uncertainty in both $k$ and $T$. A straight line over a short range supports a useful local approximation; it does not uniquely identify a <reaction mechanism>. A fixed change of units or of $k_{\mathrm{ref}}$ shifts the intercept without changing the inferred <activation energy>.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/12.5 Collision Theory>[§12.5, Example 12.13 and alternate two-point approach: estimating activation energy from a slope]{ref}

= Pre-exponential factor
{parent=Arrhenius equation}
{wiki}
{title2=$A$}

= Arrhenius prefactor
{c}
{synonym}

= Frequency factor
{synonym}

The <pre-exponential factor> multiplies the <exponential function> in the <Arrhenius equation>. It has the same units as the <rate constant>, so for a <first-order reaction> both have units $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. Its physical interpretation depends on the <reaction mechanism>; an assumed constant $A$ should not be identified with a universal collision frequency. Comparing <activation energies> alone does not rank absolute <rate constants> when the <pre-exponential factors> differ. See the <IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/pre-exponential factor>.

= Arrhenius plot
{c}
{parent=Arrhenius equation}
{wiki}
{title2=$\ln(k/k_{\mathrm{ref}})\ \text{against}\ 1/T$}

An <Arrhenius plot> places $\ln(k/k_{\mathrm{ref}})$ against $1/T$, using a fixed reference <rate constant> with the same units as $k$. Under the constant-parameter <Arrhenius equation>,
$$
\ln\frac{k(T)}{k_{\mathrm{ref}}}
=\ln\frac{A}{k_{\mathrm{ref}}}-\frac{E_a}{R}\frac1T.
$$
The slope is $-E_a/R$ and the intercept is $\ln(A/k_{\mathrm{ref}})$. Changing the reference units shifts the intercept but leaves the slope unchanged. If the horizontal coordinate is displayed in $10^{-3}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}$, its numerical slope must be rescaled before multiplying by $-R$. A temperature-dependent <pre-exponential factor> or <activation energy> can make the plot curved.

= Modified Arrhenius equation
{c}
{parent=Arrhenius equation}
{title2=$k(T)=A_* (T/T_*)^n\exp[-E_0/(RT)]$}

The <modified Arrhenius equation> allows a power of <thermodynamic temperature> in the <pre-exponential factor>:
$$
k(T)=A_*\left(\frac{T}{T_*}\right)^n\exp\left(-\frac{E_0}{RT}\right),
\qquad T_*>0.
$$
Here $A_*$, $E_0$, $n$, and $T_*$ are constant model parameters. Using the <dimensionless quantity> $T/T_*$ leaves $A_*$ with the same units as $k$; in the equivalent convention $BT^n\exp[-E_0/(RT)]$, $B$ instead has units of $k$ multiplied by $\mathrm K^{-n}$. The power exponent $n$ here is not an <order of reaction>. The IUPAC definition uses that equivalent $BT^n$ convention.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/modified Arrhenius equation>{ref}

Applying the <apparent activation energy> definition yields the original short calculation
$$
\frac{d\ln(k/k_{\mathrm{ref}})}{dT}=\frac nT+\frac{E_0}{RT^2},
\qquad E_{a,\mathrm{app}}(T)=E_0+nRT.
$$
For the orange synthetic <Arrhenius plot>, $n=2$, $T_*=300\,\mathrm K$, and $E_0=60\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Its <pre-exponential factor> changes by $(310/300)^2\approx1.06778$ from $300$ to $310\,\mathrm K$; combined with the <exponential function>, $k(310\,\mathrm K)\approx0.231991\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, compared with $0.217266\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$ for the constant-<pre-exponential-factor> model. The diagram uses those models only to reveal the difference between an exponent parameter and a slope-derived <activation energy>.

= Conductometric determination of activation energy
{parent=Arrhenius equation}

A concrete laboratory route to an <Arrhenius plot> is to infer a <rate constant> at each controlled <temperature> from a changing <electrical conductivity>. The Technical University of Munich's <Laboratory Course in Physical Chemistry for Fundamental Studies: Experiments Manual/Activation Energy of a First-Order Reaction> supplies a teaching protocol, rather than a published set of measured <rate constants>.

The manual follows <tertiary amylbromide> conversion, which produces conducting <ions>. A measuring cell connects to a thermostat; an immersed <electrical conductivity> probe also measures solution <temperature>, and a magnetic stirrer mixes it. Use $80\,\mathrm{mL}$ of $80\%$ by volume <ethanol> in <water>, equilibrate, then add $0.5\,\mathrm{mL}$ of <tertiary amylbromide>. Record the baseline before addition and set time zero at addition. Repeat with fresh mixtures at $25$, $30$, and $35\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, each within $\pm0.5\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$; record for $30$ minutes at the first two <temperatures> and until completion at the last. Clean the cell between runs and keep the added volume fixed. The completed $35\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ mixture supplies endpoint <electrical conductivity> measurements at the other <temperatures>: one endpoint reading cannot be used unchanged at every <temperature>.<Laboratory Course in Physical Chemistry for Fundamental Studies: Experiments Manual/Activation Energy of a First-Order Reaction>[pp. 82–84, §§8.2.5–8.3.2, and p. 85, §8.5.1: reaction, apparatus, temperatures, endpoint and baseline]{ref}

To see what is inferred from the readout, let $\sigma_0$, $\sigma(t)$, and $\sigma_\infty$ be the baseline, current, and completed-mixture <electrical conductivities> at the same <temperature>. Assume their baseline-subtracted changes are proportional to converted <concentration>. Combining that measurement model with a <first-order reaction> gives
$$
f(t)=\frac{\sigma_\infty-\sigma(t)}{\sigma_\infty-\sigma_0}
=\frac{c(t)}{c_0}=e^{-kt},
\qquad -\ln f(t)=kt.
$$
Thus the <electrical conductivity> increases toward an endpoint, while the remaining <reactant> fraction decreases. Fit $-\ln f$ against time to obtain $k$; then fit $\ln(k/k_{\mathrm{ref}})$ against $1/T$ in <kelvin> to estimate an <apparent activation energy>. This baseline-aware derivation states the measurement assumptions explicitly; it does not infer a <rate constant> from the steepness of a raw conductivity trace alone.

The controls distinguish a faster <chemical reaction> from a larger instrumental response. Measure the actual solution <temperature>, retain the same <solvent> composition and initial <reactant> <concentration>, and compare endpoint and baseline at that <temperature>. As further diagnostic checks, repeat runs and inspect <regression residuals> for departure from a <first-order reaction>. These checks follow from the model: <electrical conductivity> also depends on how <ions> move, a baseline drift can mimic conversion, and a mixing delay can distort early points. Near completion, a small endpoint error produces a large relative error in $f$ and its <natural logarithm>. A successful fit supports an apparent rate model for this <solvent> and interval; it does not directly image a <transition state> or identify a unique <reaction mechanism>. No measured $k$ or $E_a$ is supplied here.

= Reaction mechanism
{parent=Chemical kinetics}
{wiki}

A <reaction mechanism> proposes the sequence of <elementary reactions> that produces an overall <chemical reaction>. Its predicted <rate law> must agree with observations; matching one <rate law> need not uniquely identify a <reaction mechanism>. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/12-6-reaction-mechanisms[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §12.6: reaction mechanisms].

= Reaction intermediate
{parent=Reaction mechanism}
{wiki}

A <reaction intermediate> is formed in one step of a <reaction mechanism> and consumed in a later step, cancelling from the net <chemical equation>. A <reaction intermediate> can connect the transformations in <energy coupling>; it is distinct from a <transition state>. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/I03096[IUPAC Gold Book: intermediate].

= Reaction coordinate
{parent=Reaction mechanism}
{wiki}
{title2=$q$}

A <reaction coordinate> parametrizes changes in molecular configuration along a <chemical reaction> path. It can involve a <bond length>, a <bond angle>, or a combination of geometric changes. It is not elapsed time and is not the bulk <extent of reaction>: one describes configurations, while the other accounts for how much material has converted. The precise choice matters when interpreting an <energy profile (chemistry)>. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/R05168[IUPAC Gold Book: reaction coordinate].

= Potential energy surface
{parent=Reaction coordinate}
{wiki}
{title2=$V(\mathbf R_1,\ldots,\mathbf R_N)$}

A <potential energy surface> assigns <potential energy> to the configurations of the reacting <chemical atoms>, for a specified electronic description. A <reaction coordinate> selects a path through this many-dimensional configuration space; a <potential-energy profile> then records the <potential energy> along that path. Different paths can share endpoints but cross different barriers. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/P04780[IUPAC Gold Book: potential-energy reaction surface].

= Energy profile
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Reaction coordinate}
{wiki}

= Reaction energy profile
{synonym}

An <energy profile (chemistry)> plots an explicitly specified <energy> against a <reaction coordinate>. Identify whether the vertical axis is <potential energy>, <enthalpy>, or <Gibbs free energy>; these descriptions are related but not interchangeable. A <potential-energy profile> describes a path through configurations, while a free-energy description also accounts for the relevant thermodynamic conditions.

\Image[reaction-barriers-and-endpoints.png]
{title=Two schematic <potential-energy profiles> share endpoints but have different barriers}
{description=The reactant level is $0$, the product level is $-20\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, and the two maxima are $80$ and $50\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. These chosen curves illustrate endpoint differences and barrier heights; they are not measured surfaces, trajectories in time, or a complete catalytic cycle.}

For the two chosen profiles, the <reaction energy barriers> from the <reactants> are $80$ and $50\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. From the <reaction products>, the same maxima are $100$ and $70\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ higher. Both paths have endpoint difference $-20\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The barrier is measured from the relevant starting endpoint, not from an arbitrary zero of <potential energy>.

The appeal of the diagram is that it separates two useful questions: which endpoint is lower, and which path is easier to traverse? <Catalysis> changes the route through <transition states> while retaining the endpoint thermodynamics of the same overall <chemical reaction>. A real catalytic route may have several steps and <reaction intermediates>; the two simple curves do not specify such a <reaction mechanism>. The plotted potential-energy difference alone is not an <equilibrium constant> or a calculated <rate constant>. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/12-7-catalysis[Chemistry 2e, 12.7: alternative reaction routes and catalysis].

= Potential-energy profile
{parent=Energy profile (chemistry)}

A <potential-energy profile> plots the <potential energy> of the reacting system along a specified path from <reactants> through a <transition state> to <reaction products>. It is a particular kind of <energy profile (chemistry)>. For an <elementary reaction>, a suitable <reaction coordinate> parametrizes the path; a multi-step <reaction mechanism> needs the coordinates of its successive steps. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/P04779[IUPAC Gold Book: potential-energy profile].

= Reaction energy barrier
{parent=Energy profile (chemistry)}
{title2=$\Delta E^\ddagger=E_{\mathrm{peak}}-E_{\mathrm{start}}$}

A <reaction energy barrier> is the height of a specified peak relative to the starting state in an <energy profile (chemistry)>. State which <energy> is used and which direction is being considered. A schematic barrier is not automatically the empirical <activation energy> obtained from the temperature dependence of a <rate constant>.

= Elementary reaction
{parent=Reaction mechanism}
{wiki}

An <elementary reaction> is a single step in a <reaction mechanism>. The <law of mass action> models its directional <reaction rate> using <reactant> <concentrations> raised to the step's stoichiometric multiplicities. Applying that rule requires evidence that the step is elementary; a net <chemical equation> alone supplies no such evidence.

= Molecularity
{parent=Elementary reaction}
{wiki}

<Molecularity> counts the reacting entities in one <elementary reaction>: one for a unimolecular step, two for a bimolecular step. An entity can be a <molecule>, <ion>, or <chemical atom>. <Solvent> entities involved only through <solvation> are excluded. <Molecularity> describes a microscopic event; <order of reaction> describes the <concentration> dependence of a <rate law>. Assigning <molecularity> to a net multi-step <chemical equation> is therefore inappropriate.<Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry (IUPAC Recommendations 1994): M entries by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry, IUPAC Recommendations 1994, entry “molecularity”: elementary event and exclusion of solvation medium]{ref}

= Catalysis
{parent=Chemical kinetics}
{wiki}

<Catalysis> accelerates a <chemical reaction> by providing an alternative <reaction mechanism> with a more accessible barrier through its <transition states>. A <catalyst> participates in the route and is regenerated. At fixed <temperature>, <catalysis> changes how quickly <chemical equilibrium> is reached without changing the <equilibrium constant>; it does not make an unfavourable overall change in <Gibbs free energy> favourable. <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others>[§12.7, opening definition and Figure 12.19: alternative mechanisms and regeneration]{ref}<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/13.3 Shifting Equilibria: Le Châtelier’s Principle>[§13.3, Effect of a Catalyst: equilibrium composition is unchanged]{ref}

In his 1909 lecture, <Wilhelm Ostwald> recalled the introduction of the term <catalysis> by <Jöns Jacob Berzelius> and emphasized measurable acceleration rather than merely naming an unexplained force. He also discussed <reaction intermediates> and participation of the <catalyst> in the steps whose sum gives the net <chemical reaction>. This is a participant's retrospective account, not evidence that every catalytic <reaction mechanism> is the same.<Nobel Lectures, Chemistry 1901–1921/On Catalysis>[On Catalysis, lecture dated 12 December 1909; paragraphs beginning “The factor which Berzelius regarded” and “It is appropriate here”; editorial footnote says the lecture was reconstructed from notes]{ref}

= Catalytic cycle
{parent=Catalysis}
{wiki}

A <catalytic cycle> follows the changing forms of a <catalyst> through a <reaction mechanism> until its starting form is regenerated. The schematic binding cycle in <catalyst> illustrates the bookkeeping: C is consumed and recovered, whereas AC is a <reaction intermediate> formed and consumed. Cancelling both gives the net A-to-B <chemical equation>. Regeneration concerns the completed cycle; it does not imply that C avoids forming <chemical bonds> or exchanging <electrons>. An alternative <reaction mechanism> may have more <elementary reactions> than the uncatalysed route and still be faster.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others>[§12.7, Figure 12.19 and Homogeneous Catalysts: alternative steps, intermediate formation and catalyst regeneration]{ref}

For that reversible binding cycle, ideal <thermodynamic activities> give step <equilibrium constants> $K_1=a_{\mathrm{AC}}/(a_{\mathrm A}a_{\mathrm C})$ and $K_2=a_{\mathrm B}a_{\mathrm C}/a_{\mathrm{AC}}$. Their <product> is $K_1K_2=a_{\mathrm B}/a_{\mathrm A}=K$: the same cancellations occur in the equilibrium bookkeeping. Choose consistent <standard states> for the species. A proposed cycle with $K_1K_2\ne K$ for the same net conversion at the same <temperature> would contradict its specified <thermodynamics>. This statement concerns free-species <thermodynamic activities>. Appreciable binding in AC can change how the conserved total amount is partitioned among free A, free B and bound AC. An assay counting bound and free material together therefore needs that additional balance; unchanged $a_{\mathrm B}/a_{\mathrm A}$ does not by itself guarantee identical analytical totals when the amount of C changes.

= Catalytic barriers and reaction free energy
{parent=Catalysis}

A directional <Gibbs energy of activation> measures the climb from a starting state to a <transition state>. The net standard reaction change in <Gibbs free energy> measures the difference between endpoints. Changing the climb can strongly change a <rate constant> while leaving that endpoint difference intact. For two illustrative single-barrier routes with common endpoint levels $G_{\mathrm A}^\circ$ and $G_{\mathrm B}^\circ$,
$$
\Delta G_f^{\ddagger\circ}=G_{\mathrm{TS}}^\circ-G_{\mathrm A}^\circ,
\qquad
\Delta G_r^{\ddagger\circ}=G_{\mathrm{TS}}^\circ-G_{\mathrm B}^\circ,
\qquad
\Delta G_f^{\ddagger\circ}-\Delta G_r^{\ddagger\circ}=\Delta_rG^\circ.
$$
Lowering the common <transition state> level lowers both directional <Gibbs energies of activation>; it does not lower just the forward climb. The usual statement that a <catalyst> lowers an <activation energy> describes a kinetic route, not a change in the net <Gibbs free energy>. An <activation energy> inferred from an <Arrhenius plot> is also distinct from a <Gibbs energy of activation> at one <temperature>.

\Image[catalysis-barriers-and-net-free-energy.png]
{id=image-catalysis-barriers-and-net-free-energy}
{title=Illustrative <catalysis> schematic: different barriers, identical endpoint <Gibbs free energy> difference}
{description=Chosen levels in $\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$: A plus regenerated C at $0$, B plus regenerated C at $-3$, and schematic maxima at $12$ and $7$. Forward barriers are $12$ and $7$; reverse barriers are $15$ and $10$. Neither curve is a measured molecular path. The <reaction coordinate> is not time. These values illustrate the barrier bookkeeping and do not parametrize the separate <reversible catalytic relaxation> example.}

With a common prefactor in <transition state theory>, subtracting the directional barriers yields
$$
\frac{k_f}{k_r}=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\Delta_rG^\circ}{RT}\right)=K,
$$
where $R$ is the <molar gas constant> and $T$ is the absolute <temperature>. This particular <rate-constant> ratio requires the simple compatible directional <rate laws> and consistent <standard states>; a general multistep <reaction mechanism> does not supply two arbitrary measured <rate constants> whose ratio must equal $K$. At fixed <temperature> a <catalyst> preserves $K$ for the same net conversion. The actual reaction change in <Gibbs free energy>, $\Delta_rG=\Delta_rG^\circ+RT\ln Q$, is likewise unchanged at the same <reaction quotient> $Q$. Composition changes during the faster approach to <chemical equilibrium>, so comparing mixtures at the same elapsed time need not compare the same $Q$.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/13.3 Shifting Equilibria: Le Châtelier’s Principle>[§13.3, Effect of a Catalyst: both directional rates accelerate while equilibrium composition is unchanged]{ref}

= Reversible catalytic relaxation
{parent=Catalysis}
{title2=$x(t)=x_{\mathrm{eq}}+(x_0-x_{\mathrm{eq}})e^{-t/\tau}$}

Here is an explicitly stipulated elementary kinetic model, not experimental data. A well-mixed ideal <closed system> has constant <temperature> and <volume>. Alongside an uncatalysed <elementary reaction>, allow an illustrative catalytic encounter that returns C immediately:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm A&\rightleftharpoons\mathrm B,
& k_f^u&=0.030\,\mathrm{s^{-1}},
& k_r^u&=0.010\,\mathrm{s^{-1}},\\
\mathrm A+\mathrm C&\rightleftharpoons\mathrm B+\mathrm C,
& \alpha_f&=270\,\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}},
& \alpha_r&=90\,\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Both channels obey the <law of mass action>. In the second, the forward <reaction rate> is $\alpha_f[\mathrm A][\mathrm C]$ and the reverse <reaction rate> is $\alpha_r[\mathrm B][\mathrm C]$. C cancels in each encounter, so its free <concentration> $c_C$ stays constant without an external supply. This toy <elementary reaction> encodes regeneration without binding; it is not a claim that real <catalytic cycles> universally consist of one encounter. A binding cycle can accumulate a <reaction intermediate>, in which case constant total C does not imply constant free $c_C$.

Adding the parallel <reaction rates> gives effective <first-order reaction> <rate constants>
$$
k_f=k_f^u+\alpha_f c_C,
\qquad k_r=k_r^u+\alpha_r c_C.
$$
Because $k_f^u/k_r^u=\alpha_f/\alpha_r=3$, each channel balances at the same $[\mathrm B]/[\mathrm A]=3$, and so does their sum. At $c_C=0$ the pair is $(0.030,0.010)\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$; at $c_C=0.001\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ it is $(0.300,0.100)\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. Both give $K=3$ with ideal <thermodynamic activities> and a common concentration <standard state>. At $T=300\,\mathrm K$ their compatible standard reaction change in <Gibbs free energy> is $\Delta_rG^\circ=-RT\ln3\approx-2.7403\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Choosing an unrelated reverse <rate constant> would break this consistency.

Let $S=[\mathrm A]+[\mathrm B]$ be the conserved <concentration>, and define the <dimensionless quantity> $x=[\mathrm B]/S$. Then
$$
\frac{dx}{dt}=k_f(1-x)-k_rx
=-(k_f+k_r)(x-x_{\mathrm{eq}}),
\qquad
x_{\mathrm{eq}}=\frac{k_f}{k_f+k_r}=\frac34.
$$
Solving this <first-order linear differential equation> gives
$$
x(t)=\frac34+\left(x_0-\frac34\right)e^{-(k_f+k_r)t},
\qquad
\tau=\frac{1}{k_f+k_r}.
$$
The <chemical relaxation time> is $25\,\mathrm s$ without C and $2.5\,\mathrm s$ with the chosen C. From either $x_0=0$ or $x_0=1$, the <exponential decay> of the deviation leads to the same <chemical equilibrium> $x=3/4$. Reducing that deviation to one percent takes $\ln(100)\tau$: about $115.13\,\mathrm s$ versus $11.51\,\mathrm s$.

\Image[catalysis-reversible-equilibrium-relaxation.png]
{id=image-catalysis-reversible-equilibrium-relaxation}
{title=Two initial compositions and two kinetic speeds approach the same <chemical equilibrium>}
{description=Exact curves from the stipulated <reversible catalytic relaxation> model. Solid curves include $0.001\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ of C; dashed curves omit C. Blue starts entirely as A and orange entirely as B. Both panels use the same equations; the right panel resolves the first $15\,\mathrm s$. The common dotted line is $[\mathrm B]/([\mathrm A]+[\mathrm B])=3/4$, not a fitted plateau or an experimental measurement.}

At this <chemical equilibrium>, conversion continues in both directions. For $S=1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, the uncatalysed channel has equal forward and reverse <reaction rates> $0.0075\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. Adding C gives total directional <reaction rates> $0.075\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$ each, yet zero net <reaction rate>. Faster molecular exchange and unchanged composition coexist. This exact calculation explains how <catalysis> can matter enormously within an observation window while leaving the final <equilibrium constant> intact.

= Catalytic selectivity
{parent=Catalysis}

<Catalytic selectivity> concerns which <reaction products> form when competing <reaction mechanisms> are available. Faster conversion is valuable when a process would otherwise be impractically slow; a suitable <catalyst> can also favour a useful product within the available time. In an illustrative pair of irreversible <first-order reactions> A to B and A to D, the initial fraction of converted A going to B is $k_B/(k_B+k_D)$. Multiplying both <rate constants> by the same factor changes speed but leaves this fraction unchanged; changing their relative values changes the kinetic product split. This is a finite-time kinetic illustration, not a new <equilibrium constant>.

A <catalyst> need not accelerate every competing <chemical reaction> equally. <Enzymes>, for example, can distinguish <substrates> through their <active sites>.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others>[§12.7, Enzyme Structure and Function, Figure 12.25: substrate binding and specificity]{ref} If every relevant reversible channel eventually equilibrates in a <closed system>, its final <chemical equilibrium> still obeys the endpoint <thermodynamics>. Selective acceleration, product removal, or external driving can make an observed product distribution depend on time and operating conditions. A claim of improved yield must specify those conditions.

= Catalyst deactivation
{parent=Catalysis}

<Catalyst deactivation> is loss of catalytic activity during use. Regeneration in an ideal <catalytic cycle> does not rule out a separate damaging <chemical reaction>, blocking of a surface, or physical loss of <catalyst> from the apparatus. A route can also be slowed by <diffusion> to the surface or by accumulation of a <reaction intermediate>. Such effects limit the usefulness of a low schematic <reaction energy barrier>: measured throughput depends on the whole <reaction mechanism> and on transport. Demonstrating regeneration requires more evidence than observing a faster first run. For a recovered solid <catalyst>, a useful further comparison is a second run with fresh <reactants>, matched recovered amount, and the same mixing and <temperature>, alongside a fresh-solid run and a blank. Loss during recovery or residual <reactants> can otherwise imitate <catalyst deactivation> or apparent reuse. This is a proposed control, not an observation reported for the <catalysed peroxide comparison>.

= Homogeneous catalysis
{parent=Catalysis}
{wiki}

In <homogeneous catalysis>, the <catalyst> and <reactants> occupy the same <thermodynamic phase>.

= Heterogeneous catalysis
{parent=Catalysis}
{wiki}

In <heterogeneous catalysis>, the <catalyst> and <reactants> occupy different <thermodynamic phases>; a solid surface can furnish the catalytic route.

= Catalysed peroxide comparison
{parent=Heterogeneous catalysis}

A simple motivating comparison is visible gas evolution from <hydrogen peroxide>, whose net <chemical equation> is
$$
2\mathrm{H_2O_2}\longrightarrow2\mathrm{H_2O}+\mathrm{O_2}.
$$
The products are <water> and <oxygen>. The Royal Society of Chemistry's teaching resource uses small test tubes and droppers, <hydrogen peroxide> labelled 5–10 volumes, solutions of metal salts including <copper(II) sulfate>, and approximately $1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ <sodium hydroxide>. Its qualitative procedure tests additions singly and in pairs to tubes containing about $1\,\mathrm{cm}$ depth of <hydrogen peroxide>. It reports the expected classroom result that the additions individually are not effective under those conditions, whereas the solids produced by mixing metal-salt solution with <sodium hydroxide> give effective <heterogeneous catalysis>. This is the resource's expected observation, not a newly measured ranking.<The best catalyst to decompose hydrogen peroxide by Royal Society of Chemistry>[Resources and Results: small test tubes, droppers, peroxide strength, single versus paired additions, visual readout and expected precipitate effect]{ref}

A focused adaptation compares four matched tubes: no addition, <copper(II) sulfate> alone, <sodium hydroxide> alone, and the pair. Use equal initial <hydrogen peroxide> amounts, identical tubes and the same starting <temperature>. Record stock <concentrations> and added volumes; add <water> to the other tubes to match the total added liquid. Additions can first be checked without <hydrogen peroxide> to distinguish <precipitation (chemistry)> from gas evolution. Start a timer at contact, mix consistently, and observe gas evolution over the same interval. These matching controls are proposed improvements to the qualitative resource, not claimed details of its original trials. Follow its supervised chemical-handling requirements; the stated strengths describe its teaching protocol rather than an unrestricted recipe.

The immediate readout is relative bubbling, together with whether a solid appears. More vigorous bubbling in the paired tube supports faster conversion under the comparison conditions; it does not identify the solid's exact composition or establish a unique <reaction mechanism>. Bubble counts are not a calibrated <reaction rate>: bubble sizes, gas retention and warming can differ. For a quantitative extension, use a freely moving <gas syringe> to record collected <volume> $V(t)$ and compare early slopes $dV/dt$ at matched <temperature> and <pressure>; check for leaks and repeat runs. This extension is a measurement proposal, not source-reported gas traces.

The paired treatment also changes <pH> and produces a surface, so the four tubes alone cannot assign the whole effect to one microscopic catalytic step. A filtered-liquid comparison and a separated, washed-solid comparison would help distinguish dissolved and solid contributions, although washing itself can change the active material. More gas at one time establishes neither a changed <equilibrium constant> nor indefinite <catalyst> reuse. The open gas-evolution experiment is especially unsuitable as a demonstration that two closed mixtures reach the same <chemical equilibrium>: the escaping <oxygen> changes the constraints. The reversible model above supplies that separate explanation. No numerical experimental <rate constants>, barrier heights or gas volumes are asserted here.

= Catalyst
{parent=Catalysis}

A <catalyst> makes a <chemical reaction> proceed faster by participating in an alternative <reaction mechanism> and being regenerated. This makes repeated conversion possible: the same <catalyst> can help many successive <reactants> become <reaction products>. An <enzyme> is a biological <catalyst>. The overall standard change in <Gibbs free energy> is unchanged; see the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C00876[IUPAC Gold Book: catalyst].

Regeneration does not mean that the <catalyst> sits unchanged throughout every step. For example, a schematic <reaction mechanism> can temporarily bind A to a <catalyst> C in a <reaction intermediate> AC:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm A+\mathrm C&\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{AC},\\
\mathrm{AC}&\rightleftharpoons\mathrm B+\mathrm C,\\
\text{net:}\qquad\mathrm A&\rightleftharpoons\mathrm B.
\end{aligned}
$$
Adding the steps cancels both the <reaction intermediate> AC and the <catalyst> C from the net <chemical equation>. AC is produced and later consumed; C is used and later recovered. These letters describe possible roles, not a measured <reaction mechanism>. The alternative route is useful only if its <chemical kinetics> permits faster conversion. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/12-7-catalysis[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §12.7: catalysts and alternative mechanisms].

For a worked barrier example, consider an idealized reversible A-to-B conversion at fixed <temperature> $T=300\,\mathrm K$. Stipulate standard molar <Gibbs free energy> levels $G_{\mathrm A}^\circ=0$ and $G_{\mathrm B}^\circ=-20\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, with effective <transition state> levels $80$ without a <catalyst> and $50\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ with a <catalyst>. Use the same <standard states> and the same zero for both routes; the catalytic route includes the regenerated C at both endpoints. Unlike the separate <potential-energy profile> example, this illustration uses <Gibbs free energy> at one specified <temperature>.

\Image[catalyst-alternative-reaction-path.png]
{title=A <catalyst> lowers the stipulated <Gibbs energy of activation> in both directions while retaining the same endpoints}
{description=Original smooth schematic <Gibbs free energy> profiles at $300\,\mathrm K$, with levels in $\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The horizontal <reaction coordinate> describes progress along each schematic route, not time. These are chosen effective barriers, not measured <potential energy surfaces>, actual molecular paths, or the two-step <reaction mechanism> above. A real catalytic route can have several <transition states> and <reaction intermediates>.}

The <Gibbs energy of activation> is measured from the starting endpoint in the chosen direction. Thus the four barriers are
$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta G_{f,\mathrm{uncat}}^{\ddagger\circ}&=80-0=80,&
\Delta G_{f,\mathrm{cat}}^{\ddagger\circ}&=50-0=50,\\
\Delta G_{r,\mathrm{uncat}}^{\ddagger\circ}&=80-(-20)=100,&
\Delta G_{r,\mathrm{cat}}^{\ddagger\circ}&=50-(-20)=70,
\end{aligned}
\qquad\text{all in }\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}.
$$
Both directional barriers fall by $30\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. The endpoint difference stays $\Delta_rG^\circ=-20\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$.
