To turn those barriers into <rate constants>, use the Eyring form of <transition state theory> for this effective <first-order reaction> pair, with a common prefactor and unit probability of successful passage:
$$
k=\nu\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta G^{\ddagger\circ}}{RT}\right),
\qquad \nu=\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{h}.
$$
Here $k_{\mathrm B}$ is the <Boltzmann constant>, $h$ is the <Planck constant>, and $R$ is the <molar gas constant>. The common prefactor cancels from the comparisons. These <Gibbs energies of activation> are not the empirical <activation energy> $E_a$ of the <Arrhenius equation>. With the stipulated value $R=8.314462618\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, the speedup is
$$
\frac{k_{f,\mathrm{cat}}}{k_{f,\mathrm{uncat}}}
=\frac{k_{r,\mathrm{cat}}}{k_{r,\mathrm{uncat}}}
=\exp\left(\frac{30000}{8.314462618\times300}\right)
\approx1.67248\times10^5.
$$
The exponent is a <dimensionless quantity>: convert $30\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ to $30000\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$ before dividing by $RT$. The <exponential function> explains why a modest barrier reduction can have a dramatic kinetic effect. This number follows from the chosen free-energy barriers and common prefactor; it is not a universal catalytic multiplier or a prediction across <temperatures>.

\Image[catalyst-forward-reverse-rate-ratios.png]
{title=Both directional <rate constants> increase by about $1.67\times10^5$, while their forward-to-reverse ratio stays about $3036$}
{description=Original bar plots at $300\,\mathrm K$. Left: base-ten <logarithms> of the <dimensionless> normalized <rate constants> $k/\nu$ for the four stipulated barriers. A less negative value means faster conversion. Right: the <logarithm> of $k_f/k_r$ is identical for the two routes. Absolute units factor out through the common $\nu=k_{\mathrm B}T/h$; no temperature dependence outside this one model point is asserted.}

Assume ideal <thermodynamic activities> with a common concentration <standard state>, and directional <rate laws> $J_f=k_f[\mathrm A]$ and $J_r=k_r[\mathrm B]$. At <chemical equilibrium>, equal <reaction rates> give $K=[\mathrm B]/[\mathrm A]=k_f/k_r$ for this model. The barriers give
$$
K_{\mathrm{uncat}}=\frac{e^{-80000/(RT)}}{e^{-100000/(RT)}}
=e^{20000/(RT)}
=\frac{e^{-50000/(RT)}}{e^{-70000/(RT)}}=K_{\mathrm{cat}}
\approx3035.58.
$$
The <catalyst> speeds up both directions, so it changes how quickly <chemical equilibrium> is approached without changing this <equilibrium constant>. For a general <chemical reaction>, the thermodynamic relation is $K=\exp[-\Delta_rG^\circ/(RT)]$ with consistent <standard states>; an arbitrary multistep <reaction mechanism> need not permit $K=k_f/k_r$ for two measured <rate constants>. At the same <temperature>, <pressure>, and <thermodynamic activities>, a <catalyst> also leaves the actual overall reaction change in <Gibbs free energy> unchanged. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/13.3 Shifting Equilibria: Le Châtelier’s Principle> (§13.3: a catalyst does not shift equilibrium). To drive an unfavourable overall conversion, a process such as <energy coupling> must change the combined transformation.

= Transition state theory
{parent=Catalyst}
{wiki}
{title2=$k=(k_{\mathrm B}T/h)\exp[-\Delta G^{\ddagger\circ}/(RT)]$}

<Transition state theory> relates an <elementary reaction>'s <rate constant> to a <Gibbs energy of activation>. The Eyring expression used in the <catalyst> example assumes every passage counted at the <transition state> succeeds. More generally, a factor accounting for unsuccessful passage multiplies it. Comparing barriers alone gives the quoted speedup only when the remaining prefactors agree. Free-energy profiles can depend on <temperature>, so the example's values at $300\,\mathrm K$ do not determine a temperature curve. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/T06470[IUPAC Gold Book: transition state theory].

= Gibbs energy of activation
{c}
{parent=Transition state theory}
{title2=$\Delta G^{\ddagger\circ}$}

= Gibbs energies of activation
{c}
{synonym}

= Activation free energy
{synonym}

The <Gibbs energy of activation> is the standard molar <Gibbs free energy> change associated with going from the starting <reactants> to the <transition state> in <transition state theory>. It specifies the directional barrier, rather than the overall endpoint change in <Gibbs free energy>. Use consistent <standard states> when comparing <Gibbs energies of activation> and <rate constants>. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/G02631[IUPAC Gold Book: Gibbs energy of activation].

= Enthalpy of activation
{parent=Gibbs energy of activation}
{title2=$\Delta H^{\ddagger\circ}$}

The standard molar <enthalpy> difference between the <transition state> and the starting <reactants>, at the same <temperature> and <pressure>, in <transition state theory>.<Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry (IUPAC Recommendations 1994): E entries>{ref}

= Entropy of activation
{parent=Gibbs energy of activation}
{title2=$\Delta S^{\ddagger\circ}$}

The standard molar <entropy> difference between the <transition state> and the starting <reactants>, at the same <temperature> and <pressure>, with specified <standard states>, in <transition state theory>.<Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry (IUPAC Recommendations 1994): E entries>{ref}

= Transition state
{parent=Chemical kinetics}
{wiki}

A <transition state> describes configurations at the barrier between <reactants> and <reaction products> in an <elementary reaction>. It is a crossing region, rather than a stable substance that accumulates in a bottle. The IUPAC definition characterizes these configurations by equal probabilities of proceeding toward <reactants> or <reaction products>: https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/T06468[IUPAC Gold Book: transition state]{ref}. Lowering the barrier relative to the starting configuration can increase a <reaction rate>, even when the overall change in <Gibbs free energy> is unchanged.

= Reaction rate
{parent=Chemical kinetics}
{title2=$v$}
{wiki}

For one <chemical reaction> in a <closed system> of fixed <volume> $V$, with uniform <concentrations> and no competing reactions or appreciable accumulation of unlisted species, choose the <chemical equation>
$$
a\mathrm A+b\mathrm B\longrightarrow c\mathrm C,\qquad a,b,c>0.
$$
Brackets denote <amount of substance> per <volume>, for example $[\mathrm A]=n_{\mathrm A}/V$. The <stoichiometry> gives one instantaneous <reaction rate>
$$
v=-\frac1a\frac{d[\mathrm A]}{dt}
=-\frac1b\frac{d[\mathrm B]}{dt}
=\frac1c\frac{d[\mathrm C]}{dt}
=\frac1V\frac{d\xi}{dt},
$$
where $\xi$ is the existing <extent of reaction>. The minus signs make forward consumption positive; dividing by the coefficients makes measurements of different <chemical species> agree. Units can be $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/R05156[IUPAC Gold Book: rate of reaction] and https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/12-1-chemical-reaction-rates[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §12.1: chemical reaction rates].

For the illustrative <chemical equation> $2\mathrm A+\mathrm B\longrightarrow3\mathrm C$, a measured $d[\mathrm C]/dt=0.060\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$ implies $v=0.020\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$, $d[\mathrm A]/dt=-0.040\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$, and $d[\mathrm B]/dt=-0.020\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. These numbers express <stoichiometry>, without supplying a <rate law>.

Each <derivative> is the slope of a <concentration> curve at one instant. Over a finite interval, $-\Delta[\mathrm A]/(a\Delta t)$ instead gives an average <reaction rate>. For A converting reversibly to B, the net <reaction rate> is $v=d[\mathrm B]/dt=J_f-J_r$; both directional <reaction rates> can remain positive when $v=0$ at <chemical equilibrium>.

= Disappearance rate
{parent=Reaction rate}
{title2=$v_{\mathrm A}=-d[\mathrm A]/dt$}

The <disappearance rate> of a <reactant> A is its positive net loss of <concentration> per unit time, $v_{\mathrm A}=-d[\mathrm A]/dt$. For the fixed-<volume>, single-<chemical-reaction> setting of <reaction rate>, consuming $a$ units of A per conversion gives $v_{\mathrm A}=av$. The factor belongs to the <stoichiometry>; stating it is essential when quoting a <rate constant>. A negative net <disappearance rate> means that A is accumulating.

= Rate law
{parent=Reaction rate}

A <rate law> specifies how a <reaction rate> depends on <concentrations> under stated conditions. A common form is
$$
v=k[\mathrm A]^\alpha[\mathrm B]^\beta,
$$
where $k$ is a <rate constant> and the exponents are partial <orders of reaction>. Determine them experimentally, or derive them from a justified <reaction mechanism>. Do not infer $\alpha=a$ and $\beta=b$ from the net <chemical equation>: its coefficients describe consumption ratios, whereas the exponents describe sensitivity to <concentration>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/R05141[IUPAC Gold Book: rate law] and https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/12-3-rate-laws[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §12.3: rate laws].

A <rate law> becomes a prediction of time evolution when combined with <stoichiometry> in a <rate equation>, for example $d[\mathrm A]/dt=-av$. Agreement with a measured <rate law> is useful evidence for a <reaction mechanism>, but several routes can give the same dependence.

= Integrated rate law
{parent=Rate law}

An <integrated rate law> relates <concentration> to elapsed time after solving a <rate equation> with an <initial condition>. Its validity retains the assumptions of the underlying <rate law>, including fixed <rate constants> and appropriate <stoichiometry>. The <second-order reaction> reciprocal-<concentration> relation is one example; a different <rate equation> generally requires a different <integrated rate law>.<Chemistry 2e>[§12.4, opening paragraph and “Second-Order Reactions”: concentration as a function of elapsed time]{ref}

= Order of reaction
{parent=Rate law}
{title2=$n$}

= Orders of reaction
{synonym}

= Reaction order
{synonym}

In a power-form <rate law> $v=k[\mathrm A]^\alpha[\mathrm B]^\beta$, the partial <orders of reaction> are $\alpha$ and $\beta$, and the overall <order of reaction> is $n=\alpha+\beta$. For example, doubling $[\mathrm A]$ at fixed $[\mathrm B]$ multiplies $v$ by $2^\alpha$: factors of one, two, and four correspond to partial <orders of reaction> zero, one, and two. These exponents can also be fractional or negative. A single constant <order of reaction> need not describe a <rate law> that is not a power form. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/O04322[IUPAC Gold Book: order of reaction].

For a simple irreversible conversion $\mathrm A\longrightarrow\mathrm B$ with <rate equation> $-d[\mathrm A]/dt=k[\mathrm A]^n$, initial <concentration> $A_0>0$, and fixed $k>0$, compare <zero-order reactions>, <first-order reactions>, and <second-order reactions> using the <dimensionless quantities>
$$
X=\frac{[\mathrm A]}{A_0},\qquad \tau=kA_0^{n-1}t,\qquad \frac{dX}{d\tau}=-X^n.
$$
Their integrated curves, starting at $X(0)=1$, are
$$
\begin{aligned}
n=0:&\quad X=1-\tau\quad(0\leq\tau<1),\\
n=1:&\quad X=e^{-\tau},\\
n=2:&\quad X=\frac1{1+\tau}.
\end{aligned}
$$
For the <zero-order reaction>, the plotted extension $X=\max(1-\tau,0)$ stops conversion at depletion. It does not continue to obey $dX/d\tau=-1$ once $X=0$. A negative <concentration> would be unphysical. The <first-order reaction> and <second-order reaction> approach zero without reaching it at finite time in these models.

\Image[reaction-order-concentration-curves.png]
{title=<Dimensionless> <concentration> decays for three <orders of reaction>, with <zero-order reaction> depletion and the corresponding linearizing plots}

The common $\tau$ axis compares curve shapes after each model's own time rescaling, rather than equal laboratory times for an identical numerical $k$. In the lower panels, $X$, $\ln X$, and $1/X$ respectively give straight lines with slopes $-1$, $-1$, and $+1$ for the matching <order of reaction>. The <natural logarithm> uses a <dimensionless quantity>. These transformed plots help test a proposed <rate law>; the <zero-order reaction> line applies only before depletion. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/12-4-integrated-rate-laws[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §12.4: integrated rate laws].

= First-order reaction
{parent=Order of reaction}

A <first-order reaction> has a <reaction rate> proportional to one <reactant> <concentration> in the simple single-species case. For an irreversible $\mathrm A\longrightarrow\mathrm B$ in a <closed system> at fixed <volume>, take $[\mathrm A](0)=A_0$, $[\mathrm B](0)=0$, and constant <rate constant> $k>0$. Its <rate equation> and solution are
$$
\frac{d[\mathrm A]}{dt}=-k[\mathrm A],\qquad
[\mathrm A](t)=A_0e^{-kt},\qquad
[\mathrm B](t)=A_0(1-e^{-kt}),\qquad
v(t)=kA_0e^{-kt}.
$$
A short check is enough: differentiating $A_0e^{-kt}$ gives $-kA_0e^{-kt}$, and the two <concentrations> sum to $A_0$, as required by the one-to-one <stoichiometry>. Thus the same <exponential decay> describes both remaining <reactant> and decreasing <reaction rate>. Fixed $k$ means a fixed fractional loss per unit time, not a fixed absolute consumption rate.

Choose illustrative values $A_0=1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ and $k=0.2\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, with $t$ measured in seconds. Then $[\mathrm A]=e^{-0.2t}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, $[\mathrm B]=(1-e^{-0.2t})\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, and $v=0.2e^{-0.2t}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. The <half-life> is
$$
t_{1/2}=\frac{\ln2}{k}=\frac{\ln2}{0.2}\,\mathrm s\approx3.4657\,\mathrm s.
$$
At each successive <half-life>, half the remaining <reactant> is consumed; $t_{1/2}$ is independent of $A_0$ for this <first-order reaction>. At $t=10\,\mathrm s$, the calculated values are $[\mathrm A]\approx0.135335\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, $[\mathrm B]\approx0.864665\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, and $v\approx0.027067\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. These are model predictions for chosen parameters, rather than experimental measurements.

\Image[chemical-first-order-reaction.png]
{title=Illustrative <first-order reaction>: <reactant> loss, <reaction product> formation, and declining <reaction rate> for $k=0.2\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$}

The left panel tracks the two <concentrations>; the right tracks their instantaneous conversion <reaction rate>. Their different vertical units matter: $v=k[\mathrm A]$, so the numerical curves need not have the same height. This gives a practical way to translate a measured <concentration> into a forecast of both conversion and waiting time.

= Zero-order reaction
{parent=Order of reaction}

A <zero-order reaction> has a <reaction rate> independent of the relevant <reactant> <concentration> over the range where its <rate law> applies. For $\mathrm A\longrightarrow\mathrm B$, $[\mathrm A]=A_0-kt$ is valid only before depletion at $t=A_0/k$. The constant-consumption model must stop or change there.

= Second-order reaction
{parent=Order of reaction}

A <second-order reaction> has overall <order of reaction> two: a <rate law> may depend on $[\mathrm A]^2$, or on $[\mathrm A][\mathrm B]$ with partial <orders of reaction> one and one. Doubling all relevant <reactant> <concentrations> multiplies the <reaction rate> by four at fixed conditions. This experimentally determined dependence does not establish <molecularity> or a unique <reaction mechanism>. For an <elementary reaction> obeying the <law of mass action>, <molecularity> and overall <order of reaction> agree; a net <chemical equation> alone cannot establish that the conversion is elementary.<Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry (IUPAC Recommendations 1994): N and O entries by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry, IUPAC Recommendations 1994, entry “order of reaction”, final paragraphs: elementary versus stepwise reactions]{ref}

For the single-<concentration> model, explicitly choose the <disappearance rate> convention
$$
-\frac{d[\mathrm A]}{dt}=k_{\mathrm{obs}}[\mathrm A]^2,\qquad [\mathrm A](0)=[\mathrm A]_0>0,\qquad k_{\mathrm{obs}}>0.
$$
Here $k_{\mathrm{obs}}$ is the coefficient for loss of A, held fixed at the specified <temperature> and other conditions. Its subscript labels the chosen observed <disappearance rate>; it does not imply a <pseudo-first-order reaction>. Assume a uniformly mixed <closed system>, fixed <volume>, and no appreciable reverse or competing <chemical reactions>. The <integrated rate law> is
$$
\boxed{\frac1{[\mathrm A](t)}=\frac1{[\mathrm A]_0}+k_{\mathrm{obs}}t},\qquad
[\mathrm A](t)=\frac{[\mathrm A]_0}{1+k_{\mathrm{obs}}[\mathrm A]_0t}.
$$
The reciprocal-<concentration> plot is a straight line with slope $k_{\mathrm{obs}}$ and intercept $1/[\mathrm A]_0$. A brief derivation uses the <chain rule>: $d(1/[\mathrm A])/dt=-[\mathrm A]^{-2}d[\mathrm A]/dt=k_{\mathrm{obs}}$, then applies the <initial condition>. This is a hyperbolic decay, rather than the <exponential decay> of a <first-order reaction>.<Chemistry 2e>[§12.4, “Second-Order Reactions”, integrated equation and straight-line form; Table 12.2]{ref}

As an original prescribed example, choose $[\mathrm A]_0=0.020\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ and $k_{\mathrm{obs}}=0.50\,\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. At $t=100\,\mathrm s$, the reciprocal-<concentration> calculation is $50+50=100\,\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}}$, giving $[\mathrm A]=0.010\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$. The initial <disappearance rate> is $0.50(0.020)^2=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$; after $100\,\mathrm s$ it is $5.0\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. Halving the <concentration> quarters the <disappearance rate>.

<Dimensional analysis> checks every formula: $k_{\mathrm{obs}}[\mathrm A]^2$ has units $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$, $k_{\mathrm{obs}}t$ has units $\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}}$, and $k_{\mathrm{obs}}[\mathrm A]_0t$ is a <dimensionless quantity>. Changing <concentration> units changes the numerical <rate constant>: the same example is $[\mathrm A]_0=20\,\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}$, $k_{\mathrm{obs}}=5.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$, giving $[\mathrm A](100\,\mathrm s)=10\,\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}$.

\Image[second-order-concentration-and-reciprocal-time.png]
{id=image-second-order-concentration-and-reciprocal-time}
{title=<Second-order reaction> <concentration> decay and reciprocal-<concentration> lines for three prescribed starting <concentrations>}
{description=Original model curves, not measured data. All use the <disappearance rate> coefficient $k_{\mathrm{obs}}=0.50\,\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. The reciprocal lines share a slope while their intercepts differ; marked points give each run's first <half-life>.}

For an <elementary reaction> $2\mathrm A\longrightarrow\mathrm P$, instead define the stoichiometrically normalized <reaction rate> $r=k[\mathrm A]^2$. Two A entities disappear per P formed, so
$$
r=-\frac12\frac{d[\mathrm A]}{dt}=\frac{d[\mathrm P]}{dt}=k[\mathrm A]^2,\qquad
-\frac{d[\mathrm A]}{dt}=2k[\mathrm A]^2,\qquad k_{\mathrm{obs}}=2k.
$$
Consequently the reciprocal-<concentration> slope is $2k$, not $k$. In the prescribed example, the normalized <rate constant> would be $k=0.25\,\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$; initially $r=1.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$ while the <disappearance rate> is twice that. With $[\mathrm P](0)=0$, <stoichiometry> gives $[\mathrm P](100\,\mathrm s)=([\mathrm A]_0-[\mathrm A])/2=0.0050\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$. Both <rate constants> have the same dimensions, so a units check alone cannot catch their factor-of-two difference. The symbol $r$ denotes the same normalization as $v$ in <reaction rate>.

The <half-life>, meaning the time to reach half a specified starting <concentration>, follows by setting $[\mathrm A]=[\mathrm A]_0/2$:
$$
\boxed{t_{1/2}=\frac1{k_{\mathrm{obs}}[\mathrm A]_0}}.
$$
For $r=k[\mathrm A]^2$ in $2\mathrm A\longrightarrow\mathrm P$, this becomes $t_{1/2}=1/(2k[\mathrm A]_0)$. Its units are seconds because $k_{\mathrm{obs}}[\mathrm A]_0$ has units $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. Doubling the starting <concentration> halves the first <half-life>; a quoted <half-life> needs its starting <concentration>. This differs from a <first-order reaction>, whose <half-life> is independent of starting <concentration>.<Chemistry 2e>[§12.4, “Half-Life of a Reaction”, second-order derivation and Table 12.2]{ref}

Within the prescribed run, $[\mathrm A]$ reaches $0.010$, $0.0050$, and $0.0025\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ at $100$, $300$, and $700\,\mathrm s$. The successive halving intervals are $100$, $200$, and $400\,\mathrm s$: each begins at a smaller <concentration>. More generally, reaching fraction $f$ of the initial <concentration>, with $0<f<1$, requires $t=(f^{-1}-1)/(k_{\mathrm{obs}}[\mathrm A]_0)$, so $90\%$ consumption takes nine initial <half-lives>, rather than a fixed sequence of equal waiting times.

\Image[second-order-halving-intervals-and-initial-concentration.png]
{id=image-second-order-halving-intervals-and-initial-concentration}
{title=Lengthening <second-order reaction> halving intervals and the dependence of the first <half-life> on initial <concentration>}
{description=Original model predictions, not measurements. Left: the prescribed $0.020\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ run. Right: $t_{1/2}=1/(k_{\mathrm{obs}}[\mathrm A]_0)$ at fixed $k_{\mathrm{obs}}=0.50\,\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$, compared with a <first-order reaction> chosen to have $t_{1/2}=100\,\mathrm s$. That comparison uses $k_1=\ln2/(100\,\mathrm s)$, with different units from $k_{\mathrm{obs}}$.}

A straight reciprocal-<concentration> plot tests this particular single-<concentration> <rate equation>. It does not uniquely identify a <reaction mechanism>, nor does curvature rule out every <second-order reaction>: two independently changing <reactant> <concentrations> require their own <integrated rate law>. At low <concentration>, a small <measurement error> $\delta[\mathrm A]$ becomes approximately $-\delta[\mathrm A]/[\mathrm A]^2$ in the reciprocal, magnifying late-time errors. Use the line as a diagnostic; a <nonlinear regression> of the original <concentrations> can retain their measurement scale and explicit error model.

= Conductometric saponification assay
{parent=Second-order reaction}

How can an invisible change in <concentration> produce a readable signal? The <saponification> of <ethyl acetate> by <hydroxide> in <water> gives <ethanol> and an <acetate ion>:
$$
\mathrm{CH_3COOC_2H_5}+\mathrm{OH^-}\longrightarrow\mathrm{CH_3COO^-}+\mathrm{C_2H_5OH}.
$$
The <rate law> used for this assay is $r=k_2[\mathrm{ethyl\ acetate}][\mathrm{OH^-}]$. Each <reactant> has coefficient one, so $r$ equals either <disappearance rate>. If the two initial <concentrations> are equal, they remain equal under this <stoichiometry>, giving $-dc/dt=k_2c^2$ and $k_{\mathrm{obs}}=k_2$, without a factor of two.

<Manual for Advanced Physical Chemistry Laboratory-II by Nasima Arshad> (Unit 7, Experiment 2, printed pp. 66–67) specifies a conductivity cell holding about $60\,\mathrm{mL}$ in constant-<temperature> baths, with $0.040\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ <ethyl acetate> and <sodium hydroxide> stocks equilibrated separately. Mixing $30\,\mathrm{mL}$ of each initiates the run. The readout is <electrical resistance>: every minute for ten minutes, every two minutes for ten more, then every five minutes for twenty-five. The cell is rinsed with distilled <water>. A matched <sodium hydroxide>–<water> dilution estimates the initial resistance; a <potassium chloride> standard determines the cell constant. The manual asks for comparison with the extrapolated initial signal.<Manual for Advanced Physical Chemistry Laboratory-II by Nasima Arshad>[Unit 7, Experiment 2, printed pp. 66–67: Procedure and Calculations; title page credits Nasima Arshad as manual author]{ref}

The post-mixing initial <concentration> is $c_0=0.040(30/60)=0.020\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ for each <reactant>, assuming additive <volumes>. This matches our illustrative starting <concentration>, but the prescribed $k_{\mathrm{obs}}=0.50$ is not a measured value from the manual. As <hydroxide> is replaced by the less mobile <acetate ion>, <electrical conductance> decreases. The preceding experiment describes two <platinum> <electrodes> and an alternating-voltage <Wheatstone bridge> to limit polarization at the <electrodes>. For measured <electrical resistance> $R(t)$, <electrical conductance> is $G(t)=1/R(t)$; <electrical conductivity> is $\kappa(t)=K_{\mathrm{cell}}G(t)$. Keep the probe geometry fixed and use one signal convention throughout.<Manual for Advanced Physical Chemistry Laboratory-II by Nasima Arshad>[Unit 7, Experiment 1, printed pp. 62–63: reaction, ionic conductances, inverse resistance, platinum electrodes and alternating-voltage bridge; Experiment 2, p. 66: hydroxide replaced by acetate]{ref}

For the equal-starting-<concentration> model, assume an affine calibration between <electrical conductance> and remaining <hydroxide> <concentration>, with endpoints $G_0$ and $G_\infty$ at the same <temperature>. This gives
$$
\frac{c(t)}{c_0}=\frac{G(t)-G_\infty}{G_0-G_\infty},\qquad
\frac{G_0-G(t)}{G(t)-G_\infty}=\frac{c_0-c(t)}{c(t)}=k_2c_0t.
$$
The last plot has slope $k_2c_0$, with units $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, whereas plotting $1/c$ against time has slope $k_2$, with units $\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. These are different slopes. This endpoint transformation also shows why raw resistance must not be substituted for conductance.

Useful additional controls are repeat runs, fixed immersion depth, an independently checked final signal, and comparisons of several starting <concentrations>. Mixing delay obscures early conversion; <temperature> drift changes both the <rate constant> and the signal; contamination adds mobile <ions>. The affine calibration can fail when ionic mobilities change with composition. Near completion, $G-G_\infty$ becomes small, so endpoint error is strongly amplified. Those are methodological limits and proposed checks, not new experimental observations. A conductivity trace supports a calibrated <rate law> over its tested range; it does not independently reveal the full <reaction mechanism>.

For unequal starting <concentrations> $a=[\mathrm A]_0$ and $b=[\mathrm B]_0$ in a one-to-one <chemical reaction>, let $x$ be the consumed <concentration>. Then $[\mathrm A]=a-x$, $[\mathrm B]=b-x$, and
$$
\frac{dx}{dt}=k_2(a-x)(b-x),\qquad
\frac1{b-a}\ln\!\left(\frac{a(b-x)}{b(a-x)}\right)=k_2t\quad(a\ne b).
$$
The <natural logarithm> acts on a <dimensionless quantity>; differentiating the left side with respect to $x$ gives $1/((a-x)(b-x))$, verifying the relation. Mixing $20\,\mathrm{mL}$ of the stated <ethyl acetate> stock with $40\,\mathrm{mL}$ of the <sodium hydroxide> stock would give $a=0.01333\ldots$ and $b=0.02666\ldots\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$. The difference $[\mathrm B]-[\mathrm A]=b-a$ stays constant, rather than their ratio. Neither the equal-<concentration> reciprocal formula nor a <pseudo-first-order reaction> approximation is justified solely by that twofold excess.

= Pseudo-first-order reaction
{parent=Order of reaction}

In a <second-order reaction> $\mathrm A+\mathrm B\longrightarrow\mathrm P$ with <rate law> $-d[\mathrm A]/dt=k_2[\mathrm A][\mathrm B]$, a large excess of B can keep $[\mathrm B]\approx[\mathrm B]_0$ during the observation window. Then
$$
-\frac{d[\mathrm A]}{dt}\approx k_1[\mathrm A],\qquad k_1=k_2[\mathrm B]_0,\qquad
[\mathrm A](t)\approx[\mathrm A]_0e^{-k_1t}.
$$
This is a <pseudo-first-order reaction>: the approximate <first-order reaction> describes that window, while changing the excess B <concentration> changes $k_1$. Its units are $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, distinct from the <second-order reaction> <rate constant> $k_2$. For one-to-one <stoichiometry>, the maximum fractional depletion of B is $[\mathrm A]_0/[\mathrm B]_0$; an original $100:1$ initial excess bounds its change by $1\%$. A twofold excess permits $50\%$ depletion and does not provide the same control.<Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry (IUPAC Recommendations 1994): N and O entries by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry>[Glossary of Terms Used in Physical Organic Chemistry, IUPAC Recommendations 1994, entry “order of reaction”: constant B, observed coefficient and pseudo-first-order coefficient]{ref}

= Rate constant
{parent=Chemical kinetics}
{title2=$k$}

A <rate constant> is the coefficient in a specified <rate law>, held fixed under specified conditions. It can describe an empirical overall <chemical reaction>, as well as an <elementary reaction>. Fixed <temperature> matters: changing <temperature> can change $k$, even for the same <chemical reaction>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/O04322[IUPAC Gold Book: order of reaction and rate constants] and https://old.goldbook.iupac.org/html/R/R05137.html[IUPAC Gold Book: rate coefficient].

For $v=k[\mathrm A]^n$, dimensional consistency requires
$$
\operatorname{units}(k)=(\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}})^{1-n}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}.
$$
A <zero-order reaction> therefore uses $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$; a <first-order reaction> uses $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$; a <second-order reaction> uses $\mathrm{L\,mol^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$. Comparing bare numerical values of $k$ across different <orders of reaction> is meaningless without their units and <concentrations>. Even for the same <order of reaction>, state whether $k$ multiplies the normalized <reaction rate> $v$ or a particular <reactant> disappearance rate: a stoichiometric coefficient can change that convention.

= Protonation
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

= Chemical equation
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

A <chemical equation> represents a <chemical reaction> using <chemical formulas>, with <reactants> on the left and <reaction products> on the right. A balanced <chemical equation> has the same number of <chemical atoms> of each <chemical element> on both sides. For <molecular hydrogen> and <molecular oxygen> forming <water>,
$$
2\mathrm{H}_2+\mathrm{O}_2\longrightarrow2\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}.
$$
The coefficients $2,1,2$ give the relative numbers of <molecules>; the coefficient $1$ is normally omitted. Count <hydrogen> as $2\times2=4$ on the left and $2\times2=4$ on the right, and <oxygen> as $1\times2=2$ on the left and $2\times1=2$ on the right. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/4-1-writing-and-balancing-chemical-equations[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §4.1: writing and balancing chemical equations].

\Image[chemical-equation-atom-balance.png]
{title=Two <molecular hydrogen> <molecules> and one <molecular oxygen> <molecule> account for two <water> <molecules>}
{description=Exactly four <hydrogen> and two <oxygen> <chemical atoms> occur on each side. The circles and connecting lines are an invented schematic: they indicate grouping and <chemical bonds>, without representing actual <bond lengths>, <bond angles> or a <reaction mechanism>.}

To balance the initial expression $\mathrm H_2+\mathrm O_2\longrightarrow\mathrm H_2\mathrm O$, first put $2$ before <water> to match the two <oxygen> <chemical atoms>. This creates four <hydrogen> <chemical atoms> on the right, so put $2$ before <molecular hydrogen>. Changing a coefficient multiplies the entire <chemical formula>. Changing a subscript changes composition and usually the <chemical substance>: $2\mathrm H_2\mathrm O$ is two <water> <molecules>, whereas $\mathrm H_2\mathrm O_2$ is one <hydrogen peroxide> <molecule>. Never balance a <chemical equation> by changing its subscripts.

The bookkeeping follows from conservation of the <atomic nuclei> in ordinary, non-nuclear <chemical reactions>; <chemical bonds> and the distribution of <electrons> can change. For a <chemical equation> involving <ions>, total <electric charge> must also match. For example, the existing <self-ionization of water> equation has zero total <electric charge> on each side, since the $+1$ of <hydronium> cancels the $-1$ of <hydroxide>. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/2.6 Ionic and Molecular Compounds> (§2.6: nuclei and ions).

The same $2:1:2$ ratio relates changes in <amount of substance>: two <moles> of <molecular hydrogen> consumed per <mole> of <molecular oxygen> correspond to two <moles> of <water> formed. A <mole> is a defined unit, not one <molecule>; the little whole-number picture is an abstract counting model, not a claim that a macroscopic sample contains just five <molecules>. See <molecular count and amount of substance> for the exact conversion and https://www.bipm.org/en/si-base-units/mole[BIPM, SI base unit: mole] for the definition.

This is why a balanced <chemical equation> is useful: <stoichiometry> turns the same atom accounting into relations between measurable quantities. It does not determine <reaction rate>, the final conversion set by <chemical equilibrium>, or the actual yield of recovered <reaction products>. The <limiting reagent> gives an upper bound under the specified <chemical reaction>.

= Reactant
{parent=Chemical reaction}

A <chemical species> consumed in a <chemical reaction>.

= Reagent
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

= Product
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

= Reaction product
{synonym}

= Substrate
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}

= Reaction substrate
{synonym}

A chemical species on which a reagent or catalyst acts in a <chemical reaction>. An <enzyme> acts on a biochemical <substrate>, a particular instance of this broader reaction role.

= Thermal dissociation
{parent=Chemical reaction}

Thermal dissociation is temperature-driven breaking of a molecule into simpler fragments. The <chemical equilibrium> abundance depends on pressure as well as temperature: reactions increasing particle number are generally favored at low pressure. In an <exoplanet atmosphere>, reduced <water> on a very hot dayside does not by itself establish a low planetary oxygen abundance.

= Stoichiometry
{parent=Chemical reaction}
{wiki}
{title2=$\nu_i$}

<Stoichiometry> relates the quantities consumed and formed in a <chemical reaction>. Its coefficients are ratios of entity counts, or equivalently of <amount of substance>, rather than ratios of <masses>. For the <water> <chemical equation>, consuming two <molecular hydrogen> <molecules> per <molecular oxygen> <molecule> gives the same $2:1$ ratio as consuming two <moles> of <molecular hydrogen> per <mole> of <molecular oxygen>. Multiplying every entity count by the same factor preserves the ratio. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/4-3-reaction-stoichiometry[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, reaction stoichiometry].

Coefficients and subscripts do different jobs. The coefficient in $2\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}$ counts two <water> <molecules>; the subscript says that each contains two <hydrogen> <chemical atoms>. Changing coefficients scales quantities. Changing a subscript changes the specified <chemical substance>.

\Image[stoichiometry-particle-counts.png]
{title=The same stoichiometric ratio at molecular and molar scales}
{description=The upper schematic counts individual <molecules>; the lower line multiplies each count by the <Avogadro constant> times one <mole>. Circle sizes and positions are illustrative.}

This distinction catches a common counting error: one <mole> of <water> <molecules> contains two <moles> of <hydrogen> <chemical atoms> and one <mole> of <oxygen> <chemical atoms>. Always specify what is counted.

<Stoichiometry> records reactant and product multiplicities in a <chemical reaction> or a <chemical reaction network>. The change caused by one reaction is its <stoichiometric vector>; multiplying by the reaction rate gives its contribution to the concentration equations. For two identical reactants the rate-constant convention absorbs any combinatorial factor.

= Molecular count and amount of substance
{parent=Stoichiometry}
{title2=$N=nN_{\mathrm A}$}

The molecular count $N$ is a dimensionless integer; the <amount of substance> $n$ has unit <mole>, symbol $\mathrm{mol}$. They satisfy
$$
N=nN_{\mathrm A},\qquad N_{\mathrm A}=6.02214076\times10^{23}\,\mathrm{mol}^{-1}.
$$
The <Avogadro constant> is exact by the modern definition of the <mole>: see https://www.bipm.org/en/si-base-units/mole[BIPM, SI base unit: mole]. Thus two <water> <molecules> correspond to $2/N_{\mathrm A}$ <moles>, whereas two <moles> of <water> contain $1.204428152\times10^{24}$ <molecules>. A <mole> is a unit for counting specified entities, which may also be <chemical atoms>, <ions> or <electrons>.

= Extent of reaction
{parent=Stoichiometry}
{title2=$\xi$}
{wiki}

For a closed system with one specified <chemical reaction>, the <extent of reaction> $\xi$ records progress from $\xi=0$ through $n_i=n_{i,0}+\nu_i\xi$. Here $n_i$ is the <amount of substance> of species $i$, and the signed <stoichiometric vector> has negative entries for <reactants> and positive entries for <reaction products>. For the <water> <chemical equation> in the order $\mathrm{H}_2,\mathrm{O}_2,\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}$, it is $(-2,-1,2)$. The normalization of $\xi$ depends on the chosen coefficients. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/E02283[IUPAC Gold Book, extent of reaction].

= Limiting reagent
{parent=Stoichiometry}
{wiki}

= Limiting reactant
{synonym}

The <limiting reagent> is the <reactant> exhausted first if the specified <chemical reaction> proceeds to the maximum conversion permitted by <stoichiometry>. With initial values of <amount of substance> $n_{i,0}$ for the <reactants> and positive consumption coefficients $a_i$, the largest forward <extent of reaction> is $\xi_{\max}=\min_i(n_{i,0}/a_i)$. Compare these quotients, rather than simply choosing the smallest initial quantity. This calculation predicts the maximum <reaction product> quantity and identifies which <reactant> supply must increase to raise it. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/4-4-reaction-yields[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, limiting reactants].

Start with $3\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <molecular hydrogen>, $1\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <molecular oxygen> and no <water>. Assume complete conversion by the displayed <chemical reaction>, without competing reactions. Then
$$
\xi_{\max}=\min\left(\frac{3\,\mathrm{mol}}2,\frac{1\,\mathrm{mol}}1\right)=1\,\mathrm{mol}.
$$
The <molecular oxygen> is the <limiting reagent>. Consumption of $2\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <molecular hydrogen> and $1\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <molecular oxygen> forms $2\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <water>, leaving $1\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <molecular hydrogen>. Initially there are $1.806642228\times10^{24}$ <molecular hydrogen> <molecules> and $6.02214076\times10^{23}$ <molecular oxygen> <molecules>; after conversion there are $1.204428152\times10^{24}$ <water> <molecules> and $6.02214076\times10^{23}$ leftover <molecular hydrogen> <molecules>.

\Image[limiting-reagent-amounts.png]
{title=Oxygen runs out while one mole of molecular hydrogen remains}
{description=The horizontal axis is <extent of reaction>, rather than time. The endpoint assumes complete conversion by one <chemical reaction>; the curves express <stoichiometry>.}

As a check, the initial inventory has $6\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <hydrogen> <chemical atoms> and $2\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <oxygen> <chemical atoms>. The final <water> contains $4\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <hydrogen> <chemical atoms>, and leftover <molecular hydrogen> supplies the remaining $2\,\mathrm{mol}$. The atom totals balance even though the total <amount of substance> of <molecules> falls from four to three <moles>.

= Acid-base chemistry
{parent=Chemistry}

<Acid-base chemistry> studies how <chemical acids> and <chemical bases> exchange <protons> or <electron pairs>. Here the focus is transfer of <protons> in <water>: a <chemical acid> donates a <proton>, and a <chemical base> accepts it. The resulting <chemical equilibrium> determines <pH>, while a <buffer solution> can resist changes in <pH>. This connects microscopic <chemical reactions> with a compact, measurable description of a <chemical solution>.

The important distinction is between how much <chemical acid> is present and how readily it transfers a <proton>. A <weak acid> and a <strong acid> at the same total <concentration> need not have the same <pH>. Conversely, two <buffer solutions> can have the same <pH> while containing very different total quantities of their <chemical acids> and <chemical bases>.

= Isoelectric point
{parent=Acid-base chemistry}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{pI}$}

The <isoelectric point> is the <pH> at which a specified <molecule>'s mean net <electric charge> is zero. Different <protonation> states can still carry nonzero <electric charges>; their contributions cancel in the average. For the two-step <glycine speciation model>, this gives $\mathrm{pI}=(\mathrm pK_{a1}+\mathrm pK_{a2})/2$. Additional ionizable <amino acid side chains> require their own <chemical equilibrium> calculations; averaging every <pKa> is not a general rule. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.2, discussion following Figure 26.2: isoelectric points and the dissociations surrounding the neutral form]{ref}

Below its <isoelectric point>, free <glycine> has positive mean <electric charge> and an <electric field> drives it toward the negative <electrode>; above its <isoelectric point>, the mean <electric charge> is negative and the direction reverses. At its <isoelectric point> the charge-driven contribution vanishes. <Diffusion> and bulk movement of the <chemical solution> can still move the sample. <Gel electrophoresis> applies this charge dependence to separation, although <protein> size, shape and interactions also affect migration. The <pKa> values of free <glycine> do not determine a <protein>'s <isoelectric point>: its free <N-terminus>, <C-terminus> and ionizable <amino acid side chains> supply a different inventory. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.2, Figure 26.3 and accompanying electrophoresis discussion]{ref}

= Acid
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Acid-base chemistry}
{wiki=Acid}

= Chemical acid
{synonym}

A <chemical acid> can donate a <proton>, or accept an <electron pair> to form a <covalent bond>. The <pH> and <buffer solution> examples below use the former meaning. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/A00071[IUPAC Gold Book, acid].

= Strong acid
{parent=Acid (chemistry)}

In <water>, a <strong acid> transfers its available <proton> essentially completely to <water> under the dilute conditions considered here. For the <pH> example, each initial <chemical acid> unit supplies one <hydronium> <ion>. “Strong” describes this conversion, rather than a high <concentration>. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/14.3 Relative Strengths of Acids and Bases>.

= Hydrochloric acid
{parent=Strong acid}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{HCl}(\mathrm{aq})$}

<Hydrochloric acid> is an aqueous <chemical solution> of <hydrogen chloride>. The <glycine titration> model treats its dissociation into <hydronium> and <chloride> as complete, retaining both <ions> in the <electric charge> balance.

= Weak acid
{parent=Acid (chemistry)}

A <weak acid> establishes a <chemical equilibrium> between its proton-donating form and its <conjugate base> in <water>. For a neutral <monoprotic acid> $\mathrm{HA}$, the aqueous <chemical equation> is
$$
\mathrm{HA(aq)}+\mathrm{H_2O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{H_3O^+(aq)}+\mathrm{A^-(aq)}.
$$
<Water> accepts a <proton> to become <hydronium>; $\mathrm A^-$ is the <conjugate base>. The double arrow denotes reversible exchange at <chemical equilibrium>, not equal populations. The <acid dissociation constant> and composition determine the <hydronium> <concentration>; substituting total <chemical acid> <concentration> into the <pH> formula is generally incorrect.

“Weak” describes the tendency to transfer a <proton> in a specified <solvent>, rather than how much <chemical acid> was dissolved. A <weak acid> can be concentrated, and a <strong acid> can be dilute. Even the same <weak acid> can have a large <weak acid dissociation fraction> after sufficient <dilution>, while its <acid dissociation constant> stays fixed at the same <temperature> and <standard state> conventions.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/14.3 Relative Strengths of Acids and Bases>[§14.3, acid ionization and concentration-dependent percent ionization]{ref}

= Monoprotic acid
{parent=Weak acid}

A <monoprotic acid> supplies one <proton> per acid unit in the <acid-base chemistry> model under consideration. <Acetic acid> is an example. Several successive proton transfers require several <acid dissociation constants> and a larger set of <chemical species>.

= Weak acid dissociation fraction
{parent=Weak acid}
{title2=$\alpha=[\mathrm A^-]/C_T$}

For a <monoprotic acid> with <analytical concentration> $C_T=[\mathrm{HA}]+[\mathrm A^-]$, define the <dimensionless> <weak acid dissociation fraction>
$$
\alpha=\frac{[\mathrm A^-]}{C_T},\qquad 1-\alpha=\frac{[\mathrm{HA}]}{C_T}.
$$
This counts acid units in the <conjugate base> form. Identifying it with $[\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+]/C_T$ additionally requires negligible <self-ionization of water> and no other sources of <electric charge>. In the ideal dilute <chemical solution> model, with $h=[\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+]/c^\circ$, the <acid dissociation constant> gives $\alpha=K_a/(K_a+h)$. Thus lowering <hydronium> <thermodynamic activity> favors the <conjugate base>.

\Image[weak-acid-dissociation-dilution.png]
{id=weak-acid-dilution-equilibrium}
{title=<Dilution> increases the <weak acid dissociation fraction> while raising <pH> in a synthetic aqueous <chemical equilibrium>}

The diagram uses stipulated <dimensionless> <acid dissociation constant> $K_a=10^{-5}$ and <self-ionization of water> ion product $K_w=10^{-14}$ at fixed <temperature>; these are model inputs, not measurements of a named substance. It solves the <weak acid mass and charge balances>. At $C_T=0.010\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, the calculated <weak acid dissociation fraction> is about $3.11\%$ and <pH> about $3.51$; at $C_T=10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, they are about $27.0\%$ and $4.57$. More acid units are dissociated as a fraction of the total, yet fewer <hydronium> <ions> occur per <volume (thermodynamics)>.

At extreme <dilution>, <self-ionization of water> makes $h$ approach $\sqrt{K_w}$, so <pH> approaches $7$ for the chosen model. The limiting <weak acid dissociation fraction> is $K_a/(K_a+\sqrt{K_w})\approx0.9901$, rather than exactly one. The calculation assumes pure added <water>, a neutral <monoprotic acid>, and no competing <chemical reactions>; actual contamination becomes increasingly important as $C_T$ falls.

= Weak acid mass and charge balances
{parent=Weak acid}

Consider only neutral $\mathrm{HA}$ added to pure <water>, at fixed <temperature>, with ideal dilute <thermodynamic activities>, <water> <thermodynamic activity> approximated by one, and no added salts or other <chemical species>. Normalize every <concentration> by $c^\circ=1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$:
$$
c=C_T/c^\circ,\qquad h=[\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+]/c^\circ,\qquad x=[\mathrm A^-]/c^\circ,\qquad y=[\mathrm{HA}]/c^\circ,\qquad o=[\mathrm{OH}^-]/c^\circ.
$$
Conservation of acid units and zero net <electric charge>, respectively, require
$$
x+y=c,\qquad h=x+o.
$$
The second equation balances positive <hydronium> against negative <conjugate base> and <hydroxide>. It would need all additional <ions> if salts or a <chemical base> were added. Together with <acid dissociation constant> $K_a=hx/y$ and the <self-ionization of water> relation $K_w=ho$, these give
$$
x=\frac{K_ac}{K_a+h},\qquad y=\frac{hc}{K_a+h},\qquad o=\frac{K_w}{h},\qquad
h-\frac{K_w}{h}-\frac{K_ac}{K_a+h}=0.
$$
This is exact within the stated ideal model, including depletion and <self-ionization of water>. Multiplication by $h(K_a+h)$ yields the <cubic equation>
$$
h^3+K_ah^2-(K_w+K_ac)h-K_aK_w=0.
$$
For positive $K_a,K_w,c$, the unmultiplied left-hand side increases strictly from negative to positive values as $h$ increases from zero to infinity; its <derivative> is $1+K_w/h^2+K_ac/(K_a+h)^2>0$. There is therefore exactly one positive <root of a polynomial>. A <bisection method> can find it without choosing an unphysical <root of a polynomial>.

If $K_w/h^2\ll1$, neglect <hydroxide>, so $h\approx x$ and the <quadratic equation> retaining acid depletion gives
$$
\frac{x^2}{c-x}=K_a,\qquad
x=\frac{\sqrt{K_a^2+4K_ac}-K_a}{2}
=\frac{2K_ac}{\sqrt{K_a^2+4K_ac}+K_a}.
$$
The last form avoids subtracting nearly equal numbers. If also $x/c\ll1$, neglect depletion to obtain
$$
h\approx x\approx\sqrt{K_ac},\qquad
[\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+]\approx\sqrt{K_a c^\circ C_T}.
$$
Check both conditions after computing: $x/c$ measures the fraction lost from the initial undissociated <weak acid> inventory, while $K_w/h^2$ measures the omitted <hydroxide> contribution to <electric charge> balance. A $5\%$ depletion criterion is a chosen tolerance, not a universal boundary. Keeping depletion does not cure the neglect of <self-ionization of water>. These <weak acid mass and charge balances> also explain why the <square root> estimate cannot be extended to arbitrarily dilute <chemical solutions>.

= Analytical concentration
{parent=Weak acid mass and charge balances}
{title2=$C_T$}

An <analytical concentration> records the total conserved inventory of a specified component per <volume (thermodynamics)> of <chemical solution>, summed over its relevant <chemical species>. Here $C_T=[\mathrm{HA}]+[\mathrm A^-]$; it is not generally the <chemical equilibrium> <concentration> $[\mathrm{HA}]$ of the undissociated <weak acid>.

= Weak acid dissociation measurement
{parent=Weak acid}

A <pH> measurement becomes evidence for a <weak acid> <chemical equilibrium> when paired with a known <analytical concentration> and a calibrated <glass electrode>. For the pure-<weak acid> model, measured <pH> estimates $h=10^{-\mathrm{pH}}$ only in the ideal <thermodynamic activity> approximation. Then $o=K_w/h$, $x=h-o$, $y=c-x$, and $K_a=hx/y$. Require $0<x<c$; values outside that range signal inconsistent measurements or an inadequate model. Repeating this reconstruction across a <dilution> series tests whether one <acid dissociation constant> describes the data, rather than merely fitting one point.

The Analytical Sciences Digital Library's <Analytical Electrochemistry: Potentiometry/Potentiometric Titration of an Unknown Weak Monoprotic Acid> provides a complementary <acid-base titration> experiment. Its apparatus includes a <glass electrode> and meter, a stirring magnet, a <volumetric flask>, <pipettes>, and a <burette> containing standardized <sodium hydroxide>. Prepare an aqueous acid stock of known <analytical concentration>, transfer a measured portion, and stir gently with the <glass electrode> clear of the magnet. Record added <volume (thermodynamics)> and stable <pH> after each addition; use a preliminary run to locate the steep region and smaller additions there on a fresh portion. The readout is the <pH> versus added <volume (thermodynamics)> curve, not a visual count of dissociated <molecules>.

Calibrate with fresh standard <buffer solutions> bracketing the <pH> range being interpreted. Use their assigned values at the actual <temperature>; a pair near <pH> $4$ and $7$ can cover the midpoint of many <weak acid> examples, but does not cover an entire alkaline endpoint region. Rinse between standards and samples to limit carryover, keep the sensing bulb and <reference electrode> junction immersed, and wait for a stable reading. These instrument practices are documented in <HI5221 & HI5222 Instruction Manual: pH and mV and ISE and Temperature Bench Meters by Hanna Instruments> (pp. 45–47, 49–50 and 73–74: calibration, measurement, rinsing and electrode conditioning).

Locate the <acid-base titration> equivalence region from the steepest change of <pH> with added <volume (thermodynamics)>. For one-to-one <neutralization>, standardized <sodium hydroxide> <concentration> times equivalence <volume (thermodynamics)> estimates the initial <amount of substance> of acid. Half that <volume (thermodynamics)> gives approximately equal $[\mathrm{HA}]$ and $[\mathrm A^-]$ only when subsequent <chemical equilibrium> redistribution is small. The <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> then gives $\mathrm{pH}\approx\mathrm pK_a$. Unequal <activity coefficients> or appreciable <hydronium> or <hydroxide> inventories can shift this estimate. The full <chemical equilibrium> curve supplies a stronger consistency check than its midpoint alone.

Useful controls for this model test are a fresh independently prepared acid portion, a <water> blank, a check standard after measurement to detect instrument drift, and fixed recorded <temperature>. A blank diagnoses contamination but should not be subtracted directly from <pH>, which is a <logarithm>. Keep exposure to atmospheric <carbon dioxide> comparable, especially at high <dilution>, and record any background salt that changes <ionic strength>. These are experimental-design controls suggested by the model's assumptions. The observed curve and its uncertainty determine whether the model is adequate; the diagrams here contain no experimental readouts. An aqueous <acid dissociation constant> cannot be inferred unchanged from a sample requiring a different <solvent> mixture, and a nearly neutral dilute sample makes the difference $h-K_w/h$ particularly sensitive to <pH> error.

= Glass electrode
{parent=Weak acid dissociation measurement}

A <glass electrode> is an <electrode> whose glass membrane responds to <hydrogen ion> <thermodynamic activity>. A meter measures its <electric potential difference> against a <reference electrode>; calibration with standard <buffer solutions> converts this response to operational <pH>.

= Reference electrode
{parent=Weak acid dissociation measurement}

A <reference electrode> supplies the comparison <electric potential> for the <glass electrode> measurement. A combination probe incorporates both <electrodes>; changes at its junction can contribute measurement error.

= Acid-base titration
{parent=Weak acid dissociation measurement}

An <acid-base titration> adds measured quantities of a standardized <chemical acid> or <chemical base> to determine an initially unknown acid or base inventory through <neutralization>. Monitoring <pH> also constrains <chemical equilibrium> parameters such as the <acid dissociation constant>.

= Volumetric flask
{parent=Weak acid dissociation measurement}

A <volumetric flask> prepares a <chemical solution> at a specified total <volume (thermodynamics)>, using a calibration mark.

= Pipette
{parent=Weak acid dissociation measurement}

A <pipette> transfers a measured portion of a <chemical solution>.

= Burette
{parent=Weak acid dissociation measurement}

A <burette> delivers an adjustable measured <volume (thermodynamics)> of a <chemical solution>; the change in its reading gives the delivered <volume (thermodynamics)> in an <acid-base titration>.

= Acid dissociation constant
{parent=Acid (chemistry)}
{wiki}
{title2=$K_a$}

The <acid dissociation constant> quantifies the <chemical equilibrium> for loss of one <proton> from a <chemical acid>. In <water>, write the actual proton transfer as $\mathrm{HA}+\mathrm H_2\mathrm O\rightleftharpoons\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^++\mathrm A^-$. Its full <dimensionless> <equilibrium constant> is
$$
K_{\mathrm{rxn}}=\frac{a_{\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+}a_{\mathrm A^-}}{a_{\mathrm{HA}}a_{\mathrm H_2\mathrm O}}.
$$
The usual aqueous <acid dissociation constant> uses the <solvent> convention with <water> <thermodynamic activity> approximated by one:
$$
K_a=\frac{a_{\mathrm H^+}a_{\mathrm A^-}}{a_{\mathrm{HA}}},\qquad \mathrm pK_a=-\log_{10}K_a.
$$
Here hydrated <hydrogen ions> are understood; $\mathrm H^+$ is shorthand, not an isolated bare <proton> in the <chemical solution>. All <thermodynamic activities> and $K_a$ are <dimensionless quantities>, using consistent <standard states>. If <water> <thermodynamic activity> differs appreciably from one, retain it in the full <equilibrium constant> instead of silently mixing conventions.

With <activity coefficients> $\gamma_i$ and reference <concentration> $c^\circ=1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, put $a_i=\gamma_i[i]/c^\circ$ for each <solute>. Then
$$
K_a=\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm H^+}\gamma_{\mathrm A^-}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{HA}}}
\frac{[\mathrm H^+][\mathrm A^-]}{c^\circ[\mathrm{HA}]}
\approx\frac{[\mathrm H^+][\mathrm A^-]}{c^\circ[\mathrm{HA}]}.
$$
The last expression is the dilute ideal <concentration> approximation, not the definition in a nonideal <chemical solution>. Omitting $c^\circ$ instead produces a quotient with units of <concentration>; its numerical value depends on the chosen units. Do not take a <logarithm> of that dimensional quotient. Conditional <concentration> quotients can change with <ionic strength> even at fixed <temperature>, whereas the thermodynamic <acid dissociation constant> retains the <activity coefficient> corrections. The <IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/acid dissociation constant> entry distinguishes these conventions.

At the same <temperature>, <solvent> and <standard states>, a larger <acid dissociation constant>, equivalently a smaller <pKa>, indicates a stronger tendency to donate a <proton>. It does not specify an <analytical concentration>, a <pH>, or a <reaction rate>. Composition and the <weak acid mass and charge balances> are needed to predict <pH>; <chemical kinetics> addresses how quickly the <chemical equilibrium> is approached.

= pKa
{c}
{parent=Acid dissociation constant}
{title2=$\mathrm pK_a=-\log_{10}K_a$}

The <pKa> is the negative base-ten <logarithm> of the <dimensionless> <acid dissociation constant>. A lower <pKa> means a larger <acid dissociation constant> and a stronger tendency to donate a <proton> under the same <standard state>, <solvent>, and <temperature> conventions. The <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> places equal <weak acid> and <conjugate base> <thermodynamic activities> at <pH> equal to <pKa>. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/14-6-buffers[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, the Henderson-Hasselbalch equation].

= Base
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Acid-base chemistry}
{wiki}

= Chemical base
{synonym}

A <chemical base> can accept a <proton>, using an available <electron pair> to form a <covalent bond>; more generally it can donate an <electron pair> to form a <covalent bond> with another species. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/B00601[IUPAC Gold Book, base].

= Conjugate base
{parent=Base (chemistry)}

A <conjugate base> is what remains after a <chemical acid> donates one <proton>. In the <conjugate acid-base pair> $\mathrm{HA}/\mathrm A^-$, $\mathrm{HA}$ is the <conjugate acid> of $\mathrm A^-$; accepting a <proton> reverses the conversion. For example, <acetic acid> $\mathrm{CH_3COOH}$ loses a <proton> to give its <conjugate base> $\mathrm{CH_3COO^-}$. Partners differ by one <proton> and one unit of <electric charge>; they need not always be neutral and negative. <Hydronium>/<water> and <water>/<hydroxide> are also <conjugate acid-base pairs>.

At a prescribed <pH>, the ideal dilute <acid dissociation constant> determines the partition of a <monoprotic acid> inventory between its <chemical species>:
$$
\alpha_{\mathrm A^-}=\frac{K_a}{K_a+10^{-\mathrm{pH}}},\qquad
\alpha_{\mathrm{HA}}=\frac{10^{-\mathrm{pH}}}{K_a+10^{-\mathrm{pH}}}.
$$
These <dimensionless> fractions sum to one. They are the <weak acid dissociation fraction> and its complement when the only acid-bearing <chemical species> are $\mathrm{HA}$ and $\mathrm A^-$. Equal fractions occur at $\mathrm{pH}=\mathrm pK_a$ in this ideal approximation. For nonideal <chemical solutions>, equality of <thermodynamic activities> need not mean equality of <concentrations>.

\Image[weak-acid-equilibrium-species.png]
{id=weak-acid-equilibrium-species}
{title=A synthetic <monoprotic acid> partitions between <conjugate acid> and <conjugate base> as imposed <pH> changes}

For stipulated <pKa> $5$, the <conjugate base> fractions at <pH> $4$, $5$ and $6$ are $1/11$, $1/2$ and $10/11$. Here an external reservoir sets <pH>; its <ions> must supply the additional <electric charge> balance. The diagram omits those <ions> and <water> populations. It does not represent three pure $\mathrm{HA}$ samples at identical <analytical concentration> with freely chosen <pH>. In the latter experiment, the <weak acid mass and charge balances> determine <pH> as well as the partition.

= Conjugate acid
{parent=Conjugate base}

The <conjugate acid> of a <chemical base> is the <chemical species> formed when it accepts one <proton>. This is a relationship between partners, rather than a claim that the partner is a <strong acid>.

= Conjugate acid-base pair
{parent=Conjugate base}

A <conjugate acid-base pair> consists of two <chemical species> related by transfer of one <proton>. The <conjugate acid> has one more <proton> and one more positive unit of <electric charge> than the <conjugate base>.

= Base dissociation constant
{parent=Conjugate base}
{title2=$K_b$}

For the <conjugate base> $\mathrm A^-$ accepting a <proton> from <water>,
$$
\mathrm A^-+\mathrm H_2\mathrm O\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{HA}+\mathrm{OH}^-,\qquad
K_b=\frac{a_{\mathrm{HA}}a_{\mathrm{OH}^-}}{a_{\mathrm A^-}}.
$$
This <base dissociation constant> uses the same <water> <thermodynamic activity> approximation and <standard state> conventions as the <acid dissociation constant>. Multiplying the two relations cancels the partner <thermodynamic activities>:
$$
K_aK_b=a_{\mathrm H^+}a_{\mathrm{OH}^-}=K_w,\qquad
\mathrm pK_a+\mathrm pK_b=\mathrm pK_w,\qquad
\mathrm pK_b=-\log_{10}K_b,\quad \mathrm pK_w=-\log_{10}K_w.
$$
Here $K_w$ is the aqueous ion product associated with <self-ionization of water>. At fixed <temperature>, a larger <acid dissociation constant> gives a smaller <base dissociation constant> for its own <conjugate base>; this reciprocal rule compares matching <conjugate acid-base pairs>. A <weak acid> does not necessarily have a <conjugate base> that reacts almost completely with <water>. For example, stipulated $K_a=10^{-5}$ and $K_w=10^{-14}$ give $K_b=10^{-9}$.<Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/14.3 Relative Strengths of Acids and Bases>[§14.3, Relative Strengths of Conjugate Acid-Base Pairs, cancellation giving Ka Kb = Kw]{ref}

The value of $K_w$ changes with <temperature>; $10^{-14}$ is the familiar approximate value near $25\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, not a universal constant. If <water> is retained explicitly in both full <equilibrium constants>, their product instead equals $a_{\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+}a_{\mathrm{OH}^-}/a_{\mathrm H_2\mathrm O}^2$, the full <equilibrium constant> for $2\mathrm H_2\mathrm O\rightleftharpoons\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^++\mathrm{OH}^-$. The <acid dissociation constant>, <base dissociation constant> and <self-ionization of water> convention must agree.

= pH
{c}
{parent=Acid-base chemistry}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{pH}=-\log_{10}a_{\mathrm H^+}$}

The <pH> of a <chemical solution> is the negative base-ten <logarithm> of its <hydrogen ion> <thermodynamic activity>:
$$
\mathrm{pH}=-\log_{10}a_{\mathrm H^+},\qquad a_{\mathrm H^+}=10^{-\mathrm{pH}}.
$$
The <thermodynamic activity> is a <dimensionless quantity>, so the <logarithm> has a <dimensionless> argument. The definition uses <thermodynamic activity>, rather than total <chemical acid> <concentration>. Single-<ion> <thermodynamic activities> require a convention; practical <pH> measurements are tied to calibrated standards. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/P04524[IUPAC Gold Book, pH].

In a dilute ideal <chemical solution> in <water>, a useful approximation is
$$
a_{\mathrm H^+}\approx\frac{[\mathrm H^+]}{c^\circ},\qquad c^\circ=1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}},\qquad
\mathrm{pH}\approx-\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{[\mathrm H^+]}{1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}}\right).
$$
Square brackets denote <concentration> in <moles> per litre. In <water>, $[\mathrm H^+]$ is shorthand for the <hydronium> <concentration>, often written $[\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+]$; it does not describe isolated bare <protons>. Departures from ideal behavior need <activity coefficients>. The <concentration> formula approximates the <thermodynamic activity> definition.

For an ideal <strong acid> supplying one <proton> per initial unit, stipulate a total <concentration> of $0.001\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ and negligible <self-ionization of water>. Then $[\mathrm H^+]\approx10^{-3}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, so
$$
\mathrm{pH}\approx-\log_{10}(10^{-3})=3.
$$
Reducing that <concentration> tenfold by <dilution> gives $10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ and <pH> $4$, under the same assumptions. More generally, dividing the <hydrogen ion> <thermodynamic activity> by ten raises <pH> by one. This is why the scale compresses an enormous range: equal steps of <pH> represent multiplicative changes, rather than equal differences in <concentration>. These are model calculations, rather than measured samples.

\Image[ph-logarithmic-scale.png]
{title=One step of <pH> represents a tenfold change in <hydrogen ion> <thermodynamic activity>}
{description=The <logarithm> makes equal spacing on the <pH> axis correspond to factors of ten on the <thermodynamic activity> axis. The marked <strong acid> example uses the dilute ideal <concentration> approximation and negligible <self-ionization of water>. The plotted interval is illustrative; it does not bound possible <pH> values.}

Neutrality depends on <temperature>. In the dilute ideal <water> model, define the <dimensionless> ion product $K_w=([\mathrm H^+]/c^\circ)([\mathrm{OH}^-]/c^\circ)$ and $\mathrm pK_w=-\log_{10}K_w$. Equal <hydronium> and <hydroxide> <concentrations> imply
$$
\mathrm{pH}_{\mathrm{neutral}}=\frac{\mathrm pK_w}{2}.
$$
At $25\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, the textbook approximation $K_w\approx10^{-14}$ gives neutral <pH> $7$. Since $K_w$ varies with <temperature>, neutrality need not mean <pH> $7$. Nor is <pH> restricted to $0$–$14$: the activity definition permits negative <pH> when $a_{\mathrm H^+}>1$, and <pH> above $14$ when $a_{\mathrm H^+}<10^{-14}$. High <concentrations> can invalidate the dilute ideal approximation. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/14-2-ph-and-poh[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, pH and pOH].

<S. P. L. Sørensen> introduced <pH> at the Carlsberg Laboratory in 1909, as recorded in the https://carlsbergfondet.dk/en/about-the-foundation/our-history/timeline-of-the-foundation-s-history/[Carlsberg Foundation historical timeline].

= Buffer solution
{parent=Acid-base chemistry}
{wiki}

A <buffer solution> contains appreciable quantities of a <weak acid> and its <conjugate base>, and resists changes in <pH> caused by small quantities of added <chemical acid> or <chemical base>. The <conjugate base> accepts added <protons>; the <weak acid> supplies <protons> when a <chemical base> consumes them. Consuming most of either partner undermines the buffering action. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/14-6-buffers[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, buffers].

For a separate toy calculation, stipulate <pKa> $5$ for a <weak acid> $\mathrm{HA}$; this is an input, not measured material data. Initially $1\,\mathrm L$ of <buffer solution> contains $0.10\,\mathrm{mol}$ of $\mathrm{HA}$ and $0.10\,\mathrm{mol}$ of its <conjugate base> $\mathrm A^-$. In the ideal <concentration> approximation, the <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> gives initial <pH> $5$. Add $0.010\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <hydroxide> with negligible volume change. The dominant <neutralization> has one-to-one <stoichiometry>:
$$
\mathrm{HA}+\mathrm{OH}^-\longrightarrow\mathrm A^-+\mathrm H_2\mathrm O.
$$
Each <mole> of consumed <hydroxide> removes one <mole> of <weak acid> and forms one <mole> of <conjugate base> and <water>. Thus the inventories after <neutralization> are
$$
n_{\mathrm{HA}}\approx(0.10-0.01)\,\mathrm{mol}=0.09\,\mathrm{mol},\qquad
n_{\mathrm A^-}\approx(0.10+0.01)\,\mathrm{mol}=0.11\,\mathrm{mol}.
$$
The shared volume cancels from the <concentration> ratio. The <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> therefore predicts
$$
\mathrm{pH}\approx5+\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{0.11}{0.09}\right)=5+\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{11}{9}\right)=5.0871501757\ldots.
$$
This small increase illustrates how conversion between abundant partners absorbs added <chemical base>. The calculation neglects the tiny free <hydronium> and <hydroxide> inventories, changes during re-establishment of <chemical equilibrium>, <self-ionization of water>, and <activity coefficient> corrections. The displayed inventories approximate the final <chemical species> distribution; they are not an exact solution of all <chemical equilibrium> conditions.

\Image[buffer-neutralization-inventory.png]
{title=Added <hydroxide> transfers a finite inventory from <weak acid> to <conjugate base>}
{description=Toy <pKa> $5$, initial volume $1\,\mathrm L$, and negligible added volume. Bars show the dominant <neutralization> bookkeeping before and after adding $0.010\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <hydroxide>; the <pH> values use the ideal <concentration> approximation. The acid-plus-base inventory stays $0.20\,\mathrm{mol}$. Neither the bars nor the <pH> values are measured material data or exact speciation.}

A <buffer solution> has a finite <buffer capacity>: its <weak acid> inventory supplies protection against added <chemical base>, and its <conjugate base> inventory supplies protection against added <chemical acid>. Here an addition approaching $0.10\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <hydroxide> uses up the available $\mathrm{HA}$ in the dominant <stoichiometry> calculation. Close to that limit, the neglected <chemical equilibrium> contributions matter; once $\mathrm{HA}$ is exhausted, inserting a zero denominator into the <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> cannot predict the actual <pH>. Excess <hydroxide> and the remaining <chemical species> require a fresh <chemical equilibrium> calculation. This is why a <buffer solution> stabilizes conditions for <chemical reactions> over a finite range of additions. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/14-6-buffers[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, buffer capacity and added base].

For the pair $\mathrm{HA}/\mathrm A^-$, rearranging the <acid dissociation constant> gives the activity relation
$$
\mathrm{pH}=\mathrm pK_a+\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{a_{\mathrm A^-}}{a_{\mathrm{HA}}}\right).
$$
With <thermodynamic activity> of <water> approximated by one and ideal dilute <thermodynamic activities> for both partners, this yields the familiar <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation>:
$$
\mathrm{pH}\approx\mathrm pK_a+\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{[\mathrm A^-]}{[\mathrm{HA}]}\right).
$$
The <concentrations> are at <chemical equilibrium>; using initial values requires negligible equilibration changes. Below, also neglect <self-ionization of water> and competing <chemical reactions>.

Stipulate $\mathrm pK_a=4.76$ for an illustrative <weak acid>, without identifying it with a measured substance. A <conjugate base> to <weak acid> <concentration> ratio of two gives
$$
\mathrm{pH}\approx4.76+\log_{10}2=5.061029995\ldots\approx5.06103.
$$
Equal <concentrations> give a ratio of one, whose <logarithm> is zero, hence <pH> $4.76$. Multiplying the ratio by ten raises <pH> by one: ratios $0.1$, $1$, and $10$ give <pH> values $3.76$, $4.76$, and $5.76$. The ratio controls the ideal prediction, while the absolute quantities control how much added <chemical acid> or <chemical base> the <buffer solution> can accommodate.

\Image[buffer-ratio-ph.png]
{title=The <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> turns a tenfold ratio change into one unit of <pH>}
{description=The stipulated <weak acid> has $\mathrm pK_a=4.76$. Points mark <conjugate base> to <weak acid> <concentration> ratios $0.1$, $1$, $2$, and $10$. The curve uses ideal dilute <thermodynamic activities>, <chemical equilibrium>, and negligible <self-ionization of water>; it is a calculation, rather than measured data.}

= Henderson-Hasselbalch equation
{c}
{parent=Buffer solution}
{wiki=Henderson–Hasselbalch_equation}
{title2=$\mathrm{pH}\approx\mathrm pK_a+\log_{10}([\mathrm A^-]/[\mathrm{HA}])$}

The <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> estimates <buffer solution> <pH> using a <weak acid>'s <acid dissociation constant> and the <conjugate base> to <weak acid> <concentration> ratio.

Its activity form is an exact rearrangement of the chosen <chemical equilibrium> relation. Write the acid dissociation step as $\mathrm{HA}\rightleftharpoons\mathrm H^++\mathrm A^-$, with hydrated <hydrogen ions> understood in <water> and the <solvent> convention incorporated into the <acid dissociation constant>. For consistent <standard states>,
$$
K_a=\frac{a_{\mathrm H^+}a_{\mathrm A^-}}{a_{\mathrm{HA}}},\qquad
\mathrm pK_a=-\log_{10}K_a,\qquad
\mathrm{pH}=\mathrm pK_a+\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{a_{\mathrm A^-}}{a_{\mathrm{HA}}}\right).
$$
Here each $a_i$ is a <dimensionless> <thermodynamic activity> at <chemical equilibrium>. Exactness refers to this specified <acid dissociation constant> relation and the chosen single-<ion> <thermodynamic activity> convention, not to identifying the ratio with quantities initially mixed. If <water> is instead written explicitly as a <reactant>, its <thermodynamic activity> must be treated consistently. See https://eprints.whiterose.ac.uk/id/eprint/167121/1/BM_5_2020_Unterricht_Hippler_Metcalfe.pdf[Hippler and Metcalfe, Using activities to correct the Henderson-Hasselbalch equation (2020), equations 3–4].

For a toy <weak acid> with stipulated <pKa> $5$, use the ideal dilute <concentration> approximation and put $r=[\mathrm A^-]/[\mathrm{HA}]$. The <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> becomes $\mathrm{pH}\approx5+\log_{10}r$:
$$
r=0.1\ \Rightarrow\ \mathrm{pH}\approx4,\qquad
r=1\ \Rightarrow\ \mathrm{pH}\approx5,\qquad
r=10\ \Rightarrow\ \mathrm{pH}\approx6.
$$
More <conjugate base> relative to <weak acid> means higher <pH>. Each tenfold increase in the ratio contributes exactly one to the calculated <pH>. The stipulated <pKa> is not a tabulated value for an identified substance.

\Image[buffer-ph-versus-species-ratio.png]
{title=Toy <pKa> $5$: ratios $0.1$, $1$, and $10$ give calculated <pH> $4$, $5$, and $6$}
{description=Both panels show the same <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> in the ideal <concentration> approximation, with linear and <logarithmic scales> for the <conjugate base> to <weak acid> ratio. The <logarithmic scale> turns the curve into a straight line. The plotted range is illustrative, rather than a guarantee of useful <buffer capacity>.}

The distinction between <thermodynamic activity> and <concentration> is explicit when $a_i=\gamma_i[i]/c^\circ$, where $\gamma_i$ is an <activity coefficient> and $c^\circ$ is the reference <concentration>:
$$
\mathrm{pH}=\mathrm pK_a+\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{[\mathrm A^-]}{[\mathrm{HA}]}\right)+\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm A^-}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{HA}}}\right).
$$
Neglecting the final term gives the usual approximate <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation>. It is justified when the ratio of <activity coefficients> is sufficiently close to one, as in a suitable dilute ideal approximation. <Ionic strength>, including contributions from other dissolved <ions>, affects <activity coefficients>; a nominally dilute <weak acid> alone does not establish that all corrections are small. The ratio must use <chemical equilibrium> <concentrations>. Replacing these by prepared amounts or post-<neutralization> inventories is a further approximation, suitable only when subsequent redistribution is small. See https://eprints.whiterose.ac.uk/id/eprint/167121/1/BM_5_2020_Unterricht_Hippler_Metcalfe.pdf[Hippler and Metcalfe, concentration, activity, and analytical-concentration approximations].

The short derivation is to solve the <acid dissociation constant> relation for $a_{\mathrm H^+}$, take a negative base-ten <logarithm>, and use the definitions of <pH> and <pKa>. Its useful beauty is that a <chemical equilibrium> becomes a simple rule about a ratio: select a <weak acid> with <pKa> near the desired <pH>, then adjust the partner ratio. Scaling both partner <concentrations> equally leaves the ideal <pH> prediction unchanged, while changing <buffer capacity>. In practical <buffer solutions>, <temperature>, <ionic strength>, and the available inventories still matter.

= Buffer capacity
{parent=Buffer solution}
{title2=$\beta$}

<Buffer capacity> measures resistance to a change in <pH>: how much additional <chemical acid> or <chemical base> a <chemical solution> accommodates per small change in <pH>. Its differential form is a local <derivative>, rather than the total inventory available before a <buffer solution> is exhausted. Two <buffer solutions> can have identical <pH> and very different <buffer capacities>.

Choose a constant <volume (thermodynamics)> $V$ and fixed <temperature>. Let $n_B$ count added <moles> of fully dissociated, monovalent <chemical base> supplying <hydroxide>, and $n_A$ added <moles> of fully dissociated, monovalent <strong acid> supplying <hydrogen ions>. Neither addition supplies the <weak acid> component itself. The signed addition is $b=(n_B-n_A)/V$, with units $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, and
$$
\beta=\frac{\mathrm db}{\mathrm d\mathrm{pH}}=\frac1V\frac{\mathrm dn_B}{\mathrm d\mathrm{pH}}=-\frac1V\frac{\mathrm dn_A}{\mathrm d\mathrm{pH}}.
$$
The last two forms describe separate base-only and acid-only additions. The <buffer capacity> is positive for the stable <chemical equilibrium> model below and is reported in $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ per unit of <pH>. Since <pH> is <dimensionless>, the dimensional unit is just $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$; stating “per <pH>” makes the measurement convention clear. Here constant <volume (thermodynamics)> is an idealization, realized approximately by small additions of concentrated reagent.

For a sufficiently small addition, $\Delta\mathrm{pH}\approx\Delta b/\beta$. A large <buffer capacity> means a small local change of <pH> for a given addition. Over a finite interval, use the changing <buffer capacity> instead:
$$
\Delta b=\int_{\mathrm{pH}_0}^{\mathrm{pH}_1}\beta(p)\,\mathrm dp.
$$
This <integral> gives a finite dose in $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$; it need not equal the initial <buffer capacity> times the eventual change in <pH>.

= Buffer capacity of a monoprotic acid
{parent=Buffer capacity}

Assume an ideal dilute aqueous <chemical solution> with just one <monoprotic acid> pair $\mathrm{HA}/\mathrm A^-$, <self-ionization of water>, and other <ions> that balance <electric charge> without undergoing further <chemical reactions>. Hold the <analytical concentration> $C=[\mathrm{HA}]+[\mathrm A^-]$ constant. Keep the <acid dissociation constant> $K_a$ and the <self-ionization of water> constant $K_w$ <dimensionless>, consistently with <pH>:
$$
c^\circ=1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}},\qquad H=10^{-\mathrm{pH}}=\frac{[\mathrm H^+]}{c^\circ},\qquad K_w=\frac{[\mathrm H^+][\mathrm{OH}^-]}{(c^\circ)^2}.
$$
Thus $H$ denotes normalized <hydrogen ion> <concentration>, not a dimensional <concentration>. The <chemical equilibrium> and conserved <analytical concentration> give
$$
[\mathrm A^-]=\frac{C K_a}{K_a+H},\qquad [\mathrm{OH}^-]=\frac{c^\circ K_w}{H},\qquad [\mathrm H^+]=c^\circ H.
$$
Adding <chemical base> converts <weak acid> to <conjugate base>, increases free <hydroxide>, and decreases free <hydrogen ion> <concentration>. Balancing <electric charge>, including the added reagent's accompanying <ions>, gives the signed dose relative to any initial <chemical equilibrium> at $p_0$:
$$
b(p)-b(p_0)=Q(p)-Q(p_0),\qquad Q(p)=[\mathrm A^-]+[\mathrm{OH}^-]-[\mathrm H^+].
$$
Differentiate with respect to <pH>, using $\mathrm dH/\mathrm dp=-(\ln10)H$. The result is
$$
