\boxed{\beta=(\ln10)\left(\frac{C K_a H}{(K_a+H)^2}+c^\circ H+\frac{c^\circ K_w}{H}\right).}
$$
The first term is the <monoprotic acid> pair's contribution, $\beta_{\mathrm{pair}}$; the remaining terms are the free <hydrogen ion> and <hydroxide> contributions, $\beta_{\mathrm{water}}$. Each has units $\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ per <pH>. Equivalently, with dimensional $H_c=[\mathrm H^+]$, $K_{a,c}=c^\circ K_a$ and $K_{w,c}=(c^\circ)^2K_w$, the expression is $(\ln10)(C K_{a,c}H_c/(K_{a,c}+H_c)^2+H_c+K_{w,c}/H_c)$. Mixing the dimensional and <dimensionless> conventions would give incorrect units.

For $r=K_a/H=10^{\mathrm{pH}-\mathrm pK_a}$,
$$
\beta_{\mathrm{pair}}=(\ln10)C\frac{r}{(1+r)^2}\leq\frac{\ln10}{4}C,
$$
because $(1+r)^2-4r=(1-r)^2\geq0$. Equality occurs at $r=1$: equal <weak acid> and <conjugate base> <concentrations>, or <pH> equal to <pKa>. This is the exact maximum of the pair contribution in this model. The full <buffer capacity> need not have its maximum there: free <hydrogen ions> and <hydroxide> make it grow toward the acidic and alkaline extremes. When these contributions are small, the central peak lies close to <pKa>.

= Buffer capacity and concentration
{parent=Buffer capacity}

Stipulate <pKa> $5$ and $K_w=10^{-14}$; these are model inputs, not measurements of an identified substance. At <pH> $5$ the pair contribution is $0.575646\ldots C$. For <analytical concentrations> $C=0.010$ and $0.100\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, respectively, it is
$$
\beta_{\mathrm{pair}}=0.00575646\ldots\quad\text{and}\quad0.0575646\ldots\;\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\ per\ pH}.
$$
The free <hydrogen ion> and <hydroxide> contribution is the same at this <pH>, $\beta_{\mathrm{water}}=(\ln10)(10^{-5}+10^{-9})\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}=0.0000230282\ldots\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ per <pH>. The full <buffer capacities> are therefore approximately $0.00577949$ and $0.0575877\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ per <pH>. Identical <pH> fixes the free <hydrogen ion> <thermodynamic activity>; it does not fix the inventory of <weak acid> and <conjugate base> available for <neutralization>.

\Image[monoprotic-buffer-capacity-concentration.png]
{id=monoprotic-buffer-capacity-concentration}
{title=At fixed <pKa>, the pair contribution to <buffer capacity> scales with <analytical concentration>}
{description=Ideal <monoprotic acid> model with stipulated <pKa> $5$ and $K_w=10^{-14}$. Left: the pair contribution for two <analytical concentrations>, with its maximum at <pH> equal to <pKa>. Right: pair, free <hydrogen ion> and <hydroxide>, and total contributions for $C=0.010\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, on a <logarithmic scale>. Curves are calculated, not measured; the constant ideal <activity coefficient> assumption limits their interpretation in real samples.}

At one unit of <pH> on either side of <pKa>, $r=10$ or $0.1$, giving $\beta_{\mathrm{pair}}/\beta_{\mathrm{pair,max}}=40/121\approx0.331$. The partners still form a <buffer solution>, but its local resistance is only about one third of the maximum for that <analytical concentration>. At fixed <pH>, the pair contribution scales exactly with $C$; the full <buffer capacity> contains the additional $C$-independent <water> contribution. Consequently, extreme <dilution> does not justify dropping <self-ionization of water>.

= Finite titrant response of a buffer
{parent=Buffer capacity}

Prepare the same ideal <monoprotic acid> model at initial <pH> $5$, with appropriate accompanying <ions> to balance <electric charge>. To predict the new <pH> after a finite signed addition $\Delta b$, solve $Q(\mathrm{pH}_1)-Q(5)=\Delta b$ using the <chemical equilibrium> expression above. For $\Delta b=0.002\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ of added <chemical base>, the calculated final <pH> is $5.366798$ at $C=0.010\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, but $5.034749$ at $C=0.100\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$. The initial local approximation gives changes of about $0.34605$ and $0.034730$, respectively; its accuracy decreases as the partner balance changes appreciably.

\Image[buffer-finite-titrant-ph-response.png]
{id=buffer-finite-titrant-ph-response}
{title=Equal finite additions produce different changes of <pH> in dilute and concentrated <buffer solutions>}
{description=Calculated constant-<volume (thermodynamics)> <chemical equilibrium> responses, initially at <pH> $5$, with stipulated <pKa> $5$ and $K_w=10^{-14}$. Positive dose means added <chemical base>, negative dose added <strong acid>. The left panel includes exhaustion of the dilute sample's useful pair inventory; the right panel compares its finite response with the initial local <derivative> approximation. Free <hydrogen ions> and <hydroxide> are included throughout.}

For the dilute example, additions beyond approximately $0.005\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ in either direction exceed the initial inventory of one partner. The full <chemical equilibrium> calculation still produces a finite <pH>; an inventory-only <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> calculation would attempt to use a negative partner quantity. At $+0.006\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ the dilute example reaches <pH> $10.99564$, whereas the concentrated example reaches only $5.104698$. This demonstrates a finite operating range, not an abrupt cessation of all <buffer capacity>.

= Buffer capacity measurement
{parent=Buffer capacity}

A proposed modern measurement extends the <weak acid dissociation measurement> by estimating the local slope of an <acid-base titration>. Transfer a measured sample with a <pipette> into a stirred vessel; clamp a combination <glass electrode>/<reference electrode> clear of the stirring magnet and place a <temperature> probe nearby. A calibrated <burette> delivers a <chemical base> of independently standardized <concentration> $c_T$, such as <sodium hydroxide>. Record the initial sample <volume (thermodynamics)> $V_0$, cumulative delivered <volume (thermodynamics)> $v$, stable <pH>, and <temperature> after each small addition. Use a separate fresh portion for an acidward series. These are proposed measurement conditions, not reported experimental results.

For the instrument calibration, use fresh standard <buffer solutions> bracketing the measured <pH> range, with values appropriate to the actual <temperature>. The Hanna manual specifies separate rinse and calibration portions, gentle stirring, and approximately $4\,\mathrm{cm}$ immersion of the probe tip. It calls for stable readings; the sensing bulb and <reference electrode> junction must remain immersed. Rinse between standards and samples and avoid carrying rinse <water> into the measured portion.<HI5221 & HI5222 Instruction Manual: pH and mV and ISE and Temperature Bench Meters by Hanna Instruments>[pp. 45–47: calibration portions, bracketing standards, temperature and immersion; pp. 49–50 and 74–75: stable readings, rinsing, junction immersion and drift]{ref}

The readout pairs each added <amount of substance> with the measured <pH>. With negligible added <volume (thermodynamics)>, neighboring readings give the <finite difference> estimate
$$
\beta\approx\frac{c_T(v_{i+1}-v_i)}{V_0(\mathrm{pH}_{i+1}-\mathrm{pH}_i)},
$$
assigned to the interval's midpoint. High <buffer capacity> appears as a small change of <pH> for a measured reagent dose. Choose increments small enough to resolve changing <buffer capacity>, but large enough that the change of <pH> exceeds measurement noise. Repeat with a fresh portion and different increment sizes; remeasure a standard afterward to check drift. A <water> blank can reveal contamination, but its <pH> must not be subtracted from the sample <pH>. These controls are proposed tests of the measurement and model assumptions.

If additions appreciably increase <volume (thermodynamics)>, even the <analytical concentration> changes: $C(v)=C_0V_0/(V_0+v)$. Merely dividing the added <moles> by the current <volume (thermodynamics)> does not restore the constant-<volume (thermodynamics)> <derivative>. One alternative is explicitly to report a changing-volume convention. For $c(v)=c_Tv/(V_0+v)$, define
$$
\beta_V=\left|\frac{\mathrm dc}{\mathrm d\mathrm{pH}}\right|=\frac{c_TV_0}{(V_0+v)^2}\left|\frac{\mathrm dv}{\mathrm d\mathrm{pH}}\right|.
$$
This definition includes the reagent's changing <concentration> and differs from the constant-<volume (thermodynamics)> <buffer capacity> defined above.<Dynamic Buffer Capacity in Acid–Base Systems by Anna M. Michałowska-Kaczmarczyk and Tadeusz Michałowski>[§2, Definition of Dynamic Buffer Capacity, equations (1)–(3): reagent concentration in the combined volume and its derivative]{ref}

To compare with the constant-<volume (thermodynamics)> theory, fit the changing-<volume (thermodynamics)> <chemical equilibrium> model instead of treating the observed slope as $\beta$. In the same ideal <monoprotic acid> system, define $S_0=[\mathrm A^-]_0+[\mathrm{OH}^-]_0-[\mathrm H^+]_0$. For baseward additions, the <electric charge> balance at each reading is
$$
\frac{S_0V_0+c_Tv}{V_0+v}=\frac{C(v)K_a}{K_a+H}+c^\circ\left(\frac{K_w}{H}-H\right).
$$
Use a minus sign before $c_Tv$ for the corresponding acidward series. From the model parameters, evaluate the constant-<volume (thermodynamics)> <buffer capacity> at the chosen <analytical concentration> and <pH>. The fit also tests whether one <acid dissociation constant> describes the observations.

Limitations include incomplete mixing, slow <glass electrode> response, uncertain reagent <concentration>, and changes in <temperature> or <ionic strength>. Dissolved atmospheric <carbon dioxide> introduces an additional <chemical acid> system; comparable exposure and freshly prepared samples help distinguish it from the intended <monoprotic acid> response. Additional reacting <chemical species> and nonideal <activity coefficients> invalidate the simple formula. Especially when <pH> increments are tiny, measurement noise can dominate the inferred <derivative>; reporting the dose, <pH> interval, <volume (thermodynamics)> convention, and repeatability is part of reporting a meaningful <buffer capacity>.

= Neutralization
{parent=Acid-base chemistry}
{wiki=Neutralization_(chemistry)}

A <neutralization> is a <chemical reaction> between a <chemical acid> and a <chemical base>. In the <buffer solution> example, <hydroxide> takes a <proton> from the <weak acid> to form <water> and the <conjugate base>. The <stoichiometry> updates the partner inventories before the approximate <chemical equilibrium> calculation. The name does not require the resulting <pH> to be $7$.

= Mixture
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

= Solution
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Mixture}
{wiki}

= Chemical solution
{synonym}

A <chemical solution> is a homogeneous <mixture> in which designated <solutes> are distributed in a <solvent>. The distinction between <solute> and <solvent> is part of the description; <water> is the <solvent> in the aqueous <pH> examples.

= Ionic strength
{parent=Solution (chemistry)}
{wiki}
{title2=$I_c=\frac12\sum_i c_i z_i^2$}

The concentration-based <ionic strength> is $I_c=\frac12\sum_i c_i z_i^2$, where $c_i$ is each dissolved <ion>'s <concentration> and $z_i$ its signed <electric charge> number. It has the same units as $c_i$; some formulas instead use the <dimensionless> ratio $I_c/c^\circ$. All dissolved <ions> contribute, including those that balance the <electric charge> of a <buffer solution>'s <conjugate base>. <Ionic strength> helps describe the interactions represented by <activity coefficients>. See https://eprints.whiterose.ac.uk/id/eprint/167121/1/BM_5_2020_Unterricht_Hippler_Metcalfe.pdf[Hippler and Metcalfe, activity coefficients and dimensionless ionic strength].

= Solute
{parent=Solution (chemistry)}

A <solute> is a component of a <chemical solution> distinguished from its <solvent>. Its <concentration> describes how much is present relative to a specified quantity of <chemical solution> or <solvent>.

= Solvent
{parent=Solution (chemistry)}
{wiki}

= Dilution
{parent=Solution (chemistry)}

<Dilution> lowers a <solute>'s <concentration> by adding <solvent>. When the <amount of substance> of that <solute> is conserved and $c$ denotes its amount per <volume (thermodynamics)> of <chemical solution>, $c_1V_1=c_2V_2$. This conservation statement does not by itself preserve the <pH>: a <chemical equilibrium> may change as the <chemical solution> is diluted.

= Nuclear magnetic resonance
{parent=Chemistry}

= NMR spectroscopy
{c}
{synonym}

<NMR spectroscopy> measures resonant responses of <atomic nuclei> to applied <electromagnetic radiation> in a <magnetic field>. In <residual-group monitoring of polyurethane formation>, distinguishable signal <integrals> supply a relative residual-group readout; overlap or very weak signals can limit that inference.

= Size-exclusion chromatography
{parent=Chemistry}

<Size-exclusion chromatography> separates dissolved <molecules> using porous columns. Elution depends on molecular size in the chosen <solvent>; calibration supplies an estimate of <molar mass>. A trace calibrated with another <polymer> does not automatically give absolute <molar masses> or a number-count <probability distribution>.

= Infrared spectroscopy
{parent=Chemistry}

= IR spectroscopy
{c}
{synonym}

<Infrared spectroscopy> measures a sample's response to <infrared> <electromagnetic radiation>. Absorption associated with stretching or bending motions of <chemical bonds> helps identify groups and their environments. Peak positions are commonly reported as reciprocal <wavelength>, in $\mathrm{cm^{-1}}$; a lower value means lower <frequency>. https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/12-6-infrared-spectroscopy[McMurry: infrared spectroscopy and its wavelength convention]{ref}

= Stereochemistry
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

= Stereochemical
{synonym}

<Stereochemistry> studies the spatial arrangement of <chemical atoms> in <molecules>. A <Fischer projection> records this information at successive <stereocenters>, whereas a <molecular formula> only counts <chemical atoms>. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-2-representing-carbohydrate-stereochemistry-fischer-projections[McMurry: representing carbohydrate stereochemistry].

= Chirality
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Stereochemistry}
{wiki}

= Chiral
{synonym}

<Chirality (chemistry)> means that a <molecule> cannot be superposed on its mirror image. A <carbon> attached to four different groups is a common source of <chirality (chemistry)>; the definition concerns the whole <molecule>, rather than requiring this particular structural feature.

The test is spatial: apply a <translation (geometry)> and a <rotation (mathematics)> to the entire <molecule> until corresponding <chemical atoms> coincide, keeping their identities and <chemical bonds>. A mirror operation is a <reflection (mathematics)>; physically turning a model is a <rotation (mathematics)>. They are different operations. If no such superposition exists, the two forms are <enantiomers>; if one succeeds, the <molecule> is <achiral>. A different-looking flat drawing alone establishes neither conclusion.

For a simple <molecule> with exactly one tetrahedral <stereocenter> and four constitutionally different <substituents>, the mirror forms cannot superpose. Compare entire attached groups, including paths around a ring, rather than just the first <chemical atom>. Several tetrahedral <stereocenters> require a whole-<molecule> check: a <meso compound> can be <achiral>. Conversely, <axial chirality> needs no tetrahedral <stereocenter>.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/5.2 The Reason for Handedness in Molecules: Chirality>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §5.2: whole-molecule chirality, four different groups and equivalent ring paths]{ref}

\Image[tetrahedral-enantiomer-mirror-geometry.png]
{title=Spatial mirror pair with four distinct substituents}
{description=Ideal <tetrahedral molecular geometry> with distinct <substituents> A, B, C and D. The two models use the same viewing direction and the <reflection (mathematics)> $x\mapsto-x$. Their opposite signed <determinants> exclude superposition by any label-preserving <rotation (mathematics)>. The letters do not denote <R and S configuration> priorities.}

Why care about this distinction? Two <enantiomers> can fit differently into an already <chiral molecule>, just as two opposite hands fit differently into one glove. Thus a <protein>'s spatial arrangement can distinguish a mirror pair even when an <achiral> measurement cannot. <Polarimetry> provides a different kind of test: it measures a sample's net <optical rotation>, which depends on the populations of its <enantiomers>.

= Enantiomer
{parent=Chirality (chemistry)}
{wiki}

<Enantiomers> are a pair of nonsuperposable mirror-image <molecules>. The D and L configurations of <alanine> are an example; <glycine> has no such pair because its <amino acid alpha carbon> carries two identical <hydrogen> <chemical atoms>. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.1, discussion of glycine and Fischer projections after Table 26.1]{ref}

The word names a relation between two <stereoisomers>. Merely finding two different spatial arrangements does not make them <enantiomers>: arrangements that are different but not mirror partners are <diastereomers>.

= Racemate
{parent=Enantiomer}

A <racemate> contains the same <amount of substance> of each member of an <enantiomer> pair. Their equal and opposite contributions cancel in ordinary solution <polarimetry> under identical conditions. Zero net <optical rotation> therefore does not establish <achirality> of the individual <molecules>.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/5.8 Racemic Mixtures and the Resolution of Enantiomers>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §5.8: equal enantiomer populations and cancelling optical rotations]{ref}

= Chiral molecule
{parent=Chirality (chemistry)}

A <chiral molecule> fails the whole-<molecule> mirror-superposition test defining <chirality (chemistry)>.

= Achirality
{parent=Chirality (chemistry)}

= Achiral
{synonym}

<Achirality> means that a <molecule> is superposable on its mirror image. A <reflection (mathematics)> plane that leaves the complete labelled spatial structure unchanged establishes <achirality>. Absence of such a plane alone is not a complete test: other orientation-reversing spatial symmetries can also make a rigid structure <achiral>.

= Tetrahedral mirror-image test
{parent=Chirality (chemistry)}

Take a central <chemical atom> at the origin and four <substituents> at the vertices of an ideal <regular tetrahedron>:
$$
a=(1,1,1),\quad b=(1,-1,-1),\quad c=(-1,1,-1),\quad d=(-1,-1,1).
$$
These dimensionless <coordinates> describe shape, not measured <bond lengths>. For distinct labels A, B, C and D, define a signed <determinant>
$$
\Delta=\det\begin{pmatrix}|&|&|\\a-d&b-d&c-d\\|&|&|\end{pmatrix}=+16.
$$
It is six times the <oriented volume> of the labelled <tetrahedron>. A <translation (geometry)> cancels from the differences. A <rotation (mathematics)> $Q$ has $\det Q=+1$ and preserves $\Delta$, whereas the mirror <matrix> $S=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,1)$ has $\det S=-1$ and changes it to $-16$. Consequently no <rotation (mathematics)> can place every distinct label on its mirror partner. This argument uses three-dimensional <coordinates>, not clockwise order on a page.

Now let C and D both be the same <substituent> H. Swapping these identical groups is allowed. For this model the half-turn
$$
Q=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\0&0&1\\0&1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad Q^{\mathsf T}Q=I,\qquad\det Q=+1,
$$
about the axis $(0,1,1)$ maps the reflected A and B to their original positions and exchanges the two H positions. The models superpose. With distinct C and D, the same half-turn fails precisely at those two labels; the <determinant> argument rules out every other <rotation (mathematics)> as well. This is the local reason two identical <hydrogen> <chemical atoms> prevent <glycine>'s <amino acid alpha carbon> from being a tetrahedral <stereocenter>.

\Image[tetrahedral-chiral-achiral-rotation-test.png]
{title=The same spatial half-turn succeeds only when the exchanged groups are identical}
{description=Original model, mirror model and rotated mirror model. Top: the distinct C and D remain mismatched, so the A/B/C/D model is <chiral>. Bottom: the identical H groups may exchange, so the A/B/H/H model is <achiral>. The operation is an actual three-dimensional $180^\circ$ <rotation (mathematics)> about $(0,1,1)$, with <determinant> $+1$.}

For a flexible <molecule>, also distinguish <conformational isomerism> from a persistent <enantiomer> pair. A particular frozen shape may be <chiral> while ordinary internal motions interconvert mirror shapes. Turning the entire model is always allowed in a spatial comparison; an internal motion must be considered with its actual accessibility and timescale.

= Meso compound
{parent=Chirality (chemistry)}
{wiki}

A <meso compound> is an <achiral> <molecule> despite having <stereocenters>. <Tartaric acid>, $\mathrm{HOOC{-}CH(OH){-}CH(OH){-}COOH}$, has two tetrahedral <carbon> <stereocenters>. Its $(2R,3R)$ and $(2S,3S)$ forms are <enantiomers>, but $(2R,3S)$ and $(2S,3R)$ name the same meso form after equivalent ends are identified. A symmetric spatial arrangement has a mirror plane exchanging its two halves. Thus there are three <stereoisomers>, not four.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/5.7 Meso Compounds>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §5.7, Figure 5.12: meso-tartaric acid and its symmetry plane]{ref}

Opposite <R and S configurations> alone do not guarantee a <meso compound>: the rest of the <molecule> must permit mirror superposition. Likewise, a <meso compound> is one <achiral> <chemical compound>, whereas a <racemate> is a mixture of two <chiral molecule> populations. Both can give zero <optical rotation> for different reasons.

= Axial chirality
{parent=Chirality (chemistry)}

An <allene> has a $\mathrm{C=C=C}$ unit whose terminal <substituents> lie in perpendicular planes. In $\mathrm{abC=C=Ccd}$, with $a\ne b$ and $c\ne d$, the two terminal pairs can define <enantiomers> without any tetrahedral <carbon> <stereocenter>. The groups need be different within each end pair, not all four mutually different. This is <axial chirality>.<Basic Terminology of Stereochemistry by G. P. Moss/Axial Chirality>[Basic Terminology of Stereochemistry, entry Axial Chirality: nonplanar pairs about an axis, including allenes]{ref}

= Optical activity
{parent=Chirality (chemistry)}

<Optical activity> is the ability to rotate the direction of <linear polarization> of transmitted <light>. In an <achiral> <solvent>, the two members of an <enantiomer> pair have equal and opposite <optical rotations> at the same <wavelength>, <temperature> and <concentration>. The measured angle concerns a bulk sample; it is not a geometric rotation of the <molecules>.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/5.3 Optical Activity>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §5.3, Figure 5.6 and specific-rotation discussion]{ref}

= Optical rotation
{parent=Optical activity}
{title2=$\alpha$}

<Optical rotation> is the signed <angle> measured in <polarimetry>. Positive rotation is clockwise for an observer facing the incoming <light>; negative rotation is counterclockwise. State the viewing convention, <wavelength>, <temperature>, <solvent>, <concentration> and path length when comparing measurements. <R and S configuration> specifies spatial arrangement, and does not predict the sign of <optical rotation>.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/5.5 Sequence Rules for Specifying Configuration>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §5.5, discussion after Figure 5.10: no simple relation between R/S and rotation sign]{ref}

= Specific rotation
{parent=Optical rotation}
{title2=$[\alpha]_{\lambda}^{T}$}

For a dilute <chemical solution> in the usual linear regime,
$$
\alpha=[\alpha]_{\lambda}^{T}\,l\,c,
$$
where $l$ is path length in $\mathrm{dm}$ and $c$ is mass <concentration> in $\mathrm{g\,mL^{-1}}$. These conventions give <specific rotation> units $\mathrm{deg\,dm^{-1}\,mL\,g^{-1}}$. Specify <solvent> as well as <temperature> $T$ and <wavelength> $\lambda$. <Specific rotation> is conditional on these settings, not a universal number independent of the sample environment.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/5.3 Optical Activity>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §5.3: observed rotation divided by path length and mass concentration]{ref}

For an ideal additive mixture of just two <enantiomers>, write $k$ for the <specific rotation> of the positive member under the chosen conditions and $c_+,c_-$ for their mass <concentrations>. Then $\alpha=kl(c_+-c_-)$, while total mass <concentration> is $c_++c_-$. Equal populations give zero without either member losing its <chirality (chemistry)>. A small signal can also mean low <concentration> or weak <optical rotation> at the selected <wavelength>; other sample components can cancel or alter the signal. Inferring composition from $\alpha$ requires a validated mixture model and a reference measured under matching conditions.

= Polarimetry
{parent=Optical activity}

<Polarimetry> measures <optical rotation> with a <polarimeter>. A concrete modern experiment uses the vertical Vernier Go Direct instrument: a $589\,\mathrm{nm}$ light source, fixed <polarizer>, sample cell, motor-driven analyzer, <photodetector> and optical <rotary encoder>. Its readout is transmitted illumination versus analyzer <angle>. Calibrate with the same <solvent> filled to $10\,\mathrm{cm}$, then rinse with sample and fill to $10.0\,\mathrm{cm}$, reading the bottom of the meniscus to $0.1\,\mathrm{cm}$. The shifted maximum nearest zero gives $\alpha$; a fit with the square of a <cosine> uses the whole trace.<Go Direct Polarimeter User Manual by Vernier>[Go Direct Polarimeter User Manual, Using the Product steps 2–4; Specifications; How the Sensor Works]{ref}

In an ideal optical model, <Malus's law> gives $I(\theta)=I_{\max}\cos^2(\theta-\alpha)$ after solvent zeroing. Maxima repeat every $180^\circ$, so tracking the same peak matters. As an illustrative calculation, not a reported measurement, a peak shift from $0^\circ$ to $+5^\circ$ at $l=1.00\,\mathrm{dm}$ and $c=0.100\,\mathrm{g\,mL^{-1}}$ gives $[\alpha]=+50\,\mathrm{deg\,dm^{-1}\,mL\,g^{-1}}$. This calculation says nothing about <R and S configuration>.

The solvent blank controls the baseline. A reference sample and a repeat blank can check scale and drift; record <temperature>, <concentration> and fill height. Use a transparent homogeneous <chemical solution>, and remove bubbles and particles. The manual distinguishes $\pm0.1^\circ$ fit reproducibility from its $\pm1^\circ$ accuracy specification, and warns that a failed fit can give an unreasonable angle. A precise-looking fit therefore need not be accurate.<Go Direct Polarimeter User Manual by Vernier>[Go Direct Polarimeter User Manual, cosine-squared fit in Using the Product; Specifications; Troubleshooting]{ref} Neither a zero reading nor a matching <specific rotation> uniquely identifies a <molecule>; <polarimetry> must be interpreted with the sample composition and these controls.

= Polarimeter
{parent=Polarimetry}

A <polarimeter> uses <polarizers> to compare the direction of <linear polarization> before and after <light> passes through a sample; <polarimetry> explains the apparatus, readout and controls.

= Stereoisomer
{parent=Stereochemistry}

<Stereoisomers> have the same <chemical bond> connectivity but different spatial arrangements. A mirror pair of distinct <stereoisomers> comprises <enantiomers>; <diastereomers> have no mirror-pair relation.

= Diastereomer
{parent=Stereoisomer}

<Diastereomers> are different <stereoisomers> that are not <enantiomers>. The <meso compound> form of <tartaric acid> is a <diastereomer> of either member of its <enantiomer> pair.

= Absolute configuration
{parent=Stereochemistry}

<Absolute configuration> records a spatial arrangement without leaving its handedness relative to its mirror unspecified. <R and S configuration> is one naming convention; <optical rotation> is a measured response.

= R and S configuration
{c}
{parent=Absolute configuration}
{title2=$R,\ S$}

For a tetrahedral <stereocenter>, rank its four <substituents> using <stereochemical sequence rules>. With lowest-priority group pointing away, a clockwise $1\to2\to3$ sequence is $R$ and a counterclockwise sequence is $S$. Looking from the other side reverses the apparent sequence, so the viewing condition is essential. The geometric letters A/B/C/D in the <tetrahedral mirror-image test> carry no assigned priority. Neither $R$ nor $S$ determines positive or negative <optical rotation>.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/5.5 Sequence Rules for Specifying Configuration>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §5.5, Figure 5.8 and discussion after Figure 5.10]{ref}

= Stereochemical sequence rule
{parent=Absolute configuration}

<Stereochemical sequence rules> rank <substituents> for <R and S configuration>. Compare the directly attached <chemical atoms> by <atomic number> first, then resolve ties by comparing successive shells at the first difference. <Isotopes> and multiple <chemical bonds> require additional rules. This ranking convention does not involve <optical rotation>.<Organic Chemistry by John McMurry/5.5 Sequence Rules for Specifying Configuration>[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, §5.5, Rules 1–3]{ref}

= Conformational isomerism
{parent=Stereochemistry}

<Conformational isomerism> concerns spatial arrangements interconverted by internal motion, such as turning about a single <chemical bond>. Moving the entire <molecule> or applying a <rotation (mathematics)> changes its position or viewing direction, not its internal arrangement.

= Substituent
{parent=Stereochemistry}

A <substituent> is an attached <chemical atom> or group considered relative to a chosen molecular framework. Testing whether two <substituents> are identical may require comparing their full <chemical bond> connectivity and <stereochemistry>.

= Stereocenter
{parent=Stereochemistry}
{wiki}

At a <stereocenter>, exchanging two attached groups produces a different spatial configuration. The open-chain <ribose> and <glucose> examples have <carbon> <stereocenters> bearing four different groups; C2 of <deoxyribose> has two identical <hydrogen> substituents and is not a <stereocenter>. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/stereo/RS.html#stereogenic[Moss, Basic Terminology of Stereochemistry, IUPAC Recommendations 1996: stereogenic units].

= Fischer projection
{c}
{parent=Stereochemistry}
{wiki}

In a <Fischer projection>, each cross represents a <carbon> position: horizontal <chemical bonds> point toward the viewer and vertical <chemical bonds> point away. For the open-chain <aldehyde> <sugars> illustrated here, place $\mathrm{CHO}$ at the top and $\mathrm{CH}_2\mathrm{OH}$ at the bottom. This is a convention for <stereochemistry>, not a measured molecular shape. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/stereo/FM.html#Fischer[Moss, IUPAC Recommendations 1996: Fischer projection].

In these <Fischer projections>, D specifies that the <hydroxy group> at the lowest <stereocenter> lies on the right: C4 for <ribose> and <deoxyribose>, C5 for <glucose>. D does not specify every other <stereocenter>, and it does not specify the direction of rotation of polarized <light>. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-3-d-l-sugars[McMurry: the meaning of D and L].

= Functional group
{parent=Chemistry}

A <functional group> is an identifiable group of <chemical atoms> associated with characteristic <chemical reactions>. In <step-growth polymerization>, $A$ and $B$ name compatible reactive groups, not necessarily entire <monomers> or particular <chemical elements>.

= Hydroxy group
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{OH}$}

= Hydroxyl group
{synonym}

A <hydroxy group> is an $\mathrm{OH}$ group attached to the rest of a <molecule>. In the illustrated <sugars>, its <oxygen> is bonded to <carbon> and to <hydrogen>.

= Chemical substance
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

= Copper(II) sulfate
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{CuSO_4}$}

In the <catalysed peroxide comparison>, <copper(II) sulfate> supplies dissolved <ions> before addition of <sodium hydroxide> forms a solid by <precipitation (chemistry)>. The comparison tests that solid-forming treatment rather than assuming the dissolved salt is itself an effective <catalyst>.

= Amine
{parent=Chemical substance}
{title2=$R\mathrm{NH}_2,\ R_2\mathrm{NH},\ R_3\mathrm N$}

A simple neutral <amine> has <nitrogen> bonded to one, two, or three groups attached through <carbon>, with <hydrogen> filling the remaining positions in the neutral three-bond form. These are the primary, secondary, and tertiary forms respectively. An <amide> has different connectivity to a <carbonyl group>. https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/24-1-naming-amines[McMurry: classification of amines]{ref}

= Amide
{parent=Chemical substance}
{title2=$R-\mathrm C(=\mathrm O)-\mathrm{NR'R''}$}

Here an <amide> means a <carbonyl group> whose <carbon> is bonded directly to <nitrogen>; each of $R$, $R'$ and $R''$ may be <hydrogen> or a group attached through <carbon>. A <peptide bond> contains this linkage. <Chemical resonance> distinguishes its <nitrogen> from that of an <amine>. https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/24-3-basicity-of-amines[McMurry: amide resonance]{ref} and https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/20-4-amines-and-amides[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, 20.4: amide and peptide connectivity]{ref}

= Hydrogen peroxide
{parent=Chemical substance}
{title2=$\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}_2$}
{wiki}

= Acyl group
{parent=Chemical substance}
{title2=$\mathrm{R{-}C(=O){-}}$}

For the <carboxylic acid>-derived examples here, an <acyl group> is the group remaining after formal removal of $\mathrm{OH}$ from $\mathrm{R{-}C(=O){-}OH}$. It includes the <oxygen> at the end of the <fatty acid>-derived chain, so it is more than just a <hydrocarbon> region. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/class/carba.html[IUPAC class names: carboxylic acids and derivatives].

= Sphingosine
{parent=Chemical substance}

= Zwitterion
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}

= Zwitterionic
{synonym}

A <zwitterion> has opposite formal <electric charges> in different regions of a <molecule>, with zero net <electric charge>. <Phosphatidylcholine> provides a membrane-related example. This is stronger than merely having <chemical polarity> through fractional <partial atomic charges>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/Z06752[IUPAC Gold Book: zwitterions].

= Lipid
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}

<Lipids> are a chemically diverse family of <chemical substances>, conventionally associated with biological origin and solubility in <nonpolar> <solvents>. They include <triglycerides>, <phospholipids>, and <cholesterol>. The name does not specify one <molecular formula>, one repeating <monomer>, or one kind of <polymer>. Some <lipids> have both <hydrophilic> and <hydrophobic> regions rather than being uniformly <nonpolar>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/L03571[IUPAC Gold Book: lipids].

Structure explains the different jobs. A <triglyceride> has three <fatty acid>-derived <acyl groups> attached to <glycerol> and serves as an <energy> store. A typical <glycerophospholipid> instead combines two such chains with a <phosphate>-containing head, making it an <amphiphile> suited to a <lipid bilayer>. <Cholesterol> has a different structure and also contributes to <cell membrane> properties. Thus a <lipid> is a chemical building material, while a <lipid bilayer> is an organization of many <molecules>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-3-lipids[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 3.3: lipid structures and functions].

= Triglyceride
{parent=Lipid}

= Triacylglycerol
{synonym}

A <triglyceride> contains <glycerol> joined to three <fatty acid>-derived <acyl groups> through <ester> linkages. Compare the three chains with the two-chain, <phosphate>-headed <glycerophospholipid> illustrated below. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-3-lipids[Biology 2e: fats and oils].

= Cholesterol
{parent=Lipid}

<Cholesterol> is a <lipid> with four fused rings and a small <hydrophilic> region. It helps regulate the fluidity of animal <cell membranes>; it is structurally distinct from a <phospholipid>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-3-lipids[Biology 2e: cholesterol].

= Phospholipid
{parent=Lipid}
{wiki}

<Phospholipids> are <lipids> containing a <phosphate> group as part of their structure linked by <covalent bonds>. The familiar membrane-forming <phospholipids> are <amphiphilic>: a <hydrophilic> head region with <chemical polarity> coexists with a <hydrophobic>, largely <nonpolar> <hydrocarbon> region. These descriptions refer to different parts of the same <molecule>, not to two separate substances. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/P04559[IUPAC Gold Book: phospholipids] and https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/A00303[IUPAC Gold Book: amphiphilic].

A typical two-chain <glycerophospholipid> provides a useful example: a <glycerol> scaffold carries two <fatty acid>-derived <acyl groups> through <ester> linkages, plus a <phosphate>-containing head region. <Phosphatidylcholine> is one example. This scaffold describes an important subclass, rather than all <phospholipids>: <sphingomyelin> has a different backbone, and even <glycerophospholipids> need not all have two <acyl groups>. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/lipid/lip1n2.html[IUPAC–IUB, Nomenclature of Lipids (1976), Lip-1.5 and Lip-2.1–2.6].

\Image[phospholipid-amphiphilic-schematic.png]
{title=Polar head and <hydrophobic> chains of an illustrative <glycerophospholipid>}
{description=Colored regions represent a <phosphate>-containing head, a <glycerol> scaffold, and two <fatty acid>-derived chains. The small paired-chain symbol is shorthand for this illustrative subclass. Shapes and connectors show organization, not <chemical atoms>, exact <chemical bonds>, chain lengths, or a measured <molecular geometry>. The head is not assigned a universal <electric charge>.}

Polar does not mean negatively charged overall. At near-neutral <pH>, <phosphatidylcholine> is <zwitterionic>: its <phosphate> region carries negative <electric charge> and its <choline> region positive <electric charge>, with zero net <electric charge> for the <molecule>. Counting those opposite charges gives $(-1)+(+1)=0$; it does not remove their local interactions with <water>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK21726/[Lodish et al., Molecular Cell Biology: membrane lipids].

In a <lipid bilayer>, two <membrane leaflets> orient their <hydrophilic> heads toward the <water> on either side and their <hydrophobic> chains toward the shared interior. The <hydrophobic effect> helps explain this organization through the <free energy> of the <water> and <lipids> together. It is not a special repulsive <chemical bond> between chains and <water>. Which organization is favored also depends on <molecular geometry>, <temperature>, <concentration>, and the surrounding <chemical solution>; a <phospholipid> label alone does not guarantee a <lipid bilayer>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26871/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: the lipid bilayer].

\Image[phospholipid-bilayer-organization.png]
{title=Two <membrane leaflets> shelter a <hydrophobic> interior between water-facing heads}
{description=An illustrative <phospholipid>-based <lipid bilayer> has <water> on both sides. Individual <phospholipids> can undergo <diffusion> within a fluid <membrane leaflet>. The heads and chains are schematic regions; spacing, arrows, and chain shapes are unscaled. This is a local cross-section, without assigning either side to a particular <biological cell> compartment.}

This organization supplies a flexible barrier for compartments. A fluid <lipid bilayer> allows <lipids> to undergo <diffusion> within each <membrane leaflet>; barrier function therefore coexists with motion of <molecules>, as in the <fluid mosaic model>. It is not completely impermeable: small <nonpolar> <molecules> such as <molecular oxygen> and <carbon dioxide> cross much more readily than <ions>. In a <cell membrane>, <ion channels> and other <membrane transport proteins> provide selective routes; the existing <membrane transport> article explains their driving forces. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26815/[Alberts et al.: principles of membrane transport].

In 1925, E. Gorter and F. Grendel extracted <lipids> from <red blood cells> and compared the <surface area> of a one-<molecule>-thick film on <water> with the estimated total <surface area> of the <red blood cells>. Their table gave ratios near two, supporting a two-layer covering; they also proposed heads facing the aqueous surroundings on both sides. The inference depends on recovering the relevant <lipids>, estimating the <red blood cell> <surface area> correctly, and comparing compatible packing in the film and the <cell membrane>. It was indirect evidence, not an image of the <lipid bilayer>. The paper itself notes possible losses during evaporation. See https://doi.org/10.1084/jem.41.4.439[Gorter and Grendel, On Bimolecular Layers of Lipoids on the Chromocytes of the Blood, Journal of Experimental Medicine 41 (1925), 439–443].

= Glycerophospholipid
{parent=Phospholipid}
{wiki}

<Glycerophospholipids> are <phospholipids> built on a <glycerol>-<phosphate> scaffold. The illustrated two-<acyl group> form is common, but the subclass also includes other substitutions and one-chain derivatives. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/lipid/lip1n2.html[IUPAC–IUB lipid nomenclature, Lip-2.2 and Lip-2.6].

= Phosphatidylcholine
{parent=Glycerophospholipid}
{wiki}

= PC
{c}
{synonym}

<Phosphatidylcholine>, abbreviated <PC>, is a <glycerophospholipid> with a <phosphate>-<choline> head. Its <zwitterionic> head illustrates why a <hydrophilic> region need not have a nonzero net <electric charge>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK21726/[Lodish et al.: membrane lipids].

= Sphingomyelin
{parent=Phospholipid}
{wiki}

<Sphingomyelin> is a <phospholipid> whose backbone is based on <sphingosine>, rather than <glycerol>. One long <hydrocarbon> region belongs to that backbone and another comes from an attached <fatty acid>. It is a counterexample to defining every <phospholipid> by the illustrated <glycerol> scaffold. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper, The Cell: the molecular composition of cells].

= Aldehyde
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}

An <aldehyde> has a group $\mathrm R\mathrm C(=\mathrm O)\mathrm H$, written $\mathrm{CHO}$ at the top of the illustrated open-chain <Fischer projections>; $R$ denotes the attached remainder of the <molecule>.

= Carbohydrate
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}

<Carbohydrates> are a family of <chemical substances> encompassing <monosaccharides>, combinations of <monosaccharides>, and their derivatives. <Glucose> is a small <carbohydrate>; <cellulose> is a long-chain <polysaccharide>. <Deoxyribose> illustrates why composition alone does not define the family: replacing a <hydroxy group> in <ribose> by <hydrogen> still leaves a <carbohydrate>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C00820[IUPAC Gold Book: carbohydrates].

Many familiar <monosaccharides> have a <molecular formula> of the form $\mathrm C_n\mathrm H_{2n}\mathrm O_n$, equivalently $\mathrm C_n(\mathrm H_2\mathrm O)_n$. This explains the historical name, “hydrate of <carbon>”, but does not mean the <molecule> consists of <carbon> mixed with intact <water> <molecules>. Nor is it a universal formula: <deoxyribose> has $\mathrm C_5\mathrm H_{10}\mathrm O_4$. The family is defined through chemical structure and relationships, not that ratio. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C00820[IUPAC's definition and origin of the name carbohydrate].

The useful surprise is the variety of roles obtainable from related building blocks. <Glucose> participates in <metabolism>, <ribose> forms part of <RNA>, and linked <glucose> units make structural <cellulose> in a plant <cell wall>. Understanding <carbohydrates> therefore connects cellular <energy>, molecular information, and material structure. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26883/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: the chemical components of a cell].

= Sucrose
{parent=Carbohydrate}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{C_{12}H_{22}O_{11}}$}

<Sucrose> dissolves in <water> as neutral <molecules>; it is a <nonelectrolyte> in this <solvent>.

= Sugar
{parent=Carbohydrate}
{wiki}

In chemical usage, <sugars> commonly means small <carbohydrates>, including single-unit <monosaccharides> and two-unit <disaccharides>. <Glucose>, <ribose>, and <deoxyribose> are <monosaccharides>. <Cellulose> is instead a <polysaccharide> assembled from many <glucose> units. The distinction counts chemically linked units; it does not classify every <carbohydrate> by sweetness or biological role. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-2-carbohydrates[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 3.2: carbohydrates].

A useful first description of a <monosaccharide> asks how many <carbon> <chemical atoms> it contains and what its open-chain structure is. <Ribose> is an <aldopentose>, a five-<carbon> <aldehyde> <sugar>; <glucose> is an <aldohexose>, with six <carbon> <chemical atoms>. A <Fischer projection> then records the <stereochemistry>, information missing from the <molecular formula>. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-1-classification-of-carbohydrates[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, 25.1: carbohydrate classification].

= Ribose
{parent=Sugar}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm C_5\mathrm H_{10}\mathrm O_5$}

<Ribose> is a <monosaccharide> with <molecular formula> $\mathrm C_5\mathrm H_{10}\mathrm O_5$. Its biologically familiar form is D-ribose, an <aldopentose> whose open-chain <Fischer projection> has $\mathrm{CHO}$ at the top, $\mathrm{CH}_2\mathrm{OH}$ at the bottom, and <hydroxy groups> on the right at C2, C3, and C4. The opposite horizontal substituents are <hydrogen>. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-3-d-l-sugars[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, 25.3: D-ribose and D-glucose configurations].

\Image[pentose-sugar-fischer-comparison.png]
{title=Open-chain <Fischer projections> of D-<ribose> and 2-deoxy-D-<ribose>}
{description=At each cross, horizontal <chemical bonds> point toward the viewer and vertical <chemical bonds> point away. Both drawings number the free <sugar> from its <aldehyde> carbon, C1. In <deoxyribose>, C2 has two <hydrogen> substituents and is not a <stereocenter>. These are open-chain reference structures, not a depiction of the predominant <chemical species> in <water>.}

Read the drawing by counting groups: $\mathrm{CHO}$ contributes $\mathrm C\mathrm H\mathrm O$, three $\mathrm{CHOH}$ groups contribute $\mathrm C_3\mathrm H_6\mathrm O_3$, and $\mathrm{CH}_2\mathrm{OH}$ contributes $\mathrm C\mathrm H_3\mathrm O$. Their sum is $\mathrm C_5\mathrm H_{10}\mathrm O_5$. The count checks composition; the right/right/right pattern additionally specifies the <stereochemistry> of D-<ribose>.

In <RNA>, <ribose> occurs as a ring within each <nucleotide>, joining a <nucleobase> to the <phosphate>-linked backbone. Its C2 position retains a <hydroxy group>, unlike <deoxyribose> in <DNA>. In a <nucleotide>, the <sugar> positions carry primes, so this is C2′. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26883/[Alberts et al.: nucleotide sugars in RNA and DNA]. Free <ribose> in <water> is also predominantly cyclic, with several ring forms in <chemical equilibrium> and very little open-chain form; the <Fischer projection> is valuable for comparing configurations, not for estimating their abundances. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3819725/[Mason et al., The Conformation of a Ribose Derivative in Aqueous Solution: A Neutron Scattering and Molecular Dynamics Study (2013), introduction].

= Deoxyribose
{parent=Sugar}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm C_5\mathrm H_{10}\mathrm O_4$}

In the usual <DNA> context, <deoxyribose> means 2-deoxy-D-<ribose>, a five-<carbon> <monosaccharide> with <molecular formula> $\mathrm C_5\mathrm H_{10}\mathrm O_4$. Relative to D-<ribose>, the <hydroxy group> at C2 is replaced by <hydrogen>. The locant 2 matters: it identifies which position loses its <oxygen>. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/BlueBook/P10.html#P-102.5.3[Favre and Powell, IUPAC Blue Book, P-102.5.3: deoxy sugars] and https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/class/nucle.html[IUPAC: nucleic acids and their component sugars].

The paired <Fischer projections> in <ribose> make the change concrete. C2 has <hydrogen> on both sides, while C3 and C4 retain their <hydroxy groups> on the right. C2 is therefore not a <stereocenter>: exchanging its two identical <hydrogen> substituents cannot produce a distinct configuration. The unchanged C3 and C4 still carry <stereochemical> information.

Replacing $\mathrm{OH}$ by $\mathrm H$ removes one <oxygen> but leaves the total <hydrogen> count unchanged: the <hydrogen> belonging to the removed <hydroxy group> is replaced by a <hydrogen> bonded directly to <carbon>. Thus $\mathrm C_5\mathrm H_{10}\mathrm O_5$ becomes $\mathrm C_5\mathrm H_{10}\mathrm O_4$. This comparison describes structures, rather than a complete <chemical reaction> or its mechanism.

In <DNA>, <deoxyribose> is incorporated as a ring in each <nucleotide>, with <phosphate> connections building the backbone. Its C2′ bears <hydrogen> where <RNA>'s <ribose> bears a <hydroxy group>. This small local difference lets the <sugar> structure identify the two kinds of <nucleic acid>; the <nucleobases> supply their sequence information. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26883/[Alberts et al.: nucleotides as the subunits of DNA and RNA].

= Glucose
{parent=Sugar}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{C}_6\mathrm{H}_{12}\mathrm{O}_6$}

<Glucose> is a <monosaccharide> with <molecular formula> $\mathrm C_6\mathrm H_{12}\mathrm O_6$. Its familiar biological form, D-glucose, is an <aldohexose>. In the open-chain <Fischer projection>, $\mathrm{CHO}$ is at the top and $\mathrm{CH}_2\mathrm{OH}$ at the bottom; the <hydroxy groups> at C2, C3, C4, and C5 point right, left, right, right. This full pattern distinguishes D-<glucose> from other six-<carbon> <aldehyde> <sugars>. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-4-configurations-of-the-aldoses[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, 25.4: configurations of the aldoses].

\Image[glucose-fischer-projection.png]
{title=D-<glucose>: six <carbon> positions and four open-chain <stereocenters>}
{description=Horizontal <chemical bonds> point toward the viewer; vertical <chemical bonds> point away. The <hydroxy group> pattern from C2 through C5 is right/left/right/right. $\mathrm{CHO}$ and $\mathrm{CH}_2\mathrm{OH}$ are condensed end groups. The diagram records an open-chain reference structure, not the predominant form in an aqueous <chemical solution>.}

The <molecular formula> can be checked directly: one $\mathrm{CHO}$ group, four $\mathrm{CHOH}$ groups, and one $\mathrm{CH}_2\mathrm{OH}$ group give six <carbon>, twelve <hydrogen>, and six <oxygen> <chemical atoms>. All four interior <carbon> positions are <stereocenters> in this open-chain structure. Neither their orientations nor the arrangement of <chemical bonds> can be recovered from the <molecular formula> alone.

In <water>, <glucose> exists mainly in six-membered rings in <chemical equilibrium> with a small amount of open-chain <glucose>. Ring closure connects the <hydroxy group> at C5 to the <aldehyde> carbon at C1; the ring contains five <carbon> <chemical atoms> and one <oxygen>, with C6 outside it. No <chemical atoms> are lost, so the <molecular formula> remains $\mathrm C_6\mathrm H_{12}\mathrm O_6$. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-5-cyclic-structures-of-monosaccharides-anomers[McMurry, Organic Chemistry, 25.5: cyclic structures of monosaccharides].

<Glucose> is useful both as a substrate in cellular <metabolism> and as a building block of <polysaccharides>. <Cellulose> chains assembled from <glucose> units help support plant <cell walls>. The same small <sugar> thus connects chemical fuel to a structural material; its role depends on how it is transformed or linked. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper, The Cell: A Molecular Approach, the molecular composition of cells].

= Monosaccharide
{parent=Sugar}
{wiki}

A <monosaccharide> is a single-unit <carbohydrate> that cannot be split into smaller <sugars> by <hydrolysis>. <Ribose>, <deoxyribose>, and <glucose> are examples. “Single-unit” does not mean a single <chemical atom> or a permanently open chain. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-1-classification-of-carbohydrates[McMurry: simple carbohydrates].

= Aldopentose
{parent=Monosaccharide}

An <aldopentose> is a five-<carbon> <monosaccharide> with an <aldehyde> group in its open-chain form. <Ribose> is an example; <deoxyribose> is its 2-deoxy derivative. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-1-classification-of-carbohydrates[McMurry: carbohydrate classification].

= Aldohexose
{parent=Monosaccharide}

An <aldohexose> is a six-<carbon> <monosaccharide> with an <aldehyde> group in its open-chain form. <Glucose> is an example. See https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/25-1-classification-of-carbohydrates[McMurry: carbohydrate classification].

= Disaccharide
{parent=Sugar}
{wiki}

A <disaccharide> contains two linked <monosaccharide> units. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-2-carbohydrates[Biology 2e: disaccharides].

= Polysaccharide
{parent=Carbohydrate}
{wiki}

A <polysaccharide> is a <polymer> built from many linked <monosaccharide> units. <Cellulose> provides a structural example. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-2-carbohydrates[Biology 2e: polysaccharides].

= Cellulose
{parent=Polysaccharide}
{wiki}

<Cellulose> is a <polysaccharide> of <glucose> units whose chains form strong assemblies in plant <cell walls>. It illustrates a structural role for <carbohydrates>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26928/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: the plant cell wall].

= Triose phosphate
{parent=Carbohydrate}

A <triose phosphate> is a three-<carbon> <sugar> carrying a <phosphate> group. <Glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate> is one example. The <Calvin cycle> supplies a net <triose phosphate> for further <carbohydrate> synthesis. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: the Calvin cycle's carbohydrate product]{ref}.

= Glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate
{parent=Triose phosphate}
{wiki}

= G3P
{c}
{synonym}

= Phosphate
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}

= Inorganic phosphate
{parent=Phosphate}
{title2=$\mathrm P_i$}

<Inorganic phosphate>, abbreviated $\mathrm P_i$, denotes the free <phosphate> pool in biochemical <chemical equations>, including <ATP> <hydrolysis>. Its constituent <chemical species> depend on <pH> and <protonation>; $\mathrm P_i$ does not specify a single <electric charge> or proton count. See the https://iubmb.qmul.ac.uk/thermod2/[IUBMB recommendations on biochemical reactant pools].

= Chemical formula
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}

A <chemical formula> uses symbols of <chemical elements> and numerical subscripts to record composition. In the <molecular formula> $\mathrm H_2\mathrm O$ of <water>, the subscript $2$ counts two <hydrogen> <chemical atoms>, and the omitted subscript after $\mathrm O$ means one <oxygen> <chemical atom>. A <molecular formula> describes one <molecule>; a <chemical formula> need not describe a discrete <molecule>.

Read a preceding coefficient separately from the subscripts. One $\mathrm H_2\mathrm O$ contains two <hydrogen> and one <oxygen> <chemical atom>; $2\mathrm H_2\mathrm O$ contains four <hydrogen> and two <oxygen> <chemical atoms> distributed between two <water> <molecules>. In $\mathrm H_2\mathrm O_2$, the second subscript instead puts two <oxygen> <chemical atoms> into each <hydrogen peroxide> <molecule>. Thus $2\mathrm H_2\mathrm O\ne\mathrm H_2\mathrm O_2$: the coefficient scales the whole <chemical formula>, while a subscript specifies its composition. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/2-4-chemical-formulas[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, §2.4: chemical formulas].

\Image[chemical-formula-and-coefficient.png]
{title=A coefficient counts whole <molecules>; a subscript counts <chemical atoms> within each <molecule>}
{description=The three columns show one <water> <molecule>, two <water> <molecules>, and one <hydrogen peroxide> <molecule>. Their <hydrogen> counts are $2,4,2$ and their <oxygen> counts are $1,2,2$. Connections are schematic $\mathrm{H-O-H}$ and $\mathrm{H-O-O-H}$, not models of actual <bond angles> or <bond lengths>.}

For crystalline $\mathrm{NaCl}$, the <chemical formula> specifies a $1:1$ ratio of <sodium> <ions> $\mathrm{Na}^+$ to <chlorine> <ions> $\mathrm{Cl}^-$. Its <formula unit> is the accounting entity; the ordinary crystal is an extended arrangement of <ions>, rather than a collection of discrete $\mathrm{NaCl}$ <molecules>. Specifying the entity matters when assigning an <amount of substance>. See <formula unit> and <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/2.6 Ionic and Molecular Compounds> (§2.6: ionic and molecular compounds).

A <molecular formula> gives an atom inventory, not the full arrangement of <chemical bonds> or the <molecular geometry>. For example, $\mathrm C_6\mathrm H_{12}\mathrm O_6$ counts six <carbon>, twelve <hydrogen>, and six <oxygen> <chemical atoms> in a <glucose> <molecule>, but does not specify the pattern shown by its <Fischer projection>. Different structures can share a <molecular formula>. This compact inventory is useful for <chemical equation> accounting precisely because it leaves the structural detail to other representations.

The choice of letters has an early explanation in https://web2.lemoyne.edu/~giunta/berzelius.html[Jöns Jacob Berzelius, Essay on the Cause of Chemical Proportions (1813–1814), §III, transcribed by Carmen Giunta]: letters made chemical signs easier to write and print, and formulas compressed information about composition. His numerical notation differed from modern subscripts; the lasting idea was to make proportions readable in ordinary text.

= Molecular formula
{parent=Chemical formula}

A molecular formula gives the number of <chemical atoms> of each <chemical element> in a <molecule>. For <urea>, $\mathrm{CH}_4\mathrm N_2\mathrm O$ counts one <carbon>, four <hydrogen>, two <nitrogen> and one <oxygen> <chemical atom>. It records composition rather than the arrangement of <chemical bonds>.

= Formula unit
{parent=Chemical formula}
{wiki}

A <formula unit> is a specified compositional accounting entity for a <chemical substance> that need not consist of discrete <molecules>. For ordinary crystalline $\mathrm{NaCl}$, one <formula unit> counts one <sodium> <ion> $\mathrm{Na}^+$ and one <chlorine> <ion> $\mathrm{Cl}^-$; it does not identify an isolated pair within the crystal. https://iupac.org/wp-content/uploads/2019/05/IUPAC-GB3-2012-2ndPrinting-PDFsearchable.pdf#page=65[Cohen et al., IUPAC Green Book, 3rd ed., §2.10.1(iii)(b), p. 51] defines a <formula unit> as a specified group of <chemical atoms>, rather than a unit of measurement. An <amount of substance> of one <mole> of $\mathrm{NaCl}$ <formula units> therefore corresponds to one <mole> of each kind of <ion>.

= Organic acid
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}

= Carboxylic acid
{parent=Organic acid}
{wiki}

= Tartaric acid
{parent=Carboxylic acid}
{title2=$\mathrm{C_4H_6O_6}$}

<Tartaric acid> has two <hydroxy groups> and two <carboxylic acid> groups. Its <meso compound> form illustrates why two <stereocenters> need not make a <chiral molecule>.

= Acetic acid
{parent=Carboxylic acid}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{CH_3COOH}$}

<Acetic acid> is a <weak acid> that partly transfers <protons> to <water>, supplying <hydronium> and negative <ions>. It is therefore a <weak electrolyte> in <water>.

= Acetate ion
{parent=Acetic acid}
{title2=$\mathrm{CH_3COO^-}$}

The <conjugate base> of <acetic acid>. It is a <reaction product> in the <conductometric saponification assay>.

= Fatty acid
{parent=Carboxylic acid}

A <fatty acid> is a <carboxylic acid> with a <hydrocarbon> chain. Its chain supplies much of the <hydrophobic> region when incorporated into a <triglyceride> or typical <glycerophospholipid>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-3-lipids[Biology 2e: fatty acids].

= Amino acid
{parent=Carboxylic acid}
{wiki}

An <amino acid> combines <carboxylic acid> and <amine> functionality in the same <molecule>; the name alone does not specify their separation. An <alpha-amino acid> has its amino <nitrogen> attached to the <carbon> immediately adjacent to the carboxyl <carbon>, the <amino acid alpha carbon>. For example, $\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH_2{-}COOH}$ is <glycine>, whereas $\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH_2{-}CH_2{-}COOH}$ has an extra intervening <carbon> and is not an <alpha-amino acid>. These <chemical formulas> describe connectivity, not the predominant <protonation> state in <water>.

Most common <proteinogenic amino acids> share the skeleton $\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH}(R)\mathrm{{-}COOH}$, with $R$ the <amino acid side chain>. In <glycine>, $R$ is <hydrogen>; in <alanine>, $R=\mathrm{CH_3}$. <Proline> is a useful exception to the displayed primary-<amine> skeleton: its amino <nitrogen> is part of a ring and has a second <carbon> substituent. The shared backbone permits assembly into <polypeptides>, while different <amino acid side chains> give the resulting <proteins> diverse chemistry. <Nomenclature and Symbolism for Amino Acids and Peptides (Recommendations 1983) by IUPAC-IUB Joint Commission on Biochemical Nomenclature>[3AA-1, Table 1: glycine, alanine and proline structures; 3AA-2.2.5, backbone and side chain]{ref}

In a <chemical solution>, <protonation> changes the <electric charges> without changing this backbone. Free <glycine> near neutral <pH> is predominantly $\mathrm{H_3N^+{-}CH_2{-}COO^-}$, a <zwitterion>. Its positive and negative groups are joined within one <molecule>; zero net <electric charge> does not mean absence of local <electric charges>, nor does it mean the uncharged drawing $\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH_2{-}COOH}$ predominates. <Glycine protonation> explains how lowering or raising <pH> changes the balance. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.1, opening zwitterion and acid/base interconversion discussion]{ref}

Building a <protein> joins the carboxyl <carbon> of one <amino acid> to the amino <nitrogen> of another through a <peptide bond>, an <amide> linkage. The formal net condensation loses the elements of <water>; cellular <translation> uses activated substrates at a <ribosome>, rather than simply mixing free <amino acids> in <water>. Incorporated units are <amino acid residues>. The interior <amide> groups no longer have the same acid/base behavior as the two free terminal groups of <glycine>, so its free-<molecule> <glycine titration> cannot be copied independently at every chain position. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.4, opening peptide formation and amide resonance discussion]{ref}

<Chirality (chemistry)> distinguishes most common <proteinogenic amino acids> from <glycine>. The other nineteen members of the conventional twenty have a <stereocenter> at their <amino acid alpha carbon>; ordinary <translation> incorporates their L forms. <Glycine>'s two identical <hydrogen> substituents make it achiral. Thus being an <amino acid>, or even an <alpha-amino acid>, does not by itself imply <chirality (chemistry)>. <Nomenclature and Symbolism for Amino Acids and Peptides (Recommendations 1983) by IUPAC-IUB Joint Commission on Biochemical Nomenclature>[3AA-3.2, note: glycine itself is achiral; 3AA-3.3 and 3AA-3.5, protein-source L configuration]{ref}

= Methionine
{parent=Amino acid}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Met},\ \mathrm M$}

Methionine is an <amino acid> represented by Met or M in <protein> sequence notation. In the standard <genetic code>, AUG encodes it, as in the worked <gene expression> example. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/15-1-the-genetic-code[OpenStax Biology 2e, 15.1: the standard genetic code]{ref}

= Alanine
{parent=Amino acid}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Ala},\ \mathrm A$}

Alanine is an <amino acid> represented by Ala or A in <protein> sequence notation. GCU is one of its <codons> in the standard <genetic code>, and supplies the second residue in the worked <gene expression> example. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/15-1-the-genetic-code[OpenStax Biology 2e, 15.1: alanine codons]{ref}

= Alpha-amino acid
{parent=Amino acid}

An <alpha-amino acid> has amino functionality on the <carbon> next to its carboxyl <carbon>. The skeleton in <amino acid> shows this adjacency explicitly. Numbering the carboxyl <carbon> as C1 makes the adjacent position C2, also called the alpha position. <Nomenclature and Symbolism for Amino Acids and Peptides (Recommendations 1983) by IUPAC-IUB Joint Commission on Biochemical Nomenclature>[3AA-1, Table 1 and its note on hypothetical uncharged formulas; 3AA-2.2.1, acyclic-amino-acid numbering]{ref}

= Proteinogenic amino acid
{parent=Alpha-amino acid}
{wiki}

A <proteinogenic amino acid> is incorporated into <proteins> during <translation>. The conventional twenty include <glycine>, <alanine>, <methionine> and <proline>; they are a subset of <amino acids>, not a definition of the whole family. <Nomenclature and Symbolism for Amino Acids and Peptides (Recommendations 1983) by IUPAC-IUB Joint Commission on Biochemical Nomenclature>[3AA-1, Table 1: alpha-amino acids incorporated into protein under mRNA direction]{ref}

= Proline
{parent=Proteinogenic amino acid}
{wiki}

<Proline> is a cyclic <alpha-amino acid> whose <amine> <nitrogen> is joined to two <carbon> positions within a five-membered ring. This explains the exception to $\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH}(R)\mathrm{{-}COOH}$ in <amino acid>. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.1, primary alpha-amino-acid and secondary proline structures]{ref}

= Glycine protonation
{parent=Alpha-amino acid}

The three principal aqueous <glycine> forms differ by loss of one <proton> at each step:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{H_3N^+{-}CH_2{-}COOH}+\mathrm H_2\mathrm O&\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{H_3N^+{-}CH_2{-}COO^-}+\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+,\\
\mathrm{H_3N^+{-}CH_2{-}COO^-}+\mathrm H_2\mathrm O&\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH_2{-}COO^-}+\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+.
\end{aligned}
$$
Call these <glycine> forms $G^+$, $G^0$ and $G^-$, respectively: a <cation>, a <zwitterion> and an <anion>, with net <electric charges> $+e$, $0$ and $-e$, where $e$ is the <elementary charge>. $G^0$ specifically denotes the <zwitterion>, not an uncharged amino/carboxyl drawing. The first dissociation removes the carboxyl <proton>; the second removes a <proton> from the positively charged <nitrogen>. The <chemical equilibrium> changes are reversible, not three permanently separate kinds of <glycine>. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.1, aqueous zwitterion protonation/deprotonation; §26.2, two successive amino-acid dissociations]{ref}

For all calculations and both diagrams here, explicitly stipulate the <pKa> values $\mathrm pK_{a1}=2.35$ and $\mathrm pK_{a2}=9.78$. These are illustrative model inputs, not measurements from the <glycine titration experiment> or a claim of universal constants. Actual <acid dissociation constants> depend on <temperature> and on the convention used for <thermodynamic activity>; apparent <concentration>-based values also depend on <ionic strength>. For comparison, the inspected textbook tabulates $2.34$ and $9.60$ for <glycine>, which would give a different <isoelectric point>. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.1, Table 26.1, glycine row: pKa 2.34, 9.60 and pI 5.97]{ref}

\Image[glycine-protonation-speciation-model.png]
{title=<Glycine protonation> and calculated <chemical species> fractions}
{description=Original three-state ideal <glycine speciation model>, with stipulated <pKa> values $2.35$ and $9.78$. The structures show net <electric charges> $+1$, $0$ and $-1$ in units of the <elementary charge>. Curves are calculated fractions, not measured populations. The vertical markers locate the two <pKa> values and the model <isoelectric point> $6.065$.}

= Glycine speciation model
{parent=Glycine protonation}

In an ideal aqueous <chemical solution>, set $h=[\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^+]/c^\circ=10^{-\mathrm{pH}}$, with $c^\circ=1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, and approximate <activity coefficients> by one. Put $K_1=10^{-2.35}$ and $K_2=10^{-9.78}$ for the stipulated <dimensionless> <acid dissociation constants>. The <Henderson-Hasselbalch equation> gives the adjacent-state <ratios>
$$
\frac{[G^0]}{[G^+]}=\frac{K_1}{h},\qquad\frac{[G^-]}{[G^0]}=\frac{K_2}{h}.
$$
With <analytical concentration> $C_T=[G^+]+[G^0]+[G^-]$, normalization gives the three fractions and mean <electric charge> in units of the <elementary charge>:
$$
D=h^2+K_1h+K_1K_2,\qquad
\alpha_+=\frac{h^2}{D},\quad\alpha_0=\frac{K_1h}{D},\quad\alpha_-=\frac{K_1K_2}{D},\qquad
\bar z=\alpha_+-\alpha_-.
$$
All three fractions are nonnegative and sum to one. At <pH> $7$, the model gives about $99.832\%$ <zwitterion>, $0.00224\%$ <cation> and $0.1657\%$ <anion>; $\bar z\approx-0.00163$. Predominantly <zwitterionic> therefore need not mean exactly at the <isoelectric point>.

Setting $\bar z=0$ requires $h^2=K_1K_2$. Taking the <logarithm> yields
$$
\mathrm{pI}=\frac{\mathrm pK_{a1}+\mathrm pK_{a2}}2=\frac{2.35+9.78}2=6.065.
$$
At this model <isoelectric point>, $\alpha_+=\alpha_-\approx0.0001927$ and $\alpha_0\approx0.9996146$: about $0.0193\%$ of each charged form remains. At either <pKa>, the relevant adjacent fractions are equal, but the third fraction is not mathematically zero. This three-state model neglects the minor uncharged form $\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH_2{-}COOH}$; macroscopic <pKa> values alone do not determine its fraction separately from the <zwitterion>.

= Glycine titration
{parent=Glycine protonation}

Adding <sodium hydroxide> to acidified <glycine> successively removes its two titratable <protons>. Two broad <buffer solution> regions appear around the two <pKa> values, separated by a steep <pH> rise near the <isoelectric point>. This is useful evidence for two separated <acid-base chemistry> steps even though the changing <chemical species> are invisible. In the approximation that added <hydroxide> only changes <glycine protonation>, $b=1-\bar z$ is the added base <amount of substance> per initial <amount of substance> of <glycine>. Then $b\approx0.5$, $1$ and $1.5$ correspond to $\mathrm pK_{a1}$, $\mathrm{pI}$ and $\mathrm pK_{a2}$; endpoints are not exactly pure <chemical species>. <Organic Chemistry by John McMurry>[§26.2, Figure 26.2: calculated two-leg titration and half-equivalent interpretation]{ref}

\Image[glycine-titration-mean-charge-model.png]
{title=Calculated <glycine titration> and mean <electric charge> models}
{description=Upper panel: solid curve includes <self-ionization of water> and <dilution> for $20\,\mathrm{mL}$ of $0.100\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ <glycine> with one equivalent of <hydrochloric acid>, titrated with $0.100\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ <sodium hydroxide>. Dashed curve retains only the <glycine> proton inventory. Lower panel: $\bar z$ against <pH> from the <glycine speciation model>. Both are stipulated ideal models, not laboratory data.}

For the solid-curve model, start with $n_G=0.00200\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <glycine> in $V_0=0.0200\,\mathrm L$ and exactly $n_G$ of fully dissociated <hydrochloric acid>; this fixes the <chloride> inventory, but does not force every <glycine> <molecule> to remain $G^+$. Let the <sodium hydroxide> <concentration> be $C_B=0.100\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ and $b=n_{\mathrm{NaOH}}/n_G$. Assume <volume (thermodynamics)> is additive and no other <ions> are present; stipulate $K_w=10^{-14}$ for <self-ionization of water> at a <temperature> of $25\,\mathrm{{}^\circ C}$. Then
$$
V=V_0+\frac{bn_G}{C_B},\qquad C_T=\frac{n_G}{V},\qquad
[\mathrm{Na^+}]=bC_T,\qquad[\mathrm{Cl^-}]=C_T.
$$
Balancing all positive and negative <electric charges> requires
$$
c^\circ\left(h-\frac{K_w}{h}\right)+C_T\left(b+\bar z-1\right)=0.
$$
Solving this equation gives the solid curve. It remains finite at $b=0$ and beyond $b=2$, where ignoring free <hydronium> and <hydroxide> would fail. At the first <pKa>, the full model gives $b\approx0.436$, rather than $0.5$: appreciable free <hydronium> makes that half-equivalent shortcut inaccurate. At the second <pKa>, $b\approx1.502$. Known acid inventory, <dilution>, and <thermodynamic activity> assumptions are therefore part of any quantitative interpretation, not optional corrections inferred from the curve's shape.

= Glycine titration experiment
{parent=Glycine titration}

The Amrita Virtual Lab procedure uses $0.1\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ <glycine>, <hydrochloric acid> and <sodium hydroxide>, two <burettes>, a <pH> meter, beaker and stirrer. A <pipette> transfers $20\,\mathrm{mL}$ of <glycine> into a $100\,\mathrm{mL}$ beaker. Calibrate the meter with standard <buffer solutions> at <pH> $4$, $7$ and $10$, then record the starting <pH>. Add <hydrochloric acid> in $0.3\,\mathrm{mL}$ increments, recording <pH> after each addition until $1.6$. Rinse the <electrode> in distilled <water>, take a separate fresh $20\,\mathrm{mL}$ portion, and add <sodium hydroxide> in $0.3\,\mathrm{mL}$ increments until <pH> $12.5$. Its observed readout is paired added <volume (thermodynamics)> and <pH>, plotted as an <acid-base titration> curve; it does not measure each <glycine> form directly. <Titration Curves of Aminoacids, Amrita Virtual Lab/Procedure>[Materials Required, items 1–8; Working steps, items 1–9: separate acid and base titrations]{ref}

These are two titrations from the starting <glycine> <chemical solution>, rather than the single acidified-sample protocol assumed by the solid curve in <glycine titration>. Retain each portion's initial <volume (thermodynamics)> and the signed added acid/base <amount of substance>; do not concatenate raw <burette> readings and assume the result begins at $b=0$. For this procedure's $0.00200\,\mathrm{mol}$ of <glycine>, each $0.3\,\mathrm{mL}$ addition supplies $0.000030\,\mathrm{mol}$ of acid or base, a $0.015$ increment relative to the <glycine> inventory. This calculated resolution is not a reported measurement.

Useful additional controls are a fresh repeat portion, independently checked acid/base <concentrations>, a <water> blank, and a calibration-check <buffer solution> after the run. Stir without striking the <glass electrode>, keep its sensing region immersed and wait for stable readings; record <temperature> and background salts. Reduce addition size near a steep <pH> change. These are suggested controls for testing the model, beyond the published steps. A <water> blank can reveal contamination but must not be subtracted directly from <pH>, which is a <logarithm>.

The standards at <pH> $4$, $7$ and $10$ do not bracket either endpoint of this protocol. Extreme readings therefore require independently checked instrument performance, and alkaline samples are sensitive to atmospheric <carbon dioxide> uptake. <Electrode> drift, incomplete mixing, <volume (thermodynamics)> error, <dilution> and changing <ionic strength> can shift apparent <pKa> values. A titration constrains the acid/base model; it cannot establish <chirality (chemistry)> or separate every microscopic <protonation> form. The two original diagrams show calculated predictions to compare with measured points, not reconstructed experimental data.

= Citric acid
{parent=Carboxylic acid}

Its <chemical formula> and structure are recorded in https://www.ebi.ac.uk/chebi/CHEBI:30769[ChEBI, citric acid].

= Citrate
{parent=Citric acid}

Citrate denotes an <ion> derived from <citric acid>. The commonly specified form has <electric charge> equal to that of three <electrons> and <chemical formula> $\mathrm C_6\mathrm H_5\mathrm O_7^{3-}$; it results from removing three <hydrogen> <ions> from <citric acid>. Forms with additional <hydrogen> <ions> attached through <protonation> also occur. https://www.ebi.ac.uk/chebi/CHEBI:16947[ChEBI, citrate(3−)]{ref}

= Hydrogen deuteride
{parent=Chemical substance}
{title2=$\mathrm{HD}$}

The diatomic molecule containing one ordinary hydrogen nucleus and one deuterium nucleus is an isotopic form of <molecular hydrogen>. It has a weak electric dipole and can supply low-temperature <molecular line cooling> in primordial gas when its abundance is sufficient.

= Chemical element
{parent=Chemical substance}
{wiki}

A <chemical element> is an identity shared by all <chemical atoms> whose <atomic nuclei> contain the same number of <protons>. That number is its <atomic number>, $Z$: six means <carbon>, eleven means <sodium>. The identity survives differences in <neutron> count, which distinguish <isotopes>, and differences in <electron> count, which distinguish neutral <chemical atoms> from <ions>. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C01022[IUPAC Gold Book, chemical element].

The same name can also denote a pure <chemical substance> containing only that <chemical element>. These two uses answer different questions. <Molecular oxygen>, $\mathrm O_2$, is a <chemical substance> made entirely of <oxygen>; <water>, $\mathrm H_2\mathrm O$, is a <chemical compound> containing both <hydrogen> and <oxygen>. A <water> <molecule> contains three <chemical atoms> but only two <chemical elements>. Joining <chemical atoms> through <chemical bonds> changes their arrangement without changing their <atomic numbers>.

This distinction makes <stoichiometry> possible: ordinary <chemical reactions> rearrange <chemical atoms>, so one can count each <chemical element> before and after a transformation. Changing the <proton> count instead changes the <chemical element> and requires a <nuclear reaction>. The <periodic table> organizes these identities into a useful map, while <atomic number> bookkeeping keeps identity, <isotope>, and <electric charge> separate.
