= Periodic table
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}

The <periodic table> arranges <chemical elements> in increasing <atomic number>, across successive <periodic table periods>. Its columns are <periodic groups>, numbered 1–18. The layout places recurring outer <electron configurations> above one another, making similarities in <chemical bonds> and <chemical reactions> easier to recognize. Ordering is by <proton> count, not by <mass>; the <mass number> of a particular <isotope> does not determine its box. See the https://iupac.org/what-we-do/periodic-table-of-elements/[official IUPAC periodic table and numbering conventions].

For the first eighteen neutral <chemical atoms> in their <ground states>, a compact description counts <electrons> in successive <electron shells>. Thus <carbon> has $(2,4)$, meaning two <electrons> in the first <electron shell> and four in the second; the sum $2+4=6$ agrees with its <atomic number>. <Sodium> has $(2,8,1)$, whose sum is $11$. These are occupancy counts, not circular trajectories of <electrons>.

\Image[first-three-periods.png]
{title=The first three <periodic table periods>: <atomic numbers> and neutral <ground state> <electron shell> occupancies}
{description=Colours distinguish <periodic group> columns only, without encoding numerical property trends. The spaces in columns 3–12 preserve the eighteen-column <periodic table> layout: no <chemical elements> occupy those positions in these three <periodic table periods>. Each cell gives a <chemical element>'s symbol, <atomic number>, and <electron shell> counts.}

Reading each row from left to right gives:
* <Periodic table period> 1: <hydrogen> $(1)$ and <helium> $(2)$.
* <Periodic table period> 2: <lithium> $(2,1)$, <beryllium> $(2,2)$, <boron> $(2,3)$, <carbon> $(2,4)$, <nitrogen> $(2,5)$, <oxygen> $(2,6)$, <fluorine> $(2,7)$, and <neon> $(2,8)$.
* <Periodic table period> 3: <sodium> $(2,8,1)$, <magnesium> $(2,8,2)$, <aluminium> $(2,8,3)$, <silicon> $(2,8,4)$, <phosphorus> $(2,8,5)$, <sulfur> $(2,8,6)$, <chlorine> $(2,8,7)$, and <argon> $(2,8,8)$.

The counting outline is short: the occupied <electron subshells> in this range are $1s$, $2s$, $2p$, $3s$, and $3p$, filled in that order for these neutral <ground states>. Each $s$ <electron subshell> holds up to two <electrons> and each $p$ <electron subshell> up to six, giving $2$, $2+6=8$, and $2+6=8$ along these three rows. This $2,8,8$ summary applies only through <argon>; it is not a general rule that each <electron shell> fills completely before the next begins, nor a claim that the third <electron shell> can hold only eight <electrons>. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/6-4-electronic-structure-of-atoms-electron-configurations[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, electronic structure and electron configurations].

The recurring outer counts make the arrangement useful. <Lithium> and <sodium> each have one <valence electron>, whereas <fluorine> and <chlorine> each have seven <valence electrons>. Such similarities help anticipate <chemical reactions>, without making all properties identical throughout a <periodic group>. <Helium> is an instructive exception to an eight-<electron> shortcut: its two <electrons> complete the first <electron shell>, so it belongs above <neon> and <argon> in <periodic group> 18. <Hydrogen> shares column 1 with <lithium> and <sodium> but has its own distinct chemistry. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/2-5-the-periodic-table[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, periodic-table organization].

= Group
{disambiguate=periodic table}
{parent=Periodic table}
{wiki}

= Periodic group
{synonym}

= Periodic table group
{synonym}

A <periodic group> is a vertical column of the <periodic table>. For example, <fluorine> and <chlorine> are in <periodic group> 17, with seven <valence electrons> in each neutral <ground state> <chemical atom>. A shared outer <electron configuration> supports similarities in <chemical reactions>, rather than equality of every property.

= Period
{disambiguate=periodic table}
{parent=Periodic table}
{wiki}

= Periodic table period
{synonym}

A <periodic table period> is a horizontal row of the <periodic table>, read in increasing <atomic number>. <Periodic table period> 2 runs from <lithium>, $Z=3$, to <neon>, $Z=10$: $10-3+1=8$ <chemical elements>. In these neutral <ground states>, the second <electron shell> gains <electrons> while the first retains two.

= Sodium
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=$\mathrm{Na}$}

Sodium is the <chemical element> of <atomic number> eleven. Carbon-fusion channels in <carbon burning> include ${}^{12}\mathrm C+{}^{12}\mathrm C\longrightarrow{}^{23}\mathrm{Na}+p$, producing sodium-23 and a <proton>. Its abundance in expelled stellar material depends on later reactions and mixing, rather than on this one production channel alone.

= Calcium
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=$\mathrm{Ca}$}
{wiki}

= Potassium
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=$\mathrm{K}$}

= Atomic number
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=$Z$}
{wiki}

The <atomic number> $Z$ is the number of <protons> in an <atomic nucleus>, and fixes the <chemical element>. It is a count, with no units. An <atomic nucleus> with six <protons> is always <carbon>, regardless of how many <neutrons> accompany them or how many <electrons> surround it. See the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/A00499[IUPAC Gold Book, atomic number].

Three independent bookkeeping questions become easy once their symbols are kept separate. Let $N$ count <neutrons>, $n_e$ count <electrons>, $A$ be the <mass number>, and $Q$ be the net <electric charge>. A <proton> carries <electric charge> $+e$ and an <electron> carries $-e$, where $e$ is the positive <elementary charge>; a <neutron> carries zero net <electric charge>. Hence
$$
A=Z+N,\qquad N=A-Z,\qquad Q=(Z-n_e)e,\qquad n_e=Z-Q/e.
$$
The reasoning is just counting: add <protons> and <neutrons> for $A$, and subtract negative <electron> contributions from positive <proton> contributions for $Q$. A neutral <chemical atom> has $Q=0$, so $n_e=Z$. For a positively charged <ion>, fewer <electrons> remain. Notation ${}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm X$ gives the <mass number> at upper left and <atomic number> at lower left, with $\mathrm X$ standing for the <chemical element>; a right superscript such as $+$ specifies the <electric charge> in units of $e$. See https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/2-3-atomic-structure-and-symbolism[Flowers et al., Chemistry 2e, atomic structure and symbolism].

For neutral <carbon-12>, ${}^{12}_{6}\mathrm C$, the <atomic number> gives six <protons>, the subtraction $N=12-6=6$ gives six <neutrons>, and neutrality gives six <electrons>. Neutral <carbon-13>, ${}^{13}_{6}\mathrm C$, instead has $N=13-6=7$ <neutrons>, with the same six <protons> and six <electrons>. Thus these two <isotopes> occupy the same <carbon> box in the <periodic table>.

For neutral <sodium> of <mass number> $23$, ${}^{23}_{11}\mathrm{Na}$, the counts are eleven <protons>, $23-11=12$ <neutrons>, and eleven <electrons>. Removing one <electron> gives the <ion> ${}^{23}_{11}\mathrm{Na}^{+}$: $Q/e=1$, so $n_e=11-1=10$. Both its <atomic number> and its twelve <neutrons> stay unchanged. This is <ionization>, not conversion into the <chemical element> <neon>, even though a neutral <neon> <chemical atom> also has ten <electrons>.

\Image[atomic-number-isotopes-and-ions.png]
{title=<Atomic number> fixes <chemical element> identity: changing <isotopes> changes <neutrons>, while forming a <sodium> <ion> changes <electrons>}
{description=The dots are counting tokens, with <protons> and <neutrons> in an <atomic nucleus> inventory and <electrons> in a separate inventory. Their layout does not depict physical positions, sizes, or orbits. Neutral <carbon-12> and <carbon-13> differ by one <neutron>; neutral <sodium> of <mass number> 23 and its singly positive <ion> differ by one <electron>.}

This bookkeeping is useful whenever a symbol must be translated into a physical inventory. <Mass number> answers how many <protons> plus <neutrons> there are; <electric charge> answers the imbalance between <protons> and <electrons>; <atomic number> alone answers which <chemical element> it is. Keeping those questions distinct prevents an <isotope> or an <ion> from being mistaken for a different <chemical element>.

= Beryllium
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=$\mathrm{Be}$}
{wiki}

Beryllium is the <chemical element> with atomic number four. Its isotope <beryllium-7> provides an important route to primordial <lithium-7>.

= Beryllium-7
{parent=Beryllium}
{title2=$^7\mathrm{Be}$}

Beryllium-7 contains four <protons> and three <neutrons>. It forms in reactions including $^3\mathrm{He}+{}^4\mathrm{He}\to{}^7\mathrm{Be}+\gamma$ and becomes <lithium-7> through <electron capture>. In the early universe its ionization state affects when capture occurs, so the final primordial lithium inventory includes the earlier beryllium inventory rather than just lithium present when nuclear burning ends.

= Lithium
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=$\mathrm{Li}$}
{wiki}

Lithium is the <chemical element> with atomic number three. Its stable isotopes include lithium-6 and <lithium-7>. Lithium observed in old stellar atmospheres is an important but processing-sensitive test of <Big Bang nucleosynthesis>.

= Lithium-7
{parent=Lithium}
{title2=$^7\mathrm{Li}$}

Lithium-7 has three <protons> and four <neutrons>. In <Big Bang nucleosynthesis> it can form directly or through <beryllium-7>, which later undergoes <electron capture>. Competition between direct lithium production, lithium destruction and beryllium production produces a valley in the primordial lithium abundance versus <baryon-to-photon ratio>. Its measured stellar surface abundance need not equal its birth abundance because stellar transport and nuclear burning can remove lithium.

= Nuclide
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=${}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm X$}
{wiki}

A nuclear species specified by <atomic number>, <mass number>, and nuclear <energy> state. <Isotopes> compare <nuclides> with the same <atomic number>; changing only the nuclear <energy> state does not create a different <isotope>.

= Isotope
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=${}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm X$}
{wiki}

<Isotopes> of a <chemical element> have the same <atomic number> $Z$, hence the same number of <protons>, but different <neutron numbers> $N$ and therefore different <mass numbers> $A=Z+N$. For example, every <neon> <atomic nucleus> contains ten <protons>: <neon-20>, <neon-21>, and <neon-22> contain ten, eleven, and twelve <neutrons>, respectively. They occupy one <chemical element> box in the <periodic table>, rather than three boxes.

\Image[isotope-nucleus-neon-neutron-counts.png]
{title=<Neon> <isotopes>: the same ten <protons> with different <neutron numbers>}
{description=Every dot counts one <nucleon>. The arrays inventory <atomic nuclei>; they do not model the positions or sizes of <protons> and <neutrons>. The <mass numbers> 20, 21, and 22 are exact counts.}

Three labels answer different questions. The <chemical element> fixes $Z$; the <isotope> specifies which <neutron number> accompanies that $Z$; the <ion> label specifies the net <electric charge>. Neutral ${}^{22}_{10}\mathrm{Ne}$ and the <ion> ${}^{22}_{10}\mathrm{Ne}^{+}$ both contain ten <protons> and twelve <neutrons>; they contain ten and nine <electrons>, respectively. Ordinary <ionization> changes the <electron> count, leaving the <isotope> unchanged. Changing the <neutron number> can change <nuclear binding energy> and the possibility of <radioactive decay>. <Isotopic abundances> can help distinguish material sources, while <radioactive decay> can additionally supply chronological information.

A <mass spectrum> makes the distinction experimentally visible: a single <chemical element> can give separate <ion> peaks at different <mass-to-charge ratios>. Its bulk <atomic weight> is a <weighted mean> governed by <isotopic abundances>, rather than the location of one compulsory peak. The historical <mass spectrograph> experiment and the synthetic <neon> <mass spectrum> below illustrate these complementary observations.

= Mass number
{parent=Isotope}
{title2=$A=Z+N$}
{wiki}

The <mass number> $A$ is the total number of <nucleons>, namely <protons> plus <neutrons>, in an <atomic nucleus>:
$$
A=Z+N,\qquad N=A-Z.
$$
It is an exact positive <integer> with no units, rather than a measured <mass>. <Carbon-12> has $A=12$ because its <atomic nucleus> contains six <protons> and six <neutrons>. No improvement in a <mass> measurement can turn that count into $11.999$ or $12.001$.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/mass number>{ref}

The left superscript in ${}^{22}_{10}\mathrm{Ne}$ denotes <mass number> $22$, not an <atomic mass> of exactly $22\,\mathrm u$. For <neon-22>, the displayed reference <atomic mass> is about $21.991385114\,\mathrm u$. Even a <weighted mean> of <mass numbers>, such as $20.1877$ for the illustrative <neon> population below, is a population average, not the <mass number> of an individual <atomic nucleus>.<Atomic Weights and Isotopic Compositions for Neon>{ref}

= Neutron number
{parent=Mass number}
{title2=$N=A-Z$}
{wiki}

The number of <neutrons> in an <atomic nucleus>. Two <isotopes> of the same <chemical element> differ in <neutron number> while sharing an <atomic number>.

= Atomic mass
{parent=Isotope}
{title2=$m_{\rm a}$}
{wiki}

The <rest mass> of a <chemical atom> in its <ground state>, a <physical quantity> expressed in <SI> <mass> units or the <unified atomic mass unit>. Here $m_i$ means the <atomic mass> of a neutral <chemical atom> of <isotope> $i$, not the <mass> of its bare <atomic nucleus> and not a population average.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/atomic mass>{ref}

<Atomic mass> includes <electrons> and depends on <nuclear binding energy>; it is not just <mass number> times a universal <nucleon> <mass>. The <atomic mass> of neutral <carbon-12> in its <ground state> is exactly $12\,\mathrm u$ by definition of the <unified atomic mass unit>. That exactness establishes the reference scale, rather than making the <atomic masses> of all <isotopes> exact <integers> in the same unit.

= Dalton
{disambiguate=unit}
{parent=Atomic mass}
{title2=$\mathrm{Da}=\mathrm u$}
{wiki}

= Unified atomic mass unit
{synonym}

The <unified atomic mass unit> and <dalton (unit)> are names for the same <mass> unit:
$$
1\,\mathrm u=1\,\mathrm{Da}=\frac{m_{\rm a}({}^{12}\mathrm C)}{12},
$$
where the <carbon-12> <chemical atom> is free, at rest, and in its nuclear and electronic <ground state>. Its conversion to <SI> <mass> units is determined experimentally. See <The International System of Units (SI), ninth edition, SI Brochure> (Table 8, footnote (f), printed p. 146: dalton and unified atomic mass unit).

= Nuclear mass defect
{parent=Atomic mass}
{title2=$\Delta m=Zm_p+Nm_n-M_{\rm nuc}$}

The <mass> deficit of a bound <atomic nucleus> relative to its separated <protons> and <neutrons>. If their <rest masses> are $m_p$ and $m_n$, the <atomic nucleus> has <rest mass> $M_{\rm nuc}$, and the positive <nuclear binding energy> is $B$, then
$$
\Delta m=Zm_p+Nm_n-M_{\rm nuc}=\frac{B}{c^2},
$$
with $c$ the <speed of light>. Releasing <energy> when the <nucleons> bind reduces the <rest mass> of the bound system without changing the exact count $A=Z+N$. A neutral <atomic mass> also includes $Z$ <electron> <rest masses>, reduced by the <energy> required to separate those bound <electrons>. Consequently $A-m_{\rm a}/\mathrm u$ is not the definition of the <nuclear mass defect>: the reference unit and constituent <rest masses> must also be accounted for.

= Relative atomic mass
{parent=Atomic mass}
{title2=$A_{\rm r}=\bar m/\mathrm u$}
{wiki}

= Atomic weight
{synonym}

For a <chemical element> in a specified population, its <relative atomic mass> or <atomic weight> is the <dimensionless> ratio $A_{\rm r}=\bar m/\mathrm u$, with $\bar m$ the mean neutral <atomic mass> weighted by <isotopic abundance>. For the illustrative <neon> population, $A_{\rm r}\approx20.1800$, whereas $\bar m\approx20.1800\,\mathrm u$ has <mass> units.<Column Descriptions for Atomic Weights and Isotopic Compositions>{ref}

= Standard atomic weight
{parent=Relative atomic mass}
{title2=$A_{\rm r}^{\circ}$}
{wiki}

A recommended <atomic weight> applicable to normal materials, rather than the result for every possible enriched sample. The tabulated <neon> value $20.1797(6)$ and the arithmetic result above need not agree in every last displayed digit. Some <chemical elements> have intervals of <standard atomic weight> because their <isotopic abundances> vary across normal materials.<Standard Atomic Weights 2024>{ref}

= Isotopic abundance
{parent=Isotope}
{title2=$f_i$}

An <isotopic abundance> is an atom fraction within a specified population of one <chemical element>.<Compendium of Chemical Terminology (Gold Book), second edition/isotopic abundance>{ref} If $n_i$ counts its <chemical atoms> of <isotope> $i$, use
$$
f_i=\frac{n_i}{\sum_j n_j},\qquad f_i\geq0,\qquad\sum_i f_i=1.
$$
The denominator includes every <isotope> of that <chemical element> included in the stated population, not every <chemical atom> of every <chemical element> in the sample. Thus an <oxygen> atom fraction among all <chemical elements> is a different quantity.

For an illustrative neutral <neon> population, take the displayed central <isotopic abundances> $f_{20}=0.9048$, $f_{21}=0.0027$, and $f_{22}=0.0925$ from the reference table, so $0.9048+0.0027+0.0925=1$. Its mean <atomic mass> is the <weighted arithmetic mean>
$$
\begin{aligned}
\bar m&=\sum_i f_i m_i\\
&=\bigl(0.9048\times19.9924401762+0.0027\times20.993846685+0.0925\times21.991385114\bigr)\,\mathrm u\\
&=20.18004638052026\,\mathrm u\approx20.1800\,\mathrm u.
\end{aligned}
$$
These are rounded reference inputs with uncertainties, not exact universal <isotopic abundances>; the long decimal shows their arithmetic result, not measurement precision.<Atomic Weights and Isotopic Compositions for Neon>{ref}

The population convention matters. If instead $w_i$ is the fraction of total neutral-atom <mass> supplied by <isotope> $i$, then $w_i=f_i m_i/\bar m$ and
$$
\bar m=\left(\sum_i\frac{w_i}{m_i}\right)^{-1}.
$$
This is a <weighted harmonic mean> in the <mass> fractions. Using $\sum_i w_i m_i$ with those <mass> fractions would answer a different question. The conversion follows directly by counting <chemical atoms> and adding their <masses>.

= Uranium
{parent=Chemical element}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 92. Its long-lived radioactive isotopes illustrate actinide production by the <rapid neutron-capture process>.

= Lead
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Pb}$, $Z=82$}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 82. Lead isotopes are important heavy products of the <slow neutron-capture process> in suitable neutron-exposure conditions.

= Barium
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Ba}$, $Z=56$}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 56. Enrichment in barium can reveal transferred <slow neutron-capture process> products, as in a <barium star>.

= Silicon
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Si}$, $Z=14$}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 14. <Oxygen burning> produces silicon-group nuclei; subsequent <silicon burning> is a reaction network approaching an iron-group mixture.

= Magnesium
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Mg}$, $Z=12$}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 12. Magnesium isotopes occur among products of <carbon burning> and alpha-capture neutron-source reactions.

= Neon
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Ne}$, $Z=10$}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 10. <Neon burning> uses photodisintegration and alpha capture, while neon-22 can provide neutrons through $^{22}\mathrm{Ne}(\alpha,n){}^{25}\mathrm{Mg}$.

= Neon-20
{parent=Neon}
{title2=${}^{20}_{10}\mathrm{Ne}$}

The <neon> <isotope> with ten <protons> and ten <neutrons>.

= Neon-21
{parent=Neon}
{title2=${}^{21}_{10}\mathrm{Ne}$}

The <neon> <isotope> with ten <protons> and eleven <neutrons>.

= Neon-22
{parent=Neon}
{title2=${}^{22}_{10}\mathrm{Ne}$}

The <neon> <isotope> with ten <protons> and twelve <neutrons>.

= Cobalt
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Co}$, $Z=27$}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 27. Cobalt-56 is the intermediate radioactive nucleus in the <nickel-56 decay chain>.

= Nickel
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Ni}$, $Z=28$}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 28. Radioactive nickel-56 is produced in explosive burning; the <nickel-56 decay chain> contributes energy to supernova emission.

= Nickel-56 decay chain
{parent=Nickel}
{title2=$^{56}\mathrm{Ni}\longrightarrow{}^{56}\mathrm{Co}\longrightarrow{}^{56}\mathrm{Fe}$}

The radioactive chain converts nickel-56 through cobalt-56 into iron-56, releasing energy that can power supernova light curves. <Electron capture> and, where allowed, <beta-plus decay> convert protons into neutrons; gamma rays and charged decay products deposit some of the released energy. Ionization and escape of decay radiation affect the observed heating.

= Manganese

{title2=$\mathrm{Mn}$, $Z=25$}

= Iron
{parent=Chemical element}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Fe}$, $Z=26$}

The <chemical element> with atomic number 26. Iron-group <nuclear binding energy> and <silicon burning> are central to the termination of ordinary energy-producing stellar fusion.

The diatomic form of <oxygen>. Modern terrestrial abundance is maintained largely by oxygenic <photosynthesis>, balanced by chemical and biological sinks.

= Carbon-13

The <carbon> <isotope> with six <protons> and seven <neutrons>. A neutral <carbon-13> <chemical atom> has six <electrons>.

= Helium
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=$\mathrm{He}$}
{wiki}

= Helium-3
{parent=Helium}
{title2=$^3\mathrm{He}$}
{wiki}

This stable <helium> isotope contains two <protons> and one <neutron>. In solar proton-proton burning it is an intermediate: two such nuclei can form <helium-4> and return two <protons>, or one can capture <helium-4> to enter the second chain branch. The balance between production and destruction determines the <helium-3 equilibrium abundance> and its <helium-3 relaxation time>.

= Helium-4
{parent=Helium}
{title2=$^4\mathrm{He}$}

Helium-4 is the stable helium isotope with two protons and two neutrons; its nucleus is an alpha particle. Its strong binding makes it the principal neutron-containing product of primordial <Big Bang nucleosynthesis>.

= Hydrogen
{parent=Chemical element}
{title2=$\mathrm H$}
{wiki}

= Metallic hydrogen
{parent=Hydrogen}
{wiki}

Metallic hydrogen is a high-pressure conducting state of <hydrogen>. Its fluid form in the deep interiors of <gas giants> supplies a conducting medium for a <planetary dynamo>, unlike the iron-alloy conducting cores of <terrestrial planets>.

= Deuterium
{parent=Hydrogen}
{title2=$^2\mathrm H$}
{wiki}

This stable <hydrogen> isotope has one <proton> and one <neutron>. In the <Proton–proton chain> it is produced by the slow weak proton-pair reaction and is rapidly consumed by <proton> capture to make <helium-3>. Its short nuclear lifetime inside a stellar core allows a <deuterium quasi-equilibrium in proton-proton burning> approximation.

= Hydrogen anion
{parent=Hydrogen}
{title2=$\mathrm H^-$}
{wiki}

= Negative hydrogen ion
{synonym}

A negative hydrogen ion is a <hydrogen> nucleus with two bound electrons. Its weakly bound extra electron permits absorption at visible and infrared wavelengths in cool stellar atmospheres.

= 2-Bromo-2-methylbutane

The <chemical compound> used in the <conductometric determination of activation energy>. Its <chemical formula> and alternative name <2-bromo-2-methylbutane> are listed by the <NIST Chemistry WebBook, SRD 69/Butane, 2-bromo-2-methyl->{ref}.

= Benzene
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm C_6\mathrm H_6$}

<Benzene> has a planar ring of six <carbon> <chemical atoms>, each bearing one <hydrogen>. Its $\pi$ <electron density> is distributed over the ring; the alternating <covalent bonds> in one <resonance structure> do not describe alternating actual <bond lengths>. https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/15-2-structure-and-stability-of-benzene[McMurry: benzene structure]{ref}

= Phenol
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm C_6\mathrm H_5\mathrm{OH}$}

<Phenol> is <benzene> with one ring <hydrogen> replaced by a <hydroxy group>. That O–H group supplies the <hydrogen bond donor> in the experiment under <hydrogen bond acceptor>. https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/17-1-naming-alcohols-and-phenols[McMurry: phenol naming and structures]{ref}

= Sodium chloride
{parent=Chemical compound}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{NaCl}$}

<Sodium chloride> contains <sodium> <cations> $\mathrm{Na}^+$ and <chlorine> <anions> $\mathrm{Cl}^-$ in a $1:1$ ratio; its <formula unit> has zero net <electric charge>.

= Glycerol
{parent=Chemical compound}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{C}_3\mathrm{H}_8\mathrm{O}_3$}

<Glycerol> has three <carbon> <chemical atoms> and three $\mathrm{OH}$ groups. It supplies the scaffold of <triglycerides> and <glycerophospholipids>, but is not itself a long <hydrocarbon> chain. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-3-lipids[Biology 2e: glycerol].

= Allene
{parent=Chemical compound}
{wiki}

An <allene> contains the unit $\mathrm{C=C=C}$ with two consecutive <carbon>–<carbon> <covalent bonds> of order two. Suitable terminal <substituents> give <axial chirality>.

= Hydrocarbon
{parent=Chemical compound}
{wiki}

A <hydrocarbon> contains only <carbon> and <hydrogen>. A <fatty acid> also contains <oxygen>; calling its long chain a <hydrocarbon> region does not make the whole <fatty acid> a <hydrocarbon>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/H02889[IUPAC Gold Book: hydrocarbons].

= Ethane
{parent=Hydrocarbon}
{title2=$\mathrm{C_2H_6}$}

= Ester
{parent=Chemical compound}

The <carboxylic acid>-derived <ester> linkages in a <triglyceride> or typical <glycerophospholipid> have the form $\mathrm{R{-}C(=O){-}O{-}R'}$. Here the <acyl group> is attached through <oxygen> to the <glycerol>-derived part. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/BlueBook/P6.html[IUPAC Blue Book, P-65.6: esters].

= Thiol
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm{R-SH}$}
{wiki}

A <thiol> is a <chemical compound> of the form $\mathrm{R-SH}$, with an organic group $R$ attached to <sulfur>. The <conjugate base> is $\mathrm{R-S^-}$; <cysteine> and <mercaptoethanol> supply examples of <thiol> groups. Two <thiol> groups can undergo <oxidation> to form a <disulfide bond>. https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/T06359[IUPAC Gold Book: thiols]{ref}

= Ellman's reagent
{c}
{parent=Chemical compound}
{wiki}

= DTNB
{c}
{synonym}

<Ellman's reagent> is 5,5'-dithiobis(2-nitrobenzoic acid), abbreviated <DTNB>, a <disulfide> used to quantify accessible <thiols>. Reaction releases the colored TNB species measured by <absorbance> at <wavelength> $412\,\mathrm{nm}$. The <disulfide bond> experiment above explains why a <thiol> measurement can become an indirect bridge count only after controlled <reduction>. https://assets.thermofisher.com/TFS-Assets/LSG/manuals/MAN0011216_Ellmans_Reag_UG.pdf[Pierce Biotechnology: identity and use of Ellman's reagent]{ref}

= Diol
{parent=Chemical compound}

A <diol> has two <hydroxy groups>. Its two groups can serve as joining ends in <polyaddition>; the rest of its <molecule> can already be a long chain.

= Isocyanate
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm{R{-}N=C=O}$}

An <isocyanate> bears an $\mathrm{R{-}N=C=O}$ group. A <molecule> bearing two such groups can react with a <diol> to form <polyurethane> connections.

= Urea
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$(\mathrm{NH}_2)_2\mathrm{CO}$}
{wiki}

Urea is the <chemical compound> with <molecular formula> $\mathrm{CH}_4\mathrm N_2\mathrm O$ and structure $(\mathrm{NH}_2)_2\mathrm{CO}$. See the https://webbook.nist.gov/cgi/cbook.cgi?ID=C57136&Mask=20[NIST Chemistry WebBook, urea: formula and structure]. Concentrated urea is used to promote <protein denaturation> in the <ribonuclease A refolding experiment>.

= 2-Mercaptoethanol
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm{HOCH}_2\mathrm{CH}_2\mathrm{SH}$}
{wiki}

= Mercaptoethanol
{synonym}

2-Mercaptoethanol is the <chemical compound> $\mathrm{HOCH}_2\mathrm{CH}_2\mathrm{SH}$; see the https://webbook.nist.gov/cgi/cbook.cgi?ID=C60242&Units=SI[NIST Chemistry WebBook, 2-mercaptoethanol: structure and names]. In the <ribonuclease A refolding experiment>, it breaks <protein disulfide bonds>, while the <peptide bonds> of the <protein backbone> remain intact. This separates a change in crosslinks from cutting the <polypeptide chain>.

= Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate
{parent=Chemical compound}
{wiki}

<Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate> is a <cofactor> used in <metabolism>. Its oxidized and reduced forms, <NADP+> and <NADPH>, carry <electrons> between coupled <chemical reactions>. In <oxygenic photosynthesis>, <light-dependent reactions> produce <NADPH> for the <Calvin cycle>, which returns <NADP+> for reuse. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: recycling NADP+ and NADPH]{ref}.

= NADP+
{c}
{parent=Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate}
{title2=$\mathrm{NADP}^{+}$}

= Oxidized NADP
{synonym}

<NADP+> is the oxidized form of <nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate>. It can accept <electrons> to form <NADPH>; the <light-dependent reactions> supply this conversion during <oxygenic photosynthesis>.

= NADPH
{c}
{parent=Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate}
{title2=$\mathrm{NADPH}$}

<NADPH> is the reduced form of <nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate>. It carries <electrons> used in synthesis; in the <Calvin cycle> it becomes the oxidized form <NADP+>, available for reuse by <light-dependent reactions>. <NADPH> and <ATP> serve different roles even though both connect <light> capture to <carbon fixation>.

= Chloromethane
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm{CH}_3\mathrm{Cl}$}
{wiki}

= Methyl chloride
{synonym}

An organochlorine gas with biological as well as possible nonbiological sources. Its specialized biological production is a <secondary metabolic byproduct> example.

= Dimethyl sulfide
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$(\mathrm{CH}_3)_2\mathrm S$}
{wiki}

= DMS
{c}
{synonym}

An organic sulfur gas emitted through biological processing, including marine production associated with sulfur-containing metabolites. It is a <secondary metabolic byproduct> example and a possible <atmospheric biosignature gas> whose detectability depends on production and photochemical destruction.

= Ozone
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm O_3$}
{wiki}

A triatomic form of <oxygen>, produced photochemically from <molecular oxygen>. The <ozone layer> absorbs ultraviolet radiation; ozone can trace atmospheric oxygen without being directly emitted by metabolism.

= Nitrous oxide
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm N_2\mathrm O$}
{wiki}

A nitrogen oxide produced substantially by microbial nitrogen cycling on modern <Earth>. Its atmospheric abundance and lifetime make it a candidate <atmospheric biosignature gas>, with context-dependent abiotic alternatives.

= Phosphine
{parent=Chemical compound}
{title2=$\mathrm{PH}_3$}
{wiki}

Phosphine is $\mathrm{PH}_3$. Its abundance in giant-planet observable atmospheres can depend on transport from deeper layers faster than local chemical destruction.

= Ammonia
{title2=$\mathrm{NH}_3$}

= Methane
{title2=$\mathrm{CH}_4$}
{parent=Chemical compound}
{wiki}

= Methanogenesis
{parent=Methane}
{wiki}

Methanogenesis is biological production of <methane> by anaerobic microorganisms. Waterlogged soil and lake sediments can favour it. Warming or thaw changes substrate availability, but production is distinct from atmospheric emission because oxidation and transport intervene.

= Water
{title2=$\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}$}
{parent=Chemical compound}
{wiki}

= Self-ionization of water
{parent=Water}
{wiki}

Two <water> <molecules> can exchange a <proton>, producing <hydronium> and <hydroxide> <ions>: $2\mathrm H_2\mathrm O\rightleftharpoons\mathrm H_3\mathrm O^++\mathrm{OH}^-$. Even pure <water> therefore supplies <ions>; extreme <dilution> eventually invalidates the <strong acid> assumption that it supplies all the <hydronium>.

= Ice
{parent=Water}
{title2=$\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}(s)$}
{wiki}

= Snowflake
{parent=Ice}
{wiki}

A snowflake is an <ice> crystal or an aggregate of <ice> crystals grown in atmospheric conditions. Its morphology depends on vapour supersaturation, transport, <temperature> and crystal anisotropy. Not every snowflake is dendritic.

= Dendritic growth of a snowflake
{parent=Snowflake}

An exposed tip receives vapour more efficiently than a sheltered surface and can amplify its lead, producing <crystal dendrites> and side branches. The planar thermal <solidification> mechanism is an analogy, not a complete vapour-growth model. The <hexagonal ice crystal anisotropy> selects six preferred directions; an isotropic linear instability alone does not select six arms.

= Steady state
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

A <steady state (chemistry)> has time-independent concentrations because formation and removal rates balance for the species considered. Nonzero reaction fluxes can continue, so a steady state need not be <chemical equilibrium>. A <quasi-steady-state approximation> imposes this balance approximately for a fast intermediate while slower species evolve.

= Physical chemistry
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

= Chemical polarity
{parent=Physical chemistry}
{wiki}

<Chemical polarity> concerns unequal distribution of <electron density> in a <chemical bond> or <molecule>. A <polar molecule> has a permanent <electric dipole moment>; the effects of individual bonds must be combined with their spatial arrangement. In a symmetric linear <carbon dioxide> <molecule>, opposing bond-dipole contributions cancel. In <water>, its <bent molecular geometry> prevents that cancellation, giving a permanent <electric dipole moment>.https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/7-6-molecular-structure-and-polarity[Chemistry 2e, 7.6: geometry and molecular polarity]{ref}

= Polar molecule
{parent=Chemical polarity}

A <polar molecule> has a nonzero permanent <electric dipole moment> because of its internal <electric charge> distribution. It can still have zero total <electric charge>. <Water> is polar; a <nonpolar molecule> has no permanent <electric dipole moment>.

= Nonpolar molecule
{parent=Chemical polarity}

= Nonpolar
{synonym}

A <nonpolar molecule> has no permanent <electric dipole moment>. This distinguishes its charge distribution from the separated partial charges of a polar <molecule>.

= Hydrophilicity
{parent=Physical chemistry}
{wiki}

= Hydrophilic
{synonym}

<Hydrophilicity> describes affinity for <water>. Regions with <chemical polarity> or <electric charge> can interact favorably with <water>, including through <hydrogen bonds> where suitable groups are present. <Hydrophilic> regions of membrane-forming <phospholipids> face the <water> on either side of a <lipid bilayer>. Such affinity does not mean forming <covalent bonds> with every neighboring substance, nor does it require a net negative <electric charge>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26883/[Alberts et al.: the chemical components of a cell].

= Amphiphile
{parent=Physical chemistry}
{wiki}

= Amphiphilic
{synonym}

= Amphipathic
{synonym}

An <amphiphile> is a <molecule> containing distinct <hydrophilic> and <hydrophobic> regions. <Amphiphilic> and <amphipathic> describe this combination. Membrane-forming <phospholipids> are examples; their two regions give different parts of one <molecule> different affinities for <water>. The word describes a structure, not a guarantee of a particular aggregate shape. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/A00303[IUPAC Gold Book: amphiphilic].

= Hydrophobicity
{parent=Physical chemistry}
{wiki}

= Hydrophobic
{synonym}

<Hydrophobicity> describes unfavorable mixing with <water>, as illustrated by long <hydrocarbon> regions. The <hydrophobic> regions of membrane-forming <phospholipids> face the interior of a <lipid bilayer>. This organization is explained by the <hydrophobic effect>, rather than by imagining that chains actively repel <water>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/H02907[IUPAC Gold Book: hydrophobic interaction].

= Hydrophobic effect
{parent=Hydrophobicity}
{wiki}

The <hydrophobic effect> is the solvent-mediated tendency of <nonpolar> regions to associate in <water>. Exposing a <hydrocarbon> region changes how surrounding <water> <molecules> can arrange and interact; reducing that exposure can lower the total <free energy>. Both <water> and <lipids> enter the accounting. The effect is not an additional <covalent bond> between chains. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/H02907[IUPAC Gold Book: hydrophobic interaction].

At fixed <temperature> and <pressure>, compare two candidate organizations by the change in <Gibbs free energy>, $\Delta G=\Delta H-T\Delta S$, where $H$ is <enthalpy>, $T$ is absolute <temperature>, and $S$ is <entropy>. An organization with lower $G$ is favored in this comparison. Reducing exposed <hydrocarbon> area is one contribution; changes in chain packing and the <entropy> of the <lipids> also matter. This explains why <hydrophobicity> alone cannot predict every aggregate shape. See <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/16.4 Free Energy>.

= Absorbance
{parent=Physical chemistry}
{title2=$A$}
{wiki}

<Absorbance> is the <dimensionless> quantity $A=\log_{10}(P_0/P)$, where $P_0$ and $P$ are reference and transmitted radiant <powers> at the chosen <wavelength>. For example, $A=1$ means one tenth of the reference <power> is transmitted. Under the <Beer-Lambert law>, <absorbance> can determine an absorbing species' <concentration>; attenuation by cloudiness or unrelated colored <solutes> needs separate controls. https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/B00626[IUPAC Gold Book: absorbance and its concentration relation]{ref}

= Beer-Lambert law
{c}
{parent=Absorbance}
{title2=$A=\varepsilon lc$}
{wiki=Beer–Lambert_law}

The <Beer-Lambert law> relates <absorbance> $A$ to an absorbing species' <concentration> $c$ and path length $l$ by $A=\varepsilon lc$ in a homogeneous medium using sufficiently narrow-band light. The coefficient $\varepsilon$ depends on the species, <wavelength> and <chemical solution> conditions. This makes <Ellman's reagent> useful for quantitative <thiol> assays once its released colored species has been calibrated. https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/B00626[IUPAC Gold Book: Beer-Lambert law and validity conditions]{ref}

= Thermochemistry
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

= Hess's law
{c}
{parent=Thermochemistry}
{wiki}

<Hess's law> states that compatible <chemical reaction> <enthalpies> add when their equations add. Reversing a <chemical reaction> reverses its <enthalpy> change; multiplying the entire <chemical reaction> equation by a factor multiplies that change by the same factor. The reason is that <enthalpy> is a <state function>: intermediate states cancel from the differences. A cycle supplies one independent balance relation; it cannot determine two unknown inputs without further information.

= Standard enthalpy of formation
{parent=Thermochemistry}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Delta H_{\rm f}^{\circ}$}

The <standard enthalpy of formation> is the <enthalpy> change for forming one <mole> of a specified <chemical substance> from its <chemical elements> in their reference states at the stated <temperature>. For <sodium chloride> at $298.15\,\mathrm K$, those reactant states are <solid> <sodium> and diatomic gaseous <chlorine>. <Standard state> specifies conditions; it does not by itself select a <temperature>.

= Enthalpy of atomization
{parent=Thermochemistry}
{title2=$\Delta H_{\rm at}^{\circ}$}

An <enthalpy of atomization> is the <enthalpy> change for making one <mole> of separated gaseous <chemical atoms> from a specified starting <chemical substance>. For <sodium>, the relevant step is <sublimation> to monatomic <gas>; for diatomic <chlorine>, it is half the compatible molar <bond-dissociation energy> expressed as an <enthalpy>.

= Enthalpy change of solution
{parent=Thermochemistry}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Delta H_{\rm sol}$}

= Enthalpy of solution
{synonym}

An <enthalpy of solution> is an <enthalpy> difference for dissolution into a specified <solvent> at stated <temperature> and final <concentration>. Keep a measured finite-dilution value distinct from an infinite-dilution reference value.

= Mass spectrometry
{parent=Chemistry}
{wiki}

<Mass spectrometry> separates and detects <ions> according to <mass-to-charge ratio>. It can distinguish <isotopes> which belong to the same <chemical element> but have different <atomic masses>. The instrument measures <ions>: interpreting the result as a neutral <atomic mass> requires accounting for <electron> removal and its <ionization energy>. For a singly positive <ion> in its <ground state>, $m_+=m_{\rm a}-m_e+I/c^2$, where $m_e$ is the <electron> <rest mass>, $I$ is the <ionization energy>, and $c$ is the <speed of light>.

For a simple illustrative analyzer, positive <ions> of <mass> $m$ and <electric charge> $q=ze$ gain <kinetic energy> $qV=mv^2/2$ across an <electric potential difference> $V$ before entering a transverse uniform <magnetic field> $B$. The <Lorentz force> supplies <centripetal acceleration>, so
$$
qvB=\frac{mv^2}{r},\qquad\frac{m}{q}=\frac{B^2r^2}{2V}.
$$
At fixed $V$ and $B$, the measured bending radius $r$ separates <mass-to-charge ratios>, not <masses> independently of <electric charge>. This ideal model assumes negligible initial <kinetic energy> and speeds small compared with the <speed of light>; the historical focusing <mass spectrograph> used the geometry described below.

= Mass spectrograph
{parent=Mass spectrometry}

A <mass spectrometry> instrument with a photographic readout. In the 1919 apparatus described in <Isotopes by Francis William Aston/Chapter V: The Mass-Spectrograph>, a <gas> discharge produced positive <ions>. Two narrow slits formed a ribbon beam in a <vacuum>; plates applied an <electric field> to spread it, an aperture selected part of it, and a <magnetic field> bent it oppositely. Focusing reduced the spread caused by differing <ion> <speeds>, concentrating a given <mass-to-charge ratio> into a line on a photographic plate.

The observed quantity was line displacement from a fiducial spot. Known reference lines calibrated displacement against <mass-to-charge ratio>; repeated exposures with changed <magnetic field> tested the calibration. Evacuation limited collisions. Comparing line shapes with narrow and wide apertures helped distinguish geometric broadening from photographic spreading. Finite slit widths, plate response, and overlapping molecular or multiply charged <ions> limited interpretation. See <Isotopes by Francis William Aston/Chapter V: The Mass-Spectrograph> (§§33–40: apparatus and photographic procedure; §§43 and 45–46: calibration, aperture comparisons, and accuracy).

In <Mass spectra and isotopes by Francis William Aston> (Nobel lecture, 12 December 1922), the reported <neon> lines at 20 and 22 have companions at 10 and 11 from doubly charged <ions>. That paired pattern and comparison with reference lines support different <atomic masses> within one <chemical element>, rather than an <atomic mass> concentrated at the bulk average. The lecture also describes an earlier failed separation by distillation and partial separation by <diffusion>, showing why the focused <mass spectrum> supplied stronger evidence. These numbers used a historical <oxygen>-based reference scale. The lecture's whole-number rule was an empirical approximation at its stated accuracy, not the modern exact definition $A=Z+N$.<Mass spectra and isotopes by Francis William Aston>[pp. 9–13: separation attempts, focusing, reference lines, charge orders, and neon; pp. 15–17: whole-number rule and historical nuclear interpretation]{ref}

= Mass-to-charge ratio
{parent=Mass spectrometry}
{title2=$m/q$}
{wiki}

The ratio of an <ion>'s <mass> to its <electric charge>; in <SI> it has units $\mathrm{kg/C}$. A convenient numerical <mass spectrum> coordinate is $x=(m/\mathrm u)/z$, with $q=ze$ and positive integer $z$ here. This $x$ is <dimensionless>. Doubling $z$ at fixed <mass> halves $x$, so the <electric charge> state must be identified before assigning an <isotope>.

= Mass spectrum
{parent=Mass spectrometry}
{wiki}

= Mass spectra
{synonym}

A <mass spectrum> displays detected signal against <mass-to-charge ratio>. Peak positions identify candidate <ions>; peak areas give <isotopic abundances> only after the response and overlapping contributions have been accounted for.

\Image[neon-synthetic-mass-spectrum-weighted-mean.png]
{title=Synthetic <neon> <mass spectra> and the <weighted mean> of neutral <atomic masses>}
{description=This is a model, not a measured <mass spectrum> or a reconstruction of Aston's plate. All <ions> have $z=+1$; neutral <atomic masses> approximate <ion> <masses>. Chosen <Gaussian functions> have unit area and widths $\sigma=0.04$ and $0.70$ in the <dimensionless> coordinate $x$. Equal responses make component areas equal the displayed reference <isotopic abundances>. The inset reveals the small <neon-21> peak. The bottom diagram places the <weighted mean> between the component <atomic masses>; it is not an additional <isotope>.}

Broadening can hide a weak component without changing its area in this ideal model. An actual <mass spectrum> can also contain molecular <ions>, different <electric charge> states, background, and unequal detection responses. A missing visible peak therefore cannot by itself prove the absence of an <isotope>. Conversely, a fractional <atomic weight> is a property of a population: there is no requirement for an <ion> peak at the <weighted mean>.

= Mass resolution
{parent=Mass spectrometry}
{title2=$R=x/\Delta x$}
{wiki}

= Mass resolving power
{synonym}

<Mass resolving power> compares a peak coordinate $x$ with a stated width or separation criterion $\Delta x$. With $\Delta x$ taken as <full width at half maximum>, a <Gaussian function> of <standard deviation> $\sigma$ has
$$
\Delta x=2\sqrt{2\log2}\,\sigma,\qquad R=\frac{x}{\Delta x}.
$$
At the <neon-20> coordinate in the synthetic <mass spectrum>, $\sigma=0.04$ gives $\Delta x\approx0.0942$ and $R\approx212$, while $\sigma=0.70$ gives $\Delta x\approx1.648$ and $R\approx12.1$. The width criterion and relative peak areas both matter when judging whether a small neighboring <isotope> can be distinguished. <Mass resolution> also differs from the accuracy of a calibrated peak position.
